**WHOEVER HEARS YOU HEARS ME**

**DOGMATIC AUTHORITY AS TESTIMONY**

James Dominic Rooney

---

<!-- PDF page 2 -->
**[p. i]**

Many oppose dogma fearing that heresy accusations lead to violence; similarly, opposition to claims of infallible church authority stems from fears of fanaticism, closed-mindedness, and blind obedience. The book justifies dogma while firmly opposing violence, fanaticism, closed-mindedness, and mindless deference.

Through philosophical reasoning, this title demonstrates that Christian theists who reject dogma or dogmatic authority ultimately adopt self-defeating positions. These insights reveal why Christians should unite to restore communion within one holy, catholic, and apostolic church. The work presents a fresh perspective, showing that anathemas and dogmatic authority are essential elements of Christian charity, even toward those who are anathematized. Additionally, it advances ecumenical dialogue by demonstrating how the best of various Christian theological traditions that uphold Scripture’s truth and inspiration can find fulfillment in the restoration of an undivided Church.

The book will appeal to scholars of theology, philosophy of religion, and ecclesiology.

**James Dominic Rooney**, OP, is Assistant Professor of Philosophy at Hong Kong Baptist University (HKBU), a fellow of the Angelicum Thomistic Institute (Rome, Italy), and research fellow of the HKBU Centre for Sino-Christian Studies. A Dominican Friar of the Province of St. Albert the Great (Chicago, IL), he works primarily in metaphysics, medieval philosophy, and Chinese philosophy. He also has significant research interests in philosophy of religion and political philosophy. His most recent books are _Beyond Classical Liberalism: Freedom and the Good_ (2024, co-edited with Patrick Zoll, Routledge) and _Not a Hope in Hell_ (2024, Routledge).

# Preface

And this is eternal life, that they may know You, the only true God, and Jesus Christ whom You have sent.

(John 17:3)

The goal of the Christian life is achieving union with God forever, sharing in God’s life in intimate personal experience where we will know as we are known (1 Cor. 13:12). The means to that goal is loving trust in the person of Jesus Christ and in what He has revealed to us. Christian doctrine aims to work out the nature of these claims and their implications for Christian life. The great doctrines of the Trinity, the Incarnation, the Atonement, the nature of justification and predestination, and many others arose from deep reflection on these claims – especially in response to those who have rejected them or interpreted them in such a way that apparently entail a truly different version of the Gospel message from that to which the orthodox tradition thought itself committed. That orthodox tradition went further than simply calling attention to these variations: that tradition understood there to be a moral requirement that Christians _reject_ those variations as undermining the possibility of the Christian life – these variations led one toward something else than loving trust in the person of Jesus Christ and in what He reveals. Consequently, that tradition also understood the leaders and teachers of the Church to have the authority requisite to reject such variations in the name of the Church itself.

Nevertheless, there are a significant number of Christians today and in the past who have questioned whether the notion of “orthodoxy” is at all meaningful for Christian life, or whether the focus on right doctrinal belief is itself a serious impediment to living the Christian life. Many of these attitudes have their remote origin in the Protestant Reformation, even though the Reformers were usually as committed to dogmatic religion as their Catholic opponents. It is uncontroversial that a key part of what the Reformers rejected was the authoritative claims made by the Catholic Church and its leaders to represent an infallible doctrinal authority and the true Church, outside of which their own movement would be considered a heretical and schismatic body. The aftermath of the Reformation, however, almost from the moment of its inception, spawned in its wake a whole slew of Protestant church bodies which disagreed among themselves on fundamental issues of

---

<!-- PDF page 8 -->
**[p. vii]**

dogmatic belief. The phenomenon of a divided Christian world, without an authoritative dogmatic arbiter, put pressure on Protestants very quickly to minimize requirements that Christians be united in professing the same dogma.

Many movements which have since grown up from the Reformation and which explicitly call for the rejection of any requirements for doctrinal orthodoxy end in calling for the wholesale rejection of dogmatic religion. Historically, those strands of Protestant thought came to their initial ecclesial incarnation as a group in, e.g., the Pietists (e.g., among 16th- and 17th-century Lutherans). Later centuries worked out the same spirit into Deism and Liberal theology, being unbeholden to the exterior forms of traditional Protestant belief in Scriptural infallibility or inerrancy, even going so far as to reject the possibility of supernatural revelation. The natural logical place that the principles end up is in a general skeptical attitude toward religious belief that is indistinguishable from atheism or agnosticism. That is, at least, what I will show – by way not of historical argument, but by way of inference from the nature of what it would be for God to speak to us and for us to thereby acquire moral obligations to believe His divine speech. Rejecting those moral obligations which result from divine speech involves an implicit assumption that divine speech or supernatural revelation is impossible.

This present work, however, is not anti-Protestant. Clearly, Protestants of a classical type do not reject the possibility of revelation or its moral demands. Nevertheless, many Protestants today employ arguments against Catholic or Orthodox positions which – I will show – are ultimately self-defeating. These arguments imply that the general skepticism of the agnostic is the right religious attitude, and thus are incompatible with Protestant belief that God’s speech in Scripture makes moral demands upon us. However, those who make such arguments often do not perceive this tension, as they take arguments against _dogma_ and _dogmatic authority_ to be directed against human speech that would arrogate the place of God. It is not hard to see why dogma and dogmatic authority look problematic for someone who upholds the unique authority of God, where obedience to God involves distinct moral obligations beyond that of any creature. Nevertheless, the principled rejection of dogmatic _authority_ naturally puts pressure on belief that _dogma_ is important for living rightly as a Christian. For, if we have no requirements to believe what anyone tells us the dogmas are, it seems foolish or cruel to insist that failures to believe those dogmas according to whatever a given fallible person (or their respective church body) says should be a matter that plausibly leads to eternal separation from Christ. Indeed, there is more than a little tension here, precisely because _dogmas_ are not directly found in any Scriptural text – dogmas are necessarily _invented,_ formulated at a particular time, usually in view of a particular theological controversy. If it were true that nothing could function as an _authority_ in matters of faith except God Himself, and nobody could bind us to believe anything in the way that God’s words bind us to believe, then no dogmatic claim or creed – crafted by human beings, not God Himself – would be able to bind us in conscience either.

We cannot ignore the way in which even Christian faith is shaped by the traditions in which it has developed. Dogma necessarily _adds_ to what is in Scripture, since dogmatic claims involve interpretation of the texts that they cite, and often

---

<!-- PDF page 9 -->
**[p. viii]**

point to meta-interpretative principles of interpretation. A dogmatic statement built solely of phrases drawn directly from Scripture represents an interpretation of those Scriptural passages as much as any classical creed, since it puts them together intentionally with a certain order and priority. For instance, by highlighting the claims from John 3, I have already implied that these texts, rather than others, are relevant to the concerns I have raised about dogma. A dogmatic claim is necessarily an interpretative one. However, it does not thereby follow that every dogmatic claim is _falsifying._ The present irenic aim of this work is to bring the debate back, as it were, to the original intuitions around dogma and dogmatic authority. The initial positions involved crucial ambiguities that overshadowed a deeper source of agreement on matters of dogmatic authority that can point a way forward for Christians today to re-discover grounds for ecclesial unity. What I will argue is that Protestants are themselves also committed both to dogma and dogmatic authority in virtue of their belief in biblical inspiration. The upshot of what I will argue is that the differences between Protestant views and those of Catholic or Orthodox Christians lie not primarily in any issue concerning whether there is dogmatic authority.

The present essay aims to be a work of _philosophical theology_ which explores the logical interconnections between dogma and dogmatic authority. By that I mean to disclaim the project of offering interpretations of specific Scriptural passages, or of engaging in extensive historical work on the origins of Christian doctrine, or of the controversies that gave birth to the Christian creeds and conciliar declarations, or of working out the Scriptural foundations of those dogmas that came to be normative for the orthodox tradition, and the like. My project is rather to show that the Christian faith is a dogmatic religion and thereby committed to dogmatic authority by appeal to philosophical considerations. The project assumes such a methodology because disagreements about dogma and dogmatic authority center not around particular Scriptural passages but the way in which we ought to read Scripture – they are disagreements about the nature of Scriptural inspiration, and so of the way that Scripture has authority over believers, as well as the implications of these beliefs for the way we should go about interpreting Scripture. For that reason, engaging in disputes over Scripture passages would often be useless, since the parties do not agree on the nature of Scriptural interpretation itself.

But progress can be made _philosophically,_ since parties in the dispute are both committed to theism and to other general rules of logical reasoning about theology. Specifically, I intend to show that theistic positions which call for principled rejection of dogma and/or dogmatic authority are self-defeating for Christian theists to embrace. Arguments against the possibility of dogmatic authority are rather arguments for atheist skepticism than arguments against Catholic or Orthodox doctrinal positions, despite the way they are often presented and understood. And the parties are committed to rejecting atheist skepticism, if they are to continue as Christian theists. Consequently, progress is possible by taking a higher perspective on our differences.

I cannot claim much originality in the perspective that I will take, as my perspective derives fundamentally from John Henry Newman, a well-known theological controversialist of the last century. Initially, Newman’s own sympathies lay

---

<!-- PDF page 10 -->
**[p. ix]**

with the Evangelical movement and its anti-dogmatic prejudices, before he nevertheless ended up as the leader of the Anglo-Catholic movement in the Church of England and later converted to Catholicism. Much of the impetus for his series of conversions lay in the fundamental commitment that Christianity was _true._ Upon receiving the cardinalate, he reflected that his whole life had been spent in resisting “to the best of his powers” the cause of a pernicious view that was spreading among his contemporaries and which he felt would become the great opponent of Christianity in the years after his death. He famously described this modern pernicious doctrine as being the view that

there is no positive truth in religion, but that one creed is as good as another, and this is the teaching which is gaining substance and force daily. It is inconsistent with any recognition of any religion, as _true_. It teaches that all are to be tolerated, for all are matters of opinion. Revealed religion is not a truth, but a sentiment and a taste; not an objective fact, not miraculous; and it is the right of each individual to make it say just what strikes his fancy. Devotion is not necessarily founded on faith. Men may go to Protestant Churches and to Catholic, may get good from both and belong to neither. They may fraternise together in spiritual thoughts and feelings, without having any views at all of doctrine in common, or seeing the need of them. Since, then, religion is so personal a peculiarity and so private a possession, we must of necessity ignore it in the intercourse of man with man. If a man puts on a new religion every morning, what is that to you? It is as impertinent to think about a man’s religion as about his sources of income or his management of his family. … [Religion] is a private luxury, which a man may have if he will; but which of course he must pay for, and which he must not obtrude upon others, or indulge in to their annoyance.[^c00-1]

So too my perspective shares with Newman the conclusion that there is _truth_ in religion, and that the facts about God revealing Himself to us demand a dogmatic conception of religion. In addition, these facts naturally entail a dogmatic conception of religious _authority_, leaving as an empirical matter which authority God has so endowed to represent His interests upon earth, although I will suggest that here too the grounds for decision are rather limited. In sum, I propose that Newman’s famous disjunction is logically sound and that the options for Christian theists are mutually exhaustive and exclusive: atheism or a dogmatic Christianity, with an infallible Church, are the only choices.[^c00-2]

Now, this requires being clear at the onset that this work also aims at endorsing an irenic and _ecumenical_ approach to Christian theology that reconciles or appeals to various theological perspectives. This seems otherwise inconsistent with the disjunctive conclusion. However, my perspective on Catholicism has much in common with the ecumenical movement that Newman himself inspired. The Catholic and Orthodox churches have largely acknowledged that their differences are not as profound as they otherwise seem, but continue to work corporately toward the restoration of full communion, rather than new rounds of anathemas

---

<!-- PDF page 11 -->
**[p. x]**

or proselytizing each other’s members. Protestants today too have found much to treasure in what they inherited from an undivided Church, and many Protestant church bodies have ended up in ongoing theological dialogue with Catholics and Orthodox, making significant progress toward resolving church-dividing disagreements. This work indirectly furthers these ecumenical aims by showing that the best of the Protestant theological tradition – insisting on the _truth_ and _inspiration_ of Sacred Scripture – finds its fulfillment in the future restoration of an undivided Church. I suggest that we might disagree not so much on the existence of dogmatic authority as to the way that such authority ought to be exercised within a restored future communion. Discovering these facts then shows us we are united in our commitment to the restoration of communion within one, holy, _catholic,_ and apostolic Church.

In pursuit of clarifying the philosophical case that will be at the core of this book’s project, I will nevertheless refrain from calling attention to historical issues arising from Protestant Reformers or Newman or other figures in Church history or their theology. Instead, I will focus on defense of _dogma_ and of _dogmatic authority_ from basic claims common to a wide variety of Christians. These claims are indeed shared by theists beyond the Christian tradition. The book deals essentially with three issues. The first is whether there are dogmatic beliefs, by which I mean central truths proposed to us by God in revelation, belief in which would be necessary for our salvation and therefore morally obligatory. Chapters 1 and 2 constitute the prolegomenon to what follows, as these establish relevant facts about, respectively, the nature of faith and the moral demands made by revelation. The second issue concerns whether there is dogmatic authority that can define dogmas. Chapter 3 argues that such infallible authority follows from the nature of faith in what God reveals in Scripture and its moral demands. Disagreements about that authority concern not whether it exists but the way in which that authority is exercised in the post-apostolic Church. I will show that these disagreements are tractable in terms of plausible beliefs concerning the nature of faith itself. Chapter 4 identifies the moral fault involved in heresy and argues that Christians of all stripes should appeal to dogmatic authorities as the primary criterion of legitimacy in doctrinal developments. Chapter 5 will examine and apply these criteria to two controversial dogmatic developments (the papacy and Mariological dogmas), proposing an ecumenical case for their reception, and thus for moving beyond dogmatic authority as a primary locus of Church-dividing issues.

The fundamental justification I offer for dogma and dogmatic authority lies in our Christian belief that Christ is the Truth which we preach. God can neither deceive nor be deceived; He reveals Himself to us so that we can know and accept truths important for our salvation. God is also Love. Truth and love thus coincide ultimately and fundamentally. To pursue the truth, which is love, cannot be in principle a matter of violence, fanaticism, or mindless deference. Much opposition to the notion of dogma comes in fears that accusations of heresy or anathemas are inherently violent, unfitting for Christians whose highest vocation is to love others as God Himself loves them, just as opposition to infallible dogmatic authority tend to arise from fears of fanaticism, close-mindedness,

---

<!-- PDF page 12 -->
**[p. xi]**

and mindless deference to human authority or tradition, unfitting for Christians whose obedience is properly devoted to God alone, an obedience which requires both rationality and freedom. This argument of this book justifies dogma but not violence, fanaticism, close-mindedness, or mindless deference. Faith is none of these things. Rather, our highest vocation as Christians is to preach the truth and help others to accept it in fuller freedom. If the argument of this book is correct, we can acquire a new perspective on which anathemas and dogmatic authority are constitutive components of Christian _charity_ toward others, including the anathematized themselves.

## Notes

[^c00-1]: John Henry Newman, “The Biglietto Speech, Rome,” in _Addresses to Cardinal Newman with His Replies, etc. 1879–81,_ ed. W. P. Neville (London: Longmans, Green, and Co., 1905), 64–67.

[^c00-2]: See further Edward Kelly, “Atheism or Catholicism,” in _John Henry Newman,_ eds. M. Allsopp and R. Burke (New York: Garland Science, 1992).

# 1. Divine Speech and Faith

Now faith is the substance of things hoped for, the evidence of things not seen. For by it the elders obtained a good testimony. By faith we understand that the worlds were framed by the word of God, so that the things which are seen were not made of things which are visible. … But without faith it is impossible to please Him, for he who comes to God must believe that He is, and that He is a rewarder of those who diligently seek Him.

(Heb. 11:1–3, 6)

“Dogma” is an ancient Greek term, adapted through Latin to modern English, referring originally to belief or judgment. The term appears in the New Testament as referring to decrees whether from civil or religious authorities, such as the decree sent out by Caesar Augustus (Luke 2:1) or the commandments of the Mosaic Law (Eph. 2:15) or the decrees sent out by the apostles from Jerusalem regarding the expectations for Gentile converts to Christianity (Acts 16:4). The development of this latter usage arrives at the notion of judgments proposed by authority which their subjects are to adopt. The term “dogma” has since become a technical one for theologians, where “dogma” refers to beliefs regarding central truths of the Christian religion, proposed as definitively to be held by theological authorities.[^c01-1] Dogmas, in short, are public judgments about truths that are essential to Christian faith. This book aims to defend that dogmas are essential to the Christian life. Since dogmas are essentially public judgments about truths that ought to be believed, we first need to explore whether there are any such truths and the way in which those truths ought to be believed by us. What the first two chapters lay out is a picture in which Christian faith is inherently _dogmatic faith_. Let me say a bit more about this notion.

It is or should be uncontroversial that the Christian life – that spiritual path which the Christian religion aims to promote – is a life of faith. What I will argue in this chapter is that Christian faith necessarily involves a _dogmatic_ notion of belief. By “dogmatic,” I mean to argue that the following are among essential or necessary properties of Christian faith: faith involves [1] belief in truths, [2] based on authority of a testifier, and [3] moral obligations to hold by faith those truths proposed for our belief. If these properties are essential to Christian faith, or at least

DOI: 10.4324/9781003663485-1

---

<!-- PDF page 15 -->
**[p. 2]**

a necessary condition of it, then it would follow that life which Christians aim to live – following God in faith – relies essentially on dogmatic belief. While many Christians would admit that faith involves these three characteristics, this chapter aims to address skepticism about the nature of faith, which would deny that we can possibly make sense of Christian faith as belief in truths, etc. However, I am not going to respond to all possible skepticism about the _rationality_ of acts of faith, such as might involve questions of the motive of faith and its connection to evidence, but rather only argue that the Christian conception of faith would make sense if it were true that God spoke to us.

In short, I will defend the possibility that God can speak to us in the way required by Christian faith, and that, if He did, then dogmatic faith would be both morally and intellectually virtuous. If God could not speak to us, or belief in what God said were impossible, faith would not be possible, and so the Christian life of faith would be a non-starter. But it _is_ possible that God speaks to us, and that we would be morally obligated to believe what God says to us, simply because He does. Believing what God says would be virtuous. Consequently, dogmatic faith would be good for us if God speaks to us, and so the Christian religion of dogmatic faith is a possibly virtuous, even morally requisite, way of life. These claims about the nature of faith will be the basic premises which set up the structure of the rest of the book, where I will argue that the nature of faith itself entails facts about the existence and extent of dogmatic authority in the Christian Church, such that, e.g., questions of infallibility devolve upon questions regarding our obligations to accept and publicly witness to God’s word.

## Personal Belief in Personal Truths

It will be my contention that faith necessarily involves _belief in truths_, and that this kind of belief is a constituent of that virtuous intellectual and moral attitude which is theological faith. In what follows, I will give a few arguments that belief in truths is important for developing a personal relationship with God. Our trusting attitude toward God, which constitutively involves trusting what God says, is instrumentally valuable, deriving its value from faith being a necessary means (on our side) for achieving relationship with God. What I am doing is not laying out necessary and sufficient conditions for faith in any sense, but simply an essential characteristic of that core and paradigmatic Christian notion of faith which is widely open to ecumenical agreement. This line of argument does not rule out that other features of faith are also valuable, nor do my arguments rule out alternative conceptions of faith that would be compatible with faith’s role in promoting personal relationship with God. Faith is dogmatic in that it essentially or necessarily involves attitudes toward truths, but this aspect need not exhaust the nature of faith. Faith might therefore be characterized as promoting certain behavior or as involving other aspects of trust or fidelity or commitment. If these are compatible with faith involving the dogmatic elements of belief, there is nothing in these further characterizations that is incompatible with what I am arguing.

---

<!-- PDF page 16 -->
**[p. 3]**

Scripture comes closest to defining faith in claiming that faith is “substance of things hoped for, the evidence of things not seen” (Heb. 11:1). It goes on to characterize faith as a kind of confidence, as aimed at what is hoped for and unseen. What is hoped for by faith is unseen in a few senses. First, the goal of faith remains unobtained in this life. The reward spoken of in Heb. 11, the goal of Christian faith, is characterized in Scripture with the metaphor of face-to-face relationship with God – “for now we see in a mirror, dimly, but then face to face. Now I know in part, but then I shall know just as I also am known” (1 Cor. 13:12). Traditional Christian theology cashed this out in terms of the “Beatific Vision,” a direct experience of God’s own knowledge of Himself, in which the believer comes to participate after their death. Further, while faith involves having reasons for its hope or belief that can be communicated with others (cf. 1 Pet. 3:15), the evidence for the hope of faith is _identified_ with faith itself in Heb. 11.

There might be more complicated biblical evidence, but the _prima facie_ sense of this passage in English points to a well-known puzzle for the rationality of faith: evidence for faith can appear viciously circular. If the evidence for faith is faith itself, it seems we have no evidence for our faith prior to or independent of our belief. That seems to be just another way of saying that faith has no reasons, or that we believe simply because we do! To avoid these implications, there has been wide debate about the extent to which faith requires evidence that would warrant our faith as rationally credible.[^c01-2] Now, it is not my business in this book to work out an account of the rational warrant of faith. Instead, I will simply note that whatever evidence we have for theological faith as Christians, regardless of the way that such evidence is supposed to provide rational warrant for faith, any such evidence is nevertheless going to be _external_ to the object of faith itself on the model of faith I take to be central to Christianity – and for good reason. “Motives of credibility” is the traditional term given to external evidence that faith is rationally warranted. Such motives of credibility are not conceived of as evidence _directly_ in favor of the truth of whatever God says to us, but concern evidence which testifies or makes evident that _God_ has spoken to us. Motives of credibility purport to be instances of evidence that God, who cannot speak falsely, has testified to the truth of what we hold by faith. Clearly, then, motives of credibility are necessarily only _indirect_ evidence for the truth of what we believe.

An obvious case arises from the context of St. Paul’s claim that we wait in expectation for fulfillment of God’s promises to resurrect the dead (cf. 2 Cor. 4:18). Clearly, the resurrection has not presently occurred – it is presently “unseen.” We expect the resurrection of the dead not because, e.g., we have evidence that the physical laws will necessarily produce this outcome. Rather, that this event will occur is something only God could know, as only God could know future contingent events like these. And we believe that it will occur on the strength of being _told_ that it will occur by God. But the same is obviously true as well about the Beatific Vision. Knowing that God wants to make this experience of His own self possible for us to achieve after we die is not something we expect because we have evidence that it is necessarily the case that everyone will experience God’s self-knowledge, given some fact of the universe. Instead, God promised that those who believe in

---

<!-- PDF page 17 -->
**[p. 4]**

Him become children of God entitled to such an experience (cf. Jn. 1:12). Having saving faith thus involves expecting to see God, and to live forever (cf. Jn. 11:25), simply on the strength of God’s say-so. To believe these things on some other basis would seemingly be foolishness. For instance, it would be foolish to expect this to happen naturally, as that would require believing we are essentially divine in some way. But nobody is essentially divine who is not already experiencing God’s selfknowledge. I don’t know about you, dear reader, but most of us are in the latter class of those not currently experiencing beatific joy, fullness of life, and happiness without end.

Christians have generally accepted that the content of the Christian faith centrally concerns truths for which we can necessarily have no direct evidence aside from God’s testimony.[^c01-3] All the central dogmatic truths of Christianity are going to be of a character that they require belief in God’s testimony (and usually that a human prophet/apostle is testifying with God’s authority), because they could not otherwise be known. God could reveal things for which we have independent proof – such as His existence or the moral law – but these are not properly objects of faith, in the sense that we could have known them some other way. But the objects of faith properly speaking are those beliefs, like the belief that God is a Trinity or that Jesus is God incarnate, which could not be known to be true by us (strictly speaking) apart from God’s testimony. That there are potentially some such truths looks possible. And that such beliefs potentially concern what is valuable for us is also obvious: eternity with God would be immensely valuable, and, if faith in God’s promises of that eternal life were a condition of achieving it, then faith in those promises would be immensely valuable.

“Theological faith” is that disposition to believe God’s _speech_ (or testimony) and as such is the first necessary step to forming a relationship with God. We can work out from these considerations two further essential aspects of faith that relate to belief in truths. First, theological faith would require by its very nature that we believe in some _specific_ truths, rather than being simply a disposition to trust in a generic way, or even to have a pro-attitude toward God and religious beliefs. These aspects distinguish “theological faith” as a virtue from other kinds of trust or pro-attitudes, because they characterize the virtuous motivations that are essential to faith as virtue oriented toward God, rather than something else. That is, faith concerns trusting _God’s speech_. So, that requires some beliefs about both God and God’s speaking.[^c01-4]

Hebrews 11 thus also underlines the necessity of faith, as well as its core characteristics, in stating that “without faith it is impossible to please Him, for he who comes to God must believe that He is, and that He is a rewarder of those who diligently seek Him” (v. 6). Notice that this passage is explicit in stating that faith involves belief _that_ the following two claims are true: _that_ God exists, _that_ He rewards those who seek Him. Belief _that_ is a paradigm case of propositional belief of the sort I am alleging is necessary for Christian faith. We can see why that kind of propositional belief is constitutive of theological faith: faith requires at least two minimal beliefs _that_ so as to orient belief toward the appropriate object, one of which concerns _who_ is being trusted (God) and the other concerning _what_ is being

---

<!-- PDF page 18 -->
**[p. 5]**

trusted (God’s promises). Unless one knows that God exists, one could not trust God or expect anything from Him. Similarly, unless one knew that God cared about us or promised anything or spoke to us, one could not _believe God_.

Obviously, unless these beliefs were _true,_ we could not form a relationship with Him by means of faith. If it were false that there was a God or that He wanted to be our friend or reward those who seek Him, we would not really be _trusting God_ in believing any of those things. But for the same reason trusting God implies that we believe that God exists and that He cares about us or wants to be our friend. It would not be possible to trust God, such as to believe that I can form a friendship with Him, without those other beliefs. If I thought God did not really exist, and I nevertheless accepted typical Christian beliefs that I should live a good life or attend church services, or something like this, my reason for accepting these beliefs is not of the right sort to constitute an act by which I am _trusting God_. It might be _good_ for someone to prepare themselves for faith by attending church services or coming to find independent reasons to believe what Christians believe – but it would not be (theological) _faith_ to believe any of these things, or even _everything_ that Christians believe, as long as one believed these things for any reason other than “because I trust God, who has told us to believe them.” In short, then, faith is going to involve trusting God regarding what He says to us, because He has said it to us, and therefore is going to imply that I should also accept beliefs _that_ God exists, and _that_ God has said them.

However, we can also notice a second feature of the way faith relates to truths. Faith is an attitude to trust God in His interaction with and speech addressed to us, but that is compatible with a wide range of beliefs about _what_ God has said. The facts about what God has said to us are a contingent matter – we cannot know simply from facts that God _could_ speak to us, and that it would be good if He did, what God _would_ say. Clearly, God might also say different things at different times, and there might then be correspondingly different duties to have faith in what God says depending upon facts regarding our own particular situation relative to God’s acts in history (which I will discuss below). Nevertheless, the way our belief “aims” at God, rather than any specific things that He says, implies that what is essential to our trusting God can admit some leeway.

One way to put this is that there is a natural “hierarchy” of truths among what God reveals. Some truths are more central to the attitude of faith, being essential or necessary for us to believe about God for our faith to be rightly “ordered,” whereas others have a comparatively accidental relationship. Hebrews identified two essential truths that define the relevant order among the rest: facts about God’s nature (“that He exists”), and facts about what God intends regarding us and the way in which we interact with God (“that He rewards those who seek Him”). If one got these wrong, one would not have faith at all. In this respect, some beliefs pertain to the motivation of faith itself. And to lack these beliefs is to lack faith.

Yet there is a different sort of constraint arising from the _value_ of what God reveals. If the point of faith is to form relationship with God, then what God reveals will be integral to our process of forming an appropriate relationship with God. There are then natural constraints on what beliefs are more central to our faith

---

<!-- PDF page 19 -->
**[p. 6]**

arising from the _content_ of what God says. Some beliefs are obviously accidental to this end – as in knowing that an ephah was an ancient Jewish measure of volume is equivalent roughly to a space holding 432 hen’s eggs. While “ephah” appears in Scriptural texts, and we ought to believe what appears in Scripture on the basis that God has revealed it, it would look incidental to God’s purposes that we believe an ephah holds 432 hen’s eggs. So, belief in any such passage where “ephah” appears is not a passage aiming at God teaching us about volumetric measurements. Conversely, other beliefs very plausibly affect everything else God says to us and bear implications for how we achieve union with God, or achieve His promises. I call such central or hinge beliefs “dogmatic beliefs,” inasmuch as they pertain to the core of what God wants us to believe and bear logical implications for everything else in our theological faith, having a non-coincidental relationship with God’s purposes for our forming a relationship with Him.

For instance, it would make a serious difference whether Jesus is or is not God. Believing that Jesus is our Lord and God, and that nobody comes to God except through faith in Jesus’ person (cf. Jn. 14:6), would obviously be a central belief about how we achieve union with God. There are _many_ and _widespread_ implications of this belief. Among these is the moral or spiritual implication that we should either trust Jesus _as God,_ and therefore as an appropriate object of theological faith and worship, or trust Jesus _as a man who speaks for God,_ which would be incompatible with treating Jesus as an object of theological faith or worship. If Jesus were not God, to worship Him or have theological faith in Him could be to commit idolatry, a serious sin that impedes union with God. Or, if Jesus were a fallible human being, and not even someone who speaks for God as a prophet or messenger, we could also be easily led astray into error by trusting His words – and believing His words would not obviously either help us or be necessary for forming relationship with God.

These claims about Jesus’ person as an object of faith also point us in the direction of another insight into faith’s relation to truths. Faith is an attitude toward _persons_ primarily and propositions only secondarily – yet these do not easily come apart. That is, by faith, we not only believe these words on account of our trust in Him, but our faith _in Him_ consists in accepting _what He says._ If we did not accept what He says, we would not exemplify the right attitude toward God; conversely, if we have right trust in God, we will always accept what He says. Further, what it is to trust God involves trust in God’s _words,_ since the words are the vehicle or necessary means for us to form a relationship with God in the way that God wants for us. God’s words give us insight into God’s intentions or will, and that is what is important in faith. Yet we lack access to God directly, and so cannot perceive His intentions toward us except by means of words. We form a relationship with God by means of trust in His words spoken in time, a created intermediary, because it is necessary _for us_ to perceive God’s intentions in this way.

To put it simply, God reveals Himself in human language and concepts. So, if we want to trust what He says, we must accept human language and concepts as being able to derivatively “count” as God’s words under some relevant circumstances. Those words, although intrinsically stated in human language or concepts

---

<!-- PDF page 20 -->
**[p. 7]**

that we can understand or grasp as propositional truths, will count as God’s words. Faith would be unnecessary if we had direct access to God or what He promises; therefore, if God has made any such direct access (i.e., the “Beatific Vision”) conditional upon our prior free act of saving faith, then accepting in faith God’s word (embodied in finite sentences, concepts, and propositions) will be necessary if we are ever to see God and come to know what it is to be possessed of eternal life. God’s words then involve propositions – things that God _says_ – even if not everything God says to us is an assertion. And those propositional truths by which we assent to God and what He promises to us are rightly a part of faith, because it is necessarily _only_ by means of such truths that we could come to know and trust God in the way that He wants us to trust Him.

## Belief on Authority Is Not the Same as Belief in Evidence

Faith is first and foremost a _reactive_ attitude: a response to God’s interaction with us. If it is such a response, it is also an _intentional_ response to God’s _personal_ mode of communication, and therefore involves having intentions that imply truths about God and His own intentions toward us. Yet “faith” has also become roughly synonymous with “trust” or “confidence” in modern English – and for good reason. The letter to the Hebrews’ eulogy of various heroes of the faith lists behavior associated with faith: Abel offering sacrifice, Enoch being taken up, Noah building the Ark, Abraham leaving his own country, etc. In this respect, faith is obviously _more_ than belief in truths. Faith is action-guiding and _pro-active_. Virtuous confidence in truths that are action-guiding for forming a relationship with God will naturally support the formation of various other virtuous moral and epistemic attitudes. Virtues always bring friends. Still, faith cannot be _reduced_ to such behaviors as listed in Heb. 11, as faith is the virtue that underlies what made those behaviors praiseworthy. Specifically, those actions were praiseworthy because they were a _response_ on the part of the patriarchs to God’s word.

So, while the trusting behavior of the patriarchs is praiseworthy, what is more basic is their interior act of _assent_ to God’s word, as instanced in their acceptance of God’s commands and so forth, as this assent is what morally characterizes their intentional activity and makes it praiseworthy. Merely recognizing that God is speaking to them would not be the attitude of faith – and such an attitude receives censure elsewhere as the “faith of demons” (Jas. 2:19). Demons in the Gospels can identify with confidence God’s speech, or that Jesus Christ is the “holy one of God,” but “tremble” in being forced to accept what God says against their will. By contrast, then, morally praiseworthy or “saving” faith will involve accepting God’s word _willingly_. Being epistemically compelled to do so by the evidence presented would not be morally praiseworthy, as that is the sort of faith potentially available to demons. What makes the patriarch’s intentions praiseworthy is that they accept God’s word voluntarily, on account of some morally salient motive.

Clearly, if the demons can believe what Christians believe, and believe truly, not every kind of belief is virtuous, even when that belief pertains to truths of the Christian faith. By that same token, the value of that _specific attitude_, which is faith

---

<!-- PDF page 21 -->
**[p. 8]**

is not the same as the value of any belief in the truths of the faith. Nor is the value of faith reducible to the value of true belief in general. It is good to believe what is true, but Christian faith is valuable for reasons specific to the nature of the truths it takes as normative. This is not merely because these truths are about God. Rather, the value of dogmatic faith lies in the way that it involves a morally and intellectually virtuous response to God’s interaction with us. My account does not require denying that making an act of faith needs to be prompted by and sensitive to the evidence that the testifier to the belief has authority from God (e.g., “motives of credibility”), nor that what is believed by faith by some might be proven by others (e.g., theologians, philosophers). But it is incompatible with a view that “the assent of Christian faith is not free but is necessarily produced by arguments of human reason; or that the grace of God is necessary only for living faith which works by charity,”[^c01-5] and this is what is meant by saying that theological faith is not the same as conviction rationally necessitated by or motivated simply by the evidence. Two reasons have thus far been presented: [1] supernatural truths which are proper to theological faith are not in principle susceptible of direct proof, and [2] even if there were indirect evidence that rationally necessitated consent to those truths (such that it was strictly irrational to fail to consent in the face of that evidence), this would be insufficient to produce the faith at the core of Christian commitments, as Christian faith is motivated differently than the rationally necessitated conviction in supernatural truths which the tradition identifies as “faith of demons.”

Traditionally, it has been insisted that what makes saving faith praiseworthy is that it involves voluntary assent to God’s word _because of_ the fact it is _God’s_ word, and that this requires that faith is a gift of grace – God needs to _infuse_ faith into our hearts so that our wills are moved to assent to Him, in addition to whatever evidence we might have of His speaking to us.[^c01-6] There is an obvious way that such an assent would be epistemically praiseworthy. The omniscient and completely perfect being that is the cause of all truth could never be wrong or deceive anyone. It would be stupid to second-guess God and epistemically virtuous to accept what He says precisely because He says it. Traditionally, it is noted that faith has a special confidence surpassing the results of any other scientific mode of reasoning because, even if faith does not have evidence directly pertaining to the truth of what is believed, revelation by God would be _infallible._ Therefore, those who believe from faith are assured that they cannot be wrong in believing what God tells them, as what they believe is necessarily true. Faith provides an assurance or certainty which the letter to the Hebrews gives as one of its defining attributes: faith is “substance” or (some translate) “assurance” of things hoped for and what is presently unseen.

To say that faith is infallible is not exactly to say, however, that the believer is infallible or that the believer cannot have some subjective uncertainty in their belief. On the one hand, believers can be faced with difficulties in encountering evidence contrary to their faith and be unsure how to respond to these apparent “defeaters” for the rationality of what they believe. Or, believers might be confused about what _is_ required for their belief: they might, for instance, be confused about whether they ought to convert to another religion. In these cases, we can

---

<!-- PDF page 22 -->
**[p. 9]**

distinguish that faith does not require that believers are completely subjectively certain, such that no possible evidence could be counterevidence to their views or that they would be obligated to simply ignore all such evidence. Yet, they can still rationally believe God’s Word is infallibly objectively reliable, regardless of apparent counterevidence which might cause them (on its own weight) to be subjectively less-than-fully certain. Believers can acknowledge strong objections and even have difficulties in accepting a given belief proposed in faith. In this case, the evidence _for that belief_ is not apparent to them. But believers might still have good reasons to have theological faith under such apparent counterevidence.[^c01-7] As Newman puts it, “a thousand difficulties do not add up to one doubt.”[^c01-8]

On the other hand, even when we assume believers are not faced with recalcitrant or undermining evidence, believers make innocent mistakes in matters of the faith. Despite wanting or intending to believe God, a believer might come to believe something neither revealed by God nor true. Some of these are explainable by what they believe _in addition_ to the truths of the faith. For instance, a faithful Jewish person in the 1st century might have believed on December 26th that God’s promises to send the Messiah would be fulfilled – and would be infallibly correct in this belief – but nevertheless think that God would do so _in the future_, which would be false, since Christ had been born the day before. In this case, the human conjecture of the individual seems to have been added to the belief of faith, and nothing ensures that such conjectures or interpretations would have the infallibility attached to what God has revealed.[^c01-9]

And there are other errors of fact that seem to “fall under” faith in the sense that the believer was motivated to believe what they did precisely by faith. The believer rightly believes that God will change the bread and wine into the Body and Blood of Christ during Mass, when a validly ordained priest celebrates the sacrament. This believer could nevertheless make a mistake in thinking that _this_ bread is consecrated if the priest failed to celebrate the sacrament validly.[^c01-10] A more modern example is that the believer could believe that the faith is incompatible with a round earth, or an earth older than 6000 years, or with the truths taught in evolutionary biology. The believer could (falsely) believe that evolutionary biology requires assumptions on which atheism is true, or that human beings would not have immortal souls if it was true that they had an evolutionary history like other animals, or that the absence of miraculous intervention in evolutionary history would undermine any reason to believe that God was in control of that history and its outcomes. The believer would be wrong both in terms of their biology (since it is false that evolutionary biology assumes or implies that atheism is true) and in terms of their theology (since the biological facts do not, in fact, undermine what the faith teaches). These cases are much like that of the Jewish believer in the 1st century: the believer comes to interpret or believe something in addition to what is revealed, and _these_ auxiliary assumptions are what are false, not what they believe by faith.

Clearly, our faith remains objectively virtuous whether we make an innocent mistake. We can make innocent mistakes in other virtues. For instance, I can pick up a student’s bag left in the classroom, from good motivations, but accidentally return it to someone who is not its owner. Or, I can intend to take a pill for medical

---

<!-- PDF page 23 -->
**[p. 10]**

reasons and instead accidentally swallow an illegal narcotic drug. The intention in both cases is salient to their moral value, and that intention is not undermined by the fact that I can make mistakes. Similarly, it is the objective infallibility of God that underwrites why believing God always _aims at_ something that constitutes such a disposition as both virtuous and epistemically reliable, even when I make a mistake in identifying what God says.

Nevertheless, since such errors in fact seem to have been motivated by the believer’s faith itself, what use is it to say that faith is infallible? In some cases, the believer does not infallibly apply what God has said they ought to believe or do within concrete circumstances. But, in others, the mistakes in fact seem to undermine the objective assurance of faith, or its certainty, since the believers in question do not seem to be able to distinguish what we should believe by faith – i.e., what God has said – and what constitutes our own interpretation of what God says. Now, it looks too strong to insist that believers lack _any way_ to distinguish what they should believe by faith from their own opinions, since it looks like there are reasonable standards accessible to us regarding, for instance, better or worse evidence that God has spoken. There are, indeed, experts at biblical hermeneutics and other theological experts who can present arguments or evidence for their beliefs about what constitutes revelation and what it entails or otherwise involves.

Yet, even if we concede that the believer has _some_ way of coming to believe what they do that bears a reliable connection with what God says to them, it looks like the believer’s way of coming to their beliefs is necessarily by means of their own _fallible_ cognitive processes. For instance, the believer has used their own reasoning to discern that Scripture intends them to believe that the world was created by God from nothing. And experts make mistakes too. Thus, even experts in biblical hermeneutics arrived at their conclusions about revealed truths through intrinsically fallible capacities and processes. But then it appears as if we are right back to the initial problems that the confidence or assurance of faith could be rationally warranted only if it were proportional to the evidence, where belief that “I think God has revealed X” would be warranted to some degree, but belief that “God has revealed X” would be necessarily unwarranted. Faith thus could not in principle involve “assurance,” since no one who is rational can really believe more than that they _have good evidence that_ God has revealed or promised. Notice now that the objection rules out _de jure_ that the confidence of faith could have warrant or justification – it would be in principle impossible to come to believe that God has revealed anything to us, at least _rationally_.

One way to approach such objections is that they might rest on an assumption that the only evidence which would warrant concluding that God has in fact revealed or promised would require infallible evidence. Judgments that God has revealed Himself to us therefore necessarily involve going beyond the evidence. However, it is precisely this assumption that we should reject, for many reasons that have been provided in the literature on warrant or justification. If _knowledge_ would require infallible grounds for belief, this might undermine our knowledge of practically everything. Many think such criteria for knowledge or warrant or justification likely entail serious global skepticism, undermining all our knowledge

---

<!-- PDF page 24 -->
**[p. 11]**

whatsoever. That kind of position is self-defeating, if not just contrary to the plausible, intuitive, and apparently well-warranted belief that we _do_ know some things.[^c01-11] Consequently, without getting into much of the epistemological literature, we can reasonably reject the objector’s criteria for warrant or justification and propose a different set. Even if we had _infallible evidence_ for some beliefs, the only plausible candidates for that kind of infallible beliefs are e.g., laws of logic or facts about my own existence, and it would be impossible to _derive_ Christian faith from these.[^c01-12]

To refrain from getting bogged down in epistemological issues, I will not attempt to explain or justify the rationality of faith. There are other well-known works on the topic already and it is not necessary for my purposes, since it is an assumption common to Christians.[^c01-13] These first two chapters have the modest aim of laying out plausible commitments that Christians of a classical persuasion can accept about faith and normative obligations attaching to faith. I am not aiming to justify these commitments, and so, for that reason, will not review the extensive literature in religious epistemology or philosophy of religion around divine speech, nor the theological or scriptural data. Rather, I only propose and respond to common objections which aim to show that there is something inconsistent or _prima facie_ implausible about these assumptions that I take as the basis for the later arguments in favor of dogmatic authority. Those responses to objections are thus directly in service of showing that the assumptions which I lay out regarding faith ought to be relatively uncontroversial among the classically minded Christians my arguments address, which include mainstream Protestant evangelicals, Orthodox, and Catholic Christians.

A picture which I prefer holds that our beliefs have warrant or justification when they are properly formed through epistemic virtue.[^c01-14] (There are other epistemologies that can capture these same facts, so disagreement here will not necessarily affect the overall account of Christian faith.) Our beliefs that God has revealed Himself to us, and has revealed _x_ or _y_ to us, are appropriately justified or warranted because those beliefs proceed from a virtue: (theological) _faith_. While faith is not an epistemic faculty present in us from birth, traditional Christian theology holds that God “infuses” faith into us. That is, there is a _direct causal connection_ between God Himself and our being disposed to believe what God reveals. This certainly looks possible for God to do. Consequently, if God does exist, and He does infuse such a disposition, our disposition to believe what God reveals (because He reveals it) would be a disposition reliably delivering true judgments. Faith would be more objectively reliable than any other epistemic virtue, as God is an infallibly reliable testifier to truths, and He ensures we believe the right things.

Faith then involves properly basic and justified true beliefs in the situation where the believer is deferring to God. That is, given faith’s nature as infused by God, designed to produce infallibly true beliefs as long as the believer continues to rely appropriately on God’s testimony, the believer’s confidence that what they believe is infallibly the truth _would be_ warranted when and to the extent that the believer operates within the proper limits of that virtue. The proper limits or intended “operating environment” of faith, however, are different from perceptual or inferential faculties, as faith involves epistemic deference to God’s testimony and

---

<!-- PDF page 25 -->
**[p. 12]**

knowledge. That is, we can consider faith as a species of intellectual humility or a disposition to defer appropriately to expertise of others, where this “other” is God. But, given that God is omniscient and omnipresent, one can defer to God _at any time and place,_ in any environment. One might be mistaken about what constitutes God’s revelation in fact, but one would always be correct in holding by faith _that God cannot lie_ and that _if this is God’s word, I should believe it_.

Note, however, that this attitude of faith and its (essential) assurance or confidence that God will not lead the believer astray lies at a higher level than judgments of fact about particular articles of faith or their application, as it is precisely an attitude toward the revealer Himself rather than anything He reveals in particular. The epistemic humility proper to faith recognizes the limits of human knowledge regarding divine things, and this is what motivates the act in the first place. As I have explained, this does not mean that faith lacks any “evidence” at all. But evidence comes in different types and can be evaluated rationally at different levels. The believer is infallible and assured fundamentally in acknowledging their own inability to know the truth of what God reveals, independent of trust in Him, and so recognizes practically the difference between their own reasoning about what God reveals and trust on God’s authority, which is demanded even when they cannot understand all that God reveals. The believer having evidence _that_ a given belief is to be held with theological faith can be sufficient to make their acts of faith rational, even when they do not always understand reasons for or against various propositions they hold on faith. For instance, since the believer aims to defer to God given that recognition that they _could not otherwise know_ what God proposes for their belief except by trusting God, and that they will _never fully understand_ what God proposes for their belief, even if they make some progress in understanding what God proposes subsequent to accepting God’s word.[^c01-15] Judgments not so informed by or conditioned by their deference to God’s testimony, however, _ipso facto_ fail to fall under the infallibility proper to faith, since the believer did not make the judgment by appeal to God’s Word.

The final point to note about this attitude, then, is that the faithful believer is not solely concerned about avoiding false beliefs, as if engaged in a project of skeptical _epoche_ whereby they adopt a policy of refraining from judging rashly. Faith, as I have portrayed it, is personal trust in God, based on God’s testimony, and not skeptical suspension of judgment. But to say faith is “personal trust” is not only to say that we trust God’s person as what guarantees the truth of what God reveals. Recall that one of the two fundamental beliefs which were outlined as essential to faith by Hebrews 11: believing that God rewards those who seek Him. To have the right attitude that is theological faith, the believer needs to believe that, by their act of faith, they thereby _entrust themselves_ to God and His guidance. Faith is not adequately defined simply as assent to truths. The believer is motivated to assent to truths revealed, then, not because they have evidence which would epistemically compel them to assent to what God reveals, or evidence that God is necessarily trustworthy, since these kinds of evidence do not actually have a logical relationship to whether the believer ought to entrust themselves to God (rather than simply assent to what God reveals). Faith is assent

---

<!-- PDF page 26 -->
**[p. 13]**

motivated by the authority of God revealing, in the sense that the believer is motivated not by evidence but by a desire to attain what God promises: personal union with Him.[^c01-16] The motivation for faith is therefore that the believer wants to _respond_ to God in belief, so that their belief itself constitutes the beginning of a relationship with Him.

Augustine expresses a rational attitude when, in his _Confessions,_ he notes that “I would have found it easier to doubt whether I was myself alive than that there is no truth ‘understood from the things that are made’” (Rom. 1: 20).[^c01-17] While the direct context of his quotation might apply to his rejection of global skepticism more broadly, it also applies more narrowly to the assurance proper to those who have faith: they have an infallible basis for their confidence, as their confidence lies in God Himself and His testimony. Summarizing all the above aspects of the attitude of faith, the _Catechism of the Catholic Church_ nicely states: “Faith is man’s response to God, who reveals himself and gives himself to man, at the same time bringing man a superabundant light as he searches for the ultimate meaning of his life” (CCC 26). Faith is that by which God gives Himself to us, and thus is the “substance” of what is hoped for by faith, because faith itself constitutes the beginning of a union with God that the believer seeks to achieve by means of their belief. That “light” proper to an attitude of faith is then a confidence that the believer cannot go astray if they put their own judgments or opinions aside in favor of those provided by God’s word and entrust themselves to His guidance.

What I have provided is a picture of revelation as _testimony_, and faith as a species of _testimonial knowledge_, in which believers come to knowledge of important truths about God through trust of God. I have suggested similarly that believers can justifiably confident in their attitude of epistemic deference to divine testimony for two reasons: first, because as any such beliefs formed based on God’s word would be infallible, but also, second, because they have a justified or warranted confidence that God Himself will respond to them and guide them in their judgments, as long as they defer to Him. Borrowing from John McDowell, Wahlberg points out that the believer is thereby _sensitive_ to divine testimony, counting as a form of “doxastic responsibility.”[^c01-18] If the believer’s views of God in these matters are _true_, then the believer’s assurance in these matters is well-warranted. Wahlberg helpfully applies this account and shows how responses might be formed to objections that the believer believes irresponsibly in divine testimony and outlines how the account relates to evidential grounds such as miracles or the resurrection of Christ.[^c01-19] But the project of my book is neither to defend an account of justification nor of warrant, so I leave these aside. Rather, what I aim to build on is primarily the view that there are _normative duties_ following from such an attitude of faith. I highlight two kinds of duties in the next chapter, corresponding to normatively epistemic reasons and normatively moral reasons to defer to God’s testimony. Since my case for dogmatic authority rests primarily on the moral duties to defer to God’s testimony, and I take the moral duties to be more controversial, it is those that I will now discuss in greater detail.

---

<!-- PDF page 27 -->
**[p. 14]**

## Duties to Believe

The assurance of faith cannot be the product of evidence itself, since there is no evidence that would suffice to rationally demonstrate that God _will_ protect and care for the believer who trusts themselves to Him. These involve facts about God’s own intentions and attitudes that are not necessary but contingent truths about God. This is not to say that faith is simply blind trust, which would violate our epistemic responsibilities and undermine our ability to acquire knowledge by testimony. Rather, “to rely on testimony for one’s belief that _p_ is to believe _p_ without possessing (or at least without basing one’s belief on) a watertight argument from premises whose truth one can assure oneself of, to the truth of _p._ If one believes that _p_ on the basis of such an argument, then one’s belief is not testimonial but inferential.”[^c01-20] Instead, the attitude of confidence by which the believer chooses to trust God results from a _moral_ decision. Even if we can give an account on which the believer would be epistemically warranted in entrusting themselves to God (assuming it were true that God rewards those who seek Him, i.e., that God has promised what the believer believes He has), the believer’s trust in God is primarily a moral attitude, a virtue. And traditional Christianity has understood this virtue to be morally obligatory. That is, culpable failure to form an attitude of faith in response to God would be a serious moral failure, so that unbelief is a mortal sin.[^c01-21]

For those who already accept theism a least as a possibility, it seems obvious that – whatever theory we might have of “authority,” e.g., where it comes from or what it consists in – God is, in every respect, the exemplar case of an authoritative speaker and ruler. God being God is justification enough for His authority and for His commands binding us to follow them. Since God has such authority, such that His commands bind us morally to obey them, we would have good reasons to accept that there are moral obligations to believe, simply in virtue of the fact that God commands our belief. Everything that is morally obligatory also counts as a divine command, and vice versa; God commanding us to do _x_ would _ipso facto_ impose upon us a moral obligation to do _x_. Similarly, if God commands us _to believe some set of truths,_ we have moral obligations to believe those truths solely because of God’s command, rather than by the force of evidence associated with these truths independently. (Since faith pertains to many matters that do not admit of that sort of evidence, note too that God’s commands would be impossible to obey if we required such evidence.)

These considerations do not rest upon a controversial divine command theory of morality, which holds that what makes something obligatory or good for us is simply that God commanded it. What I am saying is only that what God commands can be known by itself to be morally obligatory upon us, even when we might not otherwise be aware of the way in which what God is commanding us to do is good for us or where we don’t have independent evidence that what God commands is morally obligatory. What makes God’s commands binding comes apart from our perception of whether these things are good for us, just as the morally obligatory or what is objectively good for us is not identical with the set of things we _think_ to be morally obligatory or good for us, since we can be wrong about the

---

<!-- PDF page 28 -->
**[p. 15]**

mind-independent facts about these things. God’s testimony or command then has independent epistemic weight, since whatever He commands would necessarily be morally obligatory and good for us, even when we do not understand _why_ He commands it.[^c01-22]

However, believing God’s word is instrumentally necessary for forming a personal relationship with God – and this kind of relationship is immensely valuable. Consequently, since great goods of salvation would be unattainable without faith, God speaking to us and promising us eternal life would give us conclusive reasons to believe what God says; otherwise, we would be unable to have eternal life! There are then “hypothetical” imperatives to believe God on account of that desire we have for perfect happiness. If you want to be happy, believe God. But there would also seem to be obvious “categorical” imperatives backing faith too. Disobeying God’s commands, clearly, would be morally inappropriate. God is the source of all wisdom and truth and goodness. God is more important than anything else, and it would be irrational to prefer anything else to God. For the same reason, it would be irrational _not_ to trust God and thereby form a relationship with Him, regardless of whatever else might be obtainable from this relationship. God is intrinsically good in a way that nothing else could be. There could be no good reason, then, to prefer anything else to God, and thus no good reason for failing to believe God or obey His word.

These points seem to me obvious. Instead of defending the idea that God’s speech to us would generate moral obligations directly, I will conclude this chapter by addressing a few common reasons given to reject dogmatic conceptions of faith. I will point out that many of these call into question the rationality of faith. Now, as I have said, responding to skepticism or justifying the rationality of faith is not the overall aim of this book, which begins from assumptions which all Christians admit – primarily, that God actually has spoken to us – and then aims to show that this assumption commits us to dogmatic authority. For that reason, I will only outline the way responses to these skeptical objections might go, with the aim not of defending that faith is rational or providing evidence in favor of Christian or theistic faith, but only of showing that _Christians_ who appeal to such objections would in fact be embracing a self-defeating skeptical attitude. These objections involve assumptions which would undermine Christian faith in general and so be inappropriate for any Christian to endorse.

The most radical objections to faith as involving the kinds of obligations that I have been discussing, which arise from the fact that God reveals truths, involve arguments that _revealed truth_ is impossible in a principled way. There are a few versions of these kinds of radical objections. One strand of objection has its origin in the thought of Immanuel Kant and has been popular among liberal Christian theologians. This sort of objection claims that we cannot meaningfully _refer_ to God, because God is not directly present to experience, and is instead “transcendent.”[^c01-23] I will not explore this objection in much detail, since this objection undermines our ability to talk or know about God in general and thus is not simply an argument against dogmatic faith.

---

<!-- PDF page 29 -->
**[p. 16]**

Nevertheless, as Plantinga outlines, most versions of the Kantian objection are not plausible when we try to make sense of them. The objection is hard to state perspicuously, since the objector needs to state what we are talking about – God – and then say our concepts do not apply to that. But if they can state the subject, it is obvious that at least one concept _does_ apply to God: namely, that our concepts do not apply to God. The objection therefore looks confused, since to state the objection implies that its own claims are either a statement about nothing or a false statement.[^c01-24] Plantinga helpfully points out that these Kantian objections are closely related to objections made by logical positivists. Logical positivists accepted a criterion of meaning – the “verifiability criterion” – such that a sentence makes sense or has meaning if and only if it can be “empirically verified” (which is roughly: established as true by the natural sciences). It was on this basis that they argued that claims such as those made by theists that “God exists” are not empirically verifiable and therefore meaningless.[^c01-25]

There are well-known problems for verificationist theories of meaning, however, which is why they have largely fallen off the map of contemporary philosophical discourse. In short, there is a circularity worry; e.g., a principle that “we cannot meaningfully state anything that is not verifiable in sense experience” is not itself verifiable in sense experience and therefore (by its own lights) meaningless. The epistemological issues are larger and more complicated than I can deal with here, since the discussion of verificationist principles of meaning revealed much wider issues regarding the nature of language and logic,[^c01-26] and one sees a similar phenomenon in Kantian objections to supernatural revelation, as they too depend on a full-scale epistemological package of views. Plantinga has, I think, shown decisively that Kantian claims about God’s unknowability – even in their strongest formulations – are deeply incoherent.[^c01-27] For my purposes, however, such responses show _at least_ that there is reasonable disagreement on these significant issues – there is clearly no knock-down reason to accept Kantianism or logical positivism as the One True epistemology. Further, accepting such objections requires accepting a radical skeptical view on which we cannot know, let alone refer to, God. The objection therefore conflicts with the perspective I have taken in this book – which begins by assuming that Christian faith _is_ valuable and possible – and thus should be rejected.

There are contemporary versions of this objection which have been raised against the possibility of dogmatic faith. Steven Nemes, for instance, has proposed a series of related objections to dogmatic faith, which flesh out arguments against dogmatic faith in different ways. But Nemes thinks these arguments do not require rejecting faith or Christianity.[^c01-28] He argues, for instance, that we are unable to judge whether we are in possession of supernatural revelation at all. Nemes defines the “supernatural” as “that dimension of reality which is not accessible or knowable to human beings in keeping with their natural powers of knowing and cognitive endowments,”[^c01-29] as well as “that which exceeds the limits of the possibilities of nature.”[^c01-30] As our judgments about whether something has occurred which is supernatural, or which is relevantly related (“coupled”) with supernatural

---

<!-- PDF page 30 -->
**[p. 17]**

facts, the only way we could know this is because it appears to us within the scope of our natural experience. But then, he claims,

For any x whatsoever appearing in the world of experience to which one might point, it will always be possible to provide a description of that thing in natural terms. This follows from the mere fact of its appearing in the world of experience. It will be describable in the same sorts of terms that apply to all the various objects of sense, or of reason, or of memory, or of moral intuition, or of transcendental or phenomenological reflection. All its various natural properties will be discoverable through these means. But it will always remain an open question whether x should also be described in any supernatural terms at all. That is because the supernatural is defined in terms of what exceeds the limits of the possibilities of the natural. Any inference from some natural description of a thing to a supernatural description of it will be a _non sequitur_.[^c01-31]

We can therefore rule out the possibility of inferring from “any natural description of a thing to the supernatural description of it on the basis of some proposition about the supernatural,” as this would be to beg the question in favor of the existence and nature of the supernatural – which is “the very question at stake [in asking] whether there is reason for believing in a supernature beyond nature.”[^c01-32] Nemes concludes that traditional Christian theology, which concludes to the inspiration of Scripture, or the infallibility of the Church, or the occurrence of miracles, or the divinity of Christ, etc., are all unwarranted beliefs.[^c01-33] No such judgments can be made without invalid or circular reasoning, he claims, for reasons that are similar to those given in logical positivist and Kantian critique: the supernatural is beyond experience and therefore we are unable to make judgments about it that would be either justified or constitute knowledge.

Nemes’ argument itself, however, rests on at least two clear instances of _petitio principii_, which is to say his argument is implicitly circular. First, Nemes seems to think that every judgment about the existence or nature of the supernatural is necessarily an inference from a _description_ of an event. But this is an assumption we can and should reject. We do not conclude, for instance, that the resurrection of a person from the dead is a miracle by inferring a new description “resurrected body” _from_ a description of the body at two different times. We do not even conclude that the body was brought to life again by inferring one description from another. Whatever the way in which we conclude that a body is alive, given the facts known about that body from sense experience, it is not to infer one description from another. Indeed, that epistemological perspective on which we infer the nature or existence of the world from the content of our sense impressions would result in global skepticism and should be rejected, since we could not make any such inferences in a valid or non-question-begging way, and therefore would know literally _nothing_ about the world beyond those sense impressions.[^c01-34] Judgements that someone is alive are not plausibly inferences from a description of their body,

---

<!-- PDF page 31 -->
**[p. 18]**

or sense impressions of their body, but a judgment that a given description (“alive”) is accurate or true of the subject in question.
