Presumably, if we judged someone coming back to life from the dead to be a true miracle (e.g., Christ’s raising of Lazarus), we would have judged or inferred that a given person was dead, and then subsequently alive, and then conclude that a miracle occurred, because we also judge that it was impossible, given the powers and natures of the things involved, that anything natural could have brought the body back to life. There is nothing special or question-begging in _this form of reasoning_, however, since it is a familiar form associated with inferring to a nonobserved cause from facts about observed objects. From the fact that a surface is hot to the touch, we judge that there must be a source of heat.

Now, at this point it might seem there is a relevant difference between inferring to natural causes, such as a flame, or to supernatural causes, such as divine intervention. Some might think that all inferences to physical causes are grounded within the realm of what is, in principle, observable phenomena, whereas inferences to supernatural causes would necessarily be inferences to unobservable, non-physical causes like God. However, to draw the conclusion that inferences to unobservable causes are always illegitimate would be too quick. Many physical causes are unobservable, relative to various factors. For instance, electrons or X-rays are not directly observable; we infer their existence and nature from their effects in physical systems. In fact, events in the past are not observable in the present, and we often make judgments about present states of affairs as having been caused by events in the past (e.g., the President is currently the President for having won the election in the past). To hold as a general principle that it is always unsound to judge that observed present facts or events have a cause which is (presently) unobservable would entail rejecting much of what we count as knowledge and be self-defeating.

Second, Nemes’ argument rests on a question-begging move, inferring from the fact that any event _can be given_ a description in natural terms (that is, excluding supernatural causes) to the conclusion that any description of an event in terms of its natural causes is _necessarily as reasonable as_ the judgment that a given event has a supernatural cause. But the fact that an event can be described as a nonmiracle does not make it _ipso facto_ reasonable to believe it was not a miracle, any more than the fact that one could possibly describe my body as that of a corpse entails that it is reasonable to believe that I am not alive. Nemes would likely reject that we can _accurately_ describe my body as a corpse, given its ongoing biological functions, such as breathing. But then it becomes clear that Nemes has assumed, in saying it is possible to describe a given situation or event in wholly natural terms, that we can _wholly accurately_ or _reasonably_ describe that event in those terms. Yet this is the very question at issue: whether it is wholly accurate or reasonable to describe that situation or event in wholly natural terms. Nemes’ argument is circular.

Nemes’ argument that a miracle (or other “motive of credibility”) could never make it reasonable to believe in a divine author or agent is likewise circular. Nemes argues that “every purported miracle is at the very least characterizable in natural terms as an event in the world that occurs contrary to reasonable and well-informed expectations pertaining to the natural tendencies and possibilities of things. But to

---

<!-- PDF page 32 -->
**[p. 19]**

try to infer a supernatural description of the event in question from this natural description is a non sequitur. One could always say instead that some highly rare but otherwise entirely natural power has been at work.”[^c01-35] Being able to give a natural description of an event is not to give a _reasonable_ description of that event or one that is _as comparatively reasonable_ as another. So, for instance, if it would be obvious that the event has no reasonable natural cause, it would not be reasonable for us to describe the event as having a natural cause, any more than it would be reasonable to describe a corpse as living (despite being _able_ to describe it in that way).

Nemes at one point implies that there are _no possible criteria_ that could make it reasonable for anyone to believe a given miracle has occurred. He argues that we could give no criteria according to which we make a reasonable judgement that God has spoken, because it would be fallacious to presume that we know what God is like or how He would speak. As he says, “this would be to presuppose that one knows what the supernatural is like and how it would express itself. One could just as well propose some other set of purported marks of the supernatural which they lack and conclude that they are not specially or uniquely related to the sphere of supernature.”[^c01-36] However, first, Nemes is inconsistent in his commitments on this score. Nemes is not an atheist, and he admits in the same essay that it could be reasonable to believe that God exists based on proofs for the existence of God. This then bolsters the reasonableness of making judgments that a given event is supernaturally caused or an instance of supernatural speech: if we can know facts about God and what He can do, we can know that God can perform miracles and speak to us, just as we can know that we ought to believe God’s speech to us.

Also, second, while Nemes claims that God’s existence would thereby be a “natural reality,” since we infer to God’s existence from observable facts,[^c01-37] this claim looks to conflict with Nemes’ other commitments. Clearly, God is no more observable _in Himself_ than the other things Nemes rejects as unknowable on account of being unobservable in themselves (e.g., Jesus’ divinity or facts about the past resurrection of Jesus). In addition, proofs for God’s existence typically move from observable phenomena to God as their cause on the basis that natural phenomena are not sufficient to explain why they engage in the activities they do, or why they exist, etc. In this case, we come to know what God is like _given_ what He must be like to cause a given effect. But, if this is valid reasoning in natural theology, it is valid reasoning in judging a given event is supernaturally caused.

That is, if an event occurs which seems to have no possible cause in the powers of the natural things observable to us, and it is reasonable to believe that it was caused by some other agent (as in Jesus’ raising of Lazarus), we could reasonably infer that this agent has _the kind of power_ sufficient to bring about that effect: Jesus has the power to raise the dead, etc. This reasoning is not fallacious, since it is simply to attribute to the agent that power sufficient to bring about the effect we observe. We do not need an independent criterion according to which we already know what Jesus is capable of, since we can _infer to_ what Jesus is capable of from the fact that we already know other things in the situation cannot bring about that effect. Similarly, we do not need to know what Jesus is capable of to reasonably attribute the causality of the effect to Him. We would simply infer to

---

<!-- PDF page 33 -->
**[p. 20]**

the best explanation: the other things cannot bring about the effect; Jesus seems to be causally involved, given the words He speaks and prayers He offers; and thus, we conclude, Jesus is the agent who raised Lazarus. This reasoning is not different, in principle, from the reasoning by which we attribute causality to any given agent. Nemes’ arguments against dogmatic faith on account of the fact that we could not know whether any supernatural claim is true are fallacious.

In addition, Nemes’ alternative notion of faith is incoherent. He claims that, since nobody can know infallibly that their theological views are true, it would be unreasonable to hold that (saving) faith, “in the normal case, is at least in part a matter of assenting to certain precise and well-defined doctrinal formulae.”[^c01-38] Instead, Nemes argues that we should embrace a non-dogmatic view on which faith does not consist in having the right theological views at all. We should affirm that “salvation is already a reality,” or “Christ has already accomplished the salvation of all participants,” so that disbelief in particular dogmatic formulae could not _possibly_ affect our salvation – thus evacuating theological controversy of any stakes.[^c01-39] Nemes concedes that Christian claims can be either true or false but rejects that believing falsely would affect our salvation. On his view, we have no obligation to believe one way or another. It is a matter of mere _curiosity_ whether, e.g., Jesus is God.

Nemes’ views are inconsistent. Nemes does not hold, for instance, that faith is a non-cognitive attitude that has nothing to do with truths at all (where, e.g., Christian faith would be akin to a practical or moral or aesthetic attitude that could be shared with atheists). Rather, Nemes insists that Christian faith essentially involves belief in truths.

A person is not yet a Christian who only thinks of Christ as ‘possibly’ or ‘potentially’ one’s savior … being a Christian is a matter of understanding oneself to belong ‘body and soul, in life and in death’ to Jesus the Savior. Presumably one cannot believe oneself to belong to Jesus if He is dead and gone. Neither can one believe that one has been saved by Christ if one does not understand there to be any ‘problem’ from which to be saved. But it is also clear that such a self-understanding is consistent with a variety of opinions in matters of theological controversy such as Trinity, Incarnation, and Atonement. [Thus,] being a Christian does not essentially require or consist in knowing that fact of the matter or assenting to some well-defined doctrinal statement. What is essential to Christian faith is not so much its ‘propositional content,’ but rather its object and its mode of intending: Christian faith intends the person of Christ as Savior.[^c01-40]

Nemes then does not accept that faith is compatible with _any_ possible attitude toward propositions about Jesus. Nemes accepts that faith is dogmatic, in my sense of the term: faith essentially involves assent to or belief in definite truths. Nemes also accepts, as per my account, that faith involves different potential relation to a range of propositions (factors involve epistemic situation, hierarchy of truths, etc.) since faith has trust in a person (God) as its essential object. So, Nemes rules

---

<!-- PDF page 34 -->
**[p. 21]**

out only that belief in “precise and well-defined” doctrinal formulae as requisite for having faith. For Nemes, the only belief that is requisite for salvation is the “obvious” truth that: “All people always already are claimed by Christ, because He has died for them and committed Himself to their salvation.”[^c01-41] Belief in this proposition alone is necessary, if not sufficient, for salvation; belief in any other proposition is unnecessary.

Nemes’ advocacy of a purportedly nondogmatic faith is inconsistent with his own commitments, in a few further ways. First, Nemes seems to think that believers hear the “proclamation of the Gospel” (or, have an interior experience that they are “claimed by Christ”), and therefore come to _know_ that they are claimed by Christ: “Christ claims people as His own, and they come to know about it through the preaching of the Gospel.”[^c01-42] Yet, as we saw above, Nemes has argued that any given natural situation will necessarily be describable in purely natural terms, and therefore that it is unreasonable to conclude a supernatural judgment about that situation. One’s own life or experience, by those same standards, can be described in a way that the proposition above is false. Nobody can _know_ – by Nemes’ own lights – that they are claimed by Christ from these experiences. These facts pose a difficulty for Nemes’ account of faith, since Nemes wants to insist that the above belief should be _obvious_ in such a way that the believer can infer that it follows immediately from the data (e.g., the preaching of the Gospel). Given Nemes’ epistemological claims, however, Nemes’ singular truth cannot be uniquely inferable from the data and judged to be true. But then Nemes’ arguments that dogmatic faith in supernatural facts or in any well-defined doctrinal formulation is unreasonable would also apply to Nemes’ own singular proposition. What makes it unreasonable to believe that belief in X is necessary for salvation is that belief X is not uniquely inferable from the data. But Nemes’ own proposition cannot be inferred uniquely from the data. It would therefore be unreasonable – by Nemes’ own lights – to think that belief in his proposition is required for salvation.

Second, Nemes is inconsistent in proposing that requiring belief in any welldefined doctrinal formulation (like a requirement to belong to the one true Church, or to be baptized) would inspire an unreasonable fear (“salvation-anxiety”). Nemes argues that a dogmatic faith would entail that, while one has been informed that they need salvation, “Christ, as He has been presented, does not straightforwardly assure one of His help.” One would therefore be “put in the position of constantly interrogating oneself and attempting to discern whether one fulfills the conditions of salvation.” This would be unreasonable, because this state of anxiety would lead one either to “despair of salvation altogether, or else eventually realize that one has no other option except simply to trust in Christ’s goodness.”[^c01-43] This presentation confuses at least two orders of normativity. “A condition of salvation” is simultaneously being used in two equivocal ways. Nemes means to say that there would be an unreasonable moral demand in insisting that the believer _save themselves_ by doing something that would cause that person to be claimed by Christ and so saved. This is clearly a _causal_ condition of salvation which – for theological reasons – would be unfulfillable by a human being, since no human being could do anything independent of God that would cause God to do anything, let alone save them. Yet

---

<!-- PDF page 35 -->
**[p. 22]**

Nemes conflates rejecting this causal condition on salvation with rejecting _logical_ conditions on faith that would require belief in particular articles of faith.

What is inconsistent is that Nemes himself straightforwardly also affirms that there are such logical conditions. Nobody has faith, on Nemes’ own view, who fails to believe that they are claimed by Christ. That is a logically necessary condition for having faith. But it is not a causally necessary condition of having faith that one believes this and _thereby_ earns salvation from Jesus. Yet, by the same token, there is nothing implied in saying that orthodox belief (even in “precise and well-defined doctrinal formulae”), or receiving baptism, or belonging to the one true Church, is a necessary _logical_ condition of being saved. Now, it might seem implausible that nobody is saved who fails to believe the Nicene Creed, or fails to be baptized, but it would be obviously just as implausible to hold that nobody is saved who fails to believe Nemes’ unique proposition. The Jewish patriarchs did not believe that proposition, for instance, and were saved.

Nevertheless, we do not need to reject that there are logically necessary requirements for salvation to avoid this issue. Instead, we can qualify what is logically necessary to situations – as I have done – regarding epistemic accessibility or position. That is, having _right_ dogmatic faith is a necessary condition of salvation, and having right faith logically requires (variously) receiving baptism, or believing the Nicene Creed, or getting circumcised, but these requirements correlate to one’s epistemic situation or position. Abraham would not have had faith if he had not obeyed God’s word and gotten circumcised. Paul would not have had faith if he had not obeyed Jesus’ word and gotten baptized. So too, it could be true that no Christian _today_ could have right faith if they do not accept and profess the Nicene Creed, or the teachings of the Council of Trent, etc. There are only very general logical requirements imposed by the virtue of faith essentially, but the essential nature of faith – as “dogmatic” belief in what God reveals – will therefore morally demand that the believer believe different things in different times and places. Obligations vary depending on their situation, much like any other moral virtue. People can (as I noted) make _innocent_ mistakes in belief that do not affect whether they have faith, but not every such mistake is _necessarily_ innocent, as even Nemes admits.

Third, Nemes’ arguments about “salvation-anxiety” imply that there would be an unreasonable moral demand placed upon the believer to believe any doctrinal truths more specific than the unique proposition Nemes has proposed, given the epistemic facts being such that nobody could _determine_ whether any given specific doctrinal formulation is true. Yet Nemes’ epistemology holds that the epistemic facts regarding _anything not immediately self-evident to us_ entail that that _no_ doctrinal formulation is self-evidently true and, therefore, no good rational grounds can be given to determine that any given doctrinal belief about Christ is true in contrast to a naturalistic hypothesis that it is false (because, e.g., there is no God). But that includes Nemes’ own unique proposition (which I summarize as being): that all are already claimed by Christ and saved by His sacrifice. Clearly, Nemes might allege that we should believe _his_ proposition on faith, not because we have conclusive reason to prefer it to the naturalistic hypothesis. But Nemes’ objection presumes that an unreasonable moral demand would result from

---

<!-- PDF page 36 -->
**[p. 23]**

requiring someone to make a definite judgment as to the truth of a proposition within an indeterminate epistemic situation. That applies to the definite judgment of faith that Nemes’ own proposition ought to be believed. Therefore, if Nemes were right that the moral demand to believe X is unreasonable _simply_ because we do not have conclusive or infallible knowledge that X is true, the moral demand for anyone to be a believer even in Nemes’ minimal faith would be unreasonable. By the same token, if Nemes’ arguments were compatible with holding that it is reasonable and good to believe in Nemes’ simple faith in contrast to the naturalistic hypothesis, it could be reasonable and good to believe in the Catholic faith in contrast to Nemes’ simple faith.

I judge, by contrast, that Nemes has made a mistake in construing the demands of what is morally or epistemically reasonable in this case. Nemes counsels agnosticism as a matter of policy, understanding this “agnosticism” “as a new way of exercising faith in the face of the natural limits of human knowledge.”[^c01-44] He believes it is virtuous to make it a policy to “remain agnostic and accept ignorance about” those matters which cannot be determined with certainty or infallibly. Nemes claims “this counts as ‘faith’ because it is a matter of trusting God not to have left human beings in a truly problematic and difficult situation.”[^c01-45] But this is an obviously unreasonable attitude. It is sometimes clearly _un_ reasonable to believe a given proposition, even if that proposition is not self-evidently true or if that proposition cannot be infallibly known. For instance, it is not self-evident that the Holocaust occurred, and I do not know infallibly that it occurred, but it would be unreasonable and even potentially blameworthy to fail to believe that proposition. The facts of which faith is more reasonable or morally obligatory for us to believe will similarly depend upon the facts regarding what God has revealed and how we are epistemically situated to be reasonably aware of those facts.

It would not be reasonable (whether epistemically or morally) for me to believe I ought to trust Jesus Christ as my personal Lord and Savior, and then fail to be responsive to what He tells me to do. If Jesus told me to partake of the Eucharist, for instance, and I thought I had no reason to do what He instructs me to do, I would seemingly fail to exemplify trust in Jesus. So, if the facts about what Jesus said or Scripture teaches require me to believe more than Nemes’ simple faith, it would be unreasonable for me to believe only in Nemes’ simple faith. Counseling agnosticism as a principled policy would seem unreasonable and self-defeating (i.e., why shouldn’t we be agnostic about Nemes’ “simple faith”?) This fact should cause no more undue anxiety than deciding whether to become Christian is a cause of anxiety. Our epistemic and moral obligations are relevant to our situations. In both cases, we might counsel the same thing: do God’s will the best you know how, pray for His help, and trust in His goodness. We need not have any undue anxiety about salvation when engaging in sincere prayer while trying to discern God’s will. We are only affirming that what God wants me to believe should make a difference _to me_.

Fourth, there is unreasonable arbitrariness in the limits Nemes draws between faith in “precise and well-defined” doctrines versus the simple faith he endorses. Nemes thinks we could not have a moral obligation to believe any “precise and

---

<!-- PDF page 37 -->
**[p. 24]**

well-defined” dogmatic claims, whereas we could have a moral obligation to believe simply in Jesus, according to his own view. Yet the _precision_ or _welldefined-ness_ in stating any proposition is a feature extrinsic to that proposition itself. Precision in stating a proposition is a feature usually of our sentences, not of the proposition. To specify faith by means of whether I believe something that _can be_ very precisely stated would not be a “joint carving” difference in picking out kinds of faith. Conversely, Nemes’ own doctrinal statement implies lots of further truths about who and what Jesus Christ is, as Nemes himself makes clear in saying that we would need to believe Jesus is still alive and that there is a problem which His death resolves. These further truths would make that statement _capable_ of further precisification.

And such precisification is not irrelevant to the _meaning_ attached to Nemes’ statement, since we could offer a precisification of that statement which contradicts Nemes’ own interpretation of it. For instance, one could clarify “claimed by Christ” in such a way that it would mean “predestined from eternity either for eternal bliss or torment” or “given sufficient grace to become a baptized member of the Catholic Church.” Or, what Nemes means by “simply to trust in Christ’s goodness,” could be precisified with a (parody) extreme Calvinist reading, such that it means “acquiescing to God’s sovereign power and glory, magnified in God necessitating our sin and damnation.” In neither case would the new meaning attached to that sentence correspond to the proposition Nemes intended to indicate by means of that sentence. That is, affirming nominally that same _sentence_ proposed by Nemes, considering these precisifications, would not involve affirming the same _proposition._ But if it is necessary for salvation that we adhere to the same proposition about Jesus, we could affirm the same sentence that Nemes proposed and nevertheless end up failing to have minimal saving faith. Nemes is inconsistently insisting that simply nominal agreement on certain sentences is sufficient for salvation, despite his views implying that saving faith requires belief in at least some definite propositions.

So, Nemes’ account of faith and the limits he puts on dogmatic faith are unreasonable. Several of his objections seem to put special weight on the term “metaphysical,” calling for the elimination of metaphysics as incidental to faith. But, indeed, what we are talking about is simply whether Christian beliefs _are true,_ and whether their being true or false would make an appreciable difference to our lives. Globally denying that truth and falsity _matter_ seems to be just as inconsistent as the verificationist principle above: it is as much a matter of truth or falsity whether it is _true_ that truth or falsity matters. And it seems obvious that whether our beliefs are true is often quite important. Few people would find arguments persuasive that entail the truth or falsity of responses to questions like “is this safe to drink?” make no appreciable difference in their lives. Religious beliefs are not special in this regard. It is obviously false that “metaphysical” truths about God’s existence or nature play no important role in faith or in our moral obligations. Even if we put aside truths directly pertaining to God’s existence or nature or what God has done in history, it clearly makes a difference whether it is _true_ that murder is a sin, or whether there are no moral truths at all, or whether baptism is morally obligatory,

---

<!-- PDF page 38 -->
**[p. 25]**

etc. Yet many of these truths depend intimately on facts about God’s nature or existence. For instance, it would be morally and epistemically bad to trust Jesus Christ as an infallible authority if He were not, in fact, such an authority. It would also be bad to worship Jesus or to put absolute faith in His words or person if He were not God – it would be, indeed, the sin of idolatry. One cannot have anything more than metaphorical friendship with non-existent people. If God doesn’t exist, the Christian faith seems silly, since Christians would recommend friendship with someone who does not exist.

Clearly, too, if we believed God could lie to us, or contradict Himself, and so forth, then faith would not be virtuous, or it would not be morally obligatory. Many beliefs about God’s nature, then, are intimately related to the attitude that is faith. To claim that these beliefs are _not_ so intimately related would simply be questionbegging against the possibility and value of Christian notions of faith, unless they rested on theological argumentation that God _could not_ reveal Himself in the way that Christians believe He has. But it looks like any such argument would be both unsound and self-defeating. Unsound, because it seems a matter of God’s contingent desires whether He wants us to believe His word, and so we have no good theological reasons to rule out in principle that God could make contingent promises to us. Self-defeating, because it seems that any consideration sufficient to rule out God possibly revealing Himself to us in history would also seemingly rule out the very possibility of reasoning about God. That is, the argument implies that we ought to embrace a radical apophatic position, where nothing about God is knowable from sense experience. If God were so knowable from evidence available to us that we can rule out (from what is accessible to us) that God has contingent intentions to reveal anything, there would seem to be no reason in principle that we could not have access to God’s intentions from the evidence available to us. The objection would be _de facto_ and no longer _de jure_, and we could debate whether God in fact has revealed Himself.

Many Christians, however, do not embrace objections to dogmatic belief as radical as those I was addressing above. Many instead simply worry that _belief in truths about God_ is not very valuable and has no internal connection with Christian life. Emphasizing belief would undermine the value of Christian life. Right belief seems irrelevant to being a good person. Some of these objections to dogmatic faith thus rest on concerns about salvation. If dogmatic faith were required for salvation, many morally good persons would be unable to be saved, and this seems morally inappropriate. Others rest on moral objections that dogmatic faith is no part of what is morally valuable in faith and would even be a distraction from living our faith well.

These latter objections based on what we might call “exclusivism” about salvation, where salvation would depend upon professing belief in certain truths, seem to me to encapsulate the worry about dogmatic faith most clearly. The worry is that many who lack the right beliefs seem to be otherwise good people, holding reasonable views, and so the view that saving faith depends upon having the right beliefs would mean that many reasonable, good people would end up condemned to hell. Indeed, our religious beliefs seem to be largely formed through factors outside of

---

<!-- PDF page 39 -->
**[p. 26]**

our control, so that many embrace the religion of their parents or societies. People who fail to believe the right truths about God, at the very least, have almost never _rejected_ those truths, but simply never encountered them in the right way. It would therefore seem cruel and irrational for salvation to depend upon having the right beliefs, given the way in which this would condemn a lot of people to eternal damnation for no apparently good reason.

Worries of this kind put pressure on Christians to abandon a dogmatic conception of faith. If we do not want to reject that saving faith is necessary for salvation, this will require us to change our conception of what faith involves being able to rule out that faith requires holding the right beliefs about God. Perhaps every morally decent person has faith in their own way; perhaps only the _really_ morally vicious lack that kind of attitude and would be able to reject God when presented with Him as He truly is. In short, if the Christian wants to maintain an exclusivism about salvation, they would likely envision exclusivism to pertain to having the right _affective_ attitudes, but where our loving God or other people involves no relevant beliefs about anything. On such views, God will judge people by their moral behavior, where believing in Christianity or having faith is not among those moral behaviors that are relevant to our standing before God.

These views seem confused. For one, these concerns do not actually require a rejection of dogmatic faith. Rather, they seem to presume some such beliefs from the mere fact that _love of God and neighbor_ are supposedly the right kind of affective attitudes which are necessary for salvation. We identify and individuate our affective attitudes by way of their objects – we pick out loves in terms of _what_ we love and _in what way_. Love of cheesecake is a different kind of love than love of a friend. So too picking out the _right kind of_ love of God and neighbor would require pointing to the kinds of objects that a person has in view when they love God and their neighbor, as well as the way that they love these people. It seems to me obvious that the Christian life aims not simply as desiring to _act_ like Jesus, as if Jesus might well not exist and our Christian life simply requires us to behave in certain ways.[^c01-46] Rather, Christian life aims at cultivating a personal relationship with Jesus (and the other Persons of the Trinity). But being a friend of God, or a friend of other people, requires us to have _beliefs_ about what kind of people these are, at least implicitly, since we could not be friends with someone who we think does not exist or is a force of nature, or whathaveyou. The way love fits into a Christian life requires us to have some beliefs about _whom_ we love.

Those who reject what they take to be dogmatic conceptions of faith are not so much rejecting that we need to have some beliefs to love God or neighbor appropriately, but rejecting that we need _some specific Christian beliefs_ about this. The better way to put the objection might then be those dogmatic beliefs, such as the Nicene Creed or other specific theological definitions (e.g., the consubstantiality of the Son with the Father, papal infallibility, the Assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary), do not seem internally connected to motivating us to loving God or neighbor in the right way. The difficulty in responding to such concerns, however, is that they are far too general. It is not obvious _in principle_ that no theological definition or dogma could have this internal connection. For instance, belief that Jesus

---

<!-- PDF page 40 -->
**[p. 27]**

is God, and that it is only by being friends with Jesus that we are friends with the Father or Holy Spirit, would have an immediate bearing on how to pursue friendship with God. Or, belief there is no salvation outside the Church, or belief that there is an immortal soul and immediate judgment after this life, with no further “chance” after death to repent of sins or make an act of faith, would have a fairly obvious connection to our motivations. Or, belief that all our actions are determined by God, where nobody could have done otherwise than what God decides they will do, and where God determines that sinners necessarily will commit the sins they do and end up damned for His greater glory – this sort of belief appears highly _dis-motivating_ to many in pursuing the Christian life and has therefore been highly controversial.

In many respects, the devil is in the details. Nevertheless, we can motivate taking these doctrines seriously simply from the fact that every dogmatic definition has usually been preceded by – at one point or another – intense theological controversy. For instance, the debate about icon veneration in public worship, or the number of wills or energies in Christ, is well-known to have occasioned numerous persecutions and recriminations. It seems abstract to us now, but Maximus the Confessor ended up with his tongue cut out and in exile for refusing to accept imperially supported “compromise” formulas pertaining to the number of wills in Christ. The _fact_ of controversy raises the “antecedent probability” that the issues which were vociferously debated do indeed matter for Christian life. But we cannot decide in advance on each issue. We need dogmatic history to help us examine why past Christians thought these issues were important. Yet one finds inevitably that Christians argued these issues were connected to central issues of the Christian life. For this reason, the history of dogma is precisely an _intellectual_ history that details these arguments, and the evidence presented within its historical context, not simply a political one.

Even if we assume a cynical perspective that one party achieved a dogmatic definition (and “won” the debate) through political machination rather than by the force of truth or evidence, this cynical perspective will not imply that the debate was meaningless or that the theological issue should not have been thought to be important for Christian life. For similar reasons, a naïve or dismissive attitude toward dogmatic history – that nobody could have had any legitimate arguments for dogmatic positions or for their importance to Christian life – is not reasonable considering precisely the way such debates consumed the Christian world at the time. Debates about Arianism, Pelagianism, Donatism, and Protestantism – all such issues occasioned widespread conflict in Christian society that went far beyond theological schools or academies. At least _many_ people thought that these issues are of central importance to Christian life, then and today, and we owe our forebears the benefit of the doubt by listening to their arguments rather than simply treating them as foolish victims of dogmatic prejudice.

John Henry Newman was initially a Protestant scholar of Church history. He faced a serious intellectual problem that consumed his initial academic writing. In his time, the historical-critical study of Scripture had just begun, and the scholarship had already started to raise serious concerns about whether doctrines such

---

<!-- PDF page 41 -->
**[p. 28]**

as the divinity of Jesus or the orthodox teaching about the Trinity, that of eternal punishment in hell, or even the canon of Scripture itself were simply the result of the _political victory_ of one faction over another. He knew that many doctrinal controversies were serious and that, if Christians allowed that there was no public, reliable, normative way to decide between (say) Arianism and Trinitarian orthodoxy, that would be equivalent to concluding that this debate could not matter for our faith. But it clearly _does_ matter to Christian faith whether Jesus is God.

Newman initially responded to these problems in his early work _Via Media_ by proposing, roughly, that we can figure out historically which are the earliest and most ancient interpretations of Scripture. Newman initially thought this would be sufficient to distinguish the true Scriptural teaching from those doctrines would be introduced later “corruptions” or “accretions” that were not part of the apostolic deposit of the faith left to us: if later beliefs (like the divinity of Jesus) could be traced back to those earliest apostolic communities, Newman thought this historical evidence would be sufficient to respond to these concerns. The rule of faith was _antiquity_. We can thus fulfill our duties of obedience to respect and accept what we receive as transmitted in the Church when we accept those earliest teachings in their entirety. We ought to refrain from adding or obliging anyone to believe anything beyond what the early Church held – “the duty of every one either to believe and love what he hears, or to wish to do so, or at least, not to oppose, but to be silent.”[^c01-47] The early Newman seemingly thought that, if believers avoid believing _new_ things not found in that inherited apostolic consensus, this will suffice to resolve all ecclesial conflicts.

Newman’s mind began to shift when he noted that not all heretical views arose through “novelty” or “innovation,” or a perverse systematizing desire to draw out deductions not found in Scripture (as he had initially alleged was the explanation for the rise of Arianism).[^c01-48] The later Newman realized that the appeal to antiquity or to the literal sense of Scripture was not inviolate, since many heretical groups had appealed precisely to these grounds.[^c01-49] Newman’s own insights in this matter were prompted by a study of the Monophysite controversy. As Newman read them, the Monophysites argued that _their_ view was more ancient, representing the literal words of the Church Father St. Cyril of Alexandria _against_ the “innovations” of those thinkers whose views eventually became orthodox. Historically, the Monophysite view was condemned at the Council of Chalcedon, and Newman thought that this was justified – the arguments against Monophysitism were convincing and compelling, showing that Monophysite views were incompatible with the truths taught in Scripture. But the Monophysites broke from the rest of the Church, rejecting Chalcedon and its authority, retreating to their appeal to antiquity and the “plain sense” of Scripture. Newman saw his own justifications for Protestantism reflected in excuses offered by these ancient heretics. Any ancient heretical group could have defended themselves the same way the Monophysites did, resisting purported Catholic “innovations.” “I saw my face in that mirror, and I was a Monophysite.”[^c01-50]

Rowan Williams has argued that Newman’s analysis reflects a truth about Arianism, the first major heresy that ran rampant throughout Christendom: Arianism was a highly conservative theological movement which ostensively aimed to preserve

---

<!-- PDF page 42 -->
**[p. 29]**

the literal sense of Scripture against what its followers saw as un-Scriptural “innovations.” Arianism gained traction because many bishops were hesitant to accept that Christians should be required to profess anything not found in Scripture, such as the “homoousios” clause of the Nicene Creed, with many therefore instead advocating for ambiguous formulae drawn from Scripture itself.[^c01-51] But what became clear over time – and what constituted the core of the arguments of the pro-Nicene party – is that it _did_ matter which interpretation of Scripture was true, since it would affect the question of whether the Church maintained its faith. The Arian and the orthodox view of Christ were logically incompatible, and the view one chose regarding the nature of Christ would have a critical impact on the whole of Christian belief and practice. Yet the Arian view had begun to command an apparent “consensus” among most of the bishops of that time.

Athanasius of Alexandria was quick to recognize that Arianism would imply that other parts of Scripture must be rejected or relativized, and he argued for this fact in a way that eventually convinced others that Arianism was heretical. His _On the Incarnation of the Word_ continues to be an exemplar of widely accessible and convincing theological argument. Newman thought (and I think too) that Athanasius had good evidence that Arianism was a serious heresy incompatible with Scriptural teaching. But not everyone then or today agrees with Athanasius. Despite encountering Athanasius’ arguments or evidence against Arianism, many were unconvinced that Arianism was heretical. Then as now, there were apparent theological experts who denied that Arianism is an illegitimate interpretation of Scripture and argued that the profession of the Nicene Creed’s _homoousios_ clause is an intolerable moral demand, requiring Christians to profess more than Scripture itself demands. Individual theologians and bishops too are fallible. Indeed, these had failed on a large scale of becoming aware of what Athanasius presciently saw followed logically from Arian theological commitments.

Arianism had begun to be accepted or tolerated by what appeared to Athanasius a dangerously large number of bishops and teachers of the faith. Athanasius indeed spent many years opposed by (perhaps) most of the bishops and theological experts of his time, who thought some theoretical compromise with Arianism remained possible, and did not see the need to reject Arianism. Newman came to realize that any heretic can appeal to these same excuses – clearly, no heretic believes their view _is_ false, and thus no heretic accepts that their heresy ought to be rejected by others as a false or an illegitimate interpretation of Scripture. But heresy really _is_ a danger to the collective faith of the Church. If everyone in the Church came to accept Arianism, the Church would lose the Gospel and substitute another message for that of Christ. Newman therefore came to appreciate that the lessons of Church history show us what is at stake in doctrinal controversy and dispel naïve or simplistic stories on which orthodoxy became dominant merely through force of evidence or arguments. We need to take seriously the political dimension of Church history in the development of doctrine.

The same applies to biblical scholarship: if we follow out the results of the best current historical critical scholarship, there is _not_ one unique set of beliefs that every scholar will spontaneously agree derive from Scripture beyond reasonable

---

<!-- PDF page 43 -->
**[p. 30]**

doubt. Such scholars disagree among themselves and, at best, reach a consensus around _probabilities_ gathered from evidence. But if you take individual issues, there are many reasonable disagreements. Opting for one set of those disagreements, rather than another, leads to an entirely different account of Christian faith than the one classical Christians hold today. For instance, contemporary scholars would classify as an extraneous imposition the background set of theological beliefs that Scripture must be consistent _, so_ that one author cannot contradict another in principle. The biblical scholars are correct that our theological paradigm in reading the Scriptures as a consistent whole, or which books we choose to count as canonical, and so forth, are all theological assumptions that we bring to the Scriptures. They are not beliefs we can derive from historical scholarship alone. But this belief that Scripture is consistent is not an optional assumption for Christian faith. Nor is it an unjustified theological conclusion (as Chapter 2 proposes). And so, even if we put aside issues of Church history, we need to explain the way in which distinct Scriptural texts build upon and characterize the teaching of each other. Beyond resolving apparent contradictions among different books, we need to explain, for instance, intra-canonical “doctrinal development” such as occurs between the revelation of the Mosaic Law in the Torah to the New Testament claims that the Law is no longer binding. No Christian should dismiss such questions as idle or unimportant for our Christian faith.

For these reasons, given that many of dogmatic debates concerned central issues of Scriptural interpretation about who Christ is and how we relate to Him, it is tempting to say that ignorance of dogmatic history is ignorance of Christ. It matters to faith whether Jesus is God, the canon is as we have received it, the God of the Old Testament is the same as that of the New, etc. Those who are unconcerned about who Christ is or their relationship to Christ, and what it entails or what is necessary for that relationship, seem to be improperly motivated to live the Christian life, as that life centers precisely on following Christ in faith. Not everyone needs to be aware of the finer points of dogmatic history, certainly, but to dismiss them as meaningless or unimportant ends up dismissing much of what the Bible tells of Christ as equally unimportant. The Bible would have been simpler had it contained only the “sinner’s prayer” or some brief prayer, but that is not what God did – He gave us a complicated history and an assorted set of other documents as preparation for the Gospels, which themselves involve complicated history (since we have _four_ distinct historical narratives of the same events) and were followed by the apostolic letters and other subsequent writings which provide clarifications of many points of Christian doctrine. God would seem to have acted unnecessarily or even foolishly if all He wanted us to do in response is say a simple prayer, “get saved,” and move on with living life much as we did before becoming believers. Most Christians have instead thought that Christian life, even for ordinary believers, involves attempting to make Scriptural truths more and more an intimate part of their lives, penetrating further into what God wanted to reveal about Himself, and embracing it fully in their minds and hearts. Dogmatic history is essentially nothing more than our study of those communal processes of biblical interpretation and appropriation undertaken by our Christian forebears.

---

<!-- PDF page 44 -->
**[p. 31]**

Dogmatic truths are an essential part of Christian life and faith, since they are required both at the beginning of faith (to specify the right attitudes we have toward God) and in its process of achieving its end (to appropriate personally the mysteries that are at the core of our Christian life). But my account of faith has also given us ways to resist the pressures which result from worries about salvation, if our salvation depends upon belief in dogma. First, what is morally _necessary_ for one to believe to be saved depends on each person’s epistemic circumstances. Faith is a reactive attitude and therefore what one is obliged to believe depends very much on what God has done. For instance, those who lived before the coming of Jesus Christ would not be morally obligated to believe in the death and atonement of Jesus, for the simple fact that it had not occurred yet. Further, it depends on what God has revealed relative to each person’s epistemic situation what that person is obliged to believe, since what God has done is not always _accessible_ to each person in the same way. For instance, disciples of Christ living outside of Jerusalem would not have been aware of Christ’s death on Good Friday or on Easter Sunday of His resurrection one hour after they occurred. It would be absurd to think that, e.g., Lazarus ceased to have faith in Christ, merely because his beliefs failed to match what had occurred. He simply had no way of knowing (at that time) what transpired in Jerusalem, and it does not seem plausible that he made any moral mistake at all in _not_ believing in Christ’s death or resurrection. We might say Lazarus was not an unbeliever but rather a believer whose beliefs were blamelessly incomplete.

Second, there is an internal dependence among the dogmatic truths, given the way that they relate to God and to His promises, and the way that we believe one truth on account of another. In some cases, there is a dependence in the order of content, where some truths logically imply or entail other truths, or otherwise have other truths as specifications of the more general truth (e.g., as a determinant determines a determinable). That Mary is the mother of God, for instance, follows as a straightforward logical implication from claims that Jesus is God and Mary is His mother. Other truths are specifications of a more general truth: for instance, claims that the Bible is inspired by God might be specified as involving further judgments that nothing is in the Scripture except what God intended to be there and that nowhere would Scripture assert an error. These latter judgments are not simply entailments of inspiration, but judgments that specify implications of divine inspiration in light of our beliefs that God is fully provident and inerrant. Other truths are believed under the aspect of or on account of another. For instance, that Jesse was the father of David is a truth believed because it is taught in Scripture, and thus on account of our belief in biblical inspiration, and not because it is directly entailed by our beliefs about the divine nature or God’s promises as such. Nevertheless, this truth has an intimate _factual_ connection with those other truths, since what God commits to Scripture aims to help us understand His promises and nature. So, it is unsurprising that this truth about David’s father might be part of an overall history of God’s Providence and the way that God has remained faithful to His promises to humanity, made by covenant with Abraham. Much of these historical truths are concretely connected with God’s nature and promises, inasmuch as they are beliefs about what God _has done for us_.[^c01-52]

---

<!-- PDF page 45 -->
**[p. 32]**

The manifold ways that dogmatic truths depend upon other truths and form a natural “hierarchy”[^c01-53] are important for understanding how we understand various dogmatic truths to be necessary for faith. As we saw earlier, the fundamental truths of faith as outlined by Hebrews 11 are quite generic and simple: that God exists and rewards those who seek Him. But, as faith is a response to what is revealed, these beliefs are not as it were the only thing one might be morally obliged to believe, since it depends upon one’s epistemic context and the facts about God’s revelation what one ought to believe that God _has_ promised and God _has_ done, or even what God is _like_. So, for instance, the unique beliefs of the Christian message are summarized in the Apostles’ Creed, where the fundamentals of Christian faith in God – as Father, Son, and Spirit – the Incarnation, Passion, Death, and Resurrection of Jesus Christ, and the proximate (sacramental) and remote (eschatological) fulfillment of God’s promises are outlined. All these beliefs are akin to specifications of the generic beliefs about God’s nature and relation to us noted as essential to faith by Hebrews 11. Many other Christian dogmas are entailments of or further specifications of these essential articles of the Christian faith.[^c01-54]

Moral obligations attend these articles, since Christian faith is a response to what God has done for us in Christ. But moral obligations are specified by these articles in different ways. For example, someone who is aware of Christianity might therefore become obligated to believe in the Christian Gospel, whose essential beliefs are captured in the Apostles’ Creed. If that person failed to believe one of the articles outlined, they would not appropriately understand the other articles, given the way that these truths depend upon each other. For instance, if the person thought that each of the three Person was a distinct deity, and held a version of polytheism, they would radically misunderstand the other articles and their significance. Or, if the person did not understand Jesus is God, then they would have radically different beliefs about the value or significance of the Passion and Resurrection, as well as the sacramental and eschatological fulfillment of God’s promises.

In such cases, there are two distinct sets of concerns: one related to the _content_ of what they believed and another related to the _obligations_ to believe. The person who made a mistake about the divinity of Christ might end up not having faith in God at all, as in the case where they are inspired by Jesus Christ only as a particularly sagacious moral example of another human who lived a good life. In this case, their mistake is a radical one that undermines their having faith, as they lack faith in God’s promises or God’s person, and do not form their beliefs based on trust in God’s authority. Their faith is not _responsive_ in the right way. Imagining that they have fulfilled their moral obligations, so that they formed these false beliefs inculpably by rational pressure or some other factors and therefore committed no _sin_ in believing what they did, this by itself would not constitute faith, since they then do not believe anything based on God’s authority.

Conversely, it could be the case that they hold false beliefs about Jesus Christ, but that they do so inculpably and in a way that they have the right object in view of their faith, _given that_ they formed the false beliefs based on some other beliefs about what God had promised, which were true and which were sufficient for faith. Many Arian Christians, for instance, might have been reticent to embrace

---

<!-- PDF page 46 -->
**[p. 33]**

the _homoousios_ dogma from a theological conservatism, where they wanted to hold to older but vaguer statements about Christ’s nature. In this case, what they might have affirmed in its later context would be false, but their intentions might have nevertheless been compatible with having trust in God, since they formed their beliefs based on God’s authority and in view of obtaining God’s promises in Christ. Here, what is doing the work is the way that beliefs about Christ’s divinity are a specification or implication of other beliefs, which the Arian Christians continued to hold. Even if they were culpable in some way for arriving at their false beliefs, this alone is not sufficient to conclude that these beliefs undermined their overall faith in God, since their obligations would depend on their situation. It is not necessarily the case that every person who believes the Arian heresy to be true has thereby committed the _sin_ of heresy. Their beliefs would still be false, and their believing those false beliefs would be harmful to them in various ways, but their holding such beliefs could be compatible with having true faith. Much depends on their moral circumstances.

A relevant fact about that situation, having implications for whether they were obliged to hold a more clear and explicit view of Christ’s divinity, could be whether it had become clear in public discussion and debate that these issues were serious and central to Christian belief, and thus imposed an obligation on all parties to move beyond older formulas such as to clarify how such formulae were to be interpreted correctly. Another might be whether a dogmatic authority had decided that profession of the _homoousios_ was requisite upon all Christians. Those unaware of this debate or its seriousness, before having access to the facts when these discussions reached their issue one way or another, might then be morally excused for refraining to take a position or adopt a new set of terms about Jesus. An ordinary Arian Christian might have been blameworthy morally or epistemically for failing to perceive the truth of the _homoousios_, but might have continued to maintain a communion in faith with other Christians before Nicaea made its decision.

It might seem puzzling to hold that, after Nicaea or some other relevant event, there might come a time when continuing to affirm Arian formulae would constitute a failure for those Arian Christians to have faith. What seems puzzling about that position is that the Arian Christian was professing the same thing both before and after, e.g., the Council of Nicaea. How could that person cease to have faith, despite no obvious change in their attitudes toward the truths they believe, but simply given a change elsewhere in the world? Nevertheless, I think this position is not nearly as counterintuitive once we recognize that it is not simply the change in the world that is relevant, but the way that it is an _agent_ who holds beliefs and can thereby have duties to change their beliefs in response to the world. One and the same attitude can _become_ morally blameworthy when the agent fails to change in response to external stimuli. (e.g., a sinful failure to “update” your beliefs, given new evidence).

If a garbage-crushing technician notices that a gangster has placed an innocent victim in the trunk of a car about to go into the mechanized crusher at the junk yard and continues lazily to go about operating the machine to crush the cars on the line, the technician now becomes guilty of murder despite having no relevant

---

<!-- PDF page 47 -->
**[p. 34]**

change in their attitudes toward operating the machine. In much the same way, the Arian Christian can be morally responsible for failing to respond to morally relevant external events, and thereby cease to have faith, even if their attitudes toward a given set of propositions do not obviously change. Once the Arian notices the new external situation and continues to maintain his beliefs about Christ despite changes in the world that give them serious moral reasons to modify their beliefs, their inaction can constitute an _omission_ to do what is morally requisite.

Faith isn’t a one-time act; it is a dynamic relationship with God. Stopping to respond to God, i.e., becoming insensitive to His authority or evidence of it, is what leads to a failure to have faith. The attitude of faith is thus not simply a generic attitude of assenting to some given propositions, but an attitude that is responsive to God and to facts about the world. Faith takes on different content or forms in different epistemic situations, although there are principles which unite and specify these dispositions across instances (i.e., the two beliefs of Hebrews 11, and the kind of belief morally requisite in response to God’s authority). For this reason, failures to respond to relevant factors about the world can cause one to cease to have faith, since one is not responding adequately to God. That is, external factors can be signs according to which the believer becomes aware of God’s will and thus what God wants them to believe. Consequently, the reason that one ceases to have faith when one fails to respond to those signs in the external world is that it indicates that they maintain their attitude of belief not based on God’s authority, but on some other basis.

Finally, given that these beliefs hold together in terms of God Himself, His promises, and His deeds being the primary object of what is believed (the propositions being means for believing God), faith involves being _attentive to_ a relevant set of facts that go beyond the propositions believed. Faith involves being attentive to God and to His action in the world, since God is not a concept or a force of nature but a person with whom the believer wants to have a relationship – as is indicated by the second of those two essential beliefs of Hebrews 11. Failure to be attentive to what God _has done_ around you would mean that, even if you have those two basic beliefs, you could be failing to respond to God or not care about what He actually is doing, and you would thereby fail to have faith _in God_ at all. You would not care about _God_ in the right way as to trust _Him,_ even if you care about the propositional truths or other facts. The beliefs of Hebrews 11 are then essential to faith but not sufficient for having that attitude, since faith is not simply an assent to any given (even minimally comprehensive) set of propositions. Having faith is to entrust oneself to God and to respond to His desires. What counts as an appropriate reaction will then depend largely on God’s own interaction with us.

<!-- PDF page 50 -->
**[p. 37]**

# 2. Obligations Arising From Faith

… if you confess with your mouth the Lord Jesus and believe in your heart that God has raised Him from the dead, you will be saved.

(Rom 10:9)

Thus far, I have proposed an account of faith as belief in truths proposed by God Himself for our belief, a “reactive” virtue by which we trust God Himself as the authority behind these truths. What God proposes for our belief is important, because these truths pertain to who God is and what He promises to us. Our trust in God allows us to begin to form a relationship with Him, founded on the words He speaks to us, and which embody His intentions toward us. Since God has chosen to form a relationship with us through His word to us, it is therefore necessary to form a relationship with God that we have faith in what He speaks. Since our beatitude lies in such a relationship with God, and God has revealed that He wants us to be blessed in that personal, intimate communion with Him, without faith it would be impossible to please God. This divine faith is a gift of God, such that – while there _is_ evidence that makes faith credible – faith is motivated in a way different from rational conviction necessitated by evidence. The faithful believe God because God is trustworthy and can place their complete trust in Him. Indeed, they have a moral obligation to do so, once they perceive His authority proposing something for their belief. That is, we all have convictions to accept God’s Word _as_ God’s Word, on God’s authority, and to place our full trust in He who can neither be deceived nor deceive.

While there are generic boundaries regarding what is essential for having the disposition to trust God – one must believe that God exists and that trusting Him will establish some relationship with Him in order to have that disposition we mean by “having faith” in God – what faith concretely demands as pertains to _specific_ matters to which we need to confess, or assent, will necessarily vary within our epistemic situation. God _relates_ to us differently over time. As in the move from the ancient Jewish covenants until the New Testament, God can reveal Himself progressively through history; even aside from revealed facts, we can know that God being an intelligent, omniscient, omnibenevolent being, with total providential

DOI: 10.4324/9781003663485-2

---

<!-- PDF page 51 -->
**[p. 38]**

control over all factors of history, would relate personally to everyone within the circumstances of their lives.

We ourselves could not find God in any other way, given our epistemic limitations and situation – and it looks plausible that we cannot be obligated to do what is impossible. Consequently, those beliefs necessary for us to confess will vary depending on the epistemic access we have to God’s will. Nevertheless, in each instance, faith requires _some_ such concrete beliefs in God’s Word, as God would need to reveal His intentions so that we can form belief that He really wants relationship with us (as this fact could not otherwise be known). Faith is a then virtue which involves being responsive to God, within the circumstances we find ourselves. Faith has a _proactive_ element in which the believer actively seeks to know and do God’s will. “To live is to change, and to be perfect is to have changed often.”[^c02-1]

There are obligations that correspondingly arise once one _has_ faith that are distinct from those which oblige us to make the act of faith in the first place. In what follows, I will explore two aspects of these obligations that arise from faith. I refer to one set of these obligations as epistemic and the other as moral. This is because one set of obligations pertains to believing _rightly_ the truths proposed for us (to manage my beliefs appropriately), whereas the other set of obligations pertains to the way that my faith can affect others (to manage my relationships with others appropriately).

After outlining these obligations, I turn to consider that the intersection of these two sets of issues gives rise to many worries about the rationality of faith that seem quite distinct from the mere question of duties to believe. These objections often hinge on faith being portrayed as a species of fanaticism or intolerance, which particularly pertain to our relationship with the wider community – such as judging ourselves to be in possession of infallible truth that others do not share. Despite appearing to be secular in nature, these arguments can be given _theological_ formulation, as they are given in polemical arguments against dogmatic authority in the Church, as envisioned by Catholicism and Orthodoxy. I will argue that such arguments beg the question against the possibility of revelation in principle, being both bad arguments in themselves and bad for Christians of any stripe to endorse.

## Epistemic Obligations Arising from Faith

Some obligations which arise from faith are fundamentally epistemic, in that these obligations arise from God having revealed _truths_. For instance, we can perceive that some other potential beliefs would conflict with what God has revealed, whether as regards our interpretation of some part of God’s word or regarding some truths known independently of it. Something being revealed by God imposes epistemic obligations on the recipients of revelation to appropriately understand and affirm what God reveals. While we could put these obligations in various ways, many theists, like Christians, believe that God’s revelation is accessible to us primarily through inspired texts. Thus, what I will propose in what follows is an account of those obligations as understood through the lens of hermeneutics in reading a text. Revelation might not in principle be contained only in a text, but,

---

<!-- PDF page 52 -->
**[p. 39]**

since in any event these epistemic obligations will attach to _testimony_ contained in propositional form, I will proceed to consider those epistemic obligations deriving from revealed testimony as being given a general form by thinking of these obligations as pertaining to the hermeneutics of interpreting a text well.

In trying to make sense of what God has revealed, and trying to know or do God’s will, we justifiably presume that God reveals truths _infallibly_. I have argued that an essential element of faith consists in belief based on God’s authority, or testimonial say-so, given God’s nature as omniscient Truth (or Truth Himself subsisting). God would then not be able to reveal falsehood, and so God would not contradict Himself at any given time or over time. This results in the belief that apparent contradictions or tensions in God’s word, or between the revealed word and what we can know independently, can only be apparent – and therefore faith gives us epistemic reason to revise our beliefs accordingly, one way or another, to avoid these conflicts. In short, we can say that the very logical facts about truth, falsehood, and contradiction give us indirect duties to God: if we have duties to _believe God and what He says,_ and these duties are unique among all our other epistemic duties – since God is the First Truth – then we likewise acquire duties _only to accept_ God’s Word as if it were God’s Word and to _reject_ whatever is incompatible with what God tells us. These two “indirect” epistemic duties of faith, and the beliefs about God’s Revelation which back them, imply a whole worldview and a set of hermeneutical principles governing how the faithful ought to approach Scripture.

The illustrious ancient biblical exegete, Origen, represents the traditional views of Christians and Jews in his firm faith that “the sacred books are not the compositions of men, but … composed by inspiration of the Holy Spirit.”[^c02-2] He infers that, as these books were inspired by God, it would therefore be a mistake to treat the Scriptures as merely partially inspired; we should attend to that meaning which “the wisdom of God had constantly in view over the whole range of inspired Scripture, even to the mere letter ….”[^c02-3] That is to say, the whole of Scripture has a meaning inspired by God and aiming to teach the truth; “not a single tittle of the sacred Scriptures is without something of the wisdom of God ….”[^c02-4] It seems to me that beliefs that God is omniscient and unable to lie, as well as the beliefs that _this_ testimony is really from God, constitute sufficient reason to assume Origen’s perspective on which whatever God inspired would be inerrant, and intended by God as revealed, inclusive of each part. Further, theories of partial inspiration end up _rejecting_ that the whole of a given text is inspired. But this leaves intact the purported epistemic obligations for which I am arguing, on which belief that _this_ is an actual instance of divine testimony would then entail that this testimony is wholly inerrant and intended by God. There would be no grounds internal to faith to differentiate one part of divine testimony from another, and thus, if we have _accepted_ that a given text is inspired, it would be epistemically irrational (considering that acceptance) to reject some parts of that text as erroneous.[^c02-5]

Now, it is true that God could speak in different ways, which have been discussed in the literature, such as by speaking to people immediately in religious experiences, or by means of the utterances of humans (authorizing them to act

---

<!-- PDF page 53 -->
**[p. 40]**

as His deputies) – the first two of which seem particularly salient to _prophesy_ or being a _prophet_ – or by authorizing the written discourse of humans, as particularly seems to happen in biblical texts which are themselves authorized or appropriated despite also involving records of divine “speech acts” addressed to or through prophets.[^c02-6] In biblical interpretation, however, there is a further complication posed by distinct conceptions of revelation, some of which (like Swinburne) seem to involve a “deistic” conception on which God reveals propositions to an initial group and others hear about the divine speech by testimony, whereas a “direct” conception involves taking that divine speaking to “confront believers of all times” by means of a distinct causal action aside from what occurred when God initially revealed or authorized the text. Many Christians opt for the second of these, taking it that God appropriates Scripture as a vehicle by which to speak to every believer.[^c02-7] Wolterstorff notes that we can conceive this appropriation to occur in virtue of the ongoing activity of the Church; “the event that _counts as_ God’s appropriating this totality [the biblical books] as the medium of God’s own discourse is presumably that rather drawn-out event consisting of the Church’s settling on this totality as its canon.”[^c02-8]

My own account in the next chapter will conclude that such an event is the basis for understanding the necessity and role of dogmatic authority in Christianity. But, before turning to authority, I want to lay out preliminary assumptions about our epistemic duties to what God says, assuming simply that God speaks to us in Scripture. Traditional Christian belief is that “as God’s authoritative Word to us, Scripture is truthful in all that it teaches,” and this view is held in common by Orthodox and Catholic Christians, classical Protestant divines, and most evangelical Protestants today (as is encapsulated in formularies such as the ‘Chicago Statement on Biblical Inerrancy’).[^c02-9] What I assume is that theories on which inspired Scripture is not wholly inerrant are inconsistent with traditional Christian belief. Without aiming (again) to justify these classical Christian assumptions, let me simply outline reasoning about God that rules out “partial inspiration” theories on which God’s speech can assert falsehoods to us, thereby obliging us to believe error. There is Scriptural evidence itself against the view that God could oblige us to believe error (witnessed to in the attitude of Scriptural authors toward other parts of Scripture, such as in Jn. 10:35, 2 Tim. 3:16– 17). Similarly, Origen’s belief that Scripture is wholly inspired and therefore wholly inerrant follows from his classical doctrine of God. God is, on classical orthodox Christian theology, the source of all Goodness and Being, Truth itself subsisting. It is literally metaphysically impossible that God cause someone to assert or teach a falsehood under His inspiration. This is because falsehoods are _lacks_ or _failures_ of being, goodness, and truth. But God only causes what is good and true. What God could do is _permit_ someone to write something false. But then it would be false that God inspired or caused what was written. So, if an entire biblical text in all its parts was inspired by God, then the biblical text cannot teach or assert falsehoods, nor can one biblical text come into anything more than merely apparent contradiction with what another biblical text intends to teach or assert.

---

<!-- PDF page 54 -->
**[p. 41]**

Nevertheless, the belief that God reveals the truth and does not contradict Himself naturally raises deep questions of how we ought to navigate apparent counterevidence. That is, the chief question is how to navigate epistemic conflicts which result from our obligations to believe what God has revealed _versus_ what we seemingly know independently of our faith. Historical-critical studies in particular invite a morass of difficulties regarding, for instance, the accuracy of historical narratives in the Old Testament. Origen’s view on Scriptural inspiration is out of fashion now, because many believe that contemporary biblical scholarship has shown us that the Bible contains many instances of errors and contradictions – and thus discredited the reasonableness of faith in the inspiration of the whole of Scripture and its inerrancy. It is widely concluded, by the same token, that many instances of what is commonly called “biblical fundamentalism” is recognizable as a ridiculous version of fanaticism (easier to point to than to define), since these persons seem epistemically blameworthy inasmuch as they deny the very possibility that the results of, e.g., contemporary evolutionary biology or astrophysics could be reliable, since these contradict a “literal” reading of the first books of Genesis or the biblical genealogies (based on which some have calculated that the universe cannot be more than 6000 years old). “Fanatics” are then seen as those who are failing to fulfill their epistemic obligations toward what they know independently of Scripture.

It does not seem plausible, however, that contemporary historical-critical work gives us decisive reason to abandon belief that Scripture is inspired. For example, the fact that a false sentence occurs in Scripture is not, by itself, in contradiction with the classical orthodox view of scriptural inspiration and inerrancy expressed by Origen. This is apparent from common sense: for instance, we can _cite_ a view that is false where, in context, we intend to refute it (as happens in the book of Job); or we can tell a story and not thereby assert or intend to communicate that the story really occurred (as Christ does in His parables). There is no difficulty in holding, as Vatican II’s Dei Verbum teaches, that “everything asserted by the inspired authors or sacred writers must be held to be asserted by the Holy Spirit” (no. 11). The key word here is “assert.” All that is required to avoid the charge that the Scripture teaches error or contradicts what is taught elsewhere is to deny that the biblical author intends to _assert,_ that is, intend to teach as true, whatever apparently erroneous claim we find. The mere appearance of error or contradiction in Scripture would not by itself be a decisive “defeater” for justified or rational belief that Scripture is divine testimony. Clearly, too, if God is understood to be the author of all reality, our epistemic obligations to hold to whatever Scripture reveals can co-exist alongside our epistemic obligations to affirm what we know independently of Scripture, since God would not contradict Himself by revealing (and thus obliging us to believe) what could otherwise be known to be false. We should always prefer those interpretations which accommodate both sets of data.

Origen employs exactly this sort of principle to deal with apparent errors and contradictions. He believes that “the Scripture interwove in the history (the account of) some event that did not take place, sometimes what could not have happened ….”[^c02-10] But he thinks the appropriate response is to infer that these events were not meant to be taken as literally true. And, therefore, he famously employs

---

<!-- PDF page 55 -->
**[p. 42]**

as a principle of interpretation that, in these cases when Scripture, if taken in its literal sense, would appear to teach what is false, we should instead interpret these passages in a “spiritual” (allegorical or metaphorical) sense. And it is certainly true that God, as the author of Scripture, might intend to communicate, by means of what an author writes, _more_ than what the author intended. So, even in cases where a non-metaphorical interpretation is acceptable, Christians after Origen saw spiritual interpretation of Scripture as legitimate extensions of the literal meaning of passages. God is that sort of agent who could intend _what actually happened_ to contain various kinds or levels of spiritual significance; the story of the Israelites passing through the Red Sea might have actually been accurate – as a matter of historical fact – and also have really been intended by God to also constitute a symbol of baptism and of the Church and of many other spiritual truths. There is no reason to think this is impossible unless we assume miracles and an omniscient, omnipotent God are also impossible.

However, unlike the “fundamentalist,” and in keeping with _Dei Verbum_, Origen arguably takes this metaphorical or allegorical sense of Scripture to be what the biblical author intended to assert or teach us.
