This perspective was reflected in Dei Verbum’s note that “the books of Scripture must be acknowledged as teaching solidly, faithfully and without error that truth which God wanted put into sacred writings for the sake of salvation.” This is a principle of hermeneutical restraint: we know God inspired the authors to write what was necessary for our salvation, and since this is the ultimate purpose of scripture, we have no reason to think that its human authors had reason to teach us, besides what was necessary for our salvation, any additional/extraneous historical or scientific facts. In John Henry Newman’s short treatise on inspiration, he begins by addressing the question: “In what respect are the Canonical books inspired? It cannot be in every respect, unless we are bound de fide to believe that ‘terra in æternum stat,’ and that heaven is above us, and that there are no antipodes. And it seems unworthy of Divine Greatness, that the Almighty should in His revelation of Himself to us undertake mere secular duties, and assume the office of a narrator, as such, or an historian, or geographer, except so far as the secular matters bear directly upon the revealed truth.”[^c02-11] Galileo Galilei appealed to a similar principle in his interpretations of the Bible, when they appeared to contradict his own research: “the intention of the Holy Ghost is to teach us how one goes to heaven, not how heaven goes.”[^c02-12]

And, in fact, historical-critical studies are not at all unfriendly to this hermeneutic, for the simple reason that the biblical authors quite obviously wrote for _theological purposes,_ not for scientific or even strictly historical ones. When it comes to the creation story in Genesis, for instance, no mainstream historical-critical scholar believes that these stories were intended by their authors to be scientific accounts of how the world came to be, and so intended to teach us facts about how long it took God to create the world or even that the order of days represents a temporal order. For example, even though ancient pre-scientific people knew plants need sunlight, the biblical author wrote that the plants were created on the third day, and the sun and moon on the fourth. Whatever the author intended to teach us by giving this precise order of days in creation, it cannot _plausibly_ include asserting

---

<!-- PDF page 56 -->
**[p. 43]**

that widespread pre-scientific knowledge about plants needing light to survive is false. The stories instead are constructed to depict and teach truths about creation’s relationship with God, its internal order and goodness, and the way in which sin was a product of human choices rather than God’s design.

Fundamentalist readings that the authors intended to teach and assert that the world was created in six 24-hour days can therefore be rejected on not only on _a priori_ theological grounds of divine authorship (as failing to incorporate data known independently of Scripture) but also on grounds regarding the historical intentions of the author, just like those which would hold the biblical author intended us to teach us that plants do not need light to survive. These are not the best historical interpretation of what the text’s ancient author/editor intended us to learn. When we come upon assertions that appear to contradict good sense, or whose literal meaning would be otherwise mysterious in light of other things we know, it is always open to us either simply to suspend judgment until we have better data to surmise what the historical author intended; to see in this, as Origen saw, a spiritual meaning, intended perhaps by the author but certainly by God; or, even better, to do both.

Some could think that Scripture would be able to contain errors or contradictions in what the biblical authors properly intend to assert or teach, if what those authors are teaching about is neither faith nor morals – that is, inerrancy is limited to faith and morals. Some might even think that biblical writings incorporate errors in teaching about faith and morals. Call these theories of “partial inspiration.” This might seem appealing, since it can selectively incorporate something analogous to Origen’s appeal to a spiritual sense. The spirit of this proposal is: “Of course there are errors in Scripture – they’re right where God wants them to be!” Even if Scripture contains errors intended and asserted by the human authors, the divine author intended us to accept something like the spiritual sense of their words.[^c02-13]

There would be a serious epistemological problem with this suggestion: discovering what God wanted us to know would be practically impossible, since these claims entail that the (spiritual) sense intended by God would be _wholly contingently_ related to whatever the authors intended. That is, given that we could not know what God intended from discovering what the human authors intended to communicate, as that author might have communicated error or literally failed to communicate God’s intentions, we could not know what God intended us to believe at all, as our access to divine revelation is through the propositions proposed by those human authors. If what we knew about God or morality independently of revelation was the sole guiding principle in reading Scriptural texts and discovering the errors versus the spiritual sense intended by God, then the texts would be superfluous, as everything worth knowing would be known independently. Indeed, God would not be able to reveal Himself to us in these texts, if we were incapable of arriving at or differentiating God’s intended meaning from that which the human author wrote. Historical-critical considerations could not in practice be relevant to interpreting the meaning of revelation.

There are then very good reasons for rejecting partial inspiration. These theologians who accept a partial inspiration theory might appear to accept a principle

---

<!-- PDF page 57 -->
**[p. 44]**

on which God inspires a spiritual sense of the Scriptures, but they understand this principle not as Origen did. It is obvious that if revelation comes from the interpreter, and not the text – as the view of these contemporary thinkers entails – then both Origen’s biblical project and that of contemporary historical-critical scholarship are unnecessary or even misguided. Nothing about the historical situation of the human writers, or what they aimed to say, or what genre of writing their works fall into, or what manners of speech they employ, or how one canonical author took himself to be responding to another canonical author, would need to be known to know what God teaches by means of these texts.

Partial inspiration might seem liberating. It frees us to recover a spiritual sense of Scriptural texts and makes doctrine immune to challenges from skeptics. Yet these theories would require rejecting the very thing their modern advocates attempt to insist upon: the necessity for careful, historical, critical exegesis in understanding Scripture, rather than insisting fanatically on whatever sense _we_ read the text as teaching. Partial inerrancy insulates Christian doctrine from the hard work of biblical exegesis and systematic theology that would aim to determine what the biblical authors intended to teach (which is often difficult in many marginal cases to determine) and the way in which their views cohere.

However, all the above considerations about our epistemic obligations to read Scripture _as inspired,_ as having a claim to our belief since they embody the teaching of God as testifier, cannot be simply a set of beliefs about the text itself – instead, these beliefs also pertain indirectly to the authority of the authors themselves. That is, our aim in understanding Scripture is to understand the intentions of the authors, not simply the intention of God, since the intention of God in revealing cannot be known except by way of the intention of those human authors whom He entrusted with His message. These facts are what make a “partial” theory of inspiration untenable, but they also impose epistemic obligations that go beyond simply trying to interpret the text in a vacuum. For instance, the viewpoints of the apostles or prophets themselves would be relevant to interpreting the texts that they left us, and those viewpoints might not simply be accessible through the biblical text alone. Hence, the need for historical-critical studies on the environment of these authors, whether of the Jewish prophetic tradition or of the early Church and the immediate communities formed under the care of the apostles.

A rigorously historical-critical understanding of biblical studies as aiming to determine what the authors intended to teach or assert is compatible, on the one side, with investigating how the biblical texts were edited from pre-existing works or transmitted through a fallible manuscript tradition that involved copyist errors, etc. (Inspiration applies to the “final” compiler of a text, not to every author of preexisting materials, and it does not apply to the manuscript tradition.) On the other hand, scientific understanding of the authorial intent is compatible with recognition that the “literal” sense does not exhaust that which God intended to assert through the biblical authors. God can aim for _more_ than was before the minds of the author. Far from exempting us from investigating what the authors themselves intended, the plenary theory of inspiration holds that God intended any further senses of a passage _precisely in virtue of_ its literal sense.[^c02-14] God can, for instance, actually make

---

<!-- PDF page 58 -->
**[p. 45]**

a bush burn without being consumed, and nevertheless intend that bush to represent a spiritual meaning; or, God can raise up a real king, David, and intend that David symbolize Christ.

This might raise the partial inspiration theory in a new form, as inviting a “_dualsource”_ account of revelation, where we could only know the intended spiritual sense of Scripture (i.e., what God intends to reveal), considering a further independent source of supernatural data. This does not make Scripture superfluous, as it would not be true (as in the partial inspiration account) that we could know all that was revealed by God simply from independently known natural data regarding God or His intentions. Instead, the idea is that God intended two independent sources to form one deposit of revelation, including Scripture as only one of these, and that this is why we cannot know what God intends to reveal without appeal to the secondary source. That secondary source might be variously understood as an inherited body of knowledge passed down outside of Scripture (“Tradition”) or as the decisions of an authoritative teaching body (“Magisterium”) or as the enlightenment of the Holy Spirit operating directly and immediately on the mind of each individual believer (“perspicuity”). All these explanations would share a form: the _meaning_ of Scripture cannot be grasped independently of this other source.

Such theories of inspiration concern the _meaning_ God intended to communicate, holding that God’s meaning is inaccessible without these independent sources. To prevent misunderstandings, we should differentiate this theory from the two other potential theories in the environs. It might be that Tradition, or Magisterium, or perspicuity might be necessary for reasons other than recognizing the meaning God intended to communicate in Scripture (as indeed I will defend later); positions on which such sources are necessary for other reasons aside from grasping the meaning of the text should not be conflated with the dual-source theory of revelation. The dual-source theory, as I portray it, involves stronger modal claims that God’s message could not be grasped at all without these other sources. Theories that we need dogmatic authority or Tradition or perspicuity to address other necessities – for example, that an authority might be required to decide disputes among theological experts who have already independently arrived at various candidate meanings – need not entail a dual-source theory of revelation.

Or, we could understand claims that the revealed meaning of Scripture is epistemically inaccessible to those without faith (or right faith) as simply being a claim that nobody outside of those who have faith _assent_ to the meaning of Scripture in the right way, that is, nobody who is not already a member of the faithful _understands_ or _accepts_ the meaning God has proposed. The believer understands these texts as the voice of God speaking to her, believing that “God chose men and while employed by Him they made use of their powers and abilities, so that with Him acting in them and through them, they, as true authors, consigned to writing everything and only those things which He wanted” (_Dei Verbum_, no. 11). If one did not believe in inspiration, one would not understand the meaning of the text _as_ God’s voice. To a person without faith, a given passage would not be understood as expressing a divine intention (e.g., understanding “Pick up your cross and follow me” as a _divine command_ addressed to me, the reader), but only as a

---

<!-- PDF page 59 -->
**[p. 46]**

possible interpretation of what an ancient author intended. Thus, this position holds that only those who already believe are capable of understanding God’s intended meaning. But notice that this position does not require us to reject that those who are not believers can _recognize_ or _perceive_ the meaning of the text, or even that cannot recognize it _as_ the voice of God _as portrayed in the text_, but only that they do not recognize or accept it as being either truly the voice of God or as addressed to themselves.

Those without faith, employing a reliable historical-critical set of criteria, might be just as capable of recognizing what the ancient author intended as any person with faith, and thus able to recognize the divine intention communicated within a text – although neither _recognizing the text as true_ (and so as an instance of divine testimony) nor accepting that divine intention as morally binding. So, if we are careful, there is nothing to prevent an unbeliever from recognizing the meaning of a sacred text. However, it might be true that unbelievers would fail to be attentive to other sources of evidence that are open to believers, which qualify their exploration of the meaning of texts. For instance, believers interpret the Scriptural texts within a canon of texts, and thus as a whole, in which God cannot contradict Himself. For instance, the believer would take certain evidence from other texts in the canon as a boundary on interpretation, so that they would not accept that true contradictions exist in the Scripture, whereas the historical-critical methodology of a given text in isolation does not rule this out. These epistemic considerations are not necessarily unreasonable, even though they logically depend upon already having accepted these texts as instances of divine revelation. The believer’s hermeneutical limits would be reasonable (or unreasonable) depending upon whether it is reasonable to accept Scripture as a divinely inspired whole. (I assume here that it is reasonable for Christian believers to do so.)

A dual-source theory of the _meaning_ of revelation looks to me straightforwardly incoherent. Its claims about inaccessibility in meaning cannot be restricted only to texts, in principle. So, if there were good arguments that the meaning of propositions in Scripture was epistemically inaccessible to persons like us, this would seem to apply to any propositional form of communication. Independent sources of revelation aside from Scripture (e.g., Tradition, Magisterium), however, would seem to involve communicating propositions. This theory of meaning would generate an infinite regress: we would need interpreters for each interpreter, ad infinitum, as any meaning communicated through any given proposition would be inaccessible. Perhaps then we could halt the regression in all these cases by affirming that these independent sources of knowledge of revelation provide _non-propositional_ knowledge. But then these are not theories of meaning, since non-propositional knowledge would not be pertinent to interpreting the _meaning_ of the text of Scripture, as the latter is propositional knowledge of some kind. If dual-source theories of revelation are to be consistent, they would not rest on claims that we cannot arrive at the meaning of Scripture independently of these sources, but rather claims that there are extra sources of revealed data that _constrain_ good interpretations of Scripture. The obligations we would have for appealing to these sources, however, would simply follow from the _fact_ that God has revealed them in addition

---

<!-- PDF page 60 -->
**[p. 47]**

to Scripture, generating obligations on us to believe them, not from the principled inaccessibility of the meaning of Scripture.

While many might assume that dual-source theories of the meaning of revelation represent Catholic theories of the way in which we read Scripture, this suggestion is contrary to how the Church arrives at her views about Revelation or how own self-stated views of this: “… the Holy Spirit was not promised [to the Church’s ministers] that they might disclose a new doctrine by his revelation, but rather that, with his assistance, they might reverently guard and faithfully explain the revelation or deposit of faith that was handed down through the apostles.”[^c02-15] As we will see in the next chapter, claims that the Church is providentially protected from teaching error, and its ministers have authority from Christ to teach, do not entail that they have any independent source of revelation apart from what it received, nor does the necessity of (infallible) dogmatic authority rest on the view that we cannot discern through inference what is meant by God in Scripture.

The Church’s bishops and theologians discover what God revealed through no other way than by reading and reasoning about Scripture, just like anyone else. If the writers of the sacred texts taught falsehood, the Church would have no independent means for knowing what God intended to teach by means of these texts. In fact, nobody could. That’s because the most important things these texts teach us concern what God is like in Himself (as a Trinity), His relation to what happened (His Providence or that Jesus is God incarnate), or God’s special will – and nobody knows those things except God. It would therefore be a very _serious_ error to think of the Church as introducing novel doctrines that are not found in what it received from Christ. St. Paul condemns it in the strongest possible terms: “But even if we, or an angel from heaven, preach any other gospel to you than what we have preached to you, let him be accursed” (Gal. 1:8). This is why the Church herself condemned this theory of inspiration by acceptance of texts into the canon.[^c02-16]

A last objection comes from skeptics about biblical inspiration generally. Isn’t it irrational to engage in historical-critical studies with a presumption that what the human biblical authors intended to teach can form (somehow) a coherent whole with other works in the canon, despite being composed by disparate authors, in disparate situations and times? Doesn’t their faith prevent Christian exegetes from struggling with the text scientifically and objectively, as being open to the possibility that these texts contain true discrepancies which might be impossible to harmonize? These questions presume that coming to biblical studies in faith will necessarily lead to bad results: the Christian will _force_ the text to fit their preconceptions or will be _dogmatic_ in rejecting results that contradict their Church’s doctrine.

The assumption that believers cannot engage objectively in biblical studies presumes that belief in inspiration so controls exegesis that Christians will be unable to deal honestly with the text. The first thing to point out is that dogmatic beliefs are at a high level of generality – Catholic dogma, for instance, does not mandate reading particular passages in any specific way (even Matt. 16:18 is not so defined), beyond commitment to finding _some_ interpretation consistent with our dogma. Belief in inspiration is not particularly prejudicial to impartial

---

<!-- PDF page 61 -->
**[p. 48]**

and objective investigation of historical fact, as there is no necessary _material content_ to the way in which that whole should be formed, even if there are some high-level formal constraints. And it certainly seems an open question whether Scripture is a consistent whole in this way, as there are no “assured results” of biblical scholarship showing that there are true contradictions in Scripture. The reason is simple. Determining definitively what authors intend to assert or teach in such a way as to rule out _all_ other potential interpretations is _hard_ and _controversial_. We can often gauge a better or worse reading of a passage, certainly, but even this is often relative to criteria that change over time in the field of scholarship.

And, for example, the Christian exegete’s claims about potential links between the intended meaning of an author in one text and the prophetic meaning that text has in relation to a later event do not imply that the exegete must hold that the author had this intent _in mind_ when writing. Rather, God can intend meanings that the human author did not, in addition to what they wrote. Nevertheless, it seems to me that a solid theory of _plenary_ inspiration is what secures that this possibility of spiritual meanings does not undermine the scientific spirit of the scholar: such spiritual meanings can be intended by God, the theory holds, only insofar as they depend on what the author intended historically. Then, the appeal to spiritual meanings is unreliable unless we can first arrive at a clear idea of what the “literal” meaning of such passages is. Spiritual or prophetic readings of biblical passages are then not opposed in principle to a scientific or objective spirit.

These facts are clearly illustrated by systematic theology, which relates particular biblical texts and what that author teaches with doctrinal claims developed later, such as the Trinity. The systematic exegete attempts to determine – through ordinary reasoning and historical scholarship – what the text intends to teach. People in the ancient world understood what it was to assert truths or teach, as opposed to telling fictional stories (or bullshitting), and so were capable of speech acts that are recognizably assertions. Nobody needs a philosophical theory in mind to assert a truth.

The task of systematics is to determine the relationship among those truths asserted or taught. This is not substantially different from how philosophers move from ordinary language claims or intuitions to clarify and make consistent common-sense ethical principles, or what sort of things we ordinarily take to exist, or discover the rules of logical inference, or classify the kinds of speech acts involved in ordinary language. The success of the endeavor lies in whether the resulting systematic exegete’s dogmatic theories successfully represent the claims of the authors, making the best sense of the data, and not merely in whether it is an internally consistent theory.

As we often only over time discover further logical connections among texts, or other possible interpretations, considering progress in biblical theology more generally, it is not surprising that systematic exegesis develops over time in complexity, applying the texts to problems that the authors of the texts might not have had in mind when writing them. Nothing about this process involves an unscientific or unhistorical outlook. The systematician is merely aiming at a kind of _intellectual_

---

<!-- PDF page 62 -->
**[p. 49]**

history that ranges over the biblical texts, as opposed to a more local or manuscriptcentric perspective.

Beneath these questions is a more fundamental problem: is it rational to _believe_ when you do not _know_? This wide-ranging question is beyond the scope of this essay. But the generic problem has a specific application to biblical studies: what seems odd, many think, about reading spiritual meanings or prophecies into biblical texts is that claims of this sort would presume that God has inspired the Bible. We cannot know these facts from the historical study of the text itself or from _any_ naturalistic evidence at all that the exegete has access to while in his armchair. For that reason, it seems inappropriate for the Christian exegete to suggest their results form part of the _scientific_ results of biblical scholarship.

This apparent problem looks to be nominal disagreement. Is “science” necessarily methodologically naturalistic? It seems to me that there _is_ some broad and uncontroversial sense of “methodologically naturalistic history” which would translate in this biblical context into “aiming solely at discovering what the historical author intended.” This project would require the Christian exegete to bracket those meanings God might have intended beyond what the human author did, when aiming specifically at historical-critical study. But this kind of naturalism looks uncontroversial in light of the plenary inspiration theory, as the literal sense is the ground for any other _in addition to_ readings of the text that God might have intended. “Permission” to bracket spiritual readings is built into the theory of inspiration itself. Although the notion of “methodological naturalism” would need to be worked out carefully – as the authors themselves often claim something is a miracle or God’s will, etc., and methodology cannot merely rule _a priori_ that these authors are mistaken – I see no reason in principle such a rule about doing historical work accurately would call into question the possibility that these texts tell the truth about God and the supernatural.

One might suspect this conclusion is too quick. For instance, the 19th-century liberal Protestant “demythologizers,” such as Rudolf Bultmann, held that Scripture cannot in principle teach anything miraculous, since belief in the miraculous was unreasonable for modern people. “It is impossible to use electrical light and the wireless and to avail ourselves of modern medical and surgical discoveries, and at the same time to believe in the New Testament world of spirits and miracles.”[^c02-17] Alvin Plantinga teases out the implicit reasoning as an argument that “the traditional concept of miracle is irreconcilable with our modern understanding of science and history. Science proceeds on the assumption that whatever events occur in the world can be accounted for in terms of other events that also belong within the world.”[^c02-18] That is, roughly, that we can rule out the possibility of miracles because their possibility would conflict with the methodological naturalism that biblical scholarship should assume by default.

However, as Plantinga has argued extensively, such attempts to rule out the miraculous in principle are unreasonable. Since my purposes are (unlike Plantinga) not to argue against skepticism about faith in general, but only that such objections cannot reasonably be endorsed by Christians, it suffices to say that it would be a question-begging objection for _theists_ to make in the context of biblical

---

<!-- PDF page 63 -->
**[p. 50]**

scholarship. Clearly, many of those advocating this skeptical position regarding principled limits to biblical scholarship were, like Bultmann, theists or even Christian theologians. On what basis would these advocates claim to know that God will not or cannot work a miracle? That seems to be a question concerning God’s nature and intentions. And the reasons given for why we should embrace methodological naturalism in biblical scholarship seem to rest on assumptions ultimately that God could not work miracles in our day and age. But why believe this? As Plantinga points out, there are many reasonable, scientific people who accept that Jesus rose from the dead, that miracles are possible, etc. There is nothing obviously contradictory here.[^c02-19]

Although systematic _theology_ has its own standards of evidence and argument that aim to construct a consistent interpretation of the text, and should not rule out in principle the possibility of miracles, prophecy, and so forth, we can note that a biblical interpretation which admits that the authors believed in such things (without taking a stand on whether they actually occurred) is comprehensible to a great degree within a naturalistic picture of biblical scholarship. That is, even abstracting from theistic metaphysical commitments about God or the possibility of miracles, there is nothing obviously unintelligible in a comprehensive “scientific” reading of a text which also allows, methodologically, a scholar to draw the conclusion _that the authors intended to describe miracles._ It is a perfectly reasonable enterprise – as a matter of “intellectual history” – to attempt to understand the mindset and commitments of the authors on their own terms, and how later authors attempted to integrate their thoughts about God or His role in history as part of an ongoing theological conversation among Jews and Christians. Clearly, if the authors themselves intended to tell us about what they took to be miracles, or prophecies, or God’s actions, or God’s intentions, it seems odd to rule out such possible interpretations of a text _a priori_ on the basis of extraneous theological convictions that either God does not exist or that God cannot work miracles. And so, if we begin with faith that these texts are revealed by God, it seems quite reasonable to conclude that our epistemic obligations in trying to understand what God communicates would entail that we should accept Scriptural (revealed) texts as a conversation aimed at the truth, and that those truths He wanted us to believe are primarily discoverable in terms of what the biblical authors intended to assert.

## Moral Obligations Arising from Faith

Faith is not merely a matter of intellectual assent but also of proactive _desire_ to do God’s will. Some such desire is essential to faith, since faith is motivated precisely by belief that God wants us to believe Him, which would then be to fulfill God’s will. There is nevertheless the possibility of distinguishing “living faith” motivated properly by love of God, versus “lifeless” faith motivated improperly from something other than love of God (e.g., desire not to go to hell). “Lifeless” faith involves a desire to assent to God’s Word and thus counts as an act of faith in God. But someone who assents to God’s word on this basis seems improperly disposed toward God in faith – they have not “entrusted” themselves to God correctly out of

---

<!-- PDF page 64 -->
**[p. 51]**

a desire for relationship with God. If it is necessary for salvation that we abandon sin and fulfill our duties, out of love for God, then only living faith is sufficient for salvation. Thus, “faith” considered simply as a desire to assent to God’s word, based on God’s authority, could be motivated imperfectly – and thus would not be sufficient for salvation without those other dispositions to love God and neighbor.[^c02-20]

Nevertheless, all Christians agree that an act of faith _itself_ gives rise to moral obligations. All parties agree fundamentally that union with God lies not only in belief in God’s word, but also _desire_ for God – i.e., love of Him – and therefore desire to do His will. Apart from the generic way that faith gives us an obligation to seek God’s will, what God says to us could involve more specific duties. The faithful could have various moral obligations arising from faith, which they know themselves to have from God’s revelation, even if they have independent reason to believe that they have these moral obligations. For instance, God instructs us to obey the Ten Commandments, seek baptism, partake of the Eucharist, give away all that we have and follow Christ, and the like. That is why, while Christians might disagree whether it is essential _to faith_ to have a disposition to fulfill these moral duties, or whether having that disposition to fulfill our duties is necessary for salvation in addition to believing God’s word, most Christians do not disagree that faith gives rise to duties one way or another – and that a failure to fulfill these duties would constitute a serious sin.

Among these, God’s publicly stated intentions aim at promoting union with everyone (not just me). I therefore acquire obligations that involve aiming to help others likewise come into relationship with God by means of faith. The paradigmatic expression of these obligations is that of Christ’s “great commission” to His disciples.

And Jesus came and spoke to them, saying, ‘All authority has been given to Me in heaven and on earth. Go therefore and make disciples of all the nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, teaching them to observe all things that I have commanded you; and lo, I am with you always, even to the end of the age.’

(Matt. 28:18–20)

If by faith we rightly believe that we ought to have a desire to help others achieve union with God, we would then have moral obligations arising from faith to, e.g., to confess the Christian faith, witness to that faith by words and deeds, and to spread it among nonbelievers.

The fundamental source for these obligations lies in the moral obligation to love our neighbor as God loves them – “If someone says, ‘I love God,’ and hates his brother, he is a liar; for he who does not love his brother whom he has seen, how can he love God whom he has not seen?” (1 Jn. 4:20) – and thereby to share in God’s own desire to reveal Himself to the world. Given what has been said before about the way in which faith is necessary for salvation, it therefore follows that to fail to help others discover the faith could constitute a gravely serious moral failure of omission.[^c02-21] Similarly, to say or do anything that would lead to someone failing

---

<!-- PDF page 65 -->
**[p. 52]**

to believe the Christian message or otherwise cause them to sin would be to act contrary to those persons’ highest spiritual good. Scandalizing others in that way would be a gravely serious moral failure.[^c02-22]

But Christian faith is also _practiced_ in a community, with other believers. Just as they have duties toward others to share their faith and not scandalize unbelievers, they likewise have duties to strengthen the faith of our brothers and sisters, with correlative obligations not to scandalize other Christians by their behavior. Clearly, however, there are further moral duties toward a Christian community of other believers that go beyond those _ad extra,_ toward nonbelievers, given that the shape and duties of the Christian reflect the moral/spiritual ideal laid out for Christians by Christ. For instance, not only do Christians have duties to love one another, but they have moral obligations to follow the wishes of Christ regarding contingent matters that pertain to Christian life and practice – for instance, to celebrate the Eucharist (Mk. 14:22–25, Lk. 22:18–20), to wash each others’ feet (Jn. 13:14), to go forth and proclaim the Gospel, instructing everyone to be baptized in the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit (cited above), and so forth. Doing these things is a condition of what it is to have faith in Jesus, insofar as anyone with right faith in Christ will recognize their faith to impose these as moral obligations. These do not then reflect general or natural moral obligations, as in Christ’s command for us to love one another or follow the Ten Commandments, nor do they follow from the nature of faith generically considered, since faith in God’s revelation needs to be specified, considering what God actually reveals. Christ’s self-revelation specifies or qualifies the duties of faith such that believers ought to believe in Jesus Christ as they believe in the Father (Jn. 14:1), so that these moral obligations to obey Christ’s commands follow from their faith in Him.

Finally, Christian faith is _received_ from others. Faith constitutively involves trust in God’s testimony about Himself, but most of us are not prophets or apostles who received the message directly from God. Instead, we have received the Gospel from other human beings. In fact, it is a component of Christian revelation that God has sent or entrusted the message to human agents, throughout salvation history down to the New Testament, and thus that we can possibly have moral obligations to trust other human beings as representing God as emissaries of His word. Faith therefore involves trusting _that_ such testimony through created agents instantiates or embodies God’s testimony. Christian faith normatively occurs by means of receiving God’s word _from_ another acting as an instrument of God’s speaking, i.e., instrumentally or derivatively. There are therefore moral obligations which arise from these facts about how God wants us to receive His word from other people.

Now, these considerations are the same which give rise to the epistemic obligations that I outlined above, since we cannot coherently believe that God partially inspired His prophets or apostles. Rather, if God has appointed some persons as prophets or apostles or evangelists, we would have obligations to trust these emissaries and their word as instantiating God’s own words to us. Trusting human testimony in such circumstances, such as trusting the writer of the Gospel of Mark, is thereby to trust God’s own testimony. Faith also thus constitutively involves

---

<!-- PDF page 66 -->
**[p. 53]**

accepting the Gospel of Mark _as_ God’s word, and _wholly_ such, not as potentially or partly composed of Mark’s fallible human words. These epistemic considerations about the inspiration or infallibility attached to these human messengers also give rise to moral obligations, in addition, because they give shape to the Christian community as one which _normatively_ maintains the chain of transmission from those original emissaries of God’s word. It could be a moral failure on our part if the message were distorted through our negligence or if we did not otherwise continue to preserve what we have received so that others could benefit from it. These obligations are fundamentally communal, given Christ’s own commands addressed to His followers as a group, but also because there are correlative obligations explicitly stated in Scripture that we should maintain communion in the same faith with other believers (e.g., 1 Cor. 1:10, Eph. 4:3–5, 11–13).

From what I have said, there are then at least three sets of correlative moral obligations which arise from faith: obligations to _confess,_ to _practice,_ and to _maintain_ the faith. These are not strictly separable sets of obligations, since witnessing our faith naturally requires being attentive to other believers, and maintaining our faith in line with normative expectations will involve public confession of that faith before others, and so on. Most notably, the communal nature of obligations to maintain the faith will entail similar communal obligations to confess and practice the faith as a Christian Church. It will be a moral failure on the part of believers if their Church fails to adequately represent the ideals of belief and practice that Jesus Christ willed for them. In virtue of these communal facts about our normative relation to other believers, our obligations to love other Christians will also involve helping other Christians to fulfill _their_ obligations, as well as being properly responsive to assistance (or correction) in fulfilling our own. For instance, it would be unreasonable to think we have obligations to correct others in matters of faith or conduct, while not being ourselves amenable to that same correction by others.[^c02-23]

## Bad Objections to Dogmatic Authority

These reflections on epistemic and moral obligations arising from faith are meant to ground my subsequent discussion of dogmatic authority upon a foundation of basic agreement regarding what is broadly uncontroversial and ecumenically acceptable. I will argue in the next chapter that these obligations are what make reasonable the controversial conclusion that dogmatic authority exists in the Church, since that authority would be instrumentally and constitutively necessary for believers to fulfill these communal moral obligations, and that authority’s decisions regarding the faith would create epistemic obligations on believers to assent to its teaching. Before proposing those arguments, however, it is helpful to review the way in which the considerations about the nature of faith and its epistemic or moral obligations already give us decisive reason to reject some prominent arguments against the existence of dogmatic authority in the Christian Church. These arguments are bad arguments, as I will show, since they equally apply to faith in general and not to dogmatic authority in particular, and thus should be rejected by Christians of all stripes.

---

<!-- PDF page 67 -->
**[p. 54]**

One prominent objection to dogmatic authority relies on a very general claim that we could not be obligated to believe anyone other than God. Steven Nemes gives voice to this kind of objection in arguing that human beings cannot, in principle, possess dogmatic authority. But there are immediate problems in cashing out what Nemes means. Christians receive God’s testimony not from God immediately, but through created messengers or intermediaries, like prophets, apostles, pastors, and evangelists. If we had no reason to believe anyone other than God, we could have no reason to believe a prophet or apostle. For this reason, Nemes admits that human beings can “[testify] and [bear] witness to what God or Jesus have said or done,” while rejecting that those human beings cannot “tell another person that something must (not) be done for the sake of entering into or preserving friendship with God.”[^c02-24] But to witness to God or Jesus’ words that something must (not) be done would be clearly an instance of telling someone what must (not) be done. To say that these humans cannot _authoritatively_ tell us to do this would be unhelpful, since “authority” was the very concept in need of explication.

Nemes specifies that only God and Jesus can tell us these things “in an original, infallible, and in principle irreversible manner,” whereas human beings cannot – “the human word is only binding insofar as it succeeds in relating and bearing witness to the always distinct and separable divine word.”[^c02-25] He further defines these terms as follows: “original” since God and Jesus possess that authority in virtue of what they are; “infallible” since God and Jesus “cannot fail to meet the purpose of theological authority in their exercises of it”; “irreversible” since “God and Jesus are not legitimately called into question nor corrected by anyone else on any matter.”[^c02-26] Yet Nemes’ characterization of theological authority is quite confusing, given the context in which Catholic, Orthodox, and other Christian theological traditions teach that human beings (the pastors of the Church) can exercise theological authority in ways that certain solemnly taught dogmatic definitions are, in principle, both infallible and irreversible.

For instance, in these traditions, it is widely held that dogmatic definitions can “correct” a previous one without “reversing” that decision. The dogmatic definitions of Christological councils after Nicaea I “corrected” that definition, expanding the creedal statements and qualifying what Nicaea I had taught. Or, Vatican II is usually understood to have introduced necessary qualifications alongside Vatican I’s prior definition of papal infallibility and primacy. For these reasons, we should understand a “reversal” in the context of teaching the truth to be strictly only a “contradiction.” To say, then, that a dogmatic authority’s decisions are “irreversible” requires us only to affirm that later teachings cannot _contradict_ former teachings. But then this immediately clarifies too that God could not reverse what He Himself has said and so could not “correct” a dogmatic authority, since that would imply that God would contradict what He Himself said. And God never contradicts Himself.

Similarly, “infallible” in such traditions is not taken to mean that their authority cannot fail to meet its purpose. This is because the purpose of authority could conceivably extend further than simply teaching the truth – for instance, pastoral authority involves the ability to impose _sanctions_ of some kind on believers, such that

---

<!-- PDF page 68 -->
**[p. 55]**

they cannot (for example) teach in a Church institution or preach in Church. And these traditions do not believe they have any promise that pastoral authority cannot be misused by those who hold it, even in solemn contexts such as ecumenical councils. “Infallible” dogmatic authority, in these traditions, is therefore defined as authority such that, if the teaching is exercised legitimately, that teaching will not be false. It is obviously true that God’s authority in teaching is infallible in being unable to teach error, and, consequently, unable to entertain contradictions – since contradictions are a clear instance of error. Any derivative exercise of God’s own teaching authority would similarly be unable to teach error or contradictions.

Nemes’ notion of theological authority is a very limited sense of authority, since it pertains only to infallibility, and there might be other kinds of pastoral authority associated with dogma – such as noted earlier, the power to impose a sanction. But we still need to clarify, however, what it is to exercise this authority _derivatively_. Nemes characterizes this as possessing that authority “only through the assistance of something else.”[^c02-27] But this too is a rather confusing qualification, since theological _authorities_ are typically understood not to exercise their authority in a transient way. These traditions therefore typically understand theological authority to involve _a commission from God_ such that the commissioned is unable to teach error, within the limits specified by that commission. A “commission” in this context is to be primarily understood as a role or office attached to a mission or intended purpose. And, as Nemes notes, this mission involves acting _for the sake of entering into or preserving friendship with God._ Hence, those traditions which profess theological authorities typically hold that these authorities have been given a commission to teach infallibly only in matters of faith and morals, not in unrelated matters[^c02-28] – i.e., what is necessary for entering into and preserving friendship with God. Similarly, since that commission is attached to an office, rather than a person, exercising that office will be a necessary condition for exercising that authority; what is said outside of this office would not be infallible. And there will conceivably be other limits.

These qualifications are all important, however, as Nemes’ arguments against dogmatic authority often rest upon confusing one sense of “irreversibility” or “infallibility” with another. For instance, Nemes’ arguments against infallibility rest upon confusing _knowing something in an infallible or self-evident way_ and _being unable to be wrong._ Nemes begins by noting that the theological claims asserted by Christianity involve truths regarding unobservable facts about what God is like or how He acts (e.g., that God is a Trinity, Jesus is divine, the Eucharist is transubstantiated, that the biblical authors were inspired), as well as contingent truths regarding past events (e.g., Moses parting the Red Sea, Jesus rising from the dead) and future events (e.g., the resurrection from the dead, eternal life) which are not themselves observable in the present. These truths “cannot be accessed apart from a choice to interpret things in a certain way,” and so any judgment that they are true is therefore fallible (since we could have chosen to believe what is, in fact, false).[^c02-29] Given that Christian doctrine is not self-evidently known to be true, “Christian doctrine cannot now be known infallibly.”[^c02-30] Nemes proposes that “one should only speak about things with as much confidence as one’s access to the facts

---

<!-- PDF page 69 -->
**[p. 56]**

permits,” and therefore concludes: “we should renounce the pretense of infallibility in theology.”[^c02-31]

Nemes’ argument here is straightforwardly fallacious, however. It involves a fallacy of equivocation between two notions of “infallibility.” Specifically, Nemes infers from the fact that no believer can reasonably believe that they apprehend the truths of Christian doctrine in a self-evident way (“infallibly _knows_” or subjectively “infallible”) to the conclusion that nobody could reasonably believe that a given agent _could not teach falsehood_ (objectively “infallible”). And we can see a similar fallacy if I were to infer from the fact that it is not self-evident to me whether Paris is a beautiful city (I am not in Paris as I write this book) that it is therefore not possible for me to know whether Paris is a beautiful city. Clearly, I can know whether Paris is a beautiful city by testimony or some other means, even if I am not myself present in Paris. In the same way, Christian believers hold that the facts of Christian doctrine are not self-evident to them, but that they have moral obligations to believe that they are true, since God has told them that they are, and they believe that they have sufficiently good reasons to believe that God has in fact told them these things. Yet, it is obviously reasonable for theists to believe that God would _infallibly know_ these truths. And it is also reasonable to believe that God cannot teach error, and that God can prevent human agents from teaching error. If God does this (whether by inspiring authors or authorizing a dogmatic authority), then it can be reasonable to believe that these agents are infallible and cannot teach falsehood.

Such inspired authors or dogmatic authorities would not _infallibly know_ the truths of the faith, in the sense that these truths are self-evident to them but would be infallible in the sense that they could not teach falsehood, since they were providentially guided by God not to do so. Similarly, Nemes’ premise that we should “only speak about things insofar as our access to the facts permits” does not rule out accessing the facts by testimony in addition to self-evidence. But, if our access to the facts were via divine testimony, it will then be permissible to speak about the facts as if we knew those facts could not be false, since God’s testimony to the facts cannot be false. For the same reason, if we have access to those facts through testimony whose trustworthiness is derivative from that of God – as theological authorities are supposed to be – then we would be permitted to speak about the facts as if we knew those facts could not be false. Theological authorities have a commission from God to testify to those facts, and God’s testimony to the facts cannot be false, nor would God authorize those to speak in His name who could mislead us. So, it seems to me that Nemes’ objection presents itself as a _de jure_ objection to infallible human testimony, but it really boils down to a _de facto_ question regarding whether anyone has been authorized by God to transmit testimony about Himself. Whether churches today (or their leaders) possess a related authorization is a _de facto_ empirical question as much as whether a given person is a true or a false prophet – we cannot decide this question by general claims about the nature of revelation without simply dismissing prophecy in general. I see no good reason for Christians who accept prophesies and biblical inspiration to rule out such authorization for dogmatic authorities _de jure._

---

<!-- PDF page 70 -->
**[p. 57]**

Nemes’ arguments against the possibility of “irreversible” dogmatic decisions suffer from a similar confusion. Nemes characterizes dogmatic authority in terms of belief in “traditionalism” and “hierarchy,” where these taken together hold that there are theological authorities whose decisions cannot be “revised or corrected after the fact,” where “the consequence for willful rejection of them is exclusion from the community,” and where these authorities have the “right or standing … to demand things of others without opening themselves up to correction or legitimate refusal.”[^c02-32] The problem with this characterization is that it clearly rolls together various notions of “authority” and “correction” in ways I have already flagged as problematic. The authority to impose a _sanction_ is not obviously identical with or follows from the authority to _teach the truth_ in such a way that one cannot be wrong. (I will deal in later chapters with these other kinds of authority.) Nor does it follow that, because one has an authority to teach the truth infallibly that one has a right _to demand things of others_. The only conceivable “demand” that would be made by teaching infallibly are the epistemic or moral obligations that others should believe your teaching, given that it is derivative of divine teaching, which cannot be false. But it is not obvious that anyone has the right or standing to legitimately refuse to submit to divine teaching or to correct God. Nemes’ own characterization of divine theological authority precludes that anyone could have such a right, in fact.

Aside from biblical arguments to which I will allude later, Nemes offers philosophical arguments that no human being can, in principle, exercise authority in such a way that they are infallible, and thus that their decisions are irreversible, given God’s providential guidance of that teaching authority. One of these is that all such infallible dogmatic authorities would only derivatively teach with divine authority but that their word would necessarily be treated as “functionally original” in that “it should be treated or received in the community as having the same significance and importance as though God or Jesus were the one speaking.”[^c02-33] The primary effect is that “there is no greater court of appeal than the relevant authorities.”[^c02-34] But Nemes argues that there always is such a greater court of appeal. Given that the divine word is essentially distinct from the word of these authorities, “anything that any other person says in the church can and should always be judged according to this higher court of appeal.”[^c02-35]

This involves at least two confusions. The first is that, as these traditions hold that their ministers exercise theological authority derivatively, that is, in virtue of God’s having given them that authority, it is unclear what it would mean to say that those derivative authorities are “subject to correction” by God or Jesus. As the authority in any legitimate exercise of derivative authority is God’s own, for God to “reverse” a legitimate exercise of that derivative authority would be to contradict Himself. The second is that, while God is an authority, the Bible is not a theological authority, since the Bible is not an agent and thus cannot _exercise_ any authority. To say, then, that nobody has standing in the Christian community to teach in such a way that they cannot be corrected is to say that nobody in the Christian community has been given a commission in virtue of which they can ever teach infallibly. Nemes has then simply begged the question in presuming that there are no

---

<!-- PDF page 71 -->
**[p. 58]**

theological authorities, not given us a reason to reject their existence in principle. For the same reason, when Nemes insists that “there is always a certain ‘distance’ of agency between God and Jesus and every mere human speaker,” and therefore that “it is always possible for a person within the church other than Jesus or God to have erred in his or her understanding of things,” Nemes has simply begged the question.[^c02-36] God does not need to be identical with the speaker for God to be able to providentially protect that speaker from error.

If it were true that God cannot protect anyone distinct from Himself from error, then it would also be obvious that Nemes’ own theological commitments are false. For instance, the Bible is not identical with God. But the Bible (or, more specifically, the biblical authors) then would not be able to be protected by God from having asserted error. While Nemes is willing to accept this conclusion that the biblical authors and apostles were fallible, and that their teaching was not protected from error, and is in principle reversible,[^c02-37] many other Christians are not – and would not be convinced to abandon Scriptural inerrancy or inspiration simply on the grounds that the Bible or those human authors are not identical with God. Indeed, however, Nemes’ characterization of Jesus being a theological authority contradicts his other theological commitments, specifically, his Arian Christology. Nemes rejects in many different contexts that belief in Jesus’ divinity is essential to Christian faith; he characterizes belief in Jesus’ divinity as a “metaphysical” belief that is undecidable in principle, and at best a matter of idle curiosity, which only serves to generate unnecessary and uncharitable theological anathemas.[^c02-38]

Nevertheless, Nemes clearly and explicitly associates Jesus with God in exercising authority non-derivatively. Nemes’ Christology holds that Jesus occupies a certain high mediatorial and teaching role vis-à-vis Christian believers but holds that it is theoretically unnecessary for Jesus to be _essentially_ God to occupy this role.[^c02-39] But this is logically inconsistent or at least _ad hoc_, given how Nemes has defined theological authority. Nemes characterizes what it is to exercise theological authority in an original, infallible, and irreversible manner as to exercise that authority _in virtue of what one is essentially_. Authority is possessed “originally” in virtue of what one is; but, if Jesus is not essentially omniscient or divine in other respects, he thus fails to have any such original authority. Similarly, if Jesus is not essentially omniscient or morally perfect or impeccable, given what He is, He could fail in principle to exercise authority in a legitimate way (“fallibly”). And, clearly, if Jesus is not God, He could be corrected by at least one other person – God – in His exercise of authority, and thus Jesus’ exercise of authority is in principle reversible. So, if Jesus exercises authority as God does – originally, infallibly, and irreversibly – He could only be divine. And if Jesus does not exercise theological authority by being identical with God, but derivatively, then nothing in principle prevents an ecclesial dogmatic authority from exercising dogmatic authority in just the same way (a non-divine) Jesus does.

Many similarly allege that there is a _practical_ danger in derivative authority, since one will “inevitably” confuse the prerogatives of the derivative authority with those of divine authority, echoing Nemes’ “functional originality” critique given earlier. For example, Gavin Ortlund voices a common criticism of Roman

---

<!-- PDF page 72 -->
**[p. 59]**

Catholic dogmatic authority, which was originally given by the Protestant theologian Philip Schaff:

By making herself the final arbiter of Scripture and tradition, the Church of Rome not only blocked communion with any and all Christian traditions dissenting from her pronouncements, but also removed the possibility of any meaningful internal reform of her own prior magisterial teaching. It gave her the power to yoke the consciences of the laity to error, effectively removing them from meaningful accountability to Scripture ….[^c02-40]

Ortlund’s allegation that infallibility’s irreversibility prevents “meaningful internal reform” of the Church’s dogmatic teaching. But Ortlund’s allegation begs the question. Consider that Scriptural teachings cannot be reversed, contradicted, or dissented from. If meaningful reform of Christian dogma was desirable, then the fact that Scripture cannot be changed would be a definitive reason that no meaningful internal reform would be possible in the Christian Church, since the fact that a given teaching is not reversible was sufficient to mean that no meaningful reform was possible. Ortlund would not think, however, that the “irreversibility” of Scriptural teaching impairs the ability of the Church to fulfill its mission – indeed, the perennial character of revealed teaching is central to Christian dogma, since God’s Word is eternal.

Dogmatic teaching is held to be irreversible _because_ it represents or clarifies teaching from God and so is irreversible because God cannot contradict Himself – our task as a Church is to “stand fast and hold fast” to what was once and for all transmitted to the saints (cf. 2 Thess. 2:15; Jude 1:3). Ortlund alleges that there is a purported danger in allowing a theological authority to bind our consciences, as if they possessed God’s own authority, because this will present a danger of “yoking” ourselves to error.[^c02-41] Consequently, we can reject Ortlund’s reasoning that “it is really the magisterium that has ultimate authority [and so] in this way, the church is ultimately untethered from accountability to the inspired Word of God ….”[^c02-42] Clearly, this allegation would beg the question against the very existence of infallible dogmatic authority – an infallible authority _cannot in principle_ be wrong, and so it would be impossible to “yoke” your conscience to error in believing the teachings of that authority. If there were dangers in yoking ourselves to those Church authorities who claim to teach in solemn magisterial acts by divine authorization, such that they cannot in those moments bind us to believe falsehood, there would be identical dangers in yoking ourselves to the apostles and prophets who claim to speak with divine authorization. Ortlund has merely assumed that Church authorities _are not_ similarly authorized. But that is what is in question.

Nemes begs the question less obviously when he alleges that those who accept infallible dogmatic authorities “will not be properly disposed to see contradictions when they arise because they are always starting from the assumption” that such authorities are infallible – thus, “the divine word will inevitably be adjusted to the human word.”[^c02-43] However, this objection begs the question as much as Ortlund’s, because it presumes that we cannot reasonably believe that any authority is

---

<!-- PDF page 73 -->
**[p. 60]**

infallible. If it _were_ reasonable for us to believe that a given theological authority is infallible, it would be reasonable to believe at present that their future teachings will not contradict past teachings. And, given that we accept them as infallible, as representing divine teaching accurately, it would be reasonable for us to have a _charitable_ interpretative presumption that their teaching forms a consistent whole. But there is nothing more implied in any of this than is ordinarily implied in our belief in divine revelation. A Jewish believer like Paul would have good reason to think that future revelation by God in Christ could not contradict previous revelation from Moses. Any believer who holds that Scripture is divinely inspired and inerrant should have a charitable interpretative presumption, given their belief that it is God’s Word, that nothing God intends to teach in one part would contradict what He teaches elsewhere. If such attitudes were vicious in the case of theological authorities, they would be vicious in our interpretation of Scripture as well.

Ortlund gives a different version of this criticism when he claims that dogmatic authority, in making solemn definitions over time, will thereby “move the goalposts” to justify their own dogmatic decisions.[^c02-44] The idea is that dogmatic authority will be the ultimate authority in determining any criteria for determining what counts as a legitimate dogmatic development, rather than a contradiction with past teaching. But since those criteria are themselves subject to the whims of that authority, those who accept the dogmatic authority could not then be aware of any _corruption_ of revelation, since they would have no independent criteria for discerning whether a purported “development” contradicts a prior teaching. What Ortlund seems to imply, then, is that anyone who adheres to dogmatic authority would put themselves in a position where there would be _no possible evidence accessible to them_ which would count as a “defeater” for their beliefs that this authority is infallibly teaching the truth, no matter how absurd or contradictory. Ortlund takes it to be a particularly damning piece of evidence that contemporary Catholics understand the doctrine “extra ecclesiam nullus salus” in a way that looks to contradict how Catholics had interpreted this doctrine in the past. Ortlund thinks the magisterium has obviously contradicted itself, while explaining away the contradiction by inventing a series of subtle distinctions which nobody in the past had made or was aware of.[^c02-45]

Ortlund’s objection seems to beg the question, again. The believer in dogmatic authority has no more or less reasons to cut themselves off from counterevidence than a believer in Scriptural authority. If it were reasonable to believe that Scripture is inerrant, or that a theological authority infallibly interprets Scripture, then it would be reasonable to have charitable presumptions that contradictions in their teaching are merely apparent. This presumption, however, does not simply and absolutely _cut them off_ from considering all possible counterevidence. There are potential defeaters for their belief. Any Christian can admit that, if it were really _demonstrated_ that Jesus never rose from the tomb, or that God contradicted Himself in Scripture, then this would be a defeater for their religious beliefs in Christian revelation. The standards for determining what counts as _decisive_ counterevidence that should get someone to abandon their beliefs are difficult, however. Ruling out _all_ alternative possible ways to reconcile their beliefs with counterevidence is

---

<!-- PDF page 74 -->
**[p. 61]**

not usually possible. But most Christians would see no problem in thinking that, if there is at least one possible way to reconcile any apparent counterevidence or contradiction, they can reasonably persist in having a charitable and reasonable presumption that the alleged contradictions can be dealt with. There is nothing more obviously or uniquely epistemically vicious in offering ingenious ways to reconcile purported contradictions in magisterial teachings than there is in offering ingenious ways to reconcile purported contradictions in Scriptural teaching – and, indeed, there should not be, since the former is supposed to be derivative from God in much the same way as the latter.

What Ortlund indirectly could be referring to, nevertheless, is the phenomenon that very often dogmatic decisions _compound_ and form a “hermeneutical context” in which later developments become reasonable, rather than those later developments being supported explicitly or straightforwardly entailed by Scripture itself. Whether and how such “doctrinal development” is possible, let alone reasonable, needs explication (as I will explain and address in later chapters). However, in general, we can note that such “developments” are not in principle unreasonable. Since the reasonableness of later doctrinal definitions depends logically on the reasonableness of the prior definitions, if the earlier definitions _were_ reasonable and the later definitions properly were related to those earlier definitions, then there is nothing obviously problematic here. But then whether later developments are legitimate depends on the _logical facts_ whether those later definitions _are_ logically supported by the earlier definitions, not whether the dogmatic authority or the body of the faithful were somehow already aware of those later definitions, even in some vague or potential way. Regardless of the specifics in Catholic developments regarding their belief that nobody outside the Church is saved, then, the fact that earlier generations were unaware of the later magisterial distinctions would be irrelevant, whether it is a legitimate development in principle. What is relevant is whether those later distinctions avoid a logical contradiction with what was taught earlier. And, if Ortlund concedes that the magisterium _did_ avoid a contradiction, then there is no obvious moving of goalposts involved, since the notion of logical contradiction has not changed, and the later teaching did not contradict the _content_ of the earlier teaching, even if it expanded or precisified it in a way that earlier generations did not foresee, or which is not logically entailed by that earlier teaching.

But we can note that this phenomenon is already found in Scripture itself. Newman argued that there is an internal development in the logical of revelation, where later biblical authors appeal to earlier texts, and build upon their insights in new ways – as one finds New Testament authors appealing to Old Testament texts in allegorical interpretation.[^c02-46] Strictly speaking, the earlier authors did not necessarily intend or consider these later interpretations that were attached to their words; the literal meaning they had in mind would not likely have been the prophetic meaning seen in their words during later generations. But we can understand the allegorical interpretation as reasonably constrained and grounded in hermeneutical context, where those earlier texts can be understood to prophetically foretell later events. Whether those allegorical interpretations are reasonable, then, depends primarily on whether those earlier texts _were_ intended by God as prophecies or

---

<!-- PDF page 75 -->
**[p. 62]**

figures for later events/truths. It would be unreasonable to simply rule this possibility out in principle and hold that such prophetic foretelling is impossible, on the grounds that God can only intend us to adhere to precisely _one_ literal and explicit meaning of the text. In a roughly similar way, whether later doctrinal developments are reasonable largely depends on the facts of each development – it would be unreasonable to rule out doctrinal developments in principle, as if God could not possibly providentially intend any such developments to occur. Quite simply: He could do this. If it were unreasonable to believe God could do this, it would be unreasonable to believe God did it over the course of salvation history as described in Scripture. And Christians should not admit that. So too, if He did intend an infallible teaching authority, He would have also intended that their dogmatic definitions properly cohere over time, and that they logically permit future definitions that earlier generations were unaware of and might not even have had the right epistemic position to see were possible.

What might seem unique in Ortlund’s objection is that he alleges that dogmatic authorities are in control of the _standards_ or _criteria_ of evidence as to what counts as a contradiction with prior teaching, whereas Scripture is not. However, dogmatic authorities do not claim to be in control of rules of logic or evidence, and none have attempted to redefine what counts as, e.g., a logical contradiction. While dogmatic authorities might try to explain that a later teaching does not contradict a previous teaching, although “correcting” or “qualifying” that teaching in some way, whether this will count as a contradiction or as counterevidence – in fact – is not ultimately in their control. And such arguments would equally apply to belief in Scriptural inspiration, since it is alleged by skeptics (as I noted above) that belief in inspiration necessitates that Christians cannot engage in authentic or honest historical-critical Scripture interpretation when it might be inconvenient to their theology, given their controlling theological assumptions that any contradictions or errors are only apparent. Nonetheless, Christians have good reason to reject such skeptical arguments. For the same reasons, Christians can reject skeptical arguments that belief in dogmatic authority will lead to ignoring the evidence or otherwise failing to be intellectually honest. There is nothing about belief that revelation has the force of God’s authority, and that a dogmatic authority bears that authority in some way derivatively, which requires anyone to fail to be intellectually honest in exploring the evidence against their views.

It would be a frivolous objection to insist that Catholics or Orthodox _are inconsistent_ if they reject the possibility that their dogmatic authorities could (bindingly, infallibly) contradict previous teaching. An atheist might insist that Christian believers are unreasonable in believing in God’s Word, since God Himself could require us to modify or change our standards of logic and evidence, and adhering to any “fickle” authority like this is epistemically vicious fanaticism – faith in God’s Word could potentially justify us believing _anything,_ no matter how absurd, contradictory, or morally abhorrent. As with most believers, we might respond that not even God could change those laws of logic and evidence, nor could God make morally praiseworthy what is intrinsically morally abhorrent. And, for the same reason, defenders of dogmatic authority can insist that neither could dogmatic authorities

---

<!-- PDF page 76 -->
**[p. 63]**

who teach in God’s name change standards of logic and evidence, nor could they possibly teach that what is morally abhorrent is morally praiseworthy (note: this is not to say that a _pastoral_ authority could not try to _command_ what is morally abhorrent or _praise_ what is morally abhorrent – and these issues will be addressed later).[^c02-47] It would be equally reasonable for believers in dogmatic authority to hold this view as it is for theists in general.

For similar reasons, we can also dismiss those objections that belief in dogmatic authority necessitates or practically promotes some species of moral viciousness, whether fanatical dogmatism, intolerance, or even violence. Such allegations are often implied in these contexts, as when Ortlund notes that, “Hus’s execution was not a violation of medieval Roman Catholic theology, but its expression …” and emphasizes that “_Hus is not rare_” to imply that Hus’ execution is symptomatic of a pattern of violence or morally abhorrent attitudes naturally consequent upon belief in infallible teaching authority.[^c02-48] The “Grand Inquisitor” of Dostoyevsky’s _Brothers Karamazov_ gives novelistic flesh to these worries: an inquisitor whose fanatical trust in the authority of the Church leads him to kill Christ again for undermining ecclesiastical authority.

One might think that dogmatic authority inspires intolerance, because an infallible authority must be in possession of the truth, and everyone else who rejects their authority is wrong. Clearly, however, there is nothing about belief in an infallible authority which necessitates violence or intolerance. God is infallible, and His Word inerrant, and believing that God is an authority that cannot be wrong does not necessitate those who have faith in God’s Word to be violent or intolerant, morally speaking. Similarly, dogmatic authorities give us no obvious license for ignoring moral requirements in favor of preserving the authority itself that would not also _ipso facto_ apply to adherence to God’s authority in general. Certainly, it would be reasonable to believe that God’s Word cannot be wrong – for the same reasons it would be reasonable to believe it was God’s Word – but that epistemic view does not obviously entitle one to moral attitudes that would “reject” others’ worth or moral equality, etc. It does not even mean that those who fail to have faith are wrong! Others might believe a different set of truths than I do, and nevertheless fail to contradict any of those that I believe on revealed authority. Nor does it even mean that those who fail to believe as I do can be known to be damned or any such thing (criteria for damnation is also a substantive theological matter).

Further, renouncing dogmatic authority does not also require profession of nonviolence. Whatever classical arguments were given in favor of the use of civil penalties in religious matters, these were often and notoriously endorsed all around, by both Catholics and Protestants at different stages in time. People can ignore obvious moral requirements in favor of upholding God’s authority, regardless of whether that fanatic engages in much moral wrongdoing out of a fanatical devotion to Scriptural authority – like Oliver Cromwell – or out of fanatical devotion to Church hierarchy.

Indeed, the argument can be turned around. A common theological consensus has emerged in the modern period that such appeal to force in matters of religion is fundamentally unjust. All Christians can hold that those who use faith as

---

<!-- PDF page 77 -->
**[p. 64]**

an excuse for violence toward others are not acting in line with Christian faith. Dogmatic authority can aid tolerance, since it can officially condemn intolerant attitudes toward other believers – as has the Catholic Church since its promulgation of _Nostra Aetate, Inter Oecumenici,_ and _Dignitatis Humanae,_ which respectively condemned antisemitism, endorsed the ecumenical movement, and underlined a natural right to religious freedom (condemning use of civil force in religious matters). The ecumenical movement more generally has been promoted through theological dialogue conducted by whatever representative authorities there might be among different churches. Without dogmatic authorities, it would not be possible for a church to make commitments to theological dialogue with other churches corporately, or to authoritatively institute binding Church policy such as to make it unlawful to use force to compel others to conversion (in addition to teaching that it is morally wrong). Authorities thus are even plausibly necessary for there to be _corporate_ tolerance.

Nevertheless, there is a kind of “intolerance” which seems essential to dogmatic authority as exercised in Catholic and Orthodox churches: namely, the authority to _anathematize_ those who hold an erroneous belief or who fail to subscribe to a requisite doctrinal definition or creed or other statement. I noted that on Nemes’ earlier construal of theological authority as simply the authority to teach in such a way that one’s teaching cannot be wrong, i.e., infallibly, the authority to sanction does not immediately or obviously follow from that kind of dogmatic authority. We should hold these authorities apart: authority to punish or sanction is not the same as authority to teach.
