Anathemas are doctrinal statements which _in concreto_ have legal effects, but those effects are contingent on Church law at a given time and no sanction is necessary for making the doctrinal claim. Yet, a dogmatic authority defining that such-and-such belief is necessary for someone to believe to be orthodox Christian would _ipso facto_ constitute those who refuse to subscribe to this belief as unorthodox. Whether and what further penalties might be imposed by pastoral authorities, it is natural to think that this decision _excludes_ those who refuse to subscribe to a given definition from communion with that Church.

These objections should therefore be understood to express a view that adherence to dogmatic authority necessitates a problematic moral attitude, because it is morally wrong for anyone to hold or determine that some beliefs exclude one from being a member of the Church (or being saved, or to be a Christian in good standing, etc.). Now, there is an obvious problem with this purported moral principle from the perspective of Christianity: Christian faith itself is supposed to be a necessary condition for membership in the Church (or being saved, etc.). It seems bizarre to think it is morally wrong to hold that believing there is no God would exclude one from being a Christian. Even holding that (culpable) belief in atheism would exclude one from salvation is not an unusual Christian belief. We might think we need implicit qualifications that mere failure to believe in God, without culpability, might not be morally relevant to salvation, and so might say that _culpable_ belief in atheism would exclude one from salvation. But it does not affect the overall point that all Christians must admit that there are some beliefs, or failures to believe,

---

<!-- PDF page 78 -->
**[p. 65]**

which potentially exclude one from being a Christian or being saved. No Christian could accept the purported moral principle as stated above.

Neither Nemes nor Ortlund thinks it is wrong to recognize _some_ beliefs as essential to being a Christian or being saved. Nemes concedes that faith in “those things which Jesus taught clearly” is essential to Christian faith. That is because “one can hardly claim to be a Christian and refuse to believe in Jesus or to love God or one’s neighbor. This would be a refusal to do what Jesus plainly demands.”[^c02-49] Nemes thus implies that orthodox Christians have obligations to adhere to what Christ clearly teaches, and that failure to do what Christ clearly demands of us to do or believer would be sufficient to exclude one from being a Christian. Ortlund too holds that some beliefs or practices would exclude one from being a member of the true Church or from being saved.[^c02-50] Ortlund never directly states which beliefs are necessary for salvation; he instead similarly lists _effects_ of our beliefs – “glory unto the Trinity, glory unto the name of Jesus Christ, spiritual fruit and virtue wrought by the Holy Spirit, the pushing back of demonic powers, and the saving knowledge of the true God” – as what would be sufficient indicators that one is a true and orthodox Christian.[^c02-51] What is problematic about dogmatic authorities can then be condensed into the principle that these authorities seem to require _more_ than this.

Now, it would be question-begging to object that dogmatic authority is morally wrong simply because dogmatic authorities have determined the wrong requirements. That seems a substantive and factual issue, subject to critique. And these authors disagree among themselves about what those essential requirements are: Nemes is notoriously anti-Trinitarian and an Arian in his Christology; since giving glory to the Trinity seems important in his account, Ortlund might be plausibly held to think that Scripture teaches the substance of Trinitarian and Christological orthodoxy, and thus imply that failure to confess Jesus _as God_ could be problematic for one’s salvation. But it seems to me that Ortlund and Nemes agree fundamentally on a principle. Ortlund alleges that dogmatic authorities are morally problematic because they potentially impose a requirement to believe _more_ than Christ would command or require.[^c02-52] It is hard to gauge what Jesus would or would not do, and it would beg the question against those authorities that claim to act in His name and authority to say that they are not acting as He would – the question at stake is precisely _whether_ dogmatic authority says what Jesus would.

But we can rescue the idea. Nemes claims that what is problematic about dogmatic authority is that their decisions “are not easily proven from the teachings of Jesus and the words of Scripture themselves.”[^c02-53] Typical arguments for theological authority allege that there is a necessity for adjudicating disagreement on issues that are not easily known or clearly present in Scripture. Nemes reverses the argument: since we have no way to decide these questions regarding what is not easily known or patently obvious from Scripture, we should conclude “that these admittedly unclear and undecidable things are not in fact essential to the faith.”[^c02-54] Even if Nemes’ own epistemological skepticism is too pervasive, in thinking that theological debates on the divinity of Christ or other doctrines would be in principle _undecidable_ (which seems as question-begging as his arguments that whether a miracle or supernatural revelation occurred is undecidable), the emphasis on what

---

<!-- PDF page 79 -->
**[p. 66]**

is “easily” known or “clearly” present in Scripture, or what Jesus “plainly” demands, points to an epistemic concern which might plausibly have moral implications. As Nemes says, “the heart of Christian faith is not believing controverted things about Jesus but rather clinging to him in one’s heart and believing in him despite oneself.”[^c02-55]

Theological argumentation is not _accessible_ to most people. Further, dogmatic authority would make it possible that there is dogmatic development, as described earlier, where later dogmatic definitions build on previous ones. The possibility exists that later dogmatic developments are not in fact consistent with earlier ones or grounded upon Scripture. But the facts of the matter regarding whether these later developments are _consistent_ with earlier definitions, and indeed ultimately _grounded_ upon Scripture, would become progressively inaccessible to anyone but theologians and specialists in dogmatic history. And, in fact, _no_ theological position or claim that a given belief is a legitimate doctrinal development has gone uncontested. If it were a condition of salvation that one had evaluated the theological positions correctly and arrived at the true ones, one’s salvation would objectionably seem to depend upon one’s intellectual ability rather than moral character. The principle behind the rejection of dogmatic authority might then plausibly be construed as the view that it would be morally problematic to demand that anyone believe more than is reasonably epistemically accessible to them. Since it is unreasonable to demand that anyone believe more than is reasonably accessible to them, and the possibility of dogmatic authority would entail that people could be obligated by that authority to believe more than what is accessible to them, dogmatic authority is morally objectionable.

What one should notice about this argument is that it is exactly like arguments that it would be morally objectionable to hold that faith in Christianity is morally obligatory or a necessary condition for salvation. Such arguments (such as those we saw Nemes give in the first chapter) presuppose that nobody in principle could have evidence sufficient to reasonably conclude that Christianity is true, as opposed to a naturalistic hypothesis, and thus that it is not reasonable to believe that anyone would be obligated to believe in supernatural revelation. Or, at least, not _everyone_ could have sufficient evidence for making an act of faith, and thus it cannot be reasonable to believe that salvation would depend upon having faith. I extensively replied to these arguments, in short, by pointing out that the assumption is question-begging – God can provide sufficient evidence such that each person can reasonably make an act of faith – and that we can reconcile an obligation to have faith with varying epistemic situations. _What_ we would be obliged to believe in would differ, in view of those situations, but it would be true in all situations that each had an obligation to respond to God insofar as they could have reasonably perceived Him to be speaking to them.

A structurally analogous response should be given to these concerns about dogmatic authority. The assumption that nobody has good enough evidence to decide theological conundrums regarding purportedly recondite matters is questionbegging against the defender of theological authority, since the definition of such authority is precisely supposed to act as _evidence_ according to which we can

---

<!-- PDF page 80 -->
**[p. 67]**

determine such matters. Clearly, even granting that some parts of Scripture are clearer than others, we could potentially have clear reasons from Scripture (e.g., from Jesus’ own words) to believe that there _is_ a dogmatic authority, and that God wants us to obey that authority in matters of faith and morals. And then, since these facts are easily or plainly accessible to anyone reading Scripture, it would follow from Ortlund and Nemes’ own principles that belief in dogmatic authority – _and derivatively in its decisions_ – would be morally requisite for salvation.

We can also reconcile the moral obligations to believe doctrinal definitions of dogmatic authority by understanding those obligations to vary with epistemic situations. Those who lived _before_ a doctrinal definition are clearly not obligated to believe them. But those who belong to the Church, and so are subject to dogmatic authority, would become morally obligated to believe in such a definition, if it were promulgated and made known to them as obligatory. The _reasons_ behind the definition would not necessarily be accessible, but the fact that it is taught by the authority would be accessible to them. Even within the Church, there might be different requirements for subscription. It is hard to imagine that anyone would be morally required to consciously accept every doctrinal definition or teaching that has ever been made by dogmatic authority. There is no need for a comprehensive list of all infallible teaching, as subscription to such a list is unnecessary for most purposes. Rather, for instance, clerics might be obligated to believe explicitly all elements of the chief or important doctrinal definitions, and to know more about their rationale, whereas the rest of the Church would only be obligated to profess a simpler statement, such as the Nicene Creed, and only _disposed_ to assent to more complex statements if it were deemed necessary.[^c02-56]

Indeed, this is exactly the condition in these dogmatic traditions, such as Orthodoxy or Catholicism. Preceding reception or baptism, catechetical classes give the candidate a broad overview of the Church’s doctrine, and then, at the ceremony, only the profession of a creed is required, with a manifestation of a disposition to assent to Church teaching. In this case, it is true that not everyone needs to know or assent to every part of every dogmatic definition; nevertheless, each person is required for their membership in the Church, and for having right faith, that they be _properly responsive_ to dogmatic authority. It would be essential to their faith not so much that they assent to particular articles, but that they be properly disposed to assent in a general way to whatever was accessible to them – just as Ortlund and Nemes think believers should be disposed to assent to what Scripture teaches or Jesus commands, insofar as it is accessible to them. The only relevant question is whether dogmatic authority _was_ instituted by Christ and bears His authority derivatively. If so, there is no good objection that dogmatic authority would impose unreasonable burdens on anyone, just as there is no good objection that Christ’s words would not impose an unreasonable burden.

These arguments also resemble those objections raised against faith, which appealed to widespread disagreement among religions. Many think it follows that there is no reasonable way to decide what religion is true and therefore reject that anyone would have obligations to believe in Christian revelation. In the same way, these objections against dogmatic authority assume that the fact of widespread

---

<!-- PDF page 81 -->
**[p. 68]**

disagreement among Christian theologians in principle entails that there is no reasonable way to decide among them, and thus that nobody could be obligated to believe any views that are up for disagreement in this situation. Both objections, however, rely on a similar fallacious mode of reasoning: that if _many_ interpretations are reasonable or if there is no _unique_ reasonable interpretation, then either _all_ are reasonable or _none_ are unreasonable. But this is fallacious. It is possible that there is a range of reasonable interpretations _and_ that some are unreasonable.[^c02-57]

Further, we could simply note that criteria for “reasonability” come apart from criteria of truth or the assertability of truth; what we are interested in regarding theological disagreement is whether we can judge and _assert_ publicly that what we believe is true, and such assertions do not necessarily imply that others fail to believe what they do unreasonably. For example, I can state publicly, “There is a fire in the bathroom,” without thereby implying that everyone else who failed to believe this was unreasonable in their unbelief (since others might not have seen the fire, as I did, and I am testifying to others what I have personally witnessed). Even a dogmatic definition does not imply that others fail to believe reasonably – such definitions might instead be understood to _make_ it unreasonable for any Christian who accepts such authority to continue to believe otherwise. But, in this latter case, the definition itself functions as evidence which needs to be considered in our construal of what is reasonable for someone to believe within their circumstances. Discounting any possible role that dogmatic authority might play in what is reasonable to believe would be to beg the question.

Further, if we took the bare fact of theological disagreement as evidence that disagreement can have no moral implications, this would undermine the Christian belief that _faith_ in general is morally obligatory or has value. Not only do Christians have profound and difficult to resolve theological disagreements, but this is true of all religious beliefs. There are many people who do not believe in God at all and who seem to have reasonable views of morality, themselves, and how to live their lives. I concede that there is a sense in which such persons might believe reasonably what they do. Nevertheless, there is no necessity in concluding that it would be _impossible_ for anyone to have moral obligations to believe. Perhaps we analyze such cases of reasonable atheists as involving a _kind_ of epistemic reasonability, rather than moral permissibility, so that unbelief would be morally impermissible and blameworthy, even if there is not conclusive enough evidence to constitute unbelief as _epistemically_ unreasonable. Or we could analyze such cases in terms precisely of reasonability and say that while there is inconclusive evidence of _one_ sort that seems to make their attitudes reasonable, there is evidence of _another_ sort (or other salient facts) which make their beliefs all-things-considered unreasonable. Without rehashing discussions well-known in literature on the rationality of faith, the point is that objections to dogmatic faith or dogmatic authority which rely on the simple phenomenon of disagreement would equally undermine the moral value of faith in general. But Christians have traditionally held that faith is morally valuable, and that faith involves belief, given Christ’s own words: e.g., “He who believes in Him is not condemned; but he who does not believe is condemned already, because he has not believed in the name of the only begotten Son of God”

---

<!-- PDF page 82 -->
**[p. 69]**

(Jn 3:18). Christians should not endorse arguments which imply that Christ’s claim about the necessity of faith is irrational.

Further, the apparent recondite and inaccessible rationale behind dogmatic definitions is no more problematic than the fact that some parts of Scripture are less obvious or clear than others. And it seems no more unreasonable that we should be morally obliged, similarly, to adhere to dogmatic authority in general than it would be that we are obligated to accept Scripture as an inspired whole. Indeed, it seems more reasonable in many ways to do the former. The criterion that what is necessary for us to believe to be saved is determined in terms of what is “easily” or “plainly” or “clearly” found in Scripture seems to entail a logical problem. Not only is it the case that whatever is easily or plainly or clearly known would seem relative to things like on one’s intellectual abilities, contingent facts about the language or translation of Scripture, etc., and thus largely on what is contingent and extrinsic to Scriptural message itself, but what counts as Scripture is not itself a matter that can be non-circularly determined by Scripture. Nemes gives a circular argument of this sort where he claims that one should accept the Christian Scriptures as given because the community or individual believer “recognizes in them an authentic expression of his or her own Christian faith.”[^c02-58] Since the claim is that what is necessary for salvation is whatever is found easily or clearly in Scripture, and one cannot then have faith _in_ anything revealed except by believing what Scripture says, this view is either logically circular or tautologous: one either believes that Scripture teaches X _because_ one already believed that Scripture teaches X before reading the book, and one already believed _that_ because they believed that Scripture taught it; or one believes Scripture teaches whatever one believes Scripture teaches. This view is obviously vacuous either way.

I suspect that Nemes might presume that one has a _prior_ experience of God, which inspires one to see in the Scriptures their own experience, and therefore to accept these books as reflecting or making sense of what was already evident to you. But the difficulty is that this is not to accept the books as inspired or as a matter of faith in God’s testimony – it is to see in these books a fallible reflection of your own experience of God. This is not a recognizable form of Christian faith, however, and especially not of the Protestant faith in particular, because it does not involve adhering to the teaching of Scripture as God’s Word or as revealed doctrine. Instead, that experience would seem to be what resembles revelation in this story. And so, this is akin to treating each person as having prophetic gifts, such that they each receive personal revelation. While many Christians think this is false, and we could give arguments that this is not likely to be true of each person, it is also not the case that we can rule out _public_ revelation in principle. But that is what Scripture is supposed to be. Whereas Nemes’ position assumes that public revelation is impossible, we have no reason to do so. Conversely, Nemes has not given us any reason to rule out dogmatic authority if there is public revelation.

The belief in the _unity_ of the inspired canon – that all are an inspired whole – is the belief that leads us to reconcile passages rather than simply dismiss them as false. In addition, the way we reconcile Scriptural passages is partly determined by what books or parts we take to have logical or hermeneutical priority over others,

---

<!-- PDF page 83 -->
**[p. 70]**

and that is partly determined by internal evidence, their position in the canon, and classical Christian beliefs about Christ. For instance, Christians hold that the New Testament is a key to reading the Old, and the Gospels a key to reading the New. But it has been a matter of reasonable dispute as to what counts as Scripture (e.g., Marcion rejecting the Old Testament as canonical, Luther rejecting the Deutrocanonicals and devaluing other parts). The extent of and order of priority among books in the canon would not seem more epistemically inaccessible to any ordinary person than a recondite dogmatic argument, so that it is not easily or clearly or plainly known (from Scripture?) which books are canonical. If we employ the same criteria employed by Ortlund and Nemes, it would be morally problematic to hold that it was necessary for salvation that anyone accept the canon of Scripture. By the same token, it would also be morally problematic to hold that anyone should be expected to come to conclusions that resemble Ortlund and Nemes’ views of what is “easily” or “clearly” known as the core of the Scriptural message.

Finally, Ortlund and Nemes’ objections to dogmatic authority imply that God gave us much of Scripture for no good reason. On their views, what is necessary for salvation is only what is easily and clearly accessible from Scripture. But those passages from which they derive the beliefs that they take to be necessary for salvation – ultimately, only belief in the person of Jesus – can be found stated in a minuscule subset of passages. Indeed, Nemes has one sentence encapsulating all that is necessary. However, everything else would then not only be unnecessary – all the rest is not obviously useful in providing us any information that we would need to know that would be important to developing a friendship with God – but even potentially harmful, since its very presence encourages us to speculate on all sorts of unnecessary matters. It seems as if much of Scripture has no relation to our salvation or what we need to believe to live a good Christian life. Further, many places in Scripture are much more inaccessible to us than any of the dogmatic developments, in principle, for the simple reason that the ancient history or personages or locations described are much more temporally remote from us. It would seem morally problematic, on Ortlund and Nemes’ criteria, for God to provide us this sort of content and require that we accept it as inspired, when we have no way of being able to understand its content as having a clear and explicit connection with what we need to believe.

By contrast, the view on which there is a dogmatic authority has a cleaner response. It is literally true that “_all_ Scripture is given by inspiration of God, and is profitable for doctrine, for reproof, for correction, for instruction in righteousness [emphasis]” (2 Tim. 3:16), but that it was given to the Church for this purpose, rather than being such that everyone was to immediately know individually what God intended to communicate in every passage. While not everyone would have epistemic obligations to discover _everything_ that Scripture intends to teach, it can be true that everyone should revere Scripture as divine revelation and can have duties _collectively_ to investigate divine revelation to discover what God wants them to know. Since God can communicate to each person what He wants by means of the same text, they can see Scripture as something which – in each part – gives each person moral reasons to investigate its meaning, even when those parts are

---

<!-- PDF page 84 -->
**[p. 71]**

inaccessible to them alone. Obscure passages would be interpreted by those with gifts for this task, and communicated to those who did not have those talents – and, as each individual theologian has partial access to Scriptural truth, Christian theologians would have reason to form a theological community to correct and improve their collective grasp of Scripture and to communicate it to all. On this picture, it would be sufficient for each person to be disposed to accept what Scripture teaches in a general way, and only explicitly accept whatever is proposed as necessary by dogmatic authority, with special epistemic duties imposed on people like clerics or the theological community to be responsive to that authority, given their role in helping everyone collectively fulfill their duties to appreciate God’s word.

After having reviewed this initial set of objections to dogmatic authority, I conclude that none of them uniquely applies to views on which there is dogmatic authority – these objections are self-defeating for Christians to endorse, since they would, if true, undermine the reasonability of Christian faith. The question as to whether there is a dogmatic authority, then, seems fundamentally to rest on matters regarding whether that authority’s definitions are _necessary_. Do believers have duties of an epistemic or a moral kind such that it would not be possible to fulfill these duties without dogmatic authority? The next chapter is dedicated to defending that the duties of faith require dogmatic authority for their fulfillment. I will argue that the only serious problems for Christian believers concern not whether a dogmatic authority exists in the Christian Church, but _where_ to locate the exercise of that authority.

# 3. Dogmatic Authority and Infallibility

My speech and my preaching were not with persuasive words of human wisdom, but in demonstration of the Spirit and of power, that your faith should not be in the wisdom of men but in the power of God.

(1 Cor. 2:4–5)

The first two chapters have explored the duties we have to believe God and make an act of supernatural faith in His revelation, and then the shape of duties consequent upon faith. The faithful have epistemic obligations to accept God’s revelation and aim to understand it appropriately. The faithful also have moral duties to respond appropriately to what is revealed, such as living and practicing their faith in line with God’s will and helping others come to and live out their faith too. In sum: believers have obligations to _understand_ God’s Word addressed to them, to _confess_ that Word publicly, to _practice_ what God’s Word teaches, and to _maintain_ the Church’s proclamation of that Word. In this chapter, I will argue that these tasks could not be fulfilled by believers without there also being dogmatic authority in that community of believers.

Despite apparent disagreements among Christian traditions as to whether there is dogmatic authority in the Church, I will argue that this is not as profound or substantive disagreement as initially appears. The case made here is that Protestant, Orthodox, and Catholic Christians ought to agree that the Church has dogmatic authority. The basic rationale is that the Church needs to offer testimony to the Gospel, in every age, until Christ comes again. Their testimony does not add or take away anything from Scripture’s message but constitutes a testimony _to_ Scripture. A traditional view is that the Church has whatever powers or authority required for the Church to fulfill its purpose or mission. Since the Church has a _mission_ from God to preach the Word, the Church has a divine _commission_ to preach that Word _authoritatively_. Church authority can be understood as a divine commission to engage in the tasks listed above. If this commission did not involve authority to preach in God’s own name and address the Gospel as binding revelation to others, the Church would only be able to offer advice in a capacity as a private individual – at best, as a collective community of theologians. But Scripture tells us that the Church’s authority to testify to the Word comes from God, i.e., they preach _in_

DOI: 10.4324/9781003663485-3

---

<!-- PDF page 88 -->
**[p. 75]**

_Christ’s name_. Since the Church has been sent by Christ to offer this testimony to God’s Word – as it does when it identifies the Scriptures, proposing Scripture as wholly inspired, to be accepted by the faithful, all to the contrary being rejected – then to reject the Church’s testimony to that Word is to sin against the faith. This is what it means, fundamentally, to say that the Church has dogmatic authority. In the next chapter, I will similarly propose that Protestant, Catholic, and Orthodox Christians all have good reason to embrace the possibility of dogmatic developments and definitions.

These claims are not intended to deny differences among these traditions. Rather, the case being made over these next two chapters is that disagreements about dogma among Protestant, Catholic, and Orthodox Christians should be understood (I argue) to concern primarily _where_ and _how_ dogmatic authority is exercised, not whether there is such infallible authority.

## The Necessity of Dogmatic Authority

It was argued in the first chapter that it was morally and epistemically obligatory that we know and believe what God has revealed to us. These obligations are grounded upon the fact that believing what God reveals is necessary for our salvation. It is not that we know in general that some _content_ is by itself necessary for our salvation, as the content of what we believe by faith will depend upon what God reveals to us at any given point in time, relative to our epistemic situation. Rather, it is that _believing God_ is morally and epistemically virtuous, and one cannot truly be someone who _believes God_ if one does respond to what one has sufficient reason to believe God has said. As I have underlined, faith is virtue of right responsiveness to God. Clearly, then, if the true content of God’s Word must be known by men for their salvation, then, as I argued in the second chapter, it also follows that – after making an act of faith – it is incumbent upon the believer, for example, to _study_ that Word, so that we ourselves can discover what God says. Otherwise, the believer would fail to do what they have a moral and epistemic obligation to do. As Barth notes at the beginning of his _Church Dogmatics_, “dogmatics as an enquiry presupposes that the true content of Christian talk about God must be known by men.”[^c03-1] Theology is a natural outcome of the virtue of faith, because we have epistemic and moral duties to understand what God wants us to believe. We also have duties to confess, practice, and maintain what God wants us to believe.

These duties arising from faith can be thought of as “indirect” duties of faith, as opposed to the “direct” duties to believe God and His Word. A direct duty is the duty to believe God and His Word, whereas an indirect duty is one arising from this. Among those duties, there are two such indirect duties that serve as premises of my argument that the Church has dogmatic authority: a duty _only_ to accept God’s Word on faith, and no other word as God’s Word, and thus a duty to reject all that is incompatible with belief in God’s Word. Both duties are implied by those duties to understand, confess, practice, and maintain faith. These duties are _indirect_ rather than direct duties to believe God, since neither directly pertains to accepting God’s Word or what God says, but follow upon it. Clearly, for anyone who

---

<!-- PDF page 89 -->
**[p. 76]**

has faith, that person has these indirect duties, and so each believer should be able to fulfill those duties. But what I will show is that each believer would be unable to fulfill their indirect duties sufficiently without there being dogmatic authority vested in the Church. Even if it were true that each believer can fulfill their direct duties to God simply in virtue of Scripture being available to them, it would not be true that each believer can fulfill their indirect duties to accept _only_ God’s Word on faith, and no other word as God’s Word, or to reject all that is incompatible with belief in God’s Word. In fulfilling these duties, believers _need help_.

Ultimately, that help believers need to fulfill their duties of faith cannot be simply human help. What believers need is, instead, help from God, from the Holy Spirit. This is because the duties of faith are duties _toward God_. Indeed, regarding faith in general, we could not make an act of faith in anything without help from the Holy Spirit. This was the point of arguing that each believer is, in some sense, infallible when they believe rightly – they believe _God_ and _God_ cannot be wrong. What it is to make an act of faith involves accepting God’s Word on God’s own authority, and not on the strength of our own independent reasoning or on the strength of the evidence that God is speaking to us. Such an act would be perhaps a _justified belief_ that God revealed something to us, but if I were motivated to believe what is said in Scripture _because_ I have been convinced by the strength of the evidence or independent reasons to believe that Scripture was telling the truth, that act would not be to accept God’s Word _because_ it is God’s Word. This would be the faith of demons, or some other attitude, but it would not be theological faith.

Classically, Christians have insisted that the virtue of faith is _infused_ by God into each believer, because they recognized that one cannot suitably make an act of faith in testimony to God’s Word without supernatural aid.[^c03-2] Each believer has God’s help in terms of God’s grace, which causes them to have faith, and thus to make an act by which they accept God’s Word _as_ God’s Word _on_ God’s own authority, and not simply as something accepted on account of human reasoning or independent evidence that what a text or prophet says is true. The latter would be insufficient for an act of faith, because no one can reasonably accept that their own reasoning or evidence is in principle infallible, and thus nobody could (reasonably) accept what was said _as if_ it were God’s Word on those epistemic grounds alone. We can assume, then, that each believer is individually helped or guided by the Holy Spirit to make an act of faith and so to acquire the disposition to rightly respond to God on account of it being God who speaks. But it also follows – for the same set of reasons – that each believer will need to have the help of God’s Holy Spirit in fulfilling the indirect duties of faith. Those duties arise from God alone and require God to aid in their fulfillment.

Now, in one way, it is also a classical view that God infuses dispositions that enable believers to fulfill those indirect duties: the Gifts of the Holy Spirit. Aquinas identifies, for example, those Gifts that help us fulfill our indirect duties of faith with “Knowledge” and “Understanding.” Understanding is the Gift that enables us, by the assistance of the Spirit, to recognize or grasp what God wants us to believe and that, “whatever be the outward appearances, they do not contradict the truth,” and thus to fulfill an (indirect) duty “that we ought not to depart from matters of

---

<!-- PDF page 90 -->
**[p. 77]**

faith, for the sake of things that appear externally.”[^c03-3] This indirect duty is what I identified with the epistemic duty to understand God’s Word in Scripture, as evinced in questions of biblical hermeneutics. Knowledge is the Gift that enables us, by the assistance of the Spirit, “to discern what is to be believed, from what is not to be believed.”[^c03-4] This Gift therefore corresponds to the indirect duties I outlined earlier regarding not accepting some other words as if they were God’s Word, and rejecting whatever is incompatible with believing whatever God’s Word says.

Nevertheless, in another way, such a Gift by itself is not the only kind of assistance from the Holy Spirit that anyone needs to fulfill their indirect duties of the faith. These dispositions are internal to the believer, but they are _responsive_ dispositions just as much as the virtue of faith itself. The believer is responding indirectly to God’s Word and thus needs to have something to which they are responding which has an objective indirect connection to God’s Word. This is analogous to the way in which inspiration, as an objective situation obtaining in the world, is required to make it objectively appropriate that the believer can have a duty to believe that _those words_ spoken by the prophet are God’s Words. In the case of inspiration, inspiration does not consist either in the fact that the prophet says certain things or in the fact that a prophet works miracles. Inspiration does not consist in speaking _these_ particular words, or in working a miracle, but merely in speaking whatever God speaks. Similarly, whatever gives us indirect duties to believe what we do regarding that this text is _not_ God’s Word, or in rejecting some beliefs as incompatible with God’s Word, will likewise correspond to a similar set of objective facts that has an objective connection with God’s causal activity.

In one way, then, it is true that these are the same set of facts as those of inspiration: the objective situation that an inspired prophet speaks under the causal activity of the Holy Spirit would suffice to make it objectively appropriate that we not accept some other words as God’s Word _aside from what that person says_ and reject whatever is incompatible with believing whatever God’s Word (_as spoken by the prophet_) says. But, in another way, these facts alone are clearly not the right kind of facts to make it appropriate for us to accept what someone says as being God’s Word in any situation where one is not present to that prophet directly, that is, where one is accepting the words of the prophet _via testimony_ from someone else. When we are receiving testimony about inspiration from another person who is _not_ inspired, there is no objective situation which obtains that makes it appropriate for us to accept what _that person_ tells us as if it were God’s Word. Since that person is not inspired, they could have gotten it wrong in transmitting or relaying God’s Word to us. Consequently, faith would not impose any demand upon us to believe that this person’s words are God’s Word and would even apparently impose a moral duty upon us _not_ to accept that this person’s words are God’s Word, since their words are human words. That is, if believers followed a merely internal persuasion, without any sensitivity to facts about anything external, they would have no obligations to be sensitive in discerning which words were God’s Word. But believers do have those obligations. Therefore, they at least don’t derive fully _sufficient_ normative reasons to assent to any given purportedly revealed word as God’s Word from the internal facts about their belief or the graces they are given.

---

<!-- PDF page 91 -->
**[p. 78]**

This is especially true where we are concerned about beliefs relevant to what is _incompatible_ with God’s Word – e.g., the reasoning of another human being (or set of such persons) could constitute a basis for having justified belief that _x_ or _y_ is incompatible with God’s Word, but we would not thereby acquire a duty to accept this reasoning _in faith,_ and thus would not sin against faith in rejecting this reasoning.

When I am potentially in a situation to make an act of faith, I need to be put in touch with God Himself in _two_ objective ways. First, the Word that I encounter needs to really be God’s Word – inspired in fact. But the facts of inspiration – the bare fact God causes someone to speak for Him – do not enable us to recognize or to accept God’s Word as spoken _through testimony_. That is, God inspiring someone to speak on His behalf is not the same as God ensuring that this speech is reliably and appropriately testified to by another. The former is inspiration and the latter involves God’s providential guidance, preservation, and other means to ensure that those whom God wants to hear the inspired Word do hear that Word, rather than merely human words. Thus, second, God needs to act and ensure His Words are spoken through the testifier – by means of causal mechanism that, while not inspiration, likewise involves God’s providential and supernatural guidance. Without God ensuring that the testimony is “from Him” in a supernatural way, we would be unable to fulfill our duties to believe in this testimony as if it were _God’s_ Word to us. We would not be obligated in faith to accept the words of the testifier as God’s Word.

For that reason, to fulfill my duty to believe God’s Word, I need to have a testifier to God’s speech, whose character or situation as a testifier gives me sufficient grounds for having a moral and epistemic obligation to believe this word is God’s Word. This second help needs to be just as “from God” as the inspiration itself. Otherwise, the believer would have no moral or epistemic duties to assent to God’s Word. But, if that were the case, we would be in the situation as laid out by Steven Nemes, where faith in God’s revelation could not be plausibly required of us either morally or epistemically: nobody would plausibly violate a _duty_ to believe God’s Word who received that Word through testimony, if nobody was ever in a position to _authoritatively_ relate that inspired Word through testimony to another. This second mode of action, then, involves God providing a “sufficient normative” basis or grounds by which we would have _duties_ to recognize _this person_ or _this book_ as representing God’s Word.[^c03-5] God’s act of revelation would be incoherent without God ensuring that others would have sufficient normative reasons to embrace those human words as being God’s Word.

Many have argued that dogmatic authority is necessary for believers for epistemic reasons. For instance, Zagzebski argues that the Church might help us fulfill epistemic or practical aims, such that I am more likely to believe religious truth within that religious community than if I were to try to figure it out independently, or help me fulfill my religious duties more easily.[^c03-6] Sijuwade extends this reasoning to argue that dogmatic authority is necessary to definitively _clarify_ and provide knowledge of what God’s Word requires us to believe.[^c03-7] My argument will go in a different direction that is not epistemic but “moral.”

In the first two chapters, I proposed that theological faith involves an accepting of God’s testimony _because_ of responsiveness to God. God is speaking directly

---

<!-- PDF page 92 -->
**[p. 79]**

to believers in Scripture. But those who are testifying about Scripture to us – our pastors, our fellow Christians, evangelists, and preachers – are not God. Nor is the book we have the original documents that form the texts of the Bible; what we have is a copy that involves editorial choices and, almost always, translation. Finally, the book we have is the product of human choices in simply being _put together_ as a canonical collection, presented to us as God’s speech. Unless these choices and testimonial facts are underwritten by or appropriated by God in the right way, those choices and testimonial facts would be “merely human,” having no normative force that we ought to accept _this book_ or _these words_ being addressed to us as being God’s Word.

Stephen Darwall has referred to “second-personal reasons,” where our reasons to do something are not simply “state-of-the-world-regarding” or agent-neutral, but rather to accept reasons arising from _another person_ making a _valid claim or demand_ upon you.[^c03-8] The first chapter outlined that theological faith relies precisely on such reasons; to believe on testimony is _not_ to believe based on evidence independent of the standing of the testifier. Our reasons to believe God’s testimony derive from the standing or authority of the testifier, rather than from our third-personal investigation of the matter independently of what the testifier tells us. I will call that situation obtaining where a testifier has standing sufficient to generate secondpersonal reasons for us to believe their address with theological faith, and thus successfully generates normative duties in us to believe what they say with theological faith, a situation where the testifier has “dogmatic authority.”

Many would concede that God needs to ensure, providentially, that the inspired Word is transmitted reliably through history. This _providential protection_ is distinct, however, from being a _normative basis_ for recognizing a testifier as an authoritative witness to God’s Word, just as being _inspired_ is distinct from _being commissioned to witness_ to God’s Word. My argument relies on the claim that God’s intentions would fail to be coherent if God did not continue to commission those to transmit His Word to those who must rely on testimony regarding what was transmitted by the inspired speakers of the revealed Word. Future generations would not be able to assent to any such transmitted words as God’s Word unless they continued to have such a normative basis accessible to them, since those transmitted words are necessarily otherwise merely human words. Those words might be reliably transmitted by providential means, but, unless such speakers were _sent_ to witness to Christ, we would have duties in faith that would otherwise prevent us from adhering to the transmitted message as if it were God’s Word. In short, we could not _have divine faith in_ the Gospel unless those who preached it to us were sent by Christ.

The duties of faith follow from facts that believers need to believe responsibly. They need to have sufficient normative basis to believe that the words spoken to them are God’s Word, since they have obligations to believe God’s Word, only God’s Word, and to reject what is incompatible with God’s Word. They therefore need to be sensitive to the _standing_ of those who are proposing something to be believed by theological faith. Such testifiers to God’s Word need to be sufficiently and objectively in a situation which makes it possible for a believer to accept that testimonial word as being God’s Word, analogous to the way that a prophet needs

---

<!-- PDF page 93 -->
**[p. 80]**

to have such standing. We can therefore specify that “dogmatic authority” is the standing that a testifier has in transmitting God’s Word, such that they are sent by God in preaching that Word, providing a normative basis for others to accept their transmission of the Word as itself being God’s Word, with the consequence (I will propose) that their words are protected providentially from corrupting that deposit of faith that God wanted others to believe. Let me lay out a few distinctions before dealing with apparent counterexamples.

Since faith requires second-personal reasons to believe, as a condition of exercising faith in a manner that is doxastically responsible (not believing _just anyone_ with theological faith), God would be acting irrationally if He failed to provide dogmatic authority. If God were to allow a situation to obtain where He wanted others to accept His Word by testimony but failed to ensure providentially that they received the message God wanted them to believe, then those believers would only be able to accept human words _about_ God’s Word – the fallible testimony to God’s Word, rather than God’s Word itself. In short, God would be requiring those believers who accept testimony about His Word to violate their indirect duties of faith. They would be required to accept something that was potentially something other than God’s Word and would be required not to reject whatever was included in the testimony, even though the testimony might include what was not present in or incompatible with God’s Word. This would clearly be an intolerable theological situation, since God would be able to require believers to accept falsehood. God can neither deceive anyone nor require them to deceive themselves.

Consequently, there needs to be an objective situation obtaining for all those believers who accept testimony to God’s saving Word from others, if they are obliged to believe that Word for their salvation, such that this situation makes it so that the testifier to God’s Word _cannot fail_ to testify accurately to that Word to them. By “cannot fail,” I mean to say that this testimony could not have been anything other than a full, accurate propositional transmission of God’s Word, without any extraneous words that the believer in the testimony would be obliged to accept on faith as if they were God’s Word. Let us say that such a situation is one in which each believer who accepts God’s Word on testimony has _sufficient normative reason_ to accept that testimony as God’s Word. My notion of “sufficient normative reason” means that each believer would be liable to sin, to _culpably_ fail to fulfill their indirect duties of faith, if they failed to believe that testifier. Only in this situation would it be the case that those believers would have obligations from faith to believe these testifiers, which would arise from the duties in faith to believe those words of testimony as being truly God’s Word. If there was no such sufficient normative reason, a person would not do anything wrong by failing to accept the testimony. For, if all potential believers have _today_ is testimony to God’s Word, but nobody today has a sufficient normative reason to accept that testimony as God’s Word, then it would follow that nobody today would do anything wrong in failing to accept that testimony as God’s Word.

But it is false, according to Scripture, that nobody today could ever do anything wrong in failing to accept testimony about God’s Word. God’s own intentions, as expressed in Scripture, are that God intends believers to accept that _testimony_ to

---

<!-- PDF page 94 -->
**[p. 81]**

His Word, where accepting this testimony is necessary for the salvation of those believers. The Great Commission of Jesus to His disciples is a command to preach the Gospel to every creature so that they might accept that Gospel and be baptized: “Go therefore and make disciples of all the nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, teaching them to observe all things that I have commanded you” (Matthew 28:19–20). Parallel passages occurs where Jesus commands: “Go into all the world and preach the gospel to every creature” (Mk 16:15), and then later when He says that “you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes upon you, and you will be My witnesses in Jerusalem, and in all Judea and Samaria, and to the ends of the earth” (Acts 1:8) These commands would be incoherent if God did not also make it the case that anyone listening to their preaching could _rightly_ accept their preaching in faith, as God’s Word, i.e., while being able to fulfill their indirect duties in faith not to accept other words as God’s Word and to reject what is incompatible with God’s Word.

God did not make it the case (or promise) that _every_ testifier to God’s Word is an inspired prophet. We have no such claim. Instead, Scripture testifies indirectly to the opposite: “And so we have the prophetic word confirmed, which you do well to heed as a light that shines in a dark place … knowing this first, that no prophecy of Scripture is of any private interpretation, for prophecy never came by the will of man, but holy men of God spoke as they were moved by the Holy Spirit. But there were also false prophets among the people, even as there will be false teachers among you, who will secretly bring in destructive heresies, even denying the Lord who bought them, and bring on themselves swift destruction” (1 Pet. 1:20–21). There are many such parallel admonitions such that believers should “stand fast and hold the traditions which you were taught, whether by word or our epistle.” (2 Thess. 2:15), so that, “even if we, or an angel from heaven, preach any other gospel to you than what we have preached to you, let him be accursed” (Gal. 1:8) – that is, shunned by believers. If there is a danger that believers would be able to adhere to the words of false prophets and teachers, such that the inspired author must _instruct_ believers not to adhere to their words and instead stand firm in holding fast to the prophetic Word, then it cannot be the case that every believer is infallible in recognizing or accepting or testifying to God’s Word.[^c03-9] That is to say, there are obligations upon believers _in faith_ to be sensitive to testimony to God’s Word – we cannot treat merely human arguments, for instance, as God’s Word, nor can Christians uncritically accept even apparently miraculous happenings as truly from God; instead, Christians are told to test the spirits (1 Jn 4:1–6).

Dogmatic authority is not possessed by any individual believer merely in virtue of that person having faith. The fact that an individual believes what is truly God’s Word, or even does what God’s Word commands, is not that set of facts which makes that believer infallible in testifying to God’s Word, and is therefore not an appropriate situation to make it appropriate for other believers to have faith in that individual’s testimony about God’s Word. Any individual believer can make a mistake even when acting in good faith to transmit the Gospel to another. Whatever makes it appropriate for anyone to accept testimony from a believer cannot then be simply facts about that person having faith.

---

<!-- PDF page 95 -->
**[p. 82]**

By the same token, there is nothing about any arbitrary number of believers in terms of faith – no matter how many were gathered – that would constitute these believers as being communally infallible in transmitting the faith to others or as generating, by means of their collective experience, an obligation that others likewise accept their experience _as binding upon them in faith_. But this is precisely what Christianity claims: that the Church preaches the Gospel in such a way as to make binding demands upon the hearers. What we would need is to distinguish whether these believers are testifying to others in their own words, or in the name of that group of believers, from whether these believers are testifying to others _in the name of God_. What we therefore need evidence for is that someone is _sent_ by God to testify to others about God’s Word _in God’s name,_ that is, with a “divine commission.” If God wants us to believe His Word on the testimony of others, where believing His Word is necessary for us to be saved, He will ensure that there are those sent _by Him_ – that is, with a divine commission – to propose that Word to us for our belief. This reasoning is clearly laid out in Rom. 10:14–15: “How then shall they call on Him in whom they have not believed? And how shall they believe in Him of whom they have not heard? And how shall they hear without a preacher? And how shall they preach unless they are sent?”

It is obvious, however, that not every believer is infallible and that not every believer has”magisterial” authority. Consider little Joey, age six, who testifies on the playground to his friend Stevie, age five, that he loves Jesus and that he wants Stevie to love Jesus too. Joey is not himself a “magisterial authority”; he is not a bishop or a pastor, and he is not infallible. He might mix up the bible stories he has heard, and he might inaccurately transmit some parts of the Gospel – perhaps he tells Stevie that Jesus walks on water, shoots lasers from his eyes, turns water into wine, and leaps tall buildings in a single bound. Yet Stevie might come to real love of Jesus and Gospel faith through Joey’s witness. It seems that we do not need to impute any dogmatic authority to Joey; Joey was merely a providential means for Stevie to hear the Gospel.

It is true that Joey is not a pastor or bishop, but the account has not presumed thus far that dogmatic authority must be vested in pastors or bishops. Instead, I have only proposed that the Church _corporately_ must be invested with such authority, and that nobody can fulfill their duties of faith unless the Gospel was transmitted to each subsequent believer by a Church having standing inherited from Christ (and so from God). So, instead, we ought to interpret the passages wherein Jesus commissions his disciples to preach and baptize as being addressed to _every individual_ believer, who thereby inherit the commission and are sent to preach that Gospel, _by means of_ commissioning those to whom He was directly speaking in these passages. In all the cited passages where Jesus sends out believers to preach His Gospel, Jesus is speaking specifically to those He had named “apostles,” the Eleven who were to witness to Jesus and His message, or to others He had commissioned to be witnesses to His name during His time on earth, and only indirectly to others, since (at the very least) it is obvious that anyone other than those people commissioned directly by Jesus naturally learnt about Jesus’ commission through testimony of these people.

---

<!-- PDF page 96 -->
**[p. 83]**

I will lay out later that dogmatic authority can be conceived in one of two ways, which I refer to as “charismatic” and “institutional” theories of authority. Charismatic theories of authority hold that everyone who receives the Gospel message is sent by Jesus, merely in virtue of hearing and believing that message. Consequently, dogmatic authority in the Church is vested _communally_ in all those who have believed that message. What avoids the absurd implication that each believer is infallible is that, on the “charismatic” theory, it is only the community of believers acting _together_ which is infallible in (consensus) teaching. Institutional theories of authority, in contrast, hold that there are specific _offices_ in the Church that have inherited dogmatic authority from those originally commissioned by Jesus (that is, from the apostles and disciples). Consequently, dogmatic authority in the Church is vested in the Magisterium, in all those who have inherited such offices which derive from apostolic commissions, and these together are infallible in their teaching – all believers are infallible insofar as they are properly responsive to their divinely-commissioned teachers. Thus, on both accounts, dogmatic authority is appropriately transmitted from the community to each individual believer, but only under conditions when they are appropriately responsive to the community’s faith. For my purposes at this point, then, we do not need to evaluate these theories to determine whether the Church has dogmatic authority, since both theories fundamentally agree that the Church is dogmatically authoritative, and that each believer inherits this authoritative standing in virtue of their coherence with the faith of the Church that they have properly transmitted from the community, while also avoiding any absurd conclusions of saying that any individual believer (whether a layman or a cleric holding an office) would be infallible in what they teach in their own person. Both theories hold that one is only infallible when speaking in the name of the Church, as one legitimately sent by God.[^c03-10] And that is sufficient to deal with the case of Joey.

On my account, then, Joey participates derivatively in the Church’s dogmatic authority insofar as he properly transmitted _the faith of the Church_ to Stevie. This is much like the case of faith generically, where individual believers are infallible insofar as they adhere “formally” to what God transmitted, even if they make mistakes in judging “materially” what God wants them to believe. Since faith is reactive, however, either Joey or Stevie would fail in their duties by not aiming to respond correctly to those facts they know about God’s transmission. If Stevie or a parent tried to correct Joey, telling him that he’s confused Superman with Jesus Christ, and Joey refuses correction, Joey would no longer be aiming to transmit the faith of the Church but his own stories. Stevie, similarly, would fail to have theological faith if he preferred Joey’s version and did not care what the Church’s faith in Scripture transmitted about Jesus. Thus, both Joey’s transmission of the faith and Stevie’s uptake of it require that they participate _derivatively_ in the communal faith of the Church, since it is only insofar as the Church has normative standing that they transmit theological faith to others or acquire it from them. If they did not act in view of the Church’s faith, as historically transmitted through the apostles and in Scripture, they would fail to either transmit or acquire supernatural faith. Stevie could acquire the faith from a bad actor who is not aiming to transmit the faith of the Church, as when Joey does not care he is mixing up Superman and

---

<!-- PDF page 97 -->
**[p. 84]**

Jesus, but Stevie here acquires faith only by indirectly attending to the Church that Joey himself is not aiming to transmit or represent correctly. That is, Stevie could (for example) misunderstand Joey to represent the faith of the Church, indirectly employing Joey’s stories as an accidental means for discovering the Church’s faith and Scriptures, and thereby form faith in the Church’s testimony rather than in Joey’s own fantasies. (This is one way to understand the way that true faith is possible despite receiving heretical testimony to God’s Word).

Consider a further purported counterexample: a group of villagers in an isolated place, like pre-modern Eastern Island, discover a mysterious cargo box that washes ashore. Inside are a set of Bibles written in their native language. After reading, some of the villagers spontaneously convert to Christianity, baptize each other, etc. No dogmatic authorities were involved as testifying or witnessing the Gospel to these villagers; there were only providential means by which the Bibles arrived. No dogmatic authority seems necessary for these villagers to fulfill their duties to come to faith in Christ. To respond to this counterexample, we only need to note that the testimony by Church about the Gospel need not be that of one person to another, but that this testimony can be exemplified in many ways. In this case, the collection of books that is received instantiates or exemplifies a corporate act of the Church’s recognition that these books are the inspired canon, and their simple existence as a set of texts published together (perhaps with the printed title “The Holy Bible”) constitutes an instance of Church testimony to their being the Word of God. In fact, such a case supports the view being proposed here that such testimony needs to bottom out in sufficient normative standing.

It is obvious that these villagers do not have access to other evidence that these books contain God’s Word, aside from the act by which the books were compiled. They did not come to faith based on independent evidence, which made them certain that the Bibles exemplify God’s Word. If we assume the villagers did in fact acquire supernatural faith when they trusted that the Bibles instantiated God’s Word, the villagers would have fulfilled their duties not to adhere irresponsibly to whatever they received. So, if the villagers had received Superman comics and would have been just as disposed to accept _those_ as God’s Word, then the villagers do not appear to have the right disposition that is supernatural faith. (And private feelings that a book is inspired do not provide a normative sufficient reason to accept a book as God’s Word, for reasons given earlier.) Yet no other facts were accessible to them about the reliability of these books in transmitting God’s Word _except that they were compiled by Christians,_ somewhere. We should therefore conclude that this fact alone is normatively sufficient for the villagers to make a sensitive, responsive act of supernatural faith that fulfills their duties. They accepted the Gospel from a reliable testifier sent by God, the Church.

### Objections to the Necessity for Dogmatic Infallibility

Gavin Ortlund argues that recognizing inspired texts as inspired could not plausibly require infallible dogmatic authority. “If you think you do have to possess infallibility to discern infallibility, you have a continual regress, because now you

---

<!-- PDF page 98 -->
**[p. 85]**

need infallibility to receive and interpret the infallible teachings of your church.”[^c03-11] It would be absurd to think Moses needed a _second_ special divine voice telling him that the burning bush was God’s voice, Ortlund contends. Therefore, Ortlund thinks it would be sufficient for faith if we “fallibly recognize” the infallible books of Scripture, as anything further would generate an infinite regress. To be specific, however, Ortlund’s dilemma is not an infinite regress but simply what Ortlund thinks is an apparently absurd conclusion: that we need infallibility to assent to what God infallibly reveals. In one respect, I’ve already defended that this is not absurd, since it is implied in requiring that faith be a virtuous disposition _infused by God_ – e.g., it is classical doctrine that nobody could come to faith without the infallible guidance of the Holy Spirit prompting them to make that act of faith. It does not then seem absurd to think that, in addition to the guidance of a prophet or text by God’s Spirit to teach only what God wants the believer to accept in faith, each believer needs infallible, direct assistance from the Spirit to believe in that word properly, by an act of supernatural faith. The former is a _de jure_ or objective sense of “infallibility,” since God’s causal activity ensures that the teaching of an inspired prophet cannot be false, and the second a _de facto_ or subjective sense of “infallibility,” since the believer believes God’s Word _as God’s Word_ and thus _as infallible_ (because it is God’s Word). This second sense of infallibility does not require subjective certainty on the part of the believer, but it does require a supernatural act of God such as to prompt the _will_ of the believer to make this act appropriately, since they assent to what they do as if it were knowledge (accepting God’s own testimony), despite lacking demonstrative evidence for the truth of what they come to believe.

Further, there is a difference in epistemic situation when accepting directly the words of a prophetic inspired speaker and accepting indirectly the testimony to or about those words. In the case of accepting testimony about inspired prophets or what they said, we would be unable to accept _that testimony_ as if it were God’s Word unless an objective situation obtained that gave us sufficient normative reasons to conclude that this testimony itself was infallible. That is, we would need sufficient normative reason to believe that the testimony is infallibly transmitted (i.e., that God Himself proposes _this_ testimony for our belief today), since otherwise we would not have the right normative reasons to accept this testimony _as_ the inspired Word of God. Without an objective situation that gives us each sufficient normative reasons to accept that testimony as being infallibly transmitted, we would have no way to fulfill those duties we have not to believe what is God’s Word as if it were God’s Word. But God cannot act incoherently in expecting us to have such duties and giving us no way to fulfill them.

One might think God has provided such means in other ways than via dogmatic authority. For instance, one might think that the Gifts themselves provide sufficient normative reason for each believer to know by direct personal experience whether a text is inspired. Perhaps those Gifts ensure each individual believer will have requisite reactions to inspired texts that allow them to infallibly identify inspired texts when they are perceived. Craig proposes a story like this, on which every believer’s faith is guided by the “the self-authenticating witness of God’s Spirit who lives within him,”[^c03-12] so that, since each believer has justified belief in Christianity’s truth,

---

<!-- PDF page 99 -->
**[p. 86]**

they therefore can recognize in an epistemically basic way which books are truly the inspired Word of God, discerning the canonical books by their own experiences revealing whether a given purported book is from God.[^c03-13] The main problem with this account – as Crisp notes – is that it simply seems false by empirical evidence to the contrary.[^c03-14] Every Catholic/Orthodox believer takes and experiences their deuterocanonical books to be inspired, yet Protestant believers reject their inspiration. Craig responds by doubting that “any Christian considers the Holy Spirit’s witness on behalf of _I Enoch_ to be comparable to his witness on behalf of the Gospel of Matthew.”[^c03-15] Craig’s point is fundamentally non-responsive to the problem: the problem is that a private experience _would not generate a moral obligation on other Christians to accept this experience as binding in faith_. So, Craig’s lack of experiencing _1 Enoch_ as inspired would not generate an obligation for me (or anyone else) to reject that book as inspired, just as my experience of Sirach as inspired does not generate an obligation for Craig to accept that book as canonical. Yet, the riposte is further unconvincing both theoretically and as a matter of fact. On the one hand, it seems theoretically dubious to invoke a distinction between ‘levels’ of inspiration; a text is inspired or it isn’t. It is also simply false that everyone has such personal experiences of texts ‘as God’s Word’of each text in the canon, of each part of every verse, etc. Applying this test in reality would likely commit us to rejecting large swaths of Scripture, since it is doubtful we could arrive at a set of texts which are _unconditionally inspiring for all persons_.

Further, the appeal to direct personal experience is ultimately going to fail because it either implies that each believer is infallible in what they believe, which I have already given us reason to reject, or it will be circular. For instance, Craig believes that appealing to the Church’s consensus on Scripture and doctrine would be a reliable guide for forming a justified belief in biblical inspiration, but would not prove the authority of that Church, since the basis for the reliability of the Christian community’s judgments is that it includes many persons who are “regenerate and indwelt by the Spirit of God and so are trustworthy witnesses when it comes to hearing God’s voice in the Bible.”[^c03-16] However, this reasoning is looks circular or irrelevant. Not every believer is either regenerate or an infallibly trustworthy witness to the faith. If only a subset of believers is infallibly reliable or regenerate, and we identify such believers by way of those who accept the true Scriptures or practice what true ‘regenerate’ believers would do, the explanation has clearly gone in a logical circle. If we cannot gauge the subset of the faithful that are reliable, then the proposal is irrelevant, as we could not sufficiently discern whether (for instance) the Catholic or Protestant communions have the right canon of Scripture, as we could not discern which group is regenerate apart from doctrinal criteria internal to each’s theology. In any event, unless believers had an infallible authority, we would have insufficient normative reason to accept _in faith_ what any given believer or group of believers confessed to be the canonical list of Scriptural books. These views would be their theological opinions, rather than making dogmatic claim on our faith. But what we are looking for is a sufficient normative basis for _faith_ that _these books_ are God’s Word, and not merely someone’s humanly justified opinion that they are.

---

<!-- PDF page 100 -->
**[p. 87]**

We could instead think appeal to public evidence of past miracles is a sufficient basis for coming to faith. For instance, we could have sufficient justification that allows us to conclude with moral certainty that the resurrection of Jesus occurred and then conclude that this belief allows us to infer with moral certainty that Scripture is inspired. However, there are a few problems. Most importantly, whether a miracle would provide a sufficient basis to believe a speaker/authority is sent by God does not by itself provide us sufficient normative reason to accept testimony _that these miracles occurred_ or _that the speaker taught a given doctrine_ as a duty of faith. For instance, evidence of the resurrection of Jesus and its implications are acquired through historical testimony. Unless we had a duty to accept this evidence in faith, the testimony to this evidence would be such that we could freely reject it without sin against faith. Whether it is rational to accept this evidence, then, does not by itself generate a duty in faith to accept this evidence. So, I can come to believe _that_ Christ rose from the dead and _that_ He said certain things, on the strength of independently available evidence or reasoning, but I cannot thereby arrive at the conclusion that it is my duty in faith to accept these beliefs so transmitted to me without likewise concluding that God sanctions this testimony to Himself and makes it incumbent on me to accept. To illustrate: we have no obligation in faith to accept stories of Jesus’ miracles or of Jesus’ teaching transmitted in non-canonical writings, such as in Josephus, Pliny or the _Infancy Gospel of Thomas,_ even if these were accurately/reliably transmitted to us by those authors. What makes an act of faith in the testimony of the apostles appropriate – by contrast with these other writers’ evidence of Jesus’ life and teaching – is that God selected them to witness to Himself. Thus, miracles are at best evidences by which we discern _what testimony_ has God’s approbation, but they are not themselves a sufficient normative basis to make an act of faith.

Craig himself notes succinctly: “although arguments and evidence may be used to support the believer’s faith, they are never properly the basis of that faith.”[^c03-17] In this, the Reformed theologian John Calvin agrees: “our faith in doctrine is not established until we have a perfect conviction that God is its author … our conviction of the truth of Scripture must be derived from a higher source than human conjectures, Judgments, or reasons; namely, the secret testimony of the Spirit.”[^c03-18] However, I will argue that these claims about the nature of faith require us to conclude that testimony to God’s Word likewise requires divine approbation. Beliefs about what texts are Scriptural, about whether God’s Word is inerrant in all its parts, about what beliefs are incompatible with God’s Word, about what beliefs are ‘essential’ to God’s Word, and so forth are all beliefs that are ‘meta-scriptural.’ Meta-Scriptural beliefs cannot derive their normative valence, as _binding in faith on all believers_, from human arguments and reasoning. This does not mean that evidence from (e.g.) miracles is unhelpful, or even unnecessary, for fulfilling our duties of faith, but that the only possible role human arguments or evidence can function as is as _evidence that a testator bears divine authority_: “… human testimonies which go to confirm [faith] will not be without effect, if they are used in subordination to that chief and highest proof, as secondary helps to our weakness. But it is foolish to attempt to prove to infidels that the Scripture is the Word of God. This it cannot be known to be, except by faith.”[^c03-19]

---

<!-- PDF page 101 -->
**[p. 88]**

Consequently, if we can identify divinely approved testimony, i.e., Church as having authority communally to testify infallibly to God’s Word because it is guided by the Holy Spirit, we can then non-circularly appeal to past miracles as what gives us evidence _both_ that the Church is approved by God in its testimony to Scripture _and_ that Scripture is inspired. Yet this might seem to involve circular reasoning. We only come to know about the Church’s commission to preach the Gospel (i.e., being sent by God) from its own public testimony, namely, the Scripture that it transmits to us. The believer would reason circularly that they ought to accept Scripture as inspired because the Church authoritatively tells them Scripture is inspired, and that they ought to accept the Church’s authority in this matter because the Scripture tells them that the Church is authoritative. But the believer would not be reasoning circularly if they accept _in the same act_ both that Scripture is inspired and that the Church has dogmatic authority, even though these beliefs are logically interdependent, based on evidence which is independent of those beliefs. In this case, the believer is reasoning like someone who comes to accept both that God exists and that He is speaking to me in the same act, as being based on the same evidence. Moses can both come to believe that God exists and that God wants Him to lead the people of Israel out of Egypt, based on the same evidence: that a burning bush spoke to him. This miracle gives Moses evidence for both beliefs, despite those beliefs being interconnected logically. But there is no evident circularity in Moses’ reasoning, since a miraculous burning bush commanding Moses to lead Israel out of Egypt is good evidence for both believing God exists and that God wants Moses to do this. Nor is there necessarily any circularity in our reasoning about the evidence for these beliefs.

Believing that the Church is sent by God on the weight of the evidence of miracles performed by Christians throughout the ages, for instance, does not require that these miracles be testified to in Scripture. There might be reliable testimony of miracles in the lives of the saints, or even medical evidence (as at Lourdes). We are not bound in faith to accept any such miracles. Yet, one can come to have a justified belief that a miracle occurred based on inference or testimony, and conclude that the Church’s testimony is sanctioned by God. But – an objector might allege – if we can accept these as good evidence that the Church is divinely commissioned, and can therefore on the basis of the Church’s dogmatic authority have sufficient normative reason to make an act of true faith in the Scripture transmitted by the Church, why can’t we have good evidence from human testimony today about past miracles and so come to have sufficient normative reason to make an act of divine faith “directly” in Scripture as God’s Word?

This misunderstands the problem. The problem again is not that, without the Church being infallible, we would lack infallible access to past miracles or evidence of divine speaking. The problem is that our access to God’s Word is only via testimony, and that testimony needs to have the right normative character to bind others in faith. So, while we might have good evidence that God’s Word was spoken by a prophet in the past, and can have very high levels of justified human belief that this evidence shows that that person was really a prophet, we can only in principle have high levels of justified human belief in the _testimony_ to what that person

---

<!-- PDF page 102 -->
**[p. 89]**

said, as long as our knowledge of what they said is simply a product of human investigation.
