The grounds by which we come to have evidence of divine speaking, just as with private experience of Scripture ‘as inspired,’ would not suffice to generate the right kind of obligation by which the _testimony_ to this fact – e.g., _this prophet_ is inspired, or _this_ is the Word of God – would be morally binding upon others in faith. Only if what we presently have regarding what the prophet said was testified to _by God_, and not simply a product of human investigation, could we make an act in the present testimony as itself embodying the Word of God spoken in the past. And, once we have that sufficient normative justification to make an act of theological faith in the present, we can thereafter appeal to all that same evidence _both_ for Scripture’s inspiration _and_ for the Church’s being commissioned and guided by the Holy Spirit.[^c03-20]

It seems to me, for these reasons, that a position on which Scripture is the “only infallible rule of faith” has a problem of normative self-defeat. Ortlund, for instance, argues that “it’s perfectly consistent to accept a high view of tradition and the authority of the church while maintaining that only the Scripture is the infallible rule of the church.”[^c03-21] He therefore accepts _some_ positive normative claims to be made by confessions of the Church or its other authorities, but rejects that anything other than Scripture alone could bind in conscience upon believers, where (in short) to reject these other authorities would be to commit a sin against faith. So, whatever normative claim is made upon us by merely human authorities can be said not to bind us in conscience as a matter of faith; even if it has some normative weight, mere human authority does not have _sufficient_ normative weight to bind us in conscience as a matter of theological faith.

However, it is a _meta-_ scriptural claim that Scripture is the only infallible rule of faith which could bind us in conscience. (Beliefs about Scripture’s inspiration, what books are Scriptural, whether Scripture is the “only infallible rule of faith,” and like claims, are all meta-level claims about Scripture.) And the person/agent making such meta-Scriptural claims is – by the very thesis they endorse – not such as to make any binding claims upon us in faith. In short, it cannot be an infallible rule of faith that there is no infallible rule of faith alongside Scripture, nor is it an infallible rule of faith that Scripture is an infallible rule of faith. For that reason, these claims cannot be binding upon us in conscience, and their rejection cannot be a sin against theological faith. If it were a sin against theological faith to reject meta-Scriptural claims, these claims would have a normative status that seemingly equates them as an infallible rule of the faith, thereby undermining the view that nothing other than Scripture can make a binding claim on our faith, as being an infallible rule.[^c03-22]

Consequently, we have a “Pascal’s Wager”-type normative argument in favor of the infallibility of the Church’s dogmatic authority: belief in the infallible testimony of the Church’s teaching would be either morally harmless or morally mandatory. If there were no sin committed against faith in rejecting these metaScriptural claims – e.g., no sin in failing to believe that Scripture is the only infallible rule of faith and instead believing that the Church is infallible in its solemn teaching – then there would be no harm in rejecting this belief for good reasons

---

<!-- PDF page 103 -->
**[p. 90]**

and instead holding, for instance, a position on which the Church can teach infallibly. Conversely, if it were true that the Church taught infallibly, then the Church’s testimony to Scripture is likewise guided infallibly by the Spirit, and the beliefs in Scripture’s inspiration, the canon, and that Scripture is an infallible rule of faith would all be infallibly testified to. It would be a sin against the faith to reject the Church’s teaching on these matters, and it would be a duty of the faith to concur with the Church, adhering to doctrines such as the inspiration of Scripture, or the delineation of the canon, etc., as having dogmatic weight. It seems, therefore, that one has good reason to hold Church teaching as an infallible rule of faith, since failing to do so has a serious cost and adhering to that rule has no serious cost, even if the belief were false.

Ortlund himself (a Protestant) appeals to the guidance of the Holy Spirit in his account of the way in which the Church’s communal recognition of the canon of Scripture “was a gradual, cumulative, widespread, and organic process by which the church discerned the Word of God through _the enabling direction of the Holy Spirit_ [emphasis mine].”[^c03-23] To say that this process occurred under the “enabling direction of the Holy Spirit” is to say that God would not have allowed the Church to recognize a false canon. But that is just a different way of saying that the Church is infallible in its reception of the canon. Given that the Church would be guided communally in its reception of the canon, it is the Church corporately that has dogmatic authority, given my functional definition of dogmatic authority, since the Church’s _action_ of receiving the canon is what gives sufficient normative believers today to accept that canon based on faith. The Church’s activity generates normative reasons for believers which specify and enable them to fulfill their indirect duties arising from faith; without the Church, believers today would not be able to fulfill those duties. If the Church were _not_ infallibly guided by the Holy Spirit, Ortlund implies, we would therefore not have reasons in faith to adhere to the canon it recognizes. But then it seems Ortlund himself implies that we have a need for an “infallible” normative basis for meta-Scriptural beliefs, as I am arguing, in addition to an infallible Scriptural text. This dialectic illustrates the way in which the explicit rejection of (infallible) dogmatic authority alongside the implicit acceptance of binding meta-Scriptural beliefs is theologically inconsistent.

The reason we should locate the authority in the Church (i.e., the community of believers as a whole), and not in features of the _historical process of reception,_ or in _each believer singillatim_, is that we have no reason to believe that each believer is infallible or that there is something about the intrinsic nature of the processes by which that reception occurred which made those processes infallible. There are, as we noted above, divine promises that the Church’s faith will not fail. Thus, the only plausible subject of dogmatic authority, given the content of Scriptural promises, is the Church itself. There is no promise that each individual believer will be infallible in all that they believe is God’s Word, since believers can sincerely disagree about what constitutes God’s Word or what it entails, just as they can sincerely disagree about the extent of the canon – as Catholics and Protestants do. Similarly, nothing about a particular historical process of discerning the canon, or any mode of reasoning about which books were inspired, has a

---

<!-- PDF page 104 -->
**[p. 91]**

promise of infallibility attached to it by God’s Word. There is no place where Jesus says that the gates of hell will not triumph over the historical-critical method, or anything that would imply as much, but there are plausibly corresponding promises that the community He establishes and sends to preach will remain faithful until He returns. Since we should assume God would not act incoherently, we should assume, then, that dogmatic authority persists in the Church after the death of the last apostle and that it remains guided by the Holy Spirit such that the Holy Spirit in the community helps each believer to fulfill their indirect duties to believe _only_ God’s Word and nothing incompatible with God’s Word. That includes their faith that the canon represents Scripture.

Many nevertheless object to dogmatic authority because it seems to evacuate the need for the Church to attend to evidence regarding whether a given book is inspired or not. The Church would seem necessarily to rely on evidence as constituting a normative reason _for the Church_ to recognize the Scriptural texts as canonical. The only alternative to this would be apparently circular reasoning: it would be circular for the Church to appeal to her own recognition of the canon being inspired as decisive evidence that the Scriptural texts are inspired, just as it would be circular to appeal to our own faith that the Scripture is inspired as evidence that Scripture is inspired. Yet, if we grant that the Church’s judgment regarding what counts as a canonical book of Scripture rests on public evidence, that evidence _as public_ is also potentially accessible to every believer. Then, it would seem not to be necessary to appeal to the Church’s recognition of the canon as evidence that the canon is inspired, since each believer would have public evidence sufficient to recognize the canon as inspired.

This reasoning suffers from two flaws, resulting from the fact that it ignores that the way evidence is _publicly_ accessible is usually relative to agents and to times, i.e., to their epistemic situation vis-à-vis that evidence. The Church is a collective body of people existing over time. The Church can have public and decisive evidence in favor of its beliefs, without that evidence itself being directly accessible by all members of the Church at all times. For instance, the apostles having witnessed the Resurrection constituted good public evidence that Jesus rose from the dead, but others could not know about the Resurrection except through the _testimony_ of the apostles. That testimony serves as “indirect” evidence for subsequent Christians to believe that Jesus rose, but that testimony does not make the original “direct” evidence for Jesus’ resurrection (that the apostles had) accessible to each subsequent Christian.

Second, it does not stand to reason that, because the Church has or had good public evidence that Scriptural texts are inspired and that this fact generated a sufficient normative reason for the Church to judge that these books are inspired, it therefore is also the case that this same evidence gives each individual believer a sufficient normative reason to judge that the Church’s testimony about these books (i.e., its recognition of these books as canonical and inspired) is itself infallible. But that was the problem raised initially, regarding whether each believer who accepts Scripture on testimony can accept this testimony as sufficiently, indeed infallibly, reliable. If that testimony was not objectively reliable – guided by the

---

<!-- PDF page 105 -->
**[p. 92]**

Holy Spirit – then believers would not be able to believe God’s Word _as handed down to them_. Thus, while someone hearing the apostles preach would have sufficient normative reason to accept the preaching by the apostles as God’s Word, believers who accept testimony about the apostles’ preaching do not have sufficient normative reasons to accept that testimony as if it were the apostles’ preaching itself.

I have argued in general that there needs to be dogmatic authority vested in the Church, in the community of believers, so that believers can fulfill their indirect duties of faith only to believe God’s Word _as_ God’s Word and to reject what is incompatible with God’s Word. The key fact that generates a need for dogmatic authority is that God expects believers to believe in His Word transmitted to them by human testimony. We can understand “dogmatic authority” in terms of this functional role: providing an objective situation where there are sufficient normative reasons for believers to accept testimony to God’s Word as God’s Word. This objective situation needs to be such that believers can be assured that the Word as transmitted to them was protected by the Holy Spirit from errors, and thus that they can fulfill their indirect duties not to believe human words as God’s Words, and to reject whatever is incompatible with God’s Word. I will therefore provide two arguments based on each of these duties, concluding that there must be dogmatic authority in the Church if believers are to fulfill these duties.

In brief, these arguments are _moral_ arguments rather than epistemic ones. To understand my arguments appropriately, let me underline that these arguments hinge on the claim that Christians should hold that there are duties _in faith_ to accept certain meta-Scriptural claims, that is, beliefs _about_ Scripture/Revelation. My argument presumes that Christians should hold (as many Christians classically have) that rejecting _some_ such meta-Scriptural beliefs could constitute a sin against God. That is, there are some meta-Scriptural beliefs which impose “normative” burdens on us in faith. I will highlight two of these beliefs: that _this_ collection of books is inspired Scripture and that view X is a serious heresy. But there are many other potential meta-scriptural beliefs, such as that Scripture is the inspired Word of God in all its parts without errors of any kind. All such meta-Scriptural beliefs need grounding for that normativity, i.e., what it _is_ about the objective situation that generates an obligation upon people to accept that meta-scriptural belief in faith. Given that these beliefs are meta-Scriptural, that grounding necessarily cannot come from the content of Scripture itself. That is, meta-beliefs cannot be non-circularly demonstrated from the text in such a way that this demonstration carries divine authority, since we can only believe the text is God’s speech on God’s authority. Since the normativity in question needs to oblige us to believe claims such as these _in faith_, that authority needs to be from God.

Our conclusions in respect to any meta-Scriptural belief about Scripture need the right sort of normative backing, where rejecting them would constitute a _sin against faith_. For instance, Athanasius and Augustine both construct clear refutations of Arianism and Pelagianism (respectively) from biblical evidence. But nobody has a moral obligation in faith to accept any arguments made by Augustine or Athanasius, or any human being at all, or a community of them (e.g., the community of theologians), since no human being _qua_ human being speaks with

---

<!-- PDF page 106 -->
**[p. 93]**

God’s authority. Their arguments might be sound, helpful, strong, and persuasive, demanding near-total scholarly acceptance, but they would not thereby impose any obligation in faith on anyone else to accept these arguments. As with faith in general, we only have duties in faith to believe God, and faith in God – trust in what God reveals _because He reveals it_ – is not the same as belief in God because of the strength of the arguments or evidence. This fact does not undermine our ability to appeal to such arguments, but it does mean that no merely human authority can bind us in faith.

As Paul notes, “my speech and my preaching were not with persuasive words of human wisdom, but in demonstration of the Spirit and of power, that your faith should not be in the wisdom of men but in the power of God” (1 Cor 2:4–5). Paul’s authority _as an apostle of Jesus Christ_ is what vindicates and authorizes his message; to reject that message is therefore not simply to reject _Paul_ but to reject _Jesus_ (cf. 1 Cor 14:37, 2 Cor 13:10, Gal 1). And, since nothing false can come under faith precisely because it is trust of God’s authority, this authority must be objectively reliable such that it cannot bind us to believe what is false – the witness must be unable to teach falsehood, i.e., be _infallible_ in their teaching. That is, dogmatic authority involves divine sanction of that testimony, such as to generate an obligation in faith to believe the testimony in question. Yet, as God cannot require us to believe falsehood, that authority which generates an obligation in faith must be fundamentally infallible – otherwise, we would have divine sanction to accept falsehood in faith, which is impossible. Finally, as there is no individual (e.g., prophet or apostle) who can today claim such a charism in teaching with divine authority, and Paul’s letters or other texts do not generate the sufficient normative duties for every believer today to accept those meta-Scriptural beliefs about them with divine faith, the only remaining place that authority could be vested today (as I have argued) is the Church herself. Dogmatic authority is therefore a divine commission generating an obligation to accept testimony to God’s Word in faith, ensures the infallibility of the testifier insofar as it pertains to that which we are obliged to accept from them, and is a communal commission of the Church (ensured by divine providence) rather than a personal charism of any member. The first premise of each argument below therefore presumes that I have shown in these earlier sections that we can only appeal to a collective agent, the Church, that bears divine authority, as no human individual (or artifact, such as a scholarly process) would be _normatively_ sufficient to give someone a reason in faith to accept their testimony about God’s Word. If there were no such persons or collective agents who could testify to God’s Word in such a way that others could acquire normative reasons to accept their testimony in faith, then nobody would commit a sin against faith by rejecting those meta-Scriptural beliefs. That is to say: if the Church could not provide every believer with those sufficient normative reasons to accept the canon, creeds, doctrine of inspiration, or its condemnation of heresies, and so forth, then there is no other way those believers could have sufficient normative reasons to accept those beliefs. If believers had no such normative reasons, then they would have no duties in faith to accept, e.g., the canon, the inspiration of Scripture, or that Arianism is a serious heresy.

---

<!-- PDF page 107 -->
**[p. 94]**

Basil the Great, in his treatise on the Holy Spirit, points out that all creeds and confessions which _baptizandi_ are bound, under pain of sin, to profess (alongside other beliefs held to be binding) are all human creations; they do not simply repeat Scripture, even if drawn from Scripture.[^c03-24] If Christians believe there are _any_ such meta-Scriptural beliefs which we must accept in faith, then the only way in which those beliefs impose on us a duty in faith to accept them is if those beliefs are proposed to us by means of an authority that ultimately derives from Christ – and, as those beliefs are both meta-Scriptural and necessarily must persist through the post-apostolic era (as authoritative testimony to the inspiration and content of the apostles’ writings), Thus, the fact we have normative duties to accept _some_ metaScriptural beliefs in faith necessitates an inference to dogmatic authority, and an authority that can only be seated in the collective agency of the Church, the body of Christians sent/called/commissioned by Christ to be His witnesses and disciples in the world.

### Specific Argument 1: Canonical Reception

The easiest way to argue that the Church has dogmatic authority is that it is otherwise impossible to explain the way that the Church, and individual believers, are _bound_ to recognize and adhere to the canon of Scripture. This problem has serious ramifications: since the content of each book is understood as the inspired Word of God if and only if that content is canonical (and there are books with purported content that is not generally deemed canonical by Protestants or Catholics), if the canon of Scripture is not binding, then the content of Scripture is not binding. Not only would a Christian be free to reject parts of Scripture they find inconvenient, as Marcion did with the Old Testament, but it would be within our power (potentially) to expand the canon of Scripture. And this seems problematic simply from the standpoint of faith: faith requires adhering to God’s Word as authoritative and binding; but if Scripture could not be known to be God’s Word, it would not be able to be an object of faith; and therefore, one could not have faith in Scripture as revealing God’s Word to us. No Protestant, Catholic, Orthodox, or other mainstream Christian has therefore admitted either that Christians are free to acknowledge the content of Scripture as uninspired, or that Christians may add to the canon as they see fit.

And we need a properly theological justification, which therefore grounds _moral obligations in faith_ for each Christian to accept the results of the historical process by which Scripture was received and canonized into its modern form. Irenaeus offers a thought experiment showing that there would be a dogmatic normativity attaching to the consensus among apostolic churches in the absence of agreed upon canon of Scripture:

Suppose there arise a dispute relative to some important question among us, should we not have recourse to the most ancient Churches with which the apostles held constant intercourse, and learn from them what is certain and clear in regard to the present question? For how should it be if the apostles

---

<!-- PDF page 108 -->
**[p. 95]**

themselves had not left us writings? Would it not be necessary, [in that case,] to follow the course of the tradition which they handed down to those to whom they did commit the Churches?[^c03-25]

Unlike Bavick’s naïve claim that all the inspired books were “accepted without doubt or protest as holy, divine writings,”[^c03-26] immediately after they were written, Irenaeus’ reasoning represents the historical situation which obtained during the emergence of the canon. The writings Irenaeus refers to were – at the time he was writing in 180 AD – not a reference to a fully fixed and universally agreed upon canon. There were often _core_ books that _many_ churches agreed upon being able to be used in public worship. But many books we currently accept as canonical were never, until much later, a matter of universal consensus, and many churches admitted books that we do not currently accept as canonical. In addition, many skeptics point this out the consensus in favor of our current canon was largely established through each local church using ecclesiastical sanctions or other exercises of authority (e.g., bishops prohibiting texts to be used in public worship) to rule out the use of texts on the basis of what was understood to be a consensus in favor of orthodox dogma, rather than arising purely spontaneously or as _merely_ a matter of independent historical arguments against apostolic/prophetic authorship of those books (although such arguments played a role).[^c03-27]

Traces of this messy process remain visible. For centuries, Christians operated without any definite canonical list, and, as McDonald notes, showed little interest in compiling one until much later – they relied on the ‘rule of faith’ lived in the apostolic Churches.[^c03-28] From the early Church, much “intertestamental literature” was preserved in Church practice, especially regarding that literature present in the Septuagint (the Greek translation of the Jewish Scriptures cited in the New Testament); these books are also sometimes cited or alluded to in the New Testament. A core of such books became widely accepted in the West as canonical on authority of prominent churches (Rome) and Fathers, most notably Augustine.[^c03-29] Only relatively late did the Latin Church elevate this canonical reception to the status of dogmatic decision through the action of ecumenical councils, namely, Florence (1431–1449) and Trent (1545–1563) – before this time, the canonicity or inspiration of these books, including Revelation, was open to dispute among theologians and to varying local ecclesiastical practices.[^c03-30] Since there was no clear dogmatic claim about the canon in the early Church, such local variations did not necessitate breaking communion as long as churches were agreed on authorities that regulated their dogmatic life (e.g., ecumenical councils). And thus, those churches that maintained ecclesiastical unity longer tended to converge over time on similar canonical lists, given the way that sanctions and prescriptions by those authorities shaped their shared church practice. Eastern churches in communion with Constantinople after the falling out with Rome ended with that canonical set of books preserved in the Eastern Orthodox communion, whereas those which broke communion earlier with Rome and Constantinople (“Oriental Orthodox”) continue to maintain a different set – all of which are fully explainable in their unity as well as diversity by the “authorities” they recognized at each time. The canon accepted by most Protestants

---

<!-- PDF page 109 -->
**[p. 96]**

was shaped by the medieval theological context preceding the Reformation, but that such a question was limited to the status of “deuterocanonical” books (and not to Revelation or to other books) resulted no less from public decisions made in the preceding centuries by authorities in the Latin Church.

Irenaeus would have had no problem with these messy facts, since (as we will see later) he accepted that the Church was guided by the Holy Spirit in this decisionmaking process, given the fact that there are historical links to Christ’s commissioning of the apostles within those churches historically founded by the apostles. Irenaeus therefore took acceptance of the canonical books as morally obligatory, arguing that “gnostic” Christians and other heretical groups who had failed to subscribe to the tradition of the apostolic churches (regarding their dogmatic beliefs, as well as their acceptance of books which those apostolic churches deemed heretical) were morally and theologically blameworthy, i.e., were a sin. His reasoning was _not_ based on, e.g., independent arguments for the probability that each given book of Scripture was from an apostle or prophet (and was not textually corrupted), and that _therefore_ those Gnostics were sinning against reason in failing to be properly attentive to that evidence. Such reasoning about independent probabilities would not allow us to conclude that the Gnostics have moral obligations _in faith_ to accept the modern canon (even though they obviously played an important epistemic role alongside other considerations). Those arguments by themselves would not provide “sufficient normative reasons” to conclude that anyone failing to accept _the canon itself_ would be rejecting God’s will and committing a sin, since those arguments do not even aim to establish that the canonical collection of Scripture represents God’s will regarding what we morally ought to accept, in faith.

Augustine straightforwardly encapsulates the traditional attitude: “… it is to the canonical Scriptures alone that I am bound to yield such implicit subjection as to follow their teaching, without admitting the slightest suspicion that in them any mistake or any statement intended to mislead could find a place.”[^c03-31] “Bound” is the operative term in this claim. Any merely human research, providing arguments from historical provenance of the books, etc., would be problematic as failing to provide believers the right kind of “sufficient normative reasons” to accept _these_ books _as_ God’s Word:

Human scientific research cannot become an independent third-party arbitrator deciding what parts of Scripture, if any, are reliable and to be accepted. Those who make their doctrine of Scripture dependent on historical research into its origination and structure have already begun to reject Scripture’s self-testimony and therefore no longer believe that Scripture. … In this way, they substitute their own thoughts for, or elevate them above, those of Scripture. Historical-critical study may yield a clear insight into the origination, history, and structure of Scripture but never leads to a doctrine, a dogma of Holy Scripture.[^c03-32]

For similar reasons, arguments that the books evince a given character, such as being without error or evincing a “unity” among themselves, would be circular,

---

<!-- PDF page 110 -->
**[p. 97]**

failing to provide the right normative basis for belief that these books are _inspired,_ having God as their author.[^c03-33]

I do not here aim to _justify_ the belief that Christian theological claims require something aside from such independent arguments to give each believer sufficient normative reason which would ground a moral obligation, so that believers do not simply “sin against reason” if they were to reject, or otherwise treat as uninspired or nonauthoritative, some of those books or their parts that are received today as canonical. It is instead that I take it as a point of data and hence a premise of my argument – broadly ecumenical and uncontroversial among mainstream, traditional Christianity – that the Christian faith, and its claim that Scripture is God’s Word, requires us to say that Christians like Maricon act in an blameworthy manner not only because they are unpersuaded by arguments in favor of a given book in the current canon being apostolic (and many biblical scholars also argue some letters claiming to be from St. Paul were written by others), but because they reject the _Holy Spirit’s_ witness in the Church’s act of reception, and therefore can properly commit a sin against their faith in God rather than simply a blameworthy epistemic mistake.[^c03-34]

These claims are compatible with _some_ traditional Protestant accounts (represented classically by John Calvin) of Scripture as “self-authenticating.”[^c03-35] Michael Kruger has worked out an externalist model on which the canon’s recognition by the Church occurs through a process where the members of the Church are corporately and providentially guided to be exposed to the books of the canon, where the canonical books have properties (apostolicity) that make them recognizable as inspired, and then where the guidance of the Holy Spirit ensures that these and no other books are recognized by the Church.[^c03-36] Kruger’s theory appeals to a _consensus ecclesiae_ as guided by the Holy Spirit; he proposes that “we have biblical grounds for thinking that the consensus of the church is a reliable indicator of which books are canonical.”[^c03-37] Reformed theologian Bavinck likewise holds that “the testimony of the Holy Spirit is not a private opinion but the witness of the church of all ages, of Christianity as a whole.”[^c03-38] These authors propose, in my terms, that the consensus of the Church can serve as a sufficient normative reason for each believer to accept the canon as we find it – and imply that consensus could not have been wrong, given God’s desires for the Church to always preach the Gospel.

Kruger addresses the fact that there has been disagreement at every point in Church history about many matters, including disagreements from the earliest stage of Christianity over which books should be included in the canon, by noting (in short) that disagreement by itself does not entail that there is no fact of the matter which set of books came from the apostles and prophets.[^c03-39] Kruger points out that, while there once was disagreement, “when all the dust had settled, the church had reached an impressive degree of unity about which books it recognized as speaking with the voice of its Master.”[^c03-40] Kruger also rejects that Spirit provides believers with “absolute incontrovertible certainty” in what the consensus proposes regarding the canon or Scriptural truths – all that is implied by the account that God guides the recognition of the canon is that the consensus of the Church could not fall into error, not that anyone _recognizes_ this fact with certainty.[^c03-41] Kruger’s

---

<!-- PDF page 111 -->
**[p. 98]**

claim that the canon is “self-authenticating” therefore means not that the inspiration of the biblical text is authenticated by evidence internal to the texts themselves, or that no Christian could fail to recognize the canon, or that any Christian has infallible, direct awareness of inspiration. Instead, the “self-authenticating” character of the canon means that the Spirit will ensure that these texts are reliably recognized by the Church corporately, such that the Church was preserved from error in this regard, and therefore that the Church’s recognition is a public, normative sign by which believers may perceive and adhere to the will of the Spirit.

This corporate testimony on the part of the whole Church, whereby the Church recognizes and testifies to the canon of Scripture, needs to have the right objective character not only of being _sufficiently reliable_ but of being reliable _for the right reasons_. But there would be no clear epistemic value attached to the Church’s reception of the canon, unless it were understood that the Church’s reception could not be wrong in that the Church is uniquely _guided by the Holy Spirit._ The Protestant authors in question implicitly take theological facts about Christian apostolicity to delimit those churches whose consent and reception is normative for us; only those apostolic, orthodox churches could be counted as those which could not have been wrong in their reception of the canon. For instance, Kruger, Calvin, and Bavinck do not look to Marcionite, Muslim, or Arian communities as those whose reception of the canon was sufficient to delimit what we today ought to accept, even if these groups _did indeed_ preserve elements of the true inspired canon. They would not appeal to such groups because, in the background, they hold as a theological assumption that the community of Christian believers was founded by God and sent to preserve the message of the Gospel. Thus, the epistemic value of the Church’s reception relies on an implicit commitment to belief that, given God’s publicly declared or implied intentions regarding the Christian apostles and disciples, providential preservation of the canon was not simply a fortunate accident that could have happened through any groups whatsoever, but would be uniquely found in the Christian Church. The Church’s reception could not have been wrong, given their commission by Christ, and the Church’s ongoing testimonial act by which it receives the canon grounds sufficient normative obligations by which contemporary Christians can form a (normatively appropriate) faith in God’s Word based on that testimony. For this reason, theories of the self-authenticating character of Scripture can implicitly assign a normative authority to the Church’s reception of Scripture that goes beyond simply reliability, since at least some such theories imply that rejecting the canon would be to reject the will of the Holy Spirit.

These claims instantiate what I understand as “dogmatic authority,” as opposed to simple providential reliability in transmitting the canon: that the Church’s recognition of the canon generates normative reasons for believers who become aware of that act on the part of the Church, such that to reject the canon would be for these believers to commit a sin against the faith.[^c03-42] What I propose, therefore, is that the normativity of the canon for the faith of every Christian believer can be justified _if and only if_ the act of reception carries God’s authority in a derivative manner. The canon would make demands on our faith only if the Church’s reception carries or represents an exercise of what I will define as “dogmatic authority.” That is, we

---

<!-- PDF page 112 -->
**[p. 99]**

could not explain the prescriptive duty that we ought to receive Scripture in faith, as God’s Word, if there were no dogmatic authority.

My first argument is that, if the Church’s reception of Scripture were not guided by the Holy Spirit, and recognizable as such by believers, then no believer will violate a duty if they fail to accept Scripture as God’s Word. That is, no believer would violate an indirect duty to accept God’s Word as God’s Word if they accepted a non-inspired book as being inspired, or if they failed to accept an inspired book as being inspired, or if they accepted no Scripture at all:

- 1 If the Church’s reception of canonical Scripture as the Word of God were not guided by the Holy Spirit, and recognizable as such by each believer today, then believers today would lack a normative reason to recognize Scripture as written or preached _as_ God’s Word, that is, as requiring a response of theological faith.

- 2 If believers today lack a normative reason to recognize canonical Scripture as written or preached _as_ God’s Word, no believers today will violate their duties in failing to make an act of theological faith in that Word, and so accepting Scripture in faith could not plausibly be necessary for any believer today.

- 3 But then God’s own desires expressed in Scripture would be incoherent, since God proposes belief in the contents of Scripture as necessary for our faith.

- 4 Consequently, the Church’s reception of canonical Scripture as the Word of God was guided by the Holy Spirit and is recognizable as such by each believer, thereby providing a ground for each to have sufficient normative reasons to accept the canonical Scriptures.

- 5 But this is to say that the Church possesses dogmatic authority, since the Church’s reception gave believers a sufficient normative reason to believe the canon is God’s Word (such that failure to recognize the canon received by the Church as binding would be to violate an indirect obligation of faith), and that is what it is to have dogmatic authority.

The first argument is phrased in terms of belief in biblical inspiration and in the biblical canon, as I take this to be the most obvious issue, which all parties agree that accepting Scripture as God’s Word is what God wants us to do. And there is no dogmatic fact more logically fundamental to faith than that dogmatic fact concerning _which word is God’s Word._ While it is true that there is no other standard of authority to which one could refer except the canonical Scriptures themselves to determine directly the _content_ of God’s Word, since the Scriptures are the only instance of divine speech that we have, it is not true that the fact of inspiration alone sets up a normative situation which ensures more than what that inspired speaker once said or wrote was – _at that time_ – inspired by God. Hence, many Christians will accept that the Scriptures as inspired “in the original autographs,” or some similar phrase, because the Scriptures we currently have are not only translations of texts written in different non-modern languages, and because the Scriptural editions that we use involve editorial decisions based on critical editions of the transmitted texts. Those manuscripts exemplify many differences among themselves, which is what requires these decisions. Unless believers could have sufficient normative

---

<!-- PDF page 113 -->
**[p. 100]**

reason to believe that what is written in the Bibles they hold in church services are the Word of God, so that it were _true_ that God ensured that that book they hold was meant to be in their hands as they have it and that its teaching could not be wrong, they could not have divine faith in what is written on the pages as if it were God’s Word. We need to have the right kind of evidence from testimony that – whatever textual errors may have crept into transmission, etc. – what we have is God’s Word. (Recall the case of Joey: this does not mean our translations, translators, or editors need to be infallible, but only that they appropriately represent the faith received by the Church).

What I have argued so far directly supports premise 1. There is no non-circular way to think that believers who accept testimony to God’s Word, and therefore specifically that collection of inspired books that forms the canon, _ipso facto_ have sufficient normative reason to recognize that testimony as embodying God’s Word, except by way of the Church’s reception of the canonical books as inspired being guided by the Holy Spirit. And it seems to me not only a classical attitude about faith but simply the ordinary and obvious way most Christians come to faith that nobody _needs_ to know about the historical evidence behind any given book or set of books (let alone consider any natural theological arguments or works of scholarship) to have the right kind of evidence for accepting Scripture in faith. Without examining the scholarship, all people have morally/normatively sufficient evidence to make that judgment of faith from the testimony of other Christians who present to them that collection of Scriptures which the historical Christian Church has received as authentic. Recall the isolated villagers who, without access to any historical evidence for biblical authorship, could nevertheless come upon copies of the Bible and, after reading them, come to saving faith. Their faith that these books would be God’s Word arises from the evidence that they have that the Church receives these books as canonical, i.e., the Bible collection itself, printed and put together by Christians. Since that “embodied” fact of ecclesial reception is all they plausibly have (as I have argued above), if that fact were not sufficient normative evidence to them that these books were inspired, then they would either have no sufficient normative reason to accept those books in faith, or they would have sinned by doing so. Therefore, the Church’s reception must be able to serve itself as a sufficient normative reason for the believer to accept Scripture as the inspired Word of God.

I have also given extensive reasons supporting premises 2 and 3. Without an objective situation in which there is sufficient normative reason for believers to accept the Scriptural books as inspired, i.e., one in which the process of forming the canon was guided by the Holy Spirit, the faithful would not be in a situation where they could non-culpably make a theological act of faith in Sacred Scripture as preached to them. They might have standing obligations to have faith in God and His Word in general, but they would have no ground to perceive that they have epistemic or moral duties to accept the canonical books of Scripture as God’s Word rather than, for instance, the Koran. And so, believers today would lack duties to accept Scripture as God’s Word or at least be excused from these duties. In the absence of those normative reasons, believers would even perhaps have good sufficient normative reason to _reject_ the canonical books as inspired, given that

---

<!-- PDF page 114 -->
**[p. 101]**

what they receive is well-known to believers as an edited translation rather than the original manuscripts.

However, such a situation could not obtain, on pain that God would act irrationally by commissioning the preaching of His Word as embodied in Scripture. In Scripture, God desires that this Word be preached in every age. Failing then to provide sufficient normative reasons for anyone after the death of the last apostle to recognize that Scripture (preached or written) as God’s Word would be for God to set up every subsequent generation of believers to fail. Now, this might not be irrational if God desired the preaching of His Word to continue for purely aesthetic reasons, or something like this, but it is explicit in Scripture, and affirmed in general Christian consensus, that God wants His Word preached because believing the Word is necessary for salvation. For instance, Jesus notes, “he who does not believe is condemned already, because he has not believed in the name of the only begotten Son of God” (Jn 3:18). One cannot be saved, then, unless they believe in the name of Jesus Christ. But one can only know Jesus Christ from the testimony of the Church, whose preaching is authoritatively embodied in its canonical Scripture. If nobody today had normative reasons to believe that this testimony of Scripture constituted God’s Word, then nobody today would have normative reasons to accept this Scripture as God’s Word, and so nobody today could be culpable for failing to accept God’s Word.

Clearly, we might have the intuition that it is irrational for God to set up a situation where people would be damned or condemned for something nobody was culpable for (although not all share my intuition). However, I mean to draw attention to the irrationality in God requiring that the Gospel be preached in every age _so that_ those living in those ages might be saved. If it turns out that the Gospel not being preached would not have affected their salvation, there seems to be no sense in which Jesus’ words above would be true. Indeed, if nobody could recognize that they had normative reasons to believe Scripture is God’s Word in our age, and God nevertheless wanted us to preach it anyway and generate those reasons for people in our age to believe the Gospel (although they could not recognize it), then the preaching of the Gospel would be _so that_ those living in our age would be damned, not saved. And this would be a situation where God would manifestly act contrary to His own stated intentions. But God cannot do this.

Therefore, I reject the situation as impossible, which shows us that the consequent of the conditional in premise 1 is false – it is false that God would act irrationally in having Scripture (or, the Gospel) preached in every age for the salvation of those in that age but also providing no normative reasons for persons in every age to accept that preaching as His Word. So, it is also false that believers have no sufficient normative reason to accept testimony to God’s Word as God’s Word. By _modus tollens_ reasoning, then, we can conclude the antecedent in premise 1 is false: it is false that the Church lacks dogmatic authority, which would give believers today a normative reason to accept Scripture (or, the Gospel) as God’s Word. Thus, I conclude that the Church has dogmatic authority from Christ, which gives believers today normative reasons to accept Scripture as God’s Word. That is, all believers ought to endorse Augustine’s dictum: “I should not believe the gospel except as moved by the authority of the Catholic Church.”[^c03-43]

---

<!-- PDF page 115 -->
**[p. 102]**

### Specific Argument 2: Heresy

A second argument can be made regarding our indirect duties arising from faith to reject whatever is incompatible with God’s Word. There are at least some claims in Scripture, aside from Scripture itself, that all parties agree are necessary for a believer to accept. This follows straightforwardly from the irrationality of God speaking _words_ to us that seem to assert or command or promise, but then not wanting us to _accept_ anything He actually says, and only to accept the _fact_ that He has spoken. This seems to serve no purpose and even be irrational, since God should not assert things for our belief in Scripture if He did not want us to believe what He said. It has thus seemed to all parties that we have duties to accept what God proposes for our belief in Scripture, not simply to accept the canon of Scripture. Therefore, on pain of logical contradiction, a believer could not accept whatever logically contradicts what is incompatible with accepting whatever God’s Word reveals. Even Nemes admits such duties, as he seemingly implies that Christians have duties to accept God and Jesus’ Word, and this means that nobody who is a good Christian can claim to follow Jesus and reject His Word.

There are many issues that are subject to dogmatic controversy, such as whether Jesus Christ is God incarnate. Many Christians – aside from Steven Nemes – have accepted that belief in the divinity of Christ is a “non-negotiable” Christian belief. What they mean is that culpably denying this belief and accepting its denial (i.e., Arianism) would put a believer’s salvation in jeopardy or disqualify them as a true Christian or believer. The rejection of Arianism is embodied in a creedal statement professed by most Protestants as well as all Orthodox and Catholic Christians – the Nicene Creed – which rules out Arianism by affirming that Jesus Christ is “consubstantial” (_homoousios_) with God the Father. Consequently, what these claims imply is that failing to subscribe to the Nicene Creed would involve a violation of their duties arising from Christian faith.

If Arianism is false, and accepting Arianism would be logically incompatible with those beliefs believers are obligated to accept as being taught by God’s Word, then there is a sense that each believer has sufficient normative reasons merely from their acceptance of Scripture to reject Arianism and profess the Nicene Creed. What each believer lacks are sufficient normative reasons to accept testimony regarding what is _incompatible_ with Scriptural truth. This position does not require it to be the case that _Scripture_ is hard to understand or that the basic beliefs required for salvation are not clearly accessible for most people. Rather, what is required for this position is the assumption that _heresy_ that is hard to identify – i.e., testimony about God or what He teaches in Scripture, which, if we accepted that testimony, would put us in a position where our beliefs would logically entail that we reject what God requires us to believe in Scripture. If each ordinary believer were put to the test whether they could infer that each of the Nicene Creed’s articles from Scripture, it would be possible in general for them to find explicit support for many of the articles. But they would necessarily fail to find either the Nicene Creed itself in the Bible, nor what makes the Nicene Creed unique: the _homoousios_ clause is nowhere in Scripture.

---

<!-- PDF page 116 -->
**[p. 103]**

One might think that Scripture is clear on the question of Christ’s divinity, or that Scripture provides a basis from which we can conclude beyond a reasonable doubt, on a scholarly basis, that Arianism is not the best interpretation of Scripture. Since Scripture does not directly address the question of whether Jesus Christ is consubstantial with the Father, Scripture does not directly address whether _it is necessary for having faith_ that we profess that Jesus is consubstantial with the Father or even simply that Arianism is false. Identifying Arianism as incompatible with Scriptural truths is the work of theology, since recognizing Arianism as serious heresy requires inference about what is entailed by Arian testimonial confessions and knowledge of how those claims are logically incompatible with what Scripture requires us to believe. The fact that it is heresy that is hard to identify, rather than Scriptural truth, is an easily overlooked premise of a famous argument proposed by Vincent of Lérins:

… owing to the depth of Holy Scripture, all do not accept it in one and the same sense, but one understands its words in one way, another in another; so that it seems to be capable of as many interpretations as there are interpreters. For Novatian expounds it one way, Sabellius another, Donatus another, Arius, Eunomius, Macedonius, another, Photinus, Apollinaris, Priscillian, another, Iovinian, Pelagius, Celestius, another, lastly, Nestorius another. Therefore, it is very necessary, _on account of so great intricacies of such various error_, that the rule for the right understanding of the prophets and apostles should be framed in accordance with the standard of Ecclesiastical and Catholic interpretation [emphasis mine].[^c03-44]

Whereas the typical lay Christian might be placed to recognize basic demands laid upon them by Scripture, only theological experts are the ones properly placed to recognize and respond to heretical beliefs, given that it takes a special set of abilities to recognize what entailments a belief might have and how that belief impacts our beliefs in other parts of Scripture. (Logic professors are keen to point out that many of their students hold beliefs whose entailments they do not recognize, and that many of them therefore do not recognize that they often hold what are, in fact, contradictory beliefs.) This does not mean the laity are simply _unable_ to recognize heresy, since many do, once it is presented to them appropriately and accessibly. It means, rather, that believers who accept testimony about God’s Word need to have sufficient normative reasons to recognize and reject Arianism as a heresy, that is, as a testimony about God’s Word which, if accepted, would be logically incompatible with accepting what God’s Word teaches (and therefore false).

I have highlighted Arianism, since it has seemed to many that the denial of the divinity of Christ is a central belief, and the profession of Arianism would be seriously bad for belief. But there are conceivably less serious errors, such as accepting that Abraham had only one wife, which are logically incompatible with what Scripture teaches about Abraham (he had two or three wives). Although believing Abraham had one wife too could involve a kind of negligence, and thus a violation

---

<!-- PDF page 117 -->
**[p. 104]**

of our indirect duties of faith, it is implausible that believing this about Abraham would lead one to fail to accept whatever is central to our faith in God’s revelation. Consequently, we should specify that “heresies” are commitments to beliefs about Scripture, the acceptance of which would entail the denial of _central_ truths taught in Scripture. For that reason, let us simply assume for the purposes of argument that believers need sufficient normative reasons for ruling out only _true_ heresies like Arianism, since believing something incompatible with _central_ truths God wants us to hold would constitute a serious violation of our duties in faith. Indeed, these violations should be so serious that those who _fail to reject_ Arianism or other such heresies would put themselves in danger of lacking faith in God’s Word at all – their faith apparently in God’s Word would be faith in the merely human word of Arius.

Since belief in heresy would be so dangerous, it is therefore as important as belief in the Scriptural canon that believers have sufficient normative reason to reject Arianism. Otherwise, God’s purposes would be thwarted in promulgating Scripture to us. Believers would be open to assenting to testimony about Scripture that is heretical and end up in a situation where they believe something as God’s Word, which is merely a human word. Since their own reasoning, by itself, would not be a suitable way to achieve this goal, there need to be publicly accessible grounds which generate sufficient normative reasons for each believer to reject serious heresies. This also follows regarding those judgments themselves by which we identify _serious_ error. Many disagree about what is an “essential” doctrine of the Christian faith. Nemes seemingly rejects that Arianism is a serious error incompatible with faith, whereas Ortlund holds that Arianism is incompatible with Christian faith. If believers were under no obligation in faith to identify Arianism as a serious error, they would be under no obligation in faith to reject Arianism. So, dogmatic authority is likewise necessary for identification of those beliefs that are serious errors, i.e., heresies, in a way that generates publicly binding obligations on Christians to reject them in faith.

Even if it were the case that Arianism were definitively excluded on scholarly grounds alone as an entirely unreasonable hypothesis – which seems highly implausible, given that these positions have been defended by highly skilled biblical scholars, and therefore at least have some _prima facie_ scholarly plausibility (even if false) – this would not be sufficient to allay the worry regarding the _normative force_ of these judgments. We can tell other people that their acceptance of Arianism would be a sin against faith, based on our reasoning about Scripture, but we would have no standing to oblige other people to accept _this judgment_ in faith. Nobody would commit a sin against the faith in failing to accept my reasoning about Scripture if I were simply speaking in my capacity as a private theologian. And, if all we have are private theological voices to warn against heresy, then nobody – no church body, no council, no confessional statement – would have any normative standing where to reject their judgment about what counts as a heresy would be to sin against the faith. It might still be true that heresies would be bad to accept, and that Arianism would be a heresy, but nobody would have an obligation in faith to accept _that Arianism is a heresy,_ since nobody would be so placed to make those public judgments about Arianism.

---

<!-- PDF page 118 -->
**[p. 105]**

It might be thought that public determinations of heresy are unnecessary, as each person would still be able to judge for themselves whether Arianism is heretical. We might further think _these_ judgments would be binding in conscience, such that you would commit a sin against the faith in accepting Arianism once you were presented with the evidence from Scripture that Arianism is heretical _and_ you yourself judge that God’s will requires you to reject Arianism. However, there is a serious problem with this model. Now, it cannot be that those who lived before Arianism existed would have sinned by failing to condemn Arianism. Instead, it can only be that faith requires us to be _disposed_ to reject Arianism _once presented_ with its claims. But, if the only sufficient normative basis for recognizing Arianism’s claims and its incompatibility with Scripture were our own judgments regarding the evidence, then nobody has such an obligation to reject Arianism unless _drawing those inferences themselves_.

This model would seemingly make the demands of orthodoxy far too weak if we had a duty in faith to reject something as a heresy _if and only if_ we can construct a sound theological argument in favor of its condemnation. As with the case of the biblical canon and the island villagers, it looks practically impossible that many people will have relevant access to these facts and theological reasoning skills requisite to attend to the evidence carefully, draw out the inferences correctly, and so forth. Things get worse when we move beyond straightforward heresies like Arianism and move to complicated questions such as Apollinarism, Monothelitism, Sabellianism, etc. Further, the model would imply that I would need to grasp the arguments for myself, and be convinced by them, before I would acquire such an obligation to reject Arianism as incompatible with God’s Word. But not every scholarly inference counts as _demonstrating_ or _proving_ this incompatibility in such a way that our rational assent is morally requisite. What is doubtful cannot oblige our conscience, so if it were simply _doubtful_ whether Arianism is orthodox, we would not acquire an _obligation in faith_ to condemn it. The sin of heresy (and obligation to reject heresy) does not even look possible for anyone beyond (at best) a very, very small subset of persons.

The model might rule out the possibility of the sin of heresy entirely. Clearly, heretics themselves almost always fail to be convinced by the relevant evidence for orthodoxy. If the reasoning and evidence need to be truly demonstrative to provide a sufficient normative basis for acquiring an obligation to reject a heresy on supernatural faith, then only those who grasp the demonstrative necessity of an inference would be so obligated. But, since it seems plausible that it is not a matter of free choice whether we assent to (truly) demonstrative inferences, heresy would be involuntary – a failure to be presented with or grasp suitable evidence that compels judgment. And that’s not a sin. If instead we thought of the obligation as following upon the force of reasoning that attains a “moral certainty” from high probability reasoning, then only those who made such inferences would be obliged to consent. But, again, heretics will not agree with this assessment of the probabilities. If we are irrational in this failure to assess correctly, it would need to be a sin against faith to fail to assess the probabilities correctly. But it’s not a sin against the faith to fail to weigh probabilities correctly or to draw inferences incorrectly. The rules

---

<!-- PDF page 119 -->
**[p. 106]**

governing how we do hermeneutics or inference are not specific to theological reasoning. Such failures would be sins against reason, not faith. So, the model seems to make it that nobody can commit the sin of heresy.

It is true that theological hypotheses are evaluated by standard scientific criteria, like rules of inference, hermeneutics, philosophical coherence, etc.; Christian believers are under obligations to profess the faith, to declare the Gospel message in such a way as to call others to conversion, not only to opine about how one might be saved. If the Church were unable to state more than its _opinion_ what conversion required, the Church could not call anyone to conversion, since it could not proclaim what conversion required. The Church is more than an academic exercise. (The Scriptures too consist primarily in testimony about God’s deeds and attitudes, or other ordinary illustrations easily understandable by all. Deductive theological arguments are hardly to be found.) Those who recognize a heresy for what it is are bound in faith to reject it. But they are likewise bound to help others to reject and avoid heresy. And that applies even when others do not recognize it for themselves. Thus, we are obliged not only to present evidence and arguments, but to _testify_ the Gospel to others. Conversely, we are sometimes obliged to respond to testimonial evidence in faith, even when we do not ourselves grasp the reasoning behind a given claim. For instance, we can rationally adhere to faith despite apparent counterexamples and contradictions in Scripture, trusting that there might be a resolution we are not yet aware of.

In this way, the model seems to unnecessarily limit the pool of potential good theological evidence, such that I ought to be sensitive (roughly) only to arguments and deductive inferences. Indeed, since the model holds that _only_ grasping the evidence by myself gives me an obligation in faith to condemn a given doctrine, testimonial evidence from other theologians or corporate groups, such as a confessional statement of my Church, would _not_ be a sufficient normative basis by which I would have an obligation to condemn that belief as heretical. The model therefore has the counterintuitive result that if I rejected Arianism because there was widespread condemnation of any given heresy by 99% of the Church’s members and pastors, and widespread scholarly consensus that such a heresy is incompatible with the best reading of Scripture, I would be committing a _sin against the faith_ in taking the rejection of Arianism to be a requirement of faith – the consensus would not be a sufficient normative basis for me to condemn Arianism, without myself grasping and concurring with the rationale for this consensus view.

But testimony _can_ be good evidence, and I sometimes have obligations to accept testimony, even when I do not have _personal, direct_ epistemic access to evidence in favor of the truths which are testified. All properly revealed truths, like the Trinity, cannot be known independent of God’s testimony, but this is also particularly true of facts like the Resurrection of Christ, which were knowable by the apostles directly but are not accessible to us today except by testimony. It would be problematic for Christians to accept an evidential standard on which _each believer_ would need the evidence _present to them_ for each given belief they hold in faith, since they would then not have obligations to adhere in faith to testimony about

---

<!-- PDF page 120 -->
**[p. 107]**

facts like the Resurrection. We thus have obligations to be responsive to testimony from other believers, since we would otherwise have no access to many revealed truths. And since God wants us to testify to others about the Gospel, and to rely on testimony from others as our means of arriving at the Gospel, God has endowed not only the Scriptural authors but our subsequent testimony to Gospel truths (such as the Resurrection) with authority of the Spirit, so that others hear God speaking through us and are presented with obligations in faith to respond.

If each believer has obligations in faith to reject heresies, and no private revelation is vouchsafed to each believer by which they can recognize heresy, the only remaining kind of evidence publicly accessible to every believer, which would even potentially carry such a normative weight, is the public testimony of the Church. Many in fact do rely on these public judgments of the Church, of its theologians or pastors, embodied in confessional statements or group attitudes, in guiding their own decisions what to reject as heretical. Such public testimony, though, needs to be invested with sufficient normative standing so that we _rightly_ allow our faith to be reliant on such judgments. Consequently, there needs to be good theological evidence publicly accessible to everyone, which does not require them themselves to have direct access to the evidence for beliefs they are obliged in faith to hold, so that _all_ have an obligation in faith to recognize and reject heresy. And, since I have already defended that our duties in faith require us to be sensitive to and only adhere as a rule of faith to those testifiers who have the right normative standing, and I have proposed that the only plausible body having such corporate commission to testify to the Gospel is the Church, I conclude that the Church’s public testimony would therefore need to provide suitable normative grounds for each believer to reject heresies.

We can therefore give the following argument for dogmatic authority, which rests on the fact that, if the Church’s rejection of serious heresies were not guided by the Holy Spirit, believers today would never be blameworthy or criticizable for failing to reject those heresies and failing to confess their contrary.

- 1 If the Church’s rejection of serious heresies were not guided by the Holy Spirit, and recognizable as such by each believer today, then believers today would lack a normative reason to recognize those heretical beliefs they ought _not_ to confess, that is, as needing to reject these beliefs as a response required by theological faith.

- 2 If believers today lack a normative reason to recognize those serious heretical beliefs they ought _not_ to confess, no believers today will violate their duties in confessing those heresies, and so rejecting those heresies and professing their contrary could not plausibly be necessary for the salvation of any believer today.

- 3 But then God’s own desires expressed in Scripture would be incoherent, since God proposes belief in the contents of Scripture as necessary for our faith, which are among the beliefs that heresies deny.

- 4 Consequently, the Church’s rejection of serious heresies was guided by the Holy Spirit and is recognizable as such by each believer, thereby providing a ground for each to have sufficient normative reasons to reject those same heresies.

---

<!-- PDF page 121 -->
**[p. 108]**

- 5 But this is to say that the Church possesses dogmatic authority, since the Church’s rejection of heresies gave believers a sufficient normative reason to believe what is and is not God’s Word (such that failure to recognize the rejection of these heresies as binding would be to violate an indirect obligation of faith), and that is what it is to have dogmatic authority.

Since I have already outlined the reasoning in support of premise 1, and the support for the other premises is similar to that provided for the first argument (_mutatis mutandis_), I conclude that this second argument gives us further reasons that believers would need to have sufficient normative reason to fulfill their indirect duties to reject whatever is incompatible with God’s Word. That is, believers need their rejection of heresies like Arianism to be grounded on _objectively reliable_ testimony to what God’s Word entails. And that sort of objective reliability which would permit them to make an act of theological faith that Arianism is false only can come from God – no believer could be culpable for failing to reject heresies if there were no dogmatic authority. But, if no believer could be culpable for failing to reject heresies, it would follow that believers would not be culpable for failing to accept God’s Word, since these heresies lead to someone professing not God’s Word but some other word. Thus, the situation would be much like the first: if there were no dogmatic authority, nobody could be culpable for accepting a merely human word as God’s Word or failing to accept God’s Word generally.

The problem posed by heresies to which dogmatic authority is the answer is then not that believers cannot be aware of what Scripture teaches without the Church or dogmatic definitions or theological experts. The problem is that we have corporate epistemic and moral duties as believers to be _careful_ about our beliefs – we have duties to understand, confess, practice, and maintain the faith as we have received it. Thinking that we cannot violate these duties is not plausible. There is such a thing as negligence in faith. Failing to be concerned about heresy would constitute a sin against the faith, since it would evince a lack of concern about whether what we believe is what God wants us to believe. But it would be impossible for many believers to fulfill their duties to maintain their faith, and avoid that negligence, if the individual could not rely _normatively_ on the community of other believers, the Church, to help them form the right beliefs – for the same reason that it would be impossible for any given believer today to accept _testimony_ to God’s Word, given that we do not receive God’s Word except by testimony from others.

In other words, the fact of the Church’s rejection of a heresy needs to be sufficient evidence for each believer to believe _with theological faith_ that this belief is indeed heretical and thus have sufficient reason to recognize that the Nicene Creed embodies the teaching of Scripture that demands their faith, just as the Church’s acceptance and transmission of these books as Scripture needs to be sufficient reason for us to believe _with theological faith_ that those books it receives are the inspired Word of God. The Church’s faith needs to be reliably guided by the Holy Spirit, or it could not function as a rule of faith for each believer. But there needs to be such a rule of faith in reading Scripture – just as Vincent of Lérins insisted – or _nobody_ would be culpable for believing in the word of men as God’s Word. But

---

<!-- PDF page 122 -->
**[p. 109]**

God allowing a situation where nobody could do wrong by believing the word of men as God’s Word is incoherent, given the fact that He has revealed to us what He wants us to believe, and that there are duties arising from this for every believer. An illustration of this is that, absent such authority, we might be able to conclude from our own reasoning about Scripture that a given belief is a serious or “damnable” heresy (a term Ortlund uses) that would preclude a Christian in good standing believing this heresy, but these arguments would not give anyone else a duty in faith to accept this judgment. Only if there were an authority that could decide the issue and thereby generate an obligation on all believers to _accept this judgment as binding in faith_ (cf. Acts 15, 1 Cor 5:5) would every believer have a sufficient normative reason to condemn or reject that heresy. I will argue further in the next chapter that there are occasions when such decisions (i.e., dogmatic definitions) become necessary for the Church to fulfill its indirect duties to maintain the faith – this conclusion, if true, reinforces the above argument.

These two arguments establish that dogmatic authority of the Church can therefore not only provide sufficient reason for each believer to accept God’s Word _as_ God’s Word, but also sufficient reason for each believer to reject belief in heresies as incompatible with God’s Word. In the end, there are only three choices. Since not every believer has sufficient normative reason from the possession of the canonical books alone to accept that what they have received _is_ the inspired Word of God (and not merely testimony to it), nor does every believer have sufficient normative reason from the possession of Scripture alone to reject merely human interpretations of God’s Word that would falsify God’s message, the acceptance of which would be to accept human words as God’s Word, we can accept either [1] that God acts incoherently in revealing Scripture, [2] that there are no possible beliefs which, if we accepted those beliefs as God’s Word, would violate our duties to believe what God reveals to us in Scripture, or [3] that God has provided a _rule of faith_, the dogmatic authority of the Church, by which every believer has sufficient normative reason to accept the inspired books as canonical and also any other necessary dogmatic facts. [3] seems to me the only acceptable option.

## The Exercise of Dogmatic Authority

Christian theologians have seen in those passages indicating the commissioning of the apostles a “Great Commission” which extends derivatively through the apostles to the whole Church, and which persists within the Church for all time, even after the death of the last apostle. There is a good reason for this. For instance, Jesus indicates He would be with those He addressed until the end of time – but the apostles are all dead, and it is not plausible that Jesus’ promises ended with the death of the last apostle. Since God’s promises could not fail, it therefore follows that the commission to preach the Gospel did not end when the last apostle died. Rather, that commission persists in the Church, as in virtue of that commission, so that people continue to be sent to preach the Gospel. This viewpoint echoes Jesus’ promise to Simon Peter that “the gates of Hades shall not prevail against” His

---

<!-- PDF page 123 -->
**[p. 110]**

Church (Mt 16:18). Clearly, if the Gospel preaching were to stop with the death of the last apostle, many people would die without hearing what was necessary for their salvation – and that seems an obvious case where the “gates of Hades” would prevail, so that Jesus’ promises would fail.[^c03-45]

Since God’s promises could not fail, it therefore follows that the commission to preach the Gospel did not end when the last apostle died. Rather, that commission persists in the Church, so that people continue to be sent by God to preach the Gospel. Indeed, we have abundant evidence from within Scripture that the apostles appointed those to succeed them in offices by which preaching would continue (e.g., Acts 6:3–6, 13:3, 14:23, 20:28; 1 Tim. 4:14, 5:22; Tit. 1:5). Ortlund thus notes: “Those Protestants who oppose apostolic succession do not, of course, deny the general idea that the apostles’ ministry succeeds to the post-apostolic Christian church. It is clear the apostles appointed various individuals to positions of leadership in the church with the intent that the ministry of the gospel would continue.”[^c03-46] Since parties can agree that the Church inherits dogmatic authority from those Jesus initially sent, and that they needed to be sent by God for having this authority and to be guided by the Holy Spirit, I will propose that the fundamental point of doctrinal disagreement concerns only _where_ and _how_ dogmatic authority is exercised.

What I will call a “dogmatic definition” is a public and binding judgement concerning what is necessary for belief. Such a judgment would be the hallmark of dogmatic authority, as a definition sets forth a dogma – i.e., a binding public judgment concerning what Christians ought to believe. Many Christian churches and communities already propose dogma to their flock in non-infallible and public ways, understanding that public judgments (embodied in confessional documents, etc.) are epistemically important for believers to avoid heresy and adhere to essential articles of the faith. What is a subject of disagreement is whether any subset of Christians can make _infallible_ public judgments around such questions, that is, whether any such judgments could be _binding_ on everyone, for all time. I have made progress by arguing that the Church needs to function as a dogmatic authority in the life of believers, as the “rule of faith,” where believers can fulfill their indirect duties of faith by adhering to the Church’s infallible testimony to God’s Word. But this does not entail that the Church can or needs to exercise this authority in infallibly proposing a dogmatic definition. The fundamental point of controversy among Christians thus concerns whether and how the Church has the power to make dogmatic definitions. Then, answering first _how_ a dogmatic definition proposed by officeholders in the Church would be normatively ensured to be representative of the whole Church (not just a subset) will then help us respond to the question of _where_ the Church is located (its boundaries) in ways that are less controversial or question-begging.

### Objections to Dogmatic Definition

The locus of disagreement concerns exemplary acts by which the dogmatic authority recognizes or specifies that God has revealed _this_ belief for our faith. These would be solemn acts whereby the authority exercises its divine commission to

---

<!-- PDF page 124 -->
**[p. 111]**

definitively define a belief as being proposed by God for all the faithful. I’ve already argued that, since the Church’s collective testimony binds us in faith – as that is what it is to have dogmatic authority – the Church’s collective testimony to God’s Word must be infallible, guided by the Holy Spirit to be free from error. And collective exercises of the Church’s authority to propose a dogmatic definition would be infallible in the same way. If these definitions gave believers obligations _in faith_ to accept them (dogmatic authority comes from being sent _by God_) but were nevertheless fallible human judgments about what ought to be accepted in faith, then God could give believers obligations arising from their theological faith in Him that they believe something that could be false. Since God cannot do this, i.e., cannot bind people to believe what is or could be false, it would follow that those solemn acts or definitions which arise from divinely commissioned authority could not bind anyone to believe what is false. Otherwise, God would contradict Himself in having sent such messengers, i.e., instituted such an authority in the first place.

Given that dogmatic authority acts with a commission from God to speak His Word, we might think that dogmatic authority would impose the same obligations upon us to believe what a dogmatic authority says as God’s own Word. This is false. Definitions are true judgments regarding God’s Word, not God’s Word itself.
