Dogmatic authorities do not supply content for faith, as they are not inspired, and thus their actions or words generate no direct duties to take their testimony to God’s Word as being itself God’s Word. Indeed, the role of doctrinal authority is simply to _testify_ to God’s Word, and so it does not even make sense to think that its word would constitute an object of belief. The _canon_ of Scripture, for instance, is obviously not God’s Word. In the ancient Church, authors such as Origen and John Chrysostom distinguished “dogmas” as those doctrines which were proposed by God for our faith (_dogmata theou_), whereas everything else proposed by the Church constituted “instructions” (_logoi ekklesiastikoi_).[^c03-47]

Clearly, theological faith ultimately devolves upon God’s authority, so that we can only be obliged in faith to believe what God proposes. Obligations to believe dogmatic definitions arise from the Word spoken, not from the words of the definition. So too dogmatic authority, by its very nature, presumes a distinction between God’s Word and what the Church preaches. Thus, it would be false, _pace_ Barth’s intimation, that authoritative dogmatic definitions would constitute a _part_ of Revelation itself. Definitions are not, alongside Scripture, two parts of a greater whole that is God’s Word.[^c03-48] But Christian theologians would still have obligations to take dogmatic definitions into account while preaching or interpreting God’s Word, since these would be akin to conclusive evidence for us regarding what God’s Word says to us. They would be testimony about God’s Word that, as infallible, would be entirely trustworthy. But we do not accept _a dogmatic definition_ on faith, as if the definition were God’s Word. We would merely accept God’s Word in the same spirit that the Church in the definition (reliably) gives testimony to.

Nemes, however, rejects the possibility of dogmatic definitions. Nemes proposes that “no disciple of Jesus possesses such authority over the others as to be able to issue orders or make proposals without the possibility of being questioned

---

<!-- PDF page 125 -->
**[p. 112]**

or contested … their only job is that of passing on the commandment of God for all people: that all believe in the name of his Son and that they love each other.”[^c03-49] Nemes therefore implies that the authority to make a dogmatic definition would entail that those who can issue these definitions cannot be contested or questioned. This objection would seem compatible with admitting (as Nemes does not) that consensus in the Church could have dogmatic “weight,” giving believers sufficient reason to accept the canon of Scripture as it has been received or to reject heresies like Arianism.

On the one hand, Nemes has mistaken dogmatic authority to make a definition for a right not to be questioned in general. But a right not to be questioned does not follow from the Church’s infallibility in dogma. It does not follow that those representatives of the Church who held an office that entitled them potentially to make a dogmatic definition under specified circumstances would therefore be exempt from question or contest; what is beyond question or contest is not the _agent_ but rather the _definition._ On the other hand, even if Nemes’ claims that every disciple of Jesus should be subject to question or contest in the exercise of their authority were taken at face value,[^c03-50] exemplar and prominent exercises of dogmatic authority are usually understood as _collective_ (i.e., ecumenical councils, the _consensus ecclesiae_). That collective exercises of authority are infallible and irreversible – and thus not subject to reasonable question or contest – is compatible with it being true that no _individual_ disciple is exempt from question or contest in their exercise of authority.

Yet it bears noting that to “pass on” a commandment of God _as God’s commandment_ is to address a command to others that, if it were truly God’s commandment, cannot be questioned or contested. The nature of this not being open to question or contest is simply because the testimony is _regarding God’s Word_ and therefore has content which does not count as a human word. It is true that “every human word must always be measured by God’s word,”[^c03-51] but the very thing in question is whether human testimony to God’s Word can embody God’s Word.[^c03-52] Indeed, Christians must believe that it can, since if we said that _no_ human word could do so, then revelation by human speech or writing would be impossible. Consequently, if a dogmatic definition constitutes testimony to divine speech, what _makes_ it unquestionable or uncontestable is not a feature of the individual testifying, but that what they are testifying to embodies God’s Word. And we cannot question or challenge God’s Word.

Similarly, if a person is a prophet or an apostle who speaks God’s commandment _as sent by God,_ that person has an authority which is not their own, but God’s. It might be true that such a person can be questioned or contested in other regards, as a private individual, but it would not be the case that one would have authority to question or contest their authority to speak in God’s name (since only God can revoke a divine commission). If the apostles were commissioned to pass on the commandment of God for all people, they would have dogmatic authority to speak in God’s name – if they did not, they would not be passing on commandments in God’s name but advice or personal opinions about God in their own name. To contest the apostles’ divine commission would be to contest God’s authority. It is

---

<!-- PDF page 126 -->
**[p. 113]**

for this reason that evidence Nemes raises concerning whether the apostles had authority to expect “unquestioned obedience” in all matters is neither here nor there regarding whether the apostles or the Church have dogmatic authority, or, specifically, the ability to make dogmatic definitions.[^c03-53] Being a holder of dogmatic authority does not entail unquestioning obedience in all things; it entails only unquestioning obedience to God’s Word, to which the authority infallibly testifies in specific circumstances.

Consequently, the most decisive reason to think that dogmatic definitions – public judgments regarding what is or is not to be accepted as God’s Word – are not _in principle_ impossible is that prophets and the apostles themselves seemed to possess such authority to determine questions of the faith. If anyone was able to make a judgment about what God or Jesus wanted us to believe, it would be a prophet or apostle. A prophet is precisely someone who speaks the Word of God to us. It should be uncontroversial that God, acting through the prophet, can tell us what we ought to believe, since, to deny this, would be to deny we have any duties to believe God’s Word. Nevertheless, being an apostle seems to be an _office_ unlike being a prophet, as indicated by the fact that Jesus sets apart only Twelve whom He names “apostles” specifically (e.g., Lk 6:12–16, Mk 3:13–19, Mt 10:1–4), and then the apostles electing Matthias “to take part in this ministry and apostleship” (Acts 1:25) and re-constitute the apostles as Twelve after the death of Judas Iscariot, and then later the apostles recognizing Paul as an apostle alongside the others (Gal. 2:9). Whereas the apostleship was an office, a “standing” situation, prophets classically seem to exercise their authority only on occasion, when moved by the Spirit (even if there are “guilds” of prophets in the Old Testament). While there are moments when the apostles are said to be so moved to speak, however, many Christians have accepted that apostles had a standing authority in the Church to determine for all believers what they ought to believe and did not need to go into a trance or be “moved” by the Spirit to be infallibly reliable authorities within the Church. Ortlund concedes this point, when he notes that “_sola Scriptura_ is a framework for the church as such, not for Christians in the apostolic age sitting under the teaching of living apostles …”[^c03-54] because, as he elsewhere implies, their words would be infallible.[^c03-55]

The reason for affirming dogmatic authority in the apostles is obvious. Aside from the Twelve, there is no one else sent by Jesus to preach in His name who could provide that kind of authoritative testimony to the facts of Jesus’ life, death, and resurrection, or His teachings.[^c03-56] Yet God’s desire that the Gospel be preached for all subsequent time would have failed if the apostles would have been able to teach their own word as God’s Word, since it would seem believers are _de facto_ and _de jure_ obliged to believe the apostles’ words: the New Testament is nothing other than their words. If we thought that the apostles’ words were not reliable testimony of God’s Word, then this is simply to deny the inspiration of the New Testament. And, if the New Testament is not God’s Word, but simply human testimony about God’s Word, we could not accept it in faith as being itself God’s Word. We would have obligations _not_ to accept the New Testament as God’s Word. Clearly, almost every Christian would reject this implication.

---

<!-- PDF page 127 -->
**[p. 114]**

Yet Nemes argues that there is no good reason for considering the apostles to be infallible or their words inspired. “[Aside from God,] all other persons—even the apostles themselves—only exercise theological authority in a derivative, fallible, and in principle reversible way.”[^c03-57] Nemes argues, for instance, that “no such promise was made to them” that the apostles would have authority, inspiration, or infallibility, and the apostles seemingly make mistakes even in teaching about Jesus (e.g., Gal 2:11) – Nemes rejects interpretations of Jesus giving the apostles the “keys of the kingdom of heaven” or powers to forgive sins (e.g., Matt 16:19, 18:18; Jn 20: 22) which involve anything like authority over other believers, since this would be contrary to Jesus’ radical egalitarianism and explicit rejection that His disciples lord it over each other (e.g., Matt 18:1–14; Lk 22:25–27).[^c03-58] Nemes argues the apostles are merely humanly reliable sources of testimony giving us “knowledge about Jesus and his teachings,” whereas to ascribe anything further than this to the apostles “is entirely irrelevant to all this. There is no basis or need for making this additional supposition.”[^c03-59]

Nemes does not directly face the issue whether the apostles’ dogmatic authority would be necessary for accepting that the New Testament is inspired. Yet his positions seem to entail that the New Testament cannot be inspired. Nemes hints at such a position when he notes that Christians have faith not in apostles (and by implication they do not have faith in those books that they wrote), but in Jesus alone. The apostles “are thus no more the objects of Christian faith than John the Baptist would be.”[^c03-60] But affirming that an apostle’s or prophet’s words can embody God’s Word, or testify to it reliably in dogmatic judgments, does not require believing that prophets or apostles’ words, _qua_ human words, are objects of our faith. It does not even require believing that _anything_ a prophet or apostle says is infallible or inspired. We only need believe that apostolic and prophetic words have been caused by God such that nothing was written or spoken which was not what God intended for us to believe, when they intended to pass along _as_ God’s Word to others (one could then accept that Paul or Peter could have made mistakes when not intending to teach God’s Word) – and, as nothing God would intend for us to believe is false, that the apostles or prophets’ inspired words cannot be false. Clearly, if we could never accept _any_ human words to represent God’s Word, that rules out the possibility of inspiration in general, and of any possible “special” revelation by God wherein God would require us to believe what He tells us. Whereas Nemes embraces that possibility, Christianity seems foolish if revelation were impossible. As I have pointed out, Nemes makes at least one exception to these claims, treating Jesus’words as representing or embodying God’s Words – which is to say that Jesus alone is a true prophet. But this exception (which Nemes needs to draw to make Christian faith in Jesus possible) is plainly inconsistent with his own principles. If no human can speak God’s Word, Jesus would be included.

If we put aside his arguments that supernatural revelation is in principle impossible (which would rule out _any_ Scripture as inspired), Nemes could take us to have independent grounds to discern whether and to what extent human fallible testimony embodies or represents God’s Word. We would therefore need to appeal to some other source of knowledge of Revelation to test whether the apostles’ words are reliable. It would seem Nemes should reject that each believer is inspired (even

---

<!-- PDF page 128 -->
**[p. 115]**

under certain definite conditions), since then the apostles, being believers too, would also be inspired and infallible. So, it seems the only kind of source to which Nemes could appeal would be the texts of the Old Testament. Imagining these alone are inspired, Nemes could take the Bereans in Acts 17:10–12 as exemplars, so that believers today could appeal to God’s Word as embodied in the Old Testament to discern which parts of the apostles’ testimony are reliable, without accepting that the apostles’ words are inspired instances of God’s Word. This position obviously pushes the problems of dogmatic authority back to Judaism – i.e., the prophets themselves would still need to be inspired, and Jewish testimony to what the prophets said would need to be dogmatically authoritative and infallibly reliable.

Nevertheless, it seems that God would act incoherently by doing this if God wants everyone to have _faith_ in Jesus Christ (as Christians do). There is no text in the Old Testament describing directly Jesus Christ, His life, death, resurrection, or teachings, except in prophetic foretelling. We would have no sufficient normative reason to accept the human testimony of the apostles to Jesus’ Word as itself embodying God’s Word to judge that we ought to accept _in faith_ that God wants us to have faith in Jesus or His Words. God would be straightforwardly acting foolishly or contrary to His own intentions if He commissioned Jesus to send apostles out to ensure that everyone had access to saving truths about Him (and intended that the apostles and prophets were to communicate to the world publicly both in speech and in writing), but then made it possible for these people to completely fail in that task. But the prophets and apostles would clearly fail in their mission if those who heard this message would not, by believing in that word spoken by the apostles or prophets, thereby believe in His Word. Their mission is not to share helpful advice or their personal experiences or opinions about God, but rather to communicate what is necessary for salvation. If, to the contrary, we could accept in faith that God sent those apostles to us, then we can also accept in faith both that God ensured their words would infallibly transmit Jesus or His Words to us and that their teachings to the whole Church (which embody the Gospel God wanted us to embrace) would be instances of God’s Word. I conclude that we have good reason to accept _prima facie_ that at least the apostles could have made an infallible, binding, public determination for Christian believers in the early Church regarding what all ought to believe.

The strongest _de jure_ objections to the Church’s ability to make dogmatic definitions instead rely on the way in which dogmatic definitions in the post-apostolic era would seem to undermine the Church’s mission, i.e., being self-defeating in some way. However, I will note initially that, if we have good _prima facie_ reason to reject that the apostles’ being able to make such judgments would have undermined the Church’s mission, as I have argued we do, it cannot also be true that the power to make public determinations regarding the faith binding on all believers in the post-apostolic era would undermine that mission. In other words, the critique would seem to apply to the apostles themselves. Therefore, we should reject arguments that the post-apostolic Church could not make dogmatic definitions on the grounds that binding public determinations about dogma would _necessarily_ undermine the Church’s mission.[^c03-61]

One species of _de jure_ objection to dogmatic definitions lies in a concern that no dogmatic definition could be free from limitation or fault, as every human attempt

---

<!-- PDF page 129 -->
**[p. 116]**

to testify to or formulate the Gospel message could be improved upon in the future. If this were true, however, it seemingly undermines the infallibility of dogmatic definitions and their authoritative character, since we should reject that any dogmatic definition defined at any point in the past could, in principle, be binding for all future believers. Wolfhart Pannenberg expresses this criticism:

Yet no such summary exhausts the subject matter of scripture as the object of Christian faith. A summary simply characterizes it provisionally. As long as biblical exposition continues, the contours of the subject matter will never be conclusively drawn. Knowledge of it will always be in flux. This applies no less to the more precise determination of the nature of the subject matter of scripture and the Christian faith than to the related issue of the truth of the saving act in God in Jesus of Nazareth to which scripture bears witness. As regards both its content and its truth, dogma, as Karl Barth said, is an ‘eschatological concept.’Only God’s final revelation at the end of history will bring with it final knowledge of the content and truth of the act of God in Jesus of Nazareth. God alone has the competence to speak the final word about God’s work in history.[^c03-62]

Pannenberg underlines that he does not reject the possibility of revelation or dogmatic weight attached to the consensus of the Church. The point is rather that any interpretation of Scripture “will always be preliminary so long as time and history endure, and with them the exposition of the testimony of scripture to God’s historical work in Jesus of Nazareth.”[^c03-63] Pannenberg infers from these facts that a dogmatic definition would be impossible, since any dogmatic definition or creedal summaries of the faith, such as the Nicene Creed, would necessarily need to be revisable at a future point in time, given facts about the failure of human words to adequately express or embody _everything_ that God wants to communicate to us. There could be no conclusive statement that all has been said about Scripture or that any Scriptural interpretation is definitive for all time.

Pannenberg’s objection is not answered in the same way I responded to Nemes and Ortlund, since Pannenberg’s objection does not rely on any principle like Nemes’ claim that no human word can express divine speech. Instead, Pannenberg is objecting that no human word can _perfectly_ or _completely_ express divine speech. That is, no testimony to the Word of God _is_ the Word of God. Such a position is uncontroversial among Christians, does not undermine the possibility of true or reliable testimony to the Word of God, and was a truth that I have already admitted regarding dogmatic authority and its definitions. Nevertheless, a couple of straightforward distinctions dissolve Pannenberg’s worry.

Consider that it is true no human word can embody the Word of God in its entirety, since that Word is identical with God. That does not stop human speech from being identified as embodying God’s Word partially – that is, Scripture contains God’s Words. If Scripture contains God’s Word, however, Scripture cannot be revised or corrected, since God’s Word cannot be revised or corrected. But this does not prevent Scripture from being expanded. Indeed, we know that prophets

---

<!-- PDF page 130 -->
**[p. 117]**

and apostles built on each other’s message, forming the Scriptural canon of revelation as we have it today. Yet, assuming (as Christians do, based on Jesus’ own words) that revelation was completed with the death of the last apostle, canonical Scripture would at that time necessarily contain all that God wants to communicate to us that is necessary for our salvation. Scripture contains the whole of God’s Word as is necessary for us to accept. So, it is also false that, after this revelation being complete, we can add anything further to the canon; it would be binding on believers _not_ to add or accept anything further beyond what was once delivered. These two facts illuminate the senses in which dogmatic definitions are binding on all future believers, without implying that such definitions express exhaustively God’s revelation.

First, definitions are binding insofar as they reliably testify to God’s Word and, by extension, to what is not God’s Word. As I noted already, definitions do not constitute further instances of God’s Word and do not add to revelation. But, since God’s revelation as passed down to us in Scripture is complete, there is an obvious sense in which any definition that reliably and infallibly testifies to God’s Word is binding in just the same way that Scripture is. That is, the dogmatic definition indicates that a given human word is _not_ among the words revealed to us as God’s Word, or that accepting that human word would otherwise contradict God’s Word. If God is not going to add anything further to revelation, then _it is true_ that if a belief is not found in God’s Word in canonical Scripture, or contradicts what God has said there, we cannot believe that this belief is God’s Word. This is just a fact logically derivative from the closure of the canon. In other words, to reject that it is in principle impossible to make a declaration of what ought to be held in faith which would be binding on all Christians, on account of the fact that no human word can completely express God’s Word or prevent God from speaking more, would also _ipso facto_ require rejecting that the canon of Scripture could be closed and that we can justifiably believe that the canon as it stands represents all that is necessary for our salvation. And this is accepted by Christians as a revealed fact about God’s will: given that God wants faith in Jesus Christ’s life, death, and resurrection to be the definitive salvific means for all humanity to achieve union with God, there will be no further revelation after His apostles’ finish proclaiming the Gospel in its entirety, since Jesus is no longer physically among us.

Second, Pannenberg has confused, plausibly, whether a dogmatic definition can be definitively binding upon future believers with whether a dogmatic definition cannot be corrected or supplemented. But there is nothing in principle that prevents a dogmatic definition from being corrected or supplemented by other interpretations, among which are _future definitions_, just as nothing prevents the revelation given through the prophets from being supplemented by Jesus and His apostles’ words. The existence of infallible dogmatic authority and its definitions would not evacuate of meaningful content those obligations laid upon the Church either to study what it ought to proclaim or define, let alone the need to explain or explore what further needs to be said, or even to _correct_ a definition that has already been made. It is clear from Church history that the definitions promulgated by the first Council of Nicaea (or the “council” of Jerusalem) did not make all other councils

---

<!-- PDF page 131 -->
**[p. 118]**

or definitions thereafter useless. Nor were they plausibly intended by their authors to resolve every future doctrinal dispute.

The Church having the authority to promulgate a Creed or make a dogmatic definition which is _per se_ binding on all believers does not ensure that this Creed or definition is, e.g., balanced, contains all the qualifications that might need to be made, or includes a sufficient explanation regarding why the dogmas are necessary as following from Scripture, and so forth. The Church could even have obligations to issue future dogmatic definitions to correct unbalanced past definitions. Infallibility only ensures that they cannot bind us to what is false, not that they will be unable to be supplemented or qualified by future teaching or definitions. Thus, anything short of a logical contradiction is within the possible scope of the corrections to prior doctrinal definitions that might _need_ to be made by later magisterial teaching. (The questions that this process obviously raises will be taken up in the next chapter.)

The Church collectively would make a definition through the same intellectual process that any given believer would use to interpret Scripture, and therefore the Church has the same general epistemic and moral obligations regarding interpreting, confessing, practicing, and maintaining the faith that any individual believer does. The Church can and has, sadly, violated many of these obligations in its history. Dogmatic authority entails _only_ the guidance of the Holy Spirit in ensuring that the Church _as a whole_ cannot fail regarding transmission of the Word. That is to say, the Church cannot end up recognizing corporately some other word as God’s Word or failing to reject dangerous heresies that would lead to that corporate failure in transmitting God’s Word to future generations. Further, there are natural qualifications that not every teaching by any Christian or the pastoral authorities would have this promise of infallibility attached to it, since dogmatic authority rests in the Church collectively and not in any individual. Only those solemn definitions taught _by the whole Church_ would count as so infallible and therefore binding on all: dogmatic definitions, properly speaking. But this leaves a very wide range for pervasive sin (failures to fulfill their duties) within the Christian Church and its members. If we consider only dogmatic authority by itself, then anything that does not pertain to _a dogmatic definition_ or to the _consensus ecclesiae_ would fall within the ambit of potential failure.

A second species of objection points to the fact that the dogmatic authority of the Church’s consensus acting as a rule of faith would be sufficient for each believer to fulfill their duties of the faith; consequently, dogmatic definitions would not only be unnecessary but would be counterproductive. Even if definitions are not technically any sort of sanction, they seem practically to lead to securing agreement among believers by something akin to coercive means. But this would then undermine any evidential value offered by the _consensus ecclesiae_ as a rule of faith, because this _consensus_ was produced artificially by the definitions themselves. Wolfhart Pannenberg raises this kind of objection:

Religious coercion is an attempt to force consensus about the truth of dogma and in this way to establish the truth itself. Consensus, it is thought, can serve as a mark of truth because the universality of truth expresses itself in

---

<!-- PDF page 132 -->
**[p. 119]**

agreement of judgment. That agreement is then to be forcibly achieved by religious coercion. Yet only a consensus that arises free from any coercion can be advanced as a criterion of truth.[^c03-64]

In response to concerns raised by Nemes and Ortlund in the last chapter, I noted that it is incidental to any dogmatic definition’s character as dogmatically authoritative that it comes with an attached penalty (or not), since dogmatic definitions are not the same as nor do they by themselves imply the appropriateness of excommunication or any sanction at all – pastoral authority to impose a sanction is not the same as dogmatic authority. But Pannenberg’s objection is more subtle and not answered by these distinctions. While dogmatic definitions are not merely canonical directives which sanction public statements contradicting what the authority says, it is nevertheless true that definitions would be such that they provide sufficient normative reason for each believer to believe what the definition indicates. But then these definitions would seemingly end up _securing_ the relevant consensus among believers of the Church regarding, for example, the canon or the rejection of Arianism, rather than being an _expression_ of that consensus. But, because they secure consensus rather than express it, the power of the Church to define dogmas would then seemingly undermine the evidential value of the consensus of the Church as a rule of faith.

I take this objection to be more serious in principle than the first, as this one makes clear why the disputes about the ability to determine dogma publicly are really disputes about the borders of the Church: obviously, we would need to determine _who counts_ as relevant “Christian believers” to identify a consensus among them. In effect, dogmatic definitions redefine the “boundaries” of whose consent counts as expressing the consent of the Church. The Marcionites would not have consented to the Old Testament being included in the canon, and Arians would not have consented to their own condemnation; these groups were simply disregarded as epistemically relevant to the _consensus ecclesiae_. Nemes and other skeptics rightly see that, however, if we accept such arguments against the possibility of dogmatic definitions, it therefore follows that dogmatic authority generally – including the dogmatic weight of the _consensus ecclesiae_ – would also need to be abandoned. If nobody can tell where the _consensus_ is, then nobody could, in principle, be culpable for rejecting that consensus. Clearly, any position which _both_ embraces that there we can reliably judge some boundaries to the Church – that is, we can judge that there are some things necessary to being a believer – _and_ that nobody can reliably delineate the boundaries of the Church – that is, nobody can judge that anything would be incompatible with being a believer – is inconsistent.

These critiques need to draw a distinction such that there might be objective facts regarding who is or is not a believer, but that nobody can _infallibly_ or _authoritatively_ declare what those facts are. All can agree that there are potentially those who appear to have faith but might not actually have faith. Nemes and Ortlund also both think it is reasonable to conclude that some people are not Christians (e.g., atheists). They want to deny that anyone can do so because they believe that our judgments about who has faith are ultimately fallible – we can be wrong that

---

<!-- PDF page 133 -->
**[p. 120]**

someone is _culpably_ failing in their duties as a believer so seriously as to lack faith, and thus could be wrong to judge that someone lacks the _virtue_ of faith. The worry seems to be that it is inappropriate for any believer to judge that another believer has failed to fulfill their duties or is not a good believer. But no believer in dogmatic authority needs to deny this. It is important to differentiate public judgments about _who_ has faith from _what_ is required for having faith. Dogmatic authority concerns fundamentally and primarily the ability to make judgments about what God’s Word is; judging whether a given doctrine is heretical is logically distinct from judging whether anyone is a heretic (the latter is at best only sometimes accidentally connected with the former). Dogmatic authority is thus necessary to provide believers with sufficient normative reasons that would ground the appropriateness of judgments about what is necessary for being a Christian, not who has the virtue of faith.

But, if my earlier arguments for dogmatic authority were sound, then we have a clear application here. Let us assume that the Church is the community of all those who have (true) faith in God. The boundaries of the Church are “set” objectively by God in two senses. God causes us to have faith, and so only He picks who has faith, as a matter of fact or descriptively. But it is also true that God _normatively_ set the boundaries: God _wants_ everyone to have faith in Jesus Christ and accept His Word. In making known His desires, God has given every person who hears about God’s desires a sufficient normative reason to have faith in Christ (since everyone has standing duties to believe God). If those people do not do so, despite being sufficiently aware of God’s will, they will sin against God. But dogmatic authority is simply testifying reliably to God’s will. But then dogmatic definitions “set” the boundaries of the Church only in the sense that they declare what is necessary for having faith by testifying to God’s Word, and therefore what He wants us to believe or do. And it is obvious that Christians today need to know some such facts if they want to be saved. If nobody today could know such facts, they could not be culpable for failing to do what God commands in Scripture. But then this is to say nobody needs to believe the Gospel to be saved. And that cannot be true.

So, if God wants us to believe or do something, these facts will _normatively_ set the boundaries of the Church. For instance, if God revealed the canonical Scriptures for our salvation and will not reveal anything further (i.e., this canon is complete and contains all that is necessary for our salvation), then it is incumbent upon those who have faith in God that they accept these books and do not accept other books as God’s Word. This normative fact about God’s desires entails that being a member of the Church _normatively_ requires that we accept the canon. The boundaries of the Church are normatively set so that those who culpably reject the canon, despite knowing God’s will, do have faith in God.

As the Church’s role is simply to reliably testify to God’s will or what God has done, a dogmatic definition is not a judgment as to whether anyone has culpably rejected God at all. It is true that acts of excommunication or anathemas directed against named heretics seem to do this, since a sanction is only appropriate when a public judgment of guilt has been made. But a dogmatic definition does not require a sanction at all to be binding and is not a claim that such-and-such a person failed to possess true faith or failed to fulfill their duties. Dogmatic definitions exemplify

---

<!-- PDF page 134 -->
**[p. 121]**

dogmatic and not pastoral authority. Judgments about what is necessary to believe, i.e., what constitutes God’s Word, do not imply whether anyone (in fact) is at fault for failing to believe them, even though they do obviously _declare_ or _generate_ moral reasons to believe what is indicated. This is not different in principle, however, from Scripture itself, since dogmatic definitions are simply testimony to what Scripture teaches (or, indirectly, to what it does not) and derive their authority from Scripture’s authority as the Word of God.

Every claim in Scripture which indicates a condition of salvation, however, declares and generates moral reasons for anyone who accepts these words as God’s Word to do what is indicated. “He who believes in the Son has everlasting life; and he who does not believe the Son shall not see life” (Jn 3:36) generates reason to believe in the Son. “He who believes and is baptized will be saved; but he who does not believe will be condemned” (Mk 16:16) generates reason to believe and get baptized. “By this you know the Spirit of God: Every spirit that confesses that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh is of God” (1 Jn 4:3) generates reason to believe that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh. The King in the parable saying to those on his left that “inasmuch as you did not do it to one of the least of these, you did not do it to Me” and then noting that “these will go away into everlasting punishment, but the righteous into eternal life” (Matt 25:45–46) generates a moral reason for us to treat the least among us as if they were Jesus Christ Himself. Since God Himself seems to judge that these things are required for our belief or practice, either the Church can testify that God has said this, or we cannot. But to say nobody can testify to what God has said in the passages above with dogmatic authority is simply to say that the Church today cannot preach the Gospel in such a way as to demand anyone’s faith in that Gospel. No Christian should accept that conclusion.

Pannenberg gives us a way to begin to answer his epistemic worries. Elsewhere, he notes that

… an actual church consensus, whether at a given time or over a longer period, cannot serve on its own as an adequate criterion of the truth of a doctrine. The consensus theory of the truth of dogma shares the weakness of a mere consensus theory of truth in general. Consensus can express and denote the universality of truth but it can also express mere conventionality among the members of a group, society, or culture. Thus the earth’s position at the center of the universe was accepted as unimpeachable truth until the early modern era showed this idea to be merely conventional. Similarly, the unity of religion was regarded as an inalienable part of the unity of society by all conflicting religious parties up to the Reformation and the early 17th century, but a later era came to view this idea as merely a conventional conviction.[^c03-65]

Not all those beliefs received by the Church’s consensus infallibly testify to God’s Word. Believers do not have sufficient normative reason to accept _whatever_ is received by Church consensus as God’s Word; some of what they receive, even as a matter of consensus, can be explained simply by sociological facts about beliefs common at a given time. Those beliefs which are normative for the Christian

---

<!-- PDF page 135 -->
**[p. 122]**

Church, to be received as being God’s Word or Gospel, are only that which the community has inherited from its founder, Christ, through the apostles.

In short, since Jesus sent His apostles to be His witnesses, and we have no access to Christ’s Word except by means of these witnesses, faith in what those apostles transmitted is thereby a normative rule of faith for Christians. Clearly, this generates the belief that the apostles’ preaching of that faith constitutes an inspired rule of the faith for all subsequent generations, and thus that their words recorded in the New Testament are to be canonically received as the Word of God. A consensus in the Church regarding matters that have nothing to do with faith or morals, e.g., the position of the earth relative to the rest of the universe, has no dogmatic weight. Historical facts also will be pertinent to expressing a dogmatically weighty consensus. Consensus among Christians in an earlier age regarding what they received from the apostles would have more dogmatic weight the closer in history it is to the apostles themselves.

Vincent of Lérins proposes as a rule that the consensus has dogmatic weight only if it exhibits “universality,” “antiquity,” and “consent.” “Universality” refers to the whole Church’s confession of that truth at present; “antiquity” refers to the belief being confessed as the faith by “holy ancestors and fathers”; “consent” refers to that belief being identified as normative by “all, or at the least of almost all priests and doctors.”[^c03-66] For Vincent, the mere fact that a doctrine constitutes a challenge to a traditional consensus position in the Church gives us _prima facie_ reason to reject it – the key to identify a heresy is its “novelty.” Vincent does not believe in some principle that what is older is always truer, but rather has rightly identified that the historical character of the testimony to the apostles’ words gives us normative reason to take historical witness seriously as dogmatically authoritative. Those who received the faith more immediately from the apostles would be much sounder witnesses to that faith than those who merely read about the apostles’ preaching in Scripture.[^c03-67] Already in the early Church, this awareness that having _apostolic_ faith was a normative rule of faith for Christians began to develop into the doctrine of “apostolic succession.” Irenaeus famously argues that the fact that a given group of bishops were appointed by the apostles themselves, where their successors can be identified as a continuous and unified organizational body, gives us reason to identify consensus among that group of bishops as having dogmatic authority (not possessed by heretical groups that can claim no such succession).[^c03-68]

Historical facts grounding what constitutes a normative “consensus” in their apostolic origin do not by themselves fully resolve the epistemic worries raised by Pannenberg. Nevertheless, they point in the right direction. Historical facts about the transmission of the apostolic message help determine the kinds of consensus that are normative, since those facts represent what the apostles normatively wanted us to accept as conditions of Church membership or faith. What is at stake is whether dogmatic definitions would be able to change the “apostolic constitution” of the Church, that deposit of faith which was received historically from the apostles and cannot be changed. The possibility of a dogmatic definition seems to leave normative features of Christian faith and requirements of being a member of the Church “up to” the dogmatic authority in a way that would be dangerous for the Church’s

---

<!-- PDF page 136 -->
**[p. 123]**

continued mission, because dogmatic definitions by their very character introduce or generate new requirements for Church membership. Ortlund voices the thought:

…democracy is clumsy, but it’s better than tyranny. And for Protestants, it is nothing less than tyranny when churches require belief in indulgences, or the assumption of Mary, or the veneration of icons, or many other points of doctrine that we have no reason to believe are apostolic. … _sola Scriptura_ is so important [because it] involves nothing less than setting the boundaries for what Christianity is. What are the necessary doctrines a Christian is required to accept? Without _sola Scriptura_, the parameters get widened to encircle all kinds of historical accretions.[^c03-69]

My own arguments would entail that, in the act of making a dogmatic definition, the Church _ipso facto_ generates a sufficient normative reason for every Christian to accept the definition as testifying to God’s Word. This indirectly generates a new requirement for being a member of the Church: subscribing to _that_ definition is now normatively necessary for Christian faith. But any such requirements regarding what was necessary for Christian faith were already given by God in Scripture. Consequently, it looks in principle impossible that anyone to add requirements for the Christian faith beyond those requirements God indicates in Scripture. By the same token, these facts seemingly rule out the possibility of dogmatic definitions.

The way to respond to these concerns is, again, to distinguish. It is obvious that nobody except God can add a normative requirement for being a faithful Christian (or, member of the Church), since nobody except God can speak God’s Word. Scripture alone constitutes the rule of faith in that sense, as we are obliged to accept in faith only God’s Word and nothing but God’s Word. And being a faithful Christian _directly_ involves only an obligation to accept in faith what God speaks to us. Nevertheless, in another sense, there is a different sense in which there can be requirements for being a faithful Christian or member of the Church. These requirements arise from those _indirect_ obligations to reject what is not God’s Word. Unlike God’s Word, what is not God’s Word can change over time. A dogmatic definition then only involves a change in Church requirements for membership in what Christians are normatively required to _reject_. These requirements do change over time. There is therefore no general objection to dogmatic definitions from the fact that they imply there are new requirements for Church membership over time.

I conclude that Pannenberg’s worry about the ways that dogmatic definitions might undermine the value of the dogmatic _consensus ecclesiae_ is not plausibly an epistemic worry, despite initial appearances. Instead, the worry is a political worry (“democracy is better than tyranny”) wherein we want to rule out the possible coopting of the Church’s dogmatic definitions by a small number of bad actors whose actions do not, in principle, represent the Church’s apostolic faith. For instance, if some group, such as the bishops, were able to make a dogmatic definition, it looks as if whatever group makes a definition is _necessarily_ a subset of all Christians existing at any given time. The way to resolve this worry is thus to show that the apostolic constitution of the Church can allow the making of dogmatic definitions

---

<!-- PDF page 137 -->
**[p. 124]**

via a normative process that does not put the Church’s mission in danger. The very appeal to consensus by Pannenberg and other Protestants points in just this direction – they take it as obvious that a small number of bad actors could not coopt _everyone_, and therefore seat dogmatic authority in the Church as a whole. But the problem with this view, as Pannenberg himself has noted, is that it is impossible to delineate the Church’s doctrinal bounds simply by appeal to articles of faith held by all Christians, as the very question at stake is: _who counts as an (orthodox) Christian_? So, as I will argue in the next chapter, even Protestants will need to give an account of the way in which the bounds of the Church’s faith can be re-identified over time, amid changing requirements of the Christian faith that arise from novel heresies emerging in the post-apostolic era.

We cannot rule out _a priori_ that the Church needs to exercise its authority against heresy. In fact, the arguments given so far already have aimed to show that every believer, at all future times, would need the Church’s dogmatic authority to persist, because the Church’s testimony is a normative condition for believers to come to Gospel faith. Further, the Church’s consensus on doctrine ordinarily provides a rule of faith, ruling out doctrinal beliefs inconsistent with the Gospel and providing a basis for each believer to accept the essentials of the Gospel. What I will show in the next chapter is thus that the Church’s mission to preach God’s Word – and reject all that is not God’s Word – _requires_ changing requirements of Church membership in response to new non-divine speech. Since the Church has a duty to preach the Gospel until Christ comes again, and new heresies arise that require a corporate response – perhaps because its members do _not_ come to spontaneous agreement and instead consensus among them breaks down – the Church needs to exercise its authority and make public judgments, i.e., doctrinal definitions, that defend and maintain the faith. The Church could not (for instance) define the canon of Scripture and abdicate its dogmatic responsibility for the rest of its life. Heresies are a test of the Church’s corporate faithfulness to God, whether we – the Church – will fulfill the duties God has entrusted to us (1 Cor. 11:19).

We do not need to appeal to the vast consensus against Nemes on his reading of this passage to underline that Jesus’ resurrection would have no effect if nobody knew about it or misapprehended its significance; Jesus’ sacrifice and resurrection would be pointless if the Church failed to preach that Paschal Mystery and therefore prevented it from being an object of faith. There are then clear independent theological reasons that such a passage, even if read in Nemes’ way, would tell in _favor_ of the fact a promise is given to the Church: if Jesus’ sacrifice has perennial value, so that He continues to be the conqueror of death “unto the ages of ages” (Rev 1:18), it cannot be that the Church’s mission failed and the Gospel ceased to be preached.

[^c03-50]: Nemes’ arguments in this regard do not even rule out hierarchy in the order of nondogmatic authority. Nemes invalidly infers that, if each disciple is potentially subject to question or contest in their decisions, then that disciple is potentially subject _by all other disciples_, and thus there should be no power distinctions at all among disciples. But this does not follow: each disciple can be subject to the possibility of questioning or contesting _by at least one other disciple,_ and thus each person subject to another, without it being true that each disciple is subject _to every other_. The same is true considering being subject _in every matter_ to each other disciple. It seems implausible from the New Testament that St. Paul and others intended to rule out any such relevant distinctions among disciples, even if we consider singly the power to impose sanctions, as St. Paul and other disciples themselves impose sanctions on other believers (e.g., Matt 18:17; 1 Cor. 5:5; 2 Thess 3:6, 3:14; 1 Tim. 1:20).

# 4. Development of Doctrine

God from the beginning chose you for salvation through sanctification by the Spirit and belief in the truth, to which He called you by our gospel, for the obtaining of the glory of our Lord Jesus Christ. Therefore, brethren, stand fast and hold the traditions which you were taught, whether by word or our epistle.

(2 Thess. 2:13–15)

Most Christians in history have held that heresy is bad. Heresies are beliefs that appear to be God’s Word but, when accepted, would be incompatible with central truths taught in God’s Word. Believing a heresy, then, would be to believe something is God’s Word which is not only false but incompatible with believing God’s Word. Whatever we might think about the conditions under which someone is culpable for believing a heresy, and the way in which someone would lose the faith in believing a heresy, everyone can agree that it would be bad for the Church if heresies became prevalent. Vincent of Lérins expresses succinctly why:

For if once this license of impious fraud [of heresy masquerading as apostolic doctrine] be admitted [into the Church], I dread to say in how great danger religion will be of being utterly destroyed and annihilated. For if any one part of Catholic truth be given up, another, and another, and another will thenceforward be given up as a matter of course, and the several individual portions having been rejected, what will follow in the end but the rejection of the whole? On the other hand, if what is new begins to be mingled with what is old, foreign with domestic, profane with sacred, the custom will of necessity creep on universally, till at last the Church will have nothing left untampered with, nothing unadulterated, nothing sound, nothing pure; but where formerly there was a sanctuary of chaste and undefiled truth, thenceforward there will be a brothel of impious and base errors. May God’s mercy avert this wickedness from the minds of his servants; be it rather the frenzy of the ungodly.[^c04-1]

If heresies became universally prevalent in the Church, the Gospel message would be lost. And this follows from the very nature of heresy. Heresies are human

DOI: 10.4324/9781003663485-4

---

<!-- PDF page 142 -->
**[p. 129]**

words masquerading as God’s Word, which lead one to deny God’s Word. So, if the world were filled only with heresies, everyone would end up rejecting God’s Word, and the Gospel would be lost.

Nevertheless, short of that catastrophic situation (which no Christian thought could obtain, given that God could not let the Gospel of Jesus Christ become a dead letter), it would still be bad if heresy got _very very_ prevalent. The Protestant Reformation began with Martin Luther protesting widespread abuses or crimes in the Church but then coming to believe that these abuses were the signs of widespread heresy regarding grace and salvation. Luther and the other Reformers believed that the leaders of the “Roman Church” – especially the Pope – were either sanctioning or enjoining heresy, and that this was an intolerable situation which justified creating a new set of church institutions.[^c04-2] These same convictions about the badness of widespread heresy have nevertheless more recently tended to lead converts in the other direction.

John Henry Newman’s _Essay on the Development of Christian Doctrine_ was occasioned by experience that led him to believe that heretical beliefs were promoted or sanctioned by the leadership in the Church of England. He had held up to that point that the appeal to ecclesial consensus as the rule of faith would be fully sufficient for discerning heresy from Scriptural truth (as was a dominant view among his contemporary Anglican theologians). But he arrived at the conclusion that this standard was ultimately insufficient for the Church to maintain the faith in the face of challenges. These considerations led him into the Catholic Church.

My task is different from Newman’s. Everything I have argued for thus far regarding dogmatic authority has shown us that those classical Christians who accept a dogmatic view of faith and reject infallible dogmatic authority are theologically confused. All classical Christians accept that there is _at least one_ meta-scriptural belief that binds believers in faith, such that to reject this belief is sinful. Such a meta-scriptural belief might involve the canon of Scripture, the inspiration of the Old Testament, plenary inspiration and inerrancy of Scripture, the atonement of Christ, Christ’s divinity, or other further orthodox Trinitarian or Christological doctrines, etc. All such beliefs require reasoning from or about Scriptural texts which is not solely a restatement of those texts. Such beliefs would not be binding on other Christians to accept in faith unless they had sufficient normative backing: dogmatic authority. I have argued that Christians cannot both assume that there are such normative meta-scriptural beliefs and deny a need for infallible dogmatic authority – these stand and fall together. But I draw the charitable conclusion that the theological differences among Christian traditions should not be understood to lie in the question of whether there is dogmatic authority vested in the Church. By the same token, I will propose in this chapter an extended abductive argument that, given the purposes and mission of the Church, _everyone_ (whether Protestants, Orthodox, Catholic) needs an account of the interrelation between dogmatic authority, dogmatic definition, and doctrinal development. Meta-scriptural beliefs necessarily _develop_ after the writing of inspired Scripture and require acts by which these beliefs are _defined,_ given the new requirements of faith in different eras.

In the first part of this chapter, I will present and evaluate reasoning that resembles Newman’s own reasons for becoming Catholic. Newman reasoned that

---

<!-- PDF page 143 -->
**[p. 130]**

the Church had corporate duties to maintain the faith and that, without dogmatic definitions, it could not fulfill that duty. I will endorse this argument and lay out the case for dogmatic development, as Newman understood it. The demands of the Christian faith change over time, in keeping with challenges to that faith, and those demands require the exercise of dogmatic authority. In the second part of the chapter, I will explore the subsequent part of Newman’s _Essay,_ which proposed “notes” by which true dogmatic development may be discerned from corruption. I will correct and expand Newman’s reasoning on these points. These notes are often misunderstood to identify the normative conditions under which dogmatic development occurs. I propose that normative constraints on development are fundamentally two-fold, pertaining to content and to process. Newman’s “notes” are insufficient to determine what those conditions are, only providing a list of necessary accidental features of that normative process by which development occurs. I will conclude by responding to objections that theories of development are otiose or problematically circular.

Dogmatic authority is then necessary for identifying and explaining sufficient doctrinal continuity over time, and all Christians ought to have the requisite resources to explain the way such development in doctrine is possible, differentiate legitimate from illegitimate developments, and explain that those legitimate developments are binding on the faithful (when they occur). I will argue that the plausible answers to these questions involve appeal to “epistemic” considerations only as a necessary but insufficient criterion, whereas “political” considerations regarding the constitution and continuity of the Church through history will be jointly necessary and sufficient alongside those epistemic ones. This will set up a hypothesis for the next chapter: that Protestants, Orthodox, and Catholic Christians differ primarily not on dogmatic authority as such but the criteria for identifying the Church over time. That is, controversial issues among Christian’s concern not the fact of dogmatic authority or the fact of dogmatic development, but merely certain features of the process of making dogmatic definitions: e.g., who is empowered to define dogma, under which conditions, etc. And I will then propose that these differences, too, are not as great as they initially might appear and that each Christian group has internal reasons to pursue corporate unity with the rest.

## The Necessity of Definition and Development

Marcion, an early Christian teacher of the 2nd century – with immaculate Christian credentials, being the son of a bishop, possibly himself a bishop,[^c04-3] and an ascetic who belonged at one point to the Church of Rome – ended up promoting the first worldwide Christian heresy. Marcion rejected the Old Testament books as inspired, and he had his own “canon within the canon” of the New Testament books, primarily being a subset of the Pauline letters. He either taught or implied that the God of the Old Testament was not identical with the “Father” of Jesus.[^c04-4] But, putting aside his views of the deity, Marcion’s heresy was concerned with a central issue affecting Christian faith in God’s Word, namely, the composition of the canon itself.

---

<!-- PDF page 144 -->
**[p. 131]**

Marcion’s heresy was not unlike the Arian heresy in many practical respects. Arianism and Mariconism were alike in being _internal_ threats to Christianity established on the basis of Scripture rather than on the basis of an alien philosophical system, as had been arguably the case with those early “gnostic” heresies referred to obliquely in the New Testament (e.g., Col 2:8, 2:2–23; Gal 1:6–8; 2 Jn 1:7–8; 1 Tim 6:20–21; Rev 2:6). Both groups were in many practical ways indistinguishable from other Christians, practicing the faith in similar ways, with similar sacraments, moral beliefs, and leadership. They could also appeal to an apparent consensus which was at least not contrary to their views, since their views were widely shared or tolerated by other Christians – Justin Martyr is the earliest opponent of the Marcionites, and he describes in his _First Apology_ that Marcion found followers of every nation.[^c04-5]

While the heresy spread quickly and Marcionites were still found around two hundred years after Marcion’s death, it was always somewhat contained in its reach and persistence, as the Church corporately had put up an effective resistance even during Maricon’s lifetime, with many bishops recognizing the threat and condemning the view publicly as heretical (beginning with the very synod Marcion had called at Rome condemning his views).[^c04-6] By contrast, Arianism had not called forth an effective unified response immediately, and ended up commanding a wide assent or tolerance among Christians (laity and bishops) for many years, and therefore ended up posing the first unique worldwide threat to Christian belief of its sort. We can imagine a possible scenario where the Marcionite heresy _had_ caught on in the same way as Arianism did: a scenario where there are many Marcionite churches to be found alongside other Christian churches today, that they appear practically to be like other Christian churches, and that among the members of these churches, Marcion’s theology has many able, academically qualified proponents.

Christians often simplify the story of the biblical canon’s formation. It was a messy process and there was no point at which the whole Christian world and every believer in it _spontaneously_ decided to accept certain books. The list was won through long academic controversy, which continued alongside decisions of different church bodies (local councils, synods, etc.) to publicly and officially endorse a given list. These simple historical facts, however, raise serious questions about how to differentiate what _is binding_ from what _happened historically_. First, how can we differentiate the emergence of a _normative_ consensus from mere sociological agreement (or agreement for perverse reasons, such as political coercion or the spread of erroneous beliefs)? Second, there was a time before a canon existed – how could the canon be anything other than an _addition_ to the Christian faith, if there was a time that the canon ever existed? Finally, what _appropriately_ guided the beliefs of Christians before the relevant consensus around the canon emerged?

We can restate these same questions about many other Christian beliefs. Newman had been studying real-world cases of heresy, unlike the imaginary scenario above. We can trace, historically, the way that Christian believers _in fact_ coalesced against a heresy. But the skeptics of his day (as our own) allege that these decisions were largely political rather than controversies about the truth; they allege that the proofs given by the victors against the purported heresy are weak or interspersed

---

<!-- PDF page 145 -->
**[p. 132]**

with falsehoods; and so they conclude that Christians today ought not to accept historical orthodox doctrines such as the Trinity, the virgin birth, an eternal hell, necessity of baptism, the inerrancy of Scripture, and so forth. It is true that historical consensus formed where certain positions were held to be orthodox, and others heretical. But the historical facts alone do not tell us whether “historical orthodoxy” is Christian orthodoxy, whether these consensus conclusions are _binding in faith_ on believers today.

If there were no sufficient normative reasons to distinguish one _process_ by which a doctrinal consensus formed in the Church from another, then all such developmental processes would be equally legitimate. If all parties had “begun” with the apostles, _either_ in terms of inheriting their Scriptures from the apostles or having episcopal succession from them, there would be no way to distinguish any group as less legitimate in principle (as less “apostolic”) than the others. But the idea of _orthodoxy,_ Newman argued (with classical Christianity, whether Protestant, Orthodox, or Catholic), was not solely a human construction. God proposes _definite truths_ for our belief; “… when we turn to the consideration of particular doctrines on which Scripture lays the greatest stress, we shall see that it is absolutely impossible for them to remain in the mere letter of Scripture, if they are to be more than mere words, and to convey a definite idea to the recipient.”[^c04-7] If God intends us to accept definite truths and ideas, such as judgments about ourselves, the world, or Himself,[^c04-8] then we have moral obligations to adhere to _those truths He revealed._ That’s the essential idea of orthodoxy, and it follows straightforwardly from the dogmatic conception of faith presented in the first two chapters.

The Marcionite heresy gives us an uncontroversial illustration of these claims, as even those who are Arians or friendly to a wide-ranging skepticism about anything being a heresy (such as Nemes) can accept that Jesus’ words impose a theological obligation in faith for us to accept His words as God’s Word. Yet, given the facts about _what_ Marcion was saying about the canonical books of Scripture, if we were to accept Marcion’s views as a valid form of Christian belief, this would be to imply that there is nothing about having theological faith that could require us to accept the Old Testament and other New Testament books Marcion rejected were God’s Word. But we have standing normative obligations in faith to accept any speech as God’s Word just because that speech _is_ God’s Word (although we do not incur or become culpable for those obligations until we become relevantly placed to recognize that it is). To accept Marcion’s views as legitimate, in the sense that people of faith have no sufficient normative reason to reject them, would therefore be to accept that there is no definite fact about whether the Old Testament and the other books form part of the canon of Scripture – and this, in turn, implies that the books Marcion rejected _are not_ God’s Word. If these books are God’s Word, by contrast, believers should have sufficient normative reason to accept these books as God’s Word and to reject the Marcionite view, as I have argued. So, really, all we need for us to notice that heresy is bad is that there are _potentially_ some beliefs which logically entail denying central truths of the Christian faith. And even the most tolerant have some such beliefs which they think are central, like belief in the canon.

---

<!-- PDF page 146 -->
**[p. 133]**

Newman concluded in light of these facts that there would need to be _new_ sufficient reasons introduced over time for each believer to reject the _new_ heresies that arise, and thus that it would be insufficient for Christians simply to adhere to “the plain sense of Scripture” or what was “obvious” to each believer from reading Scripture by themselves in abstraction or isolation from what historical and apostolic Christianity had believed and what the Church believed at the present. If faith requires belief in what God reveals, it will be required for each believer that they have grounds for rejecting new heresies each time they arise. And so there will be facts about the consensus among believers, where the Holy Spirit guides the Church in each age to maintain the faith as passed down from the apostles. Newman therefore referred to the phenomenon as “doctrinal development” for good reason.[^c04-9] It can be helpful here to call attention to a distinction between development of _theological science_, which pertains to the opinions common among theologians and in the academic study (which are not binding in faith on any Christian), from development of _doctrine,_ which pertains to the beliefs binding in faith upon Christians. Given that there will be corporate facts about changing obligations of belief, and therefore changing obligations regarding what the Church proposes regarding what beliefs are to be accepted or rejected to maintain transmission of the Gospel in new situations, doctrinal development is required on account of the way that the Church normatively has duties to _maintain_ its testimony to God’s Word within new situations. The Church herself has duties corporately to ensure that only God’s Word is transmitted or testified to as God’s Word and not to transmit or testify to anything incompatible with God’s Word.

What was inherited from the apostles, as found in Scripture, constitutes the core set of beliefs which the Church corporately held at its inception, which remain forever normative. Yet the emergence of new challenges, such as a potential heresy, creates a novel situation where there is no consensus yet established about that heresy, since what specific doctrines _are_ heresies is not itself found in what was historically inherited from the apostles or present in Scripture. Newman identifies that the consensus of the Church undergoes a historical process of evolution, such that the normative consensus of the Church at any given time will begin or be grounded in a prior standing consensus, with roots in the common beliefs of the apostles themselves, but that the process ends with a consensus around beliefs that the apostles themselves would not have always been aware.[^c04-10] This is obvious, as the apostles or prophets could not possibly have anticipated every possible challenge to or misinterpretation of the Gospel message and therefore would have been primarily concerned to set forth the truth of the Gospel as they received it from God, rather than responding to objections or proposing necessary distinctions to prevent misinterpretation (although this occurs in Scripture too, as cited above in regard to “Gnosticism”).

Consequently, a new consensus in the Church would need to be formed that addresses whether a given belief is, in fact, heretical – only then would there be sufficient normative reason for each believer to reject these threats to their faith, and only then could the Church be prevented by the Holy Spirit from losing the Gospel entirely. Newman came to realize that antiquity, consent, and universality

---

<!-- PDF page 147 -->
**[p. 134]**

are necessary features of any normative consensus, as Vincent of Lérins had identified, but that these singly or jointly are not sufficient to identify how the consensus _normatively_ develops from its past state to a future state.[^c04-11] We therefore still need to identify that _normative process_ by which the consensus of the Church formed over time, so as to clarify why heresies did not qualify as permissible interpretations of Scripture which had developed over time, whereas their condemnation did so qualify as “developments” of Scriptural truths taught by the apostles rather than as “corruptions” like the heresies. If there were no _factual_ normative constraints on the processes by which the Church develops new beliefs over time, then heresies would be no more or less legitimate than any other belief.

Before turning to those normative constraints, here is a short summary of the reasoning so far that exercises of dogmatic authority are necessary for fulfilling the Church’s mission:

- 1 The whole Church holds dogmatic authority (from the arguments in the last chapter).

- 2 If the whole Church could exercise that authority only in consensus agreement, there would be no sufficient normative reason for believers to reject a heresy after a heresy arises, as there is at that time no consensus formed among the relevant parties (or the relevant parties have not been normatively differentiated).

- 3 But it is necessary for believers to have a sufficient normative reason to reject heresies whenever they arise, otherwise the Gospel could be lost, and God’s purposes be in vain (according to the same reasoning of the arguments in the last chapter).

- 4 Consequently, there is a way for the Church to generate new sufficient normative reasons to reject a heresy and so to fulfill its mission in preaching the Gospel and maintaining the faith, i.e., there is a means for the Church to _exercise_ its authority and generate new sufficient normative reasons to reject heresies.

- 5 But those normative means cannot be already found in the preceding agreement or consensus among all baptized Christians, since (as 2 notes) there is no such (publicly binding) agreement at the time a heresy arises.

- 6 And there would be no sufficient normative reason for a consensus to develop normatively around the rejection of the heresy, if there were no normative means to develop such a consensus.

- 7 Consequently, there are normative means to exercise dogmatic authority in developing a dogmatic consensus to reject a heresy.

This argument _ipso facto_ counts as an argument in favor of what Newman calls “doctrinal development,” since what the argument proves is not so much that the Church would need to arrive at new understanding of the received deposit of faith in God’s Word – which is a naïve or simplistic understanding of doctrinal development as mere increase in understanding – but more specifically that the Church needs to be able to _generate_ new sufficient normative reasons for believers to reject heresy at different stages in the Church’s life. The generation of these new normative reasons is doctrinal development properly so called, as those who are part of

---

<!-- PDF page 148 -->
**[p. 135]**

the Church will thereafter be normatively required to reject the heresies that the Church rejects, or to accept as normative what the Church does.

We can see why this is the case in considering the rise of Marcionism or Arianism. These doctrinal crises required the Church to be able to generate a new consensus which was binding upon its members. These heresies appear nowhere in Scripture, and indeed appeal to Scripture to reject Marcionism would be circular, since the Marcionites did not accept as normative the same canon of Scripture as the “Great Church” or “Catholic” Christians did. Had the Marcionite heresy caught on universally (assuming that Marcionism is indeed a heresy, and therefore false), God’s purposes would have failed without the Church having the ability to generate new sufficient normative reasons for each believer to reject Marcionism. That is, if the Marcionite heresy persisted in being universally compelling among baptized Christians, each believer would have had insufficient normative reasons from any pre-existing dogmatic consensus alone to accept any canon at all, since there would not be any universal consensus in the Church regarding the interpretation of the Old Testament proposed by Marcion or universal consensus regarding which books should be considered canonical, inspired Scripture. If every believer had no sufficient reason to accept the canon, then God would have acted in vain in proposing His Word for our belief in those books that the Church (in our actual world) recognized as canonical. The same would be true, _mutatis mutandis_, if believers today had no normative reasons to accept that Jesus Christ was God and that Arianism was false. If belief in Jesus Christ’s divinity makes a serious difference for Christian belief – which I have argued that it does – God would have acted in vain by not ensuring that every believer had sufficient normative reason to reject Arianism.

But we might similarly notice that this argument in favor of doctrinal development is, by the same token, an argument in favor of there being sufficient normative reasons for each believer to differentiate a _normative_ doctrinal consensus from non-normative consensus, whether among a group of heretical Christians (who concur in their heretical beliefs) or simple sociological facts of concurrence among orthodox Christians regarding non-binding Christian beliefs (e.g., whether Moses wrote the Pentateuch, whether a given person lived a holy life). If there were no such normative reasons for believers to adhere to the consensus _of the Church,_ there would be no reasons to accept their testimony that a given heresy ought to be rejected or a given canon to be accepted rather than the testimony of any given heretical group. For this reason, Newman’s central insight is outlined at the beginning of the _Essay._ It is there that Newman outlines what he takes to be the sufficient condition for distinguishing that normative consensus from non-normative consensus: “a supreme authority ruling and reconciling individual judgments by a divine right and a recognized wisdom.”[^c04-12] That is, the exercise of that authority itself needs to generate such sufficient normative reasons over time for believers – and, hence, the only logically sufficient condition for differentiating a legitimate “development” of doctrine from a heretical belief or a “corruption” of Christian belief is that developments are sanctioned by exercises of legitimate dogmatic authority and corruptions are not. Theologians can recognize heresy; but theological judgments of individuals are not publicly binding on other Christians in faith. Only the Church would

---

<!-- PDF page 149 -->
**[p. 136]**

have that authority to endorse such judgments and make them dogmatic judgments. Hence, the theory of development involves identifying those normatively appropriate processes by which these judgments were made throughout Church history.

### Dogmatic Definition – Universally Inclusive Exercises of Dogmatic Authority

What Newman implicitly notes in saying that the Church’s dogmatic authority operates as a “supreme authority” is that the Church’s dogmatic authority needs to be both collectively unified and that it needs to function as a supreme or ultimate court of appeal within dogmatic matters. I will show that each of these requirements follows from the facts we have already considered.

There are good reasons for the first of these requirements: God’s Word as spoken to the Church is _one._ There are not multiple distinct or potentially conflicting instances of the Word of God. If each believer is required to accept what is minimally required to accept God’s Word and to reject what is incompatible with it, at least in serious instances, then these normative demands should not be distinct for each believer within the Christian Church. Rather, each believer should be required by God’s Word and its demands to believe the same things. There is “one Lord, one faith, one baptism” (Eph 4:5) and therefore it follows that there is one set of normative demands on all believers to concur in the same beliefs, so that all believers are required by their faith that each “speak the same thing, and that there be no divisions among [them], but that [they] be perfectly joined together in the same mind and in the same judgment” (1 Cor 1:10). These normative requirements that the Church be one in faith, as well as in moral attitudes like love or harmony, arise frequently in the New Testament (e.g., Phil 2:2, 1 Pet 3:8, 2 Cor 13:11, etc.). Since God’s Word is one, faith in God’s Word has one object and this makes one set of demands upon us, even if we are situated differently – that is, some might be obliged to believe more or less than others (as pastors or teachers would be legitimately required by the Church to be more knowledgeable), but the minimal set of beliefs proposed by dogmatic authority should be identical.

Consequently, the above argument indirectly shows us that there need to be sufficient normative reasons for believers to form _one Church,_ that is, a body of believers who normatively concur in rejecting heresies and concurring on fundamental matters of dogmatic belief, such as the canon or creeds. And, while I have presumed that authority is vested in the Church collectively, the exercise of dogmatic authority cannot simply consist in _maintaining consensus_, since at any given time that a heresy begins there is no normative consensus formed against that heresy. For the same reasons, those who form a new normative consensus in response to a doctrinal crisis cannot be identical with the full body of baptized Christians. Neither Marcion nor Arius would condemn themselves.
