But if we think dogmatic authority is necessary to give believers sufficient normative reasons to reject serious heresies, we can therefore conclude that heretical Christians are not legitimately exercising dogmatic authority in proposing their beliefs to the rest of baptized Christians. Thus, Newman’s insight is vindicated: those Christians relevant to forming a new dogmatic consensus cannot simply be identical with

---

<!-- PDF page 150 -->
**[p. 137]**

all baptized Christians whatsoever. Newman seems quite correct in holding that the only logically sufficient mark to differentiate legitimate normative consensus around dogmatic beliefs in the Church from heretical or non-normative beliefs would lie in differentiating the _legitimate exercises of dogmatic authority_ forming that consensus.

But then, if Newman’s argument is sound that doctrinal development (i.e., the exercise of dogmatic authority to form new doctrinal consensus) is required for the fulfillment of the Church’s mission, this same argument leads us to a controversial conclusion: exercises of the Church’s dogmatic authority will require _agents_ who can exercise that authority to form a new normative dogmatic consensus among believers. Only by differentiating legitimate from illegitimate agents who bring about a normative or binding consensus can we distinguish orthodoxy from heresy. But we also need to identify the normative way that they do so, for reasons that are obvious historically. Heretical and schismatic groups in Christian dogmatic history have at times preserved pastoral office in a way that was indistinguishable from the orthodox Church – e.g., Arians and Marcionites seem to have had validly ordained bishops. Among Catholic and Orthodox Christians, worked out in response to the Donatist controversy, there is a clear distinction between being a _validly_ ordained priest or bishop and holding or exercising this office _licitly_ (i.e., legitimately) in communion with the Church universal.[^c04-13] Having “valid” pastoral office is not sufficient to identify a normative consensus or give anyone the ability to exercise dogmatic authority of the Church. Consequently, agreement among a group possessing a pastoral office within the Church (e.g., bishops), or brought about by a group of those persons exercising their pastoral authority, is not by itself sufficient to constitute a new normative dogmatic consensus – what is logically sufficient lies in legitimate agents of the Church’s authority _acting together as agents of the Church,_ that is, in keeping with the legitimate conditions or normative processes by which the Church acts as a _unity_.

Yet, someone might object that a new dogmatic consensus could be formed without any subset of believers being instrumental in forming a new consensus necessarily acting as _agents_ of the whole Church’s dogmatic authority, and thus without appeal to any _exercises_ of the Church’s dogmatic authority except by way of a consensus that emerges organically or naturally from spontaneous agreement among believers. An intuitive way to understand such a process might be that we imagine such a consensus organically arises from good argument or evidence being presented to believers, persuading many to agree that Marcionism or Arianism is contrary to Scriptural witness.

The first thing to notice is that the possibility of spontaneous agreement is in no way contrary to the earlier claims about the necessity of doctrinal development. Spontaneous coalescing against a heresy might represent the way that the Marcionite heresy eventually died out, historically. Nevertheless, spontaneous agreement need not be _normative_ agreement. And we see this in the case of Marcionism’s demise. There are very good reasons to reject that mere arguments against a heresy are _sufficient_ for forming a new normative consensus against that heresy. Clearly, every heretical group can claim exemplary teachers, with qualifications and

---

<!-- PDF page 151 -->
**[p. 138]**

disciples, as well as convincing evidential arguments in favor of its teachings being grounded in Scripture – no heresy could ever be popular without _some_ grounds for appearing convincing to others! Marcion’s arguments were convincing to quite a few people. Similarly, if dogmatic consensus were simply a numbers game, there is no assurance that any heresy could not win more people to its side than the orthodox – as might have occurred at certain points with the Arian heresy. Yet, Christians are clearly not obliged to believe what numerically _more_ others believe, as whether many people accept a given belief is not relevantly related to whether God has proposed that belief in His Word and thus is irrelevant to our duties to believe God’s Word. For that reason, more fundamentally, the normative process by which doctrine develops cannot simply be that a group of teachers manage to convince many Christians, since no teacher individually or corporately _qua_ teacher is legitimately sent by Christ and therefore no teacher is _ipso facto_ qualified to impose a sufficient normative obligation on other Christians to accept their testimony as an infallible transmission of God’s Word.

In the case of Marcion, his condemnation by pastoral authority and the sanctions leveled against the Marcionites to separate them from the Church were instrumental in the process by which Marcion’s views were rejected as representative of mainstream Christianity. If Marcion’s teaching had been accepted by the Church of Rome, or at least left unsanctioned, it is likely that it would have spread significantly. In the case of Marcionism, arguably, the exercise of pastoral authority aimed simply to _maintain_ a consensus. That is, the Church’s pastors used sanctions to maintain a pre-existing Christian practice of reading the Jewish Scriptures (and the other New Testament books that Marcion rejected) in church services and thereby maintaining an underlying consensus regarding the inspiration of these texts. The Church did not define or delimit the canon as part of its response; it simply persisted in its traditional practice and sanctioned any changes to that practice. Yet, this act of maintaining a consensus was itself an exercise of dogmatic authority indirectly or derivatively, since the pastoral authority of the Church thereby identified those practices are normative for all Christians. Thus, even in the case of spontaneous agreement built by genuine consensus or by argument, Newman’s insight is vindicated that _normative_ consensus requires at least the implicit sanction of those legitimate agents of dogmatic authority – in this case, those would plausibly be the bishops or presbyters who maintained the consensus practice of the Church against Marcionite “innovations.”

For my purposes, I will not attempt to vindicate the exercise of pastoral authority or its ability to impose sanctions of any kind. But we can note that this manner of exercising the normal teaching activities employed in maintaining dogmatic consensus is the ordinary way that dogmatic authority is exercised in the Christian Church: activities such as preaching, teaching, and liturgical worship are ways by which Christians corporately passing along the faith to new generations and maintaining its integrity. These activities are in fulfillment of each Christian’s indirect duties to maintain the faith, outlined in the last chapter, which duties are _corporately_ shared among all believers. Duties to maintain the faith are fulfilled in exemplary ways by Christian pastors and theologians (or catechists) but are also

---

<!-- PDF page 152 -->
**[p. 139]**

exercised more mundanely by all its members in, e.g., a Christian parent teaching their child about the faith, or explaining their faith in Christ to an unbeliever. All of these might be thought of as “derivative” exercises of the dogmatic authority inherent in the Church as a whole, since the individual aims to convey or testify directly to God’s Word and indirectly to the beliefs that the Church corporately holds (in normative consensus). Thus, if someone aims to represent God’s Word correctly, they will also indirectly aim to represent the Church’s consensus reception of that Word. But then, while these derivative exercises are not infallible considered singillatim, they do have a participatory share in that infallible consensus of the Church as a whole. For instance, while an individual might make an accidental mistake in what they convey to be the content of God’s Word, they might nevertheless aim to inculcate in others a correct attitude toward God’s Word and thus bring about faith – that is, since any given teacher would only fulfill their indirect duties to maintain the faith if they normatively aim to represent not their own teaching but that of Christ’s Church and the Scripture they receive, then, as long as they pass along to others a proper respect for God’s Word and submissiveness to the Church’s testimony as a whole, the teacher would have maintained what was essential to the faith.

But such ordinary exercises of dogmatic authority are insufficient by themselves to generate new sufficient normative reasons for others to reject heresies, and we see that in terms of the way that maintenance of the faith ordinarily works. The ordinary exercise of dogmatic authority aims simply to maintain a pre-existing normative consensus, and that involves the teacher normatively being disponible to correction by other Christians. But this will be insufficient to deal, first, with cases where the maintenance of a pre-existing consensus cannot be sufficient in all cases to deal with heresy, for reasons that are apparent historically. In the Arian controversy, there was quite widespread and visible disagreement among the Church’s membership (including its pastors) regarding Arius’ orthodoxy, and there was no spontaneous agreement forthcoming. Indeed, the controversy raged for hundreds of years, with progressively more complicated exegetical and theological arguments being developed by Arians and their opponents. Second, the normative expectation that the ordinary exercise of dogmatic authority involves openness to correction by other Christians thereby opens any given believer up to challenge by heretical authorities, including heretical pastors or teachers. It will not do that Christians merely maintain the pre-existing consensus, since the pre-existing doctrinal consensus might give believers no sufficient normative reason either to reject or accept Arianism. The mere “ordinary” exercise of the Church’s dogmatic authority will then be an insufficient means to deal with a doctrinal crisis like that of Arianism.

Newman’s insight therefore gives us reason to affirm that the Church needs to have means to deal with doctrinal crises, and that what is sufficient for bringing about a new normative consensus in the Church is that a decision is made authoritatively, according to a normative process by which the whole Church exercises that dogmatic authority which it possesses. Since dogmatic definitions carry the weight of the Church’s dogmatic authority directly, in the same way that the consensus of the Church carries dogmatic weight, the outcome of these processes

---

<!-- PDF page 153 -->
**[p. 140]**

would therefore be binding on all and infallible for the same reasons that we affirmed the Church’s consensus reception of Scripture or normative consensus was binding on all and infallibly guided by the Spirit. Such “extraordinary” exercises of the dogmatic authority of the Church will constitute what I call a “dogmatic definition,” since these decisions will consist in a public judgment regarding what is to be believed by all Christians (a dogma), and they will be authoritative or solemnly made in virtue of conforming to that normative process for making such decisions.

Finally, these considerations show us that Newman has good reasons to affirm the second requirement that the body of believers, in exercising its dogmatic authority, needs to function as a unified supreme authority. The reason for this is obvious: the Church needs to be differentiated from heretical teachers and therefore needs to _judge_ which teaching does or does not represent that of God’s Word, situated above every potential conflict among individual believers or groups of believers. The fact that a given group of bishops or theologians manages to convince many people to follow their cause – whether Marcionite or otherwise – would not by itself be sufficient to say whether this group was _the Church_, the unique dogmatic authority. It is therefore by identifying legitimate agents of dogmatic authority (and how they are selected and act together) that we can identify the process by which a normative consensus is generated by the Church in response to heresy and _ipso facto_ identify that Church to which every believer has sufficient normative reasons to belong.

We can summarize the reasoning represented above with the following argument that the Church needs to be able to exercise its dogmatic authority by way of dogmatic definition, extending the reasoning of the previous argument (beginning with conclusion 7), and adding the constraints on collectively acting to produce a dogmatic definition:

- 8 There are normative means to exercise dogmatic authority in developing a dogmatic consensus to reject a heresy.

- 9 But if no consensus exists regarding the need to reject a given heresy, merely maintaining a pre-existing dogmatic consensus will fail to give anyone sufficient normative reason to reject the new heresy.

- 10 Consequently, only a normative exercise of dogmatic authority (beyond the maintenance of consensus) would give every believer sufficient normative reason to reject the new heresy.

- 11 And, since the pre-existing dogmatic consensus does not give us reason to reject the heresy, mere “derivative” exercise of dogmatic authority, i.e., reliable testimony to a consensus already achieved, would not give anyone new normative reasons to reject that heresy.

- 12 Consequently, only dogmatic authority properly or non-derivatively exercised would be able to generate new sufficient normative reasons for believers to reject a new heresy – call such an exercise a “dogmatic definition.”

The above argument is fundamentally a specification – within the new constraints occasioned by the rise of heresy – that, for any normative consensus to emerge from the body of believers, there must needs be a way to generate the

---

<!-- PDF page 154 -->
**[p. 141]**

requisite _new_ sufficient normative reasons to address new heresies. So, if you accept the arguments of the last chapter that there needs to be sufficient normative reasons for each believer to reject heresy, then, it will not seem controversial to accept that every believer needs to have sufficient normative reasons to embrace God’s Word or reject what is incompatible with God’s Word, and that there are therefore normative ways a _new_ consensus can be generated. Either one accepts the necessity of sufficient normative reasons for each believer to reject heresies once they arise, or one accepts that no heresy would be serious enough to endanger the integrity of the Christian faith. But the latter view is simply unsustainable.

That is, there are clearly _some_ beliefs which would be sufficient to undermine Christianity, since Christianity has definite doctrinal commitments. Christians are committed to belief in the existence of God, to the atoning death of Jesus Christ and His resurrection, etc. A purported Christianity that accepted no beliefs would be a Christianity in name only. While it is true that there are some who might accept a very “liberal” version of Christianity as a practical commitment or set of commitments to imitate Jesus, and try to eliminate all beliefs from Christianity, I will simply assume – considering the arguments presented in Chapter 1 – that such accounts are not what we are interested in. Rather, I am interested in those forms which hold that divine speech in Scripture imposes moral and epistemic obligations upon believers to accept what God’s Word says. And this is what is sufficient to generate the possibility of serious heresy, and then consequently to generate the need for sufficient normative reasons by which the individual believer can adhere to God’s Word while rejecting all that is incompatible with it. These same obligations apply _mutatis mutandis_ to the corporate body of believers: serious heresy poses a corporate threat to belief and (morally) requires a corporate response. But the only legitimate corporate response, in the face of a dogmatic crisis, is a dogmatic definition. If such a response is to be effective in giving each believer a sufficient normative reason _in faith_ to accept that response, the only decision that could _bind_ us to believe in faith what the community decides is if the community itself is sent by God and participates in His authority when it corporately testifies to God’s Word. Consequently, the community’s decision-making process must participate in dogmatic authority and be guided by the Holy Spirit to result in a definition that could not testify falsely to God’s Word.

### Normative Constraints on Dogmatic Definition

The whole Church being infallible or sufficiently reliable in its corporate consensus is relatively unproblematic for many, as opposed to the controversial view that a small group of bishops in council could bind all Christians for all time to their definitions. What is theoretically controversial about dogmatic definitions lies in the fact that those who engage in producing a dogmatic definition necessarily will only form a subset of all Christians. Yet, since the group of all baptized Christians includes those heretics who might reject a definition, total consensus could not be a necessary condition for a dogmatic definition – otherwise, dogmatic definition will be impossible. By contrast, then, if dogmatic definitions were necessary for

---

<!-- PDF page 155 -->
**[p. 142]**

the life of the Church – as I have argued that they are – then it follows that some subset of Christians can act in such a way that their decisions are publicly binding on all Christians. And, given that exercising the dogmatic authority of the Church non-derivatively will be infallibly guided by the Holy Spirit, such decisions would not be “reversible.”

Many, like Steven Nemes, have rejected that any Christians have such a power in the Church to make decisions that would be in principle irreversible or publicly binding. Others, like Ortlund, might accept that the apostles potentially had such a right but that this authority would have ceased to be operative with the death of the last apostle. Nevertheless, the arguments provided thus far constitute a basis for a further argument that this authority or right to engage in dogmatic definitions cannot have ceased to be operative in the Church. If it had ceased to be operative, the Church’s mission to maintain the faith and reject what is contrary to God’s Word would have failed. While Nemes rejects pastoral authority and the rightful ability of the Church to impose sanctions on other believers, not all Christians agree on this front. The Reformed tradition in particular places high priority on the right of the Church’s pastors to impose sanctions that keep Church practice and confession of the faith in good order.

On the assumption, then, that the Church’s pastors have such authority to impose sanctions, it might be thought that this pastoral authority is sufficient for the Church to maintain dogmatic consensus on essential matters, without invoking the need for the right of the Church to generate dogmatic definitions. Historically, there is an apparent case to be made. As I have noted, the Marcionite heresy never required a dogmatic definition, because various authoritative bishops and local synods sanctioned (i.e., excommunicated) Marcionites almost immediately. No crisis ever developed that would have required the Church as a whole to condemn the Marcionites, since pastoral authority was sufficiently competent to regulate the canon of books read in church services and ensure that no heretical material was included. No need for a dogmatic definition regarding the canon arose. It is true there was no absolute consensus on the canon – but there did not need to be, especially considering agreement on other fundamental dogmatic issues that began to be defined, starting with Nicaea. A ‘fallible’ rule of faith seems sufficient for unity.

However, this historical case breaks down. The story of the canon was much more complex. Arguably, there was at best a consensus to _use_ various “deuterocanonical” books in church services (including the highly contested book of Revelation), even though at least some prominent Fathers (Jerome, Gregory the Great) rejected or raised concerns regarding their inspiration. These Fathers were not calling for those books to cease being used in public worship, nor did they claim that these books contained heretical beliefs, and thus the matter was purely academic and speculative until much later during the Reformation.[^c04-14] Only at the Reformation was it alleged that such books contained heretical beliefs (e.g., prayers offered for the dead in 2 Macc 12:38–45) and that they were inappropriate for use in public worship, as many Reformers thought their use would inappropriately imply that Christians ought to have faith in their word as God’s Word. This generated a

---

<!-- PDF page 156 -->
**[p. 143]**

crisis with a corresponding need for a dogmatic definition regarding the inspiration of these books and other related beliefs, such as permissibility of prayers for the dead – which Catholics believe was given at the ecumenical council of Trent (1545–1563).

There is a general point to be made in this connection: without dogmatic definition, it would have been open to the Reformers to claim that anathemas or excommunications against their views were unjustly imposed, since (they could claim) it was not heretical to reject the deuterocanonical books as uninspired and the doctrines associated with them. They would seemingly have figures in Christian tradition who could support their position, and they could argue that doctrinal corruptions had “crept into” Christian practices that made it appear as if there was a dogmatic consensus around practices such as prayers for the dead – which were, nevertheless, mere sociological facts about consensus, rather than being a product of that normative dogmatic consensus grounded infallibly representing what God’s Word proposed for our belief. Clearly, the maintenance of pastorally supported consensus around the canon began to be insufficient to meet new challenges which argued that the sanctions were _unjust,_ being imposed regarding a matter on which Christians were not justly required by God’s Word to believe.

The authority to sanction (like authority to govern) does not entail that sanctions cannot be imposed unjustly, without due consideration, or be in error regarding whether a sanctioned view is heretical or whether the sanctioned individual holds the heretical view in question. And so, the general point is that a sanction imposed by pastoral authority does not supply the need for dogmatic definition, since no sanction could (by itself) generate a sufficient normative reason for every believer to accept that sanction as representing the binding or normative judgment of the Church universal or collectively. We see this fact historically in the context of the Arian heresy, where, while the priest Arius had been sanctioned immediately by his bishop Alexander, there were widespread and profound disagreements regarding whether that sanction was justified.[^c04-15] Other surrounding bishops received Arius and thought that he had been unfairly punished, and they began to defend his theological position as legitimate, whereas other bishops (including Alexander and his priest-representative Athanasius) needed to defend their sanctions publicly as being justified. Only the sanction of the ecumenical council of Nicaea, in light of what Athanasius argued was a true and proper dogmatic definition binding upon all Christians and against which there was no further appeal,[^c04-16] was sufficient to begin to change the nature of that controversy: then the controversy shifted from whether the local sanctions against Arius were just to whether this council had truly generated a judgment binding upon all Christians or whether a more moderate Arian view was compatible with the teaching of this council.[^c04-17] In short, pastoral authority without dogmatic authority would be insufficient to generate sufficient normative reasons for each believer to reject a given heresy. As long as there was the possibility of _appeal_ to a different council after Nicaea, or thinking that Nicaea did not bind Christian bishops _everywhere_ to reject Arianism (i.e., not in all ecclesial jurisdictions or at all times), the decision of Nicaea would have failed to provide a sufficient normative reason for Christians to reject Arianism.

---

<!-- PDF page 157 -->
**[p. 144]**

Indeed, if – as Nemes or others adverse to sanctions or anathemas think – pastoral authorities had no right to impose sanctions upon believers legitimately or regulate ecclesiastical affairs (such as public worship), the Church would have _more_ grounds for employing dogmatic definitions, since there would be no other recourse in bringing baptized Christians to reject a heresy. Consequently, if there were no legitimate pastoral authority, and if it were true (as I have argued it is) that the Church’s mission would fail and God’s purposes be in vain in the event that a heresy were to become so widespread and convincing as to become indistinguishable from God’s Word, then dogmatic definitions would be strictly necessary as the only means for avoiding disaster.

Conversely, the development of explicit teaching regarding dogmatic authority in the Catholic and Orthodox Churches have overall _decreased_ the need for using pastoral authority to sanction dubious teaching, especially as regards temporal aspects to those sanctions (e.g., removing them from teaching posts or pastoral ministry), as the dogmatic definitions are more easily publicly recognizable, giving teachers and pastors incentives to stay within their bounds, as well as bishops incentives not to sanction anything that is not clearly and explicitly condemned. In other words, dogmatic definitions make the exercise of pastoral authority less arbitrary (furthering “rule of law”) and less necessary (as doctrinal bounds are more comprehensible and consistent). Consequently, if it were true that nobody in the Church had power to sanction another believer, dogmatic definitions would be more necessary to the life of the Church; further, definitions reduce the need for sanctions overall. Those who worry about abuse of pastoral authority in the Church therefore have good reason to accept the possibility of dogmatic definitions.

In light of Ortlund’s hints (cited in the last chapter) regarding a tension between “tyranny” and “democracy” in the Church, I want to suggest that what underlies many of the fears about dogmatic definitions might be that the subset of believers capable of generating such a definition would seem to necessitate “tyranny” – where such a subset of authorities would be unaccountable to the rest of the Church or to God’s Word, and thus would be able to publicly bind the Church to error. But we can distinguish two quite different species of worries implicit in such concerns. One of these is political, regarding whether those who engage in making dogmatic definitions would end up unaccountable to the vast body of Christians or with those Christians unable to exercise recourse against the subset empowered to make definitions, and the other is epistemic, regarding whether dogmatic definitions would “float free” from God’s Word and from normative consensus within the Church’s history.

The easier of the two worries is the political worry. These political worries are seemingly based on unnecessary assumptions about the nature of dogmatic authority. For instance, Nemes and Ortlund had implied that a certain subset of believers would need to have a _standing right_ not to be challenged or to infallibility. That is, it would never be the case that one could challenge this subset of believers’ decisions, or their sanctions, such that this group could make definitions under any circumstances, as they saw fit. But this is no requirement of the ability to make dogmatic definitions – the Church could delegate such persons the right

---

<!-- PDF page 158 -->
**[p. 145]**

only in appropriate circumstances, within a limited remit to act collectively, such that their actions would not otherwise be immune to critique or question. Similarly, Nemes and Ortlund might think that those believers who make dogmatic definitions would have divine rights to act in the name of the Church collectively, without any recourse or mechanism by which other believers had selected them as representatives of the whole Church. But this too is unnecessary for the ability to make dogmatic definitions. It could be the case that other believers appropriately select this subset who can make dogmatic definitions, according to a representative model of group decision-making. That is, as in a representative democracy, believers might have the right to elect or otherwise normatively select those persons who would represent them in making a dogmatic definition.

For this reason, I propose the following line of reasoning in favor of the Church’s right to make dogmatic definitions, showing that we can hypothetically incorporate assumptions of the above sort:

- 13 Only dogmatic authority properly or non-derivatively exercised would be able to generate new sufficient normative reasons for believers to reject a new heresy – call such an exercise a “dogmatic definition.”

- 14 Those means for making a dogmatic definition cannot require achieving consensus agreement among the community of all baptized Christians, since heretics are themselves among that group.

- 15 Consequently, there is some subset of Christians who are sufficient for engaging in making a dogmatic definition and forming the new consensus normatively.

- 16 But dogmatic authority is vested collectively in the Church, not in any individual or group of individual baptized Christians.

- 17 Thus, this subset could only make a dogmatic definition if they acted collectively in the name of the Church as a whole.

- 18 If a group of Christians possesses the right to act in the name of the Church collectively, this could only be in virtue of the fact that this group has such a right by divine authority or because baptized Christians had a right to confer or devolve an ability to act collectively in the name of the Church to this group.

- 19 There is no baptized Christian who, after the apostles, possesses the divine right to act in the name of the Church collectively (assumption).

- 20 And, if baptized Christians lacked such a right to confer or devolve a right for some subset of Christians to act collectively in the name of the Church as a whole, dogmatic definitions would be impossible.

- 21 But, if dogmatic definitions were impossible, there would be no way for the Church to generate new sufficient normative reasons for every believer to reject serious heresies, and this would entail that the Church’s mission could fail (as outlined in the other arguments).

- 22 Consequently, baptized Christians have the right to confer or devolve a right for a subset of Christians to act collectively in the name of the Church as a whole.

I take it that, as a general matter of political fact regarding representation in a given group, acting collectively in the name of the group as a whole will require

---

<!-- PDF page 159 -->
**[p. 146]**

[1] being appropriately selected to represent the group and [2] being appropriately engaged in those processes by which the decision of these representatives is normatively representative or binding upon the group, i.e., constitutes the decision as a _group_ decision. By the same token, that subset of Christians who can make a dogmatic definition would be able to do so only if they act in the name of the Church as a whole, i.e., as _agents_ of that authority, and according to a normative _process_ by which their decisions represent the whole Church. Consequently, if there is a necessity for dogmatic development and for dogmatic definitions, as I have argued there is, then we can conclude that there is a corresponding power in the Church to do so. Since it could not be a condition of legitimacy that all the baptized will participate or consent, as the Church would then be “held hostage” by the very heretical Christian groups against whose views the Church wants to exercise its dogmatic authority, we can therefore conclude that baptized Christians being able to select representatives who have the right to legitimately act for all, i.e., legitimately appointed representatives of the whole body of faithful believers (the “Church universal”). Rejecting the above argument would then seem to require one to commit to a view like that of Nemes, where there are no dogmatic constraints on faith regarding the canon or any heresy. But that looks very unsustainable for anyone who thinks it is necessary for Christians to have sufficient normative reason to rule out serious heresies such as Marcionism.

Nevertheless, there is nothing in the above argument regarding the necessity of dogmatic definitions, or what would be involved in collective decision-making by the Church, that requires such definitions to involve a “tyrannical” or unjust political process. We can instead intuitively hold that there is a normative constraint on dogmatic definitions such that they cannot be made validly or legitimately without abiding by requisite conditions of “procedural justice” necessary for truly collectively representative ecclesial decision-making. Determining the conditions for being such a legitimate representative of the Church is a key element in determining how dogmatic definitions are made, so that considerations about the nature of Christian pastoral offices and their relation to dogmatic authority come into play. A theory of what is required for someone to represent the Church legitimately will likely appeal to those offices. Nevertheless, we do not need to take a position on whether Christians should be committed to a particular view of the pastoral office to accept the above argument.

By contrast, the epistemic worries about dogmatic definition are more complicated, because they touch indirectly upon “necessary accidents” that follows upon the possibility of doctrinal development: doctrinal developments, embodied in definitions, will not necessarily be explicitly found in Scripture (as they address heresies that arise subsequent to its completion), require exposition of assumptions or interpretations of Scripture which are contentious and which go beyond what is explicitly stated in the text, rely upon other definitions to form a coherent and consistent whole as well as for their internal intelligibility, and result in the creation of binding requirements to believe a dogma with theological faith, despite that dogma not being explicitly or universally held by Christians in any prior age of Christian tradition.

---

<!-- PDF page 160 -->
**[p. 147]**

All of these “accidental” characteristics accompany the doctrinal development surrounding the canon of Scripture. There is no statement of the canon of Scripture within the text of Scripture, for obvious reasons. The criteria for the canonicity of books involve extrapolation regarding the nature, purposes, and recognizability of inspiration, given examples in or analogical reasoning from Scripture. Further, the dogmatic reasoning which was important in the development of the canon of Scripture, such as those arguments made by Origen or Tertullian against the Marcionites, relies on other dogmatic facts which are defined or taught elsewhere, such as the need for all of Scripture to be inspired (in all its parts) in order for us to have access to truths we are obliged to believe for our salvation, or the fact that God would have acted inconsistently in becoming incarnate in Jesus Christ among the Jewish people (if He was not the God of the Jews), that the practice of the Church in reading the Old Testament Scriptures at church services was of dogmatic weight, expressing a normative dogmatic consensus, and so forth. These arguments are not independent of the already-received interpretations of Scripture then commonly held among Christians in the 2nd century, that is, these reasons were reliant upon a normative dogmatic consensus of beliefs that formed a background for the polemic against Marcion.

Finally, after a certain point in history, it would become morally problematic for Christians to fail to adhere to the canon as outlined by the Church’s consensus – whereas, prior to that point, there was no strict requirement (for instance) that Christians reject as inspired those books we today consider extra-canonical nor to accept every book as canonical which we currently do. For instance, many in the early Church accepted as inspired the _Shepherd of Hermas_ or letters of Pope St. Clement, and rejected Revelation as canonical, whereas Christians today would be under obligations _not_ to embrace such views regarding the canonical Scriptures. Those past Christians committed no fault in doing so, however, since they lived before the formation of a definitive dogmatic consensus regarding the canon. These illustrate the way in which the normative requirements of Christian belief can “develop” over time, thereby involving different requirements on believers in different epistemic situations.

Yet, we might notice that the nature of these obligations is not so different from those outlined generally as constraints imposed by theological faith in Chapter 1. Faith is a virtue by which we are properly disposed to respond to God within our given epistemic situation. Faith therefore is not defined in terms of believing a given set of propositions as much as it is in terms of the necessary object of faith: God’s Word. Consequently, as with faith in general, so too Christian faith will involve different obligations for a response to God’s Word depending upon our epistemic situation in regard to that Word and depending upon the evolution of those facts regarding God’s Word spoken in the world. Prior to God’s Word becoming incarnate, it was not necessary for believers to know about the person of Jesus Christ but only God’s promises or His providence regarding our salvation. Similarly, living subsequent to the resolution of a doctrinal crisis can lead to new epistemic and objective situations which require (correspondingly) that believers take up in faith toward God’s Word that were not present in earlier ages. Christian

---

<!-- PDF page 161 -->
**[p. 148]**

belief prior to Marcionism or Arianism involves different normative requirements than after those heresies. After those heresies arise, it becomes necessary for believers to know that such beliefs are heresies and must be rejected. The _homoousios_ of Nicaea was just such an “innovation” in the faith and in the requirements on belief, being the object of a dogmatic definition.

Nevertheless, not just anything at all can constitute a legitimate “development” of Christian belief. In general, it is obvious that no doctrinal development is a legitimate one which does not preserve the _content_ of what was once and for all transmitted in Revelation. Initially, then, those normative constraints on the processes by which the Church arrives at new beliefs look no different in principle from the normative constraints which arise from biblical hermeneutics engaged in by the individual believer, since the Church herself arrives at these new beliefs on the basis of what it has inherited from the apostolic deposit contained in Scripture. But this cannot be the whole story, for two reasons.

First, the Church is engaged not only in interpretation of Scripture but also in the task of handing on or instructing others in God’s Word, both in missionary and catechetical contexts. There are distinct norms involved in teaching the Word of God that are not identical with offering good interpretations as such; for instance, the Church’s mission does not so much require offering an exposition or commentary on Scripture as much as ensuring _basic doctrine_ is transmitted, alongside Scripture, and thus ensuring all believers are aware of what is minimally required for their salvation. The Church can leave many theological questions unanswered and permit a very wide range of differing theological beliefs or practices, potentially, while maintaining the integrity of its mission. Second, as we have already seen, a facet of the Church’s corporate duty involves maintaining the faith, with the Church ensuring by its dogmatic authority, whether by consensus or definition, that the faithful have sufficient normative reasons to maintain their faith especially in the face of heretical challenges. But then the Church’s doctrinal development is not constrained only by the content of Scripture and norms for good hermeneutics, or for good handing on of the faith, but also by the historical facts regarding the dogmatic authoritative consensus and any facts about its own binding or dogmatic interpretations.

In the background of my account is the belief that Scripture is the sole normative source of Revelation among Christian believers, being “materially sufficient” for all doctrine.[^c04-18] God’s Word, as binding upon Christian believers, is found in Scripture and not outside of Scripture. “Tradition and Scripture are not independent sources which complete one another as two separable parts of a whole.”[^c04-19] In the dogmatic context, I follow Yves Congar in taking “Tradition” to refer not to independent sources of Revelation, but only to the normative dogmatic consensus of the Church and other normative facts surrounding the development of that consensus over time – again, following Congar, “Tradition” need refers only to the transmission of the deposit of faith in the Church, the true or orthodox interpretation of Scripture.[^c04-20] This sense of “Tradition” refers, then, to the corporate normative beliefs of the undivided, orthodox, and apostolic Church regarding their normative transmission of the Word of God. For the same reasons that our contemporary

---

<!-- PDF page 162 -->
**[p. 149]**

consensus is normative and binding, the past consensus of the Church imposes obligations upon us today to be sensitive and faithful to what they held as normative and binding. If there is anything like “oral tradition” or apostolic practices that were passed down liturgically, these do not constitute an independent source of Revelation, but only testify to the Tradition of the Church – to its interpretation of Scripture. Consequently, whatever practices contemporary Christians have inherited from the early Church and even perhaps the apostles themselves (e.g., the sign of the Cross or Eucharistic liturgical forms) will be endowed with some very good reasons to preserve these practices, and as exemplifying the manner of their belief, but by themselves will fail to constitute or embody independent sources of Revelation alongside Scripture; these constitute monuments or testimony to the dogmatic consensus around Scriptural interpretation which is Tradition.[^c04-21]

For instance, the “Church Fathers” were exemplary theologians who were instrumental in developing the Church’s doctrine and in proposing the explanations of orthodox doctrine which came to be codified in its earliest dogmatic definitions, especially as these teachers or pastors exemplified the orthodox reasoning for rejecting heresies of various stripes.[^c04-22] Their theological opinions are not infallible _singillatim_ but have dogmatic weight in terms of the way that they contributed to the formation of that normative consensus within the Church and as exemplifying the rationale for its binding dogmatic decisions. Consequently, the Fathers’ writings constitute a deposit of theological reasoning that is corporately recognized by the Church as exemplifying its own pattern of Scriptural interpretation, and their reflection binds the Church to the extent that it is implicated or represented in its consensus/definitions. Writings from the early Church similarly have dogmatic weight for us today, as they witness to the normative ecclesial consensus around interpreting Scripture which had existed from its earliest moments. We reject the early Church’s witness or that of the Fathers at our own peril, as we could end up rejecting what the Church normatively inherited and preserved from the apostles.

In terms of formal epistemic requirements by which we can judge legitimate dogmatic developments, there are relatively few. Clearly, a legitimate development cannot be inconsistent with prior dogmatic definitions or matters of normative consensus. But that is not much to go by, since such developments might not have been explicit at any point in a prior doctrinal consensus, and they are not explicitly to be found in Scripture. Dogmatic developments are by their very nature essentially meta-Scriptural: testifying to how we ought to _interpret_ Scripture appropriately or regarding what _constitutes_ Scripture. And being a legitimate doctrinal development is essentially to be a “truth-preserving” development. But definitions are not truth-preserving in the same way that interpretations of Scripture are; doctrinal definitions do not aim to provide _the best_ interpretation of any Scriptural text in particular. They are directed against heresies and impermissible interpretations, usually aiming at proposing something to be believed incidentally, as proposing what is _necessary_ for believers to hold so that they reject the heresy. Indeed, there is no reason to hold that everything proposed in the context of a definition is infallible beyond the strict object of that definition, any more than everything which is a common belief among Church members at any given point in time constitutes

---

<!-- PDF page 163 -->
**[p. 150]**

a _normative_ or binding belief. So, for instance, there might be falsehoods in the Church’s explanation of a dogmatic decision without undermining the infallibility of what it defines, since the explanation does not fall under the scope of what it is defining.

For these reasons, definitions can be thought of as constrained primarily by consistency with Scripture and prior definitions, and as aiming to rule out what is _false,_ rather than as proposing true or fully accurate interpretations of Scripture. Considering these facts, we can identify criteria for legitimate developments in terms of consistency on the side of the definition with prior definitions and Scripture as well as having criteria on the side of what is condemned regarding the latter’s inconsistency with Scriptural truths. There will be good reasons to see the definition as consistent with Scripture and the heresy as not so consistent. But, as merely formal criteria, these formal constraints are not by themselves very helpful, as constraints on consistency are not by themselves sufficient to show that a given purported development involves a _true_ interpretation of Scripture. The difficulty is that definitions and development occur in moments of doctrinal crisis, when there are many potentially legitimate interpretations of Scripture available. There is necessarily an epistemic indeterminacy around whether a given doctrinal development is legitimate that cannot readily be ascertained except in hindsight, and even then, the hindsight is largely shaped by the resulting dogmatic consensus which comes to prevail among believers.

What I will do to resolve these epistemic worries is to accept the inevitable epistemic circularities involved in theological faith. Cases of “epistemic circularity” have been explored by philosophers especially as regards “track record” arguments for the reliability of sense perception, memory, and other faculties. For instance, if we want to show our sense perception is reliable, we can appeal to the fact that we have formed reliably true beliefs over time through a given sense faculty. By building up a “track record” of successes, we build an inductive case in favor of the conclusion that our senses are reliable sources of information.[^c04-23] This circularity can be non-vicious _if_ we reject an assumption that we need to know or have justified belief that sensation is reliable before accepting the premises of these track record arguments. “Externalists” in epistemology reject those kinds of requirements, as such requirements end up in global skepticism – as Thomas Reid famously argued, there are no good ways we could justify our beliefs independent of _any_ faculty we have, since all of our epistemic means to access reality are through our faculties and so, if we accepted we needed justification independent of all our faculties, we would be stuck in skepticism.[^c04-24] If we work from such an externalist model (as I have already argued we should), our beliefs can be reliable if they proceed from a reliable source, whether or not we ourselves are aware of that reliability. What we instead need to be able to do is to discriminate a reliable from an unreliable source in the appropriate circumstances. For instance, we need to be sensitive to those changes in the environment that would make our sight unreliable, such as entering a carnival funhouse or recognizing that my sight is unreliable at very long distances away.

In the same way, I propose we should reject the requirement that there be epistemic criteria sufficient for discerning or explicating a development which are _fully_

---

<!-- PDF page 164 -->
**[p. 151]**

_independent_ of the dogmatic consensus itself. Most obviously, we cannot argue for or against any development without appealing to Scripture – to do so involves an implicit assumption that the dogmatic consensus is reliable regarding which books are canonical, whether those books are fully inspired in all parts, and so on. There are no fully independent rational demonstrations of the truth of the faith, such as would (for instance) prove some given texts to be inspired Scripture, and consequently, no grounds independent of appeal to theological authority to establish the truth of any given dogma. All doctrinal truths held on faith are necessarily of such a character, and, for the same reason, no dogma could be demonstrated to be a development rather than a corruption independently of appeal to theological authorities, whether the dogmatic consensus or Scripture or other definitions. Epistemic criteria then provide merely necessary but individually insufficient criteria for determining dogmatic developments. This follows from the arguments outlined in the last chapter. Doctrinal developments involve _meta-scriptural_ beliefs, in all cases. Consequently, they cannot be binding on all Christians unless they are endorsed by dogmatic authority. A belief’s status as logically congruent with or an entailment of other beliefs does not make it an object of theological faith, if the reasoning by which it is identified as such remains merely human. Only when the belief is endorsed by dogmatic authority does it become binding on other Christians to accept in faith.

The only sufficient criterion of dogmatic development is then that the Church herself – the one that Jesus founded – holds the doctrine in question as normative. Absent the Church’s dogmatic authority, we would have no sufficient normative reason to think that this doctrine is _binding_ upon all believers. This epistemic circularity is non-problematic if we have ways to distinguish Scripture, the true Church, and its normative dogmatic consensus from that of heretics. And, I think, most Christians will readily grant that there are sufficient normative reasons open to every believer to identify Scripture as God’s Word, in contrast to other collections of writings that are uninspired. So too, since the Church is the infallible testifier to God’s Word, as receiving and recognizing that Word canonically, the sufficient normative reasons to identify Scripture are bound up logically and epistemically with the reasons to identify the true Church or the normative dogmatic consensus among its members. That is, each believer can recognize the true Scriptures, the true Church authority, and the normative dogmatic consensus (Tradition) of that Church implicitly in the same epistemic act, given the logical connections among these beliefs. Reasons in favor of one are reasons in favor of the others.

While it is therefore true that there are no ways to explicate the continuity of a development in terms that are fully independent of appeal to dogmatic consensus, we can nevertheless aim to identify at least _partially_ independent criteria for discerning legitimate from illegitimate dogmatic developments, in a way analogous to that “track record” arguments provide support for the reliability of our senses or memory. By appeal to both the epistemic and political formal criteria of developments, we can identify accidental characteristics of the _process of doctrinal development itself_ which are independent of the content of the definition as such (i.e., independent of arguments that the heretical interpretation of Scripture is, in fact,

---

<!-- PDF page 165 -->
**[p. 152]**

heretical), but which would accidentally indicate that a definition was legitimate in principle, rather than a corruption. We can then give an inductive case, akin to inference to the best explanation, where we hypothetically assume that dogmatic authority exists in the Church, that there is an identifiable normative dogmatic consensus along with the potential to exercise its authority in dogmatic definitions, and then aim to identify which historical events best represent the consistent application of that authority.

While we can obviously appeal to Scriptural data (or other doctrinally weighty sources) that are relatively uncontroversial within a dialectic context and aim to establish arguments that a given development preserves the true sense of Scripture, there is a problem with such approaches: scriptural interpretations are usually precisely what is at issue in any given doctrinal crisis. Every heresy has apparently strong reasons to accept an interpretation of Scripture and to reject the orthodox consensus as corruption; disputes can even involve whether a given text is Scripture. If there is a fundamental disagreement regarding which consensus is normative, discerning which Church or consensus is normative constitutes a serious foundational disagreement that cannot be resolved by appeal to Scriptural data alone, for reasons similar to those preventing us from determining the canon from appeal to the content of Scripture: “How can the Gospel be the measure of dogma if our access to Gospel content is dogma?”[^c04-25]

By contrast, the inductive case would not demonstrate the truth of purported definitions or that they _are_ developments. Instead, each definition apparently consistent with a previous definition, and building on or extending the same principles, as brought about through an apparently legitimate political process, represents an _inductive confirmation_ of the hypothesis regarding the necessity of dogmatic development in the Church’s historical life. While any purported dogmatic definition would be controversial and open to dispute as consonant with Scripture, we have greater antecedent reasons to identify the true development of doctrine with those decisions by a set of similar processes (claiming to represent/instantiate the dogmatic authority of the Church) that, over time, corporately preserve basic principles throughout their development and form a coherent, plausible whole.

While epistemically circular, such an argument is not viciously so; as noted by Alston, such an inductive case has explanatory power to the extent that we accept the hypothesis. What makes this inductive case stronger is that I have argued that the hypothesis should be held by _all_ Christians, not just Catholics or Orthodox. The hypothesis that there is identifiable legitimate development of doctrine is a consequence of our Scriptural belief that God will not abandon His Church – if there will continue to be sufficient normative reasons for each believer to assent to revelation, given God’s desires that the Gospel continue to be preached in every age, then there will be identifiable and coherent developments proposed by legitimate dogmatic authority as responses to heretical challenges and doctrinal crises. Since I have already argued in favor of such formal criteria, and in favor of the view that the hypothesis ought to be held by all Christians, I have set up a dialectical context in which a “track record” argument can be given to distinguish doctrinal developments from corruptions. We can identify developments historically by appealing

---

<!-- PDF page 166 -->
**[p. 153]**

to their accidental characteristics, and thereby lend credence to the hypothesis by giving it inductive explanatory power. If each of the definitions has a good case to be at least consistent with Scripture, and mutually consistent with each other, and these definitions “proceed” in the same or roughly analogous manner, then we would have an accidental set of marks or prima facie case for identifying the resulting body of doctrine as legitimate developments of the Scriptural truths from which they arise.

### Newman’s “Notes” or “Tests” of Legitimate Doctrinal Development

J.H. Newman famously outlined a list of seven “tests” (which he later referred to as “notes”) for distinguishing corruption from legitimate doctrinal development, which he summarizes as follows: “There is no corruption if it retains one and the same type, the same principles, the same organization; if its beginnings anticipate its subsequent phases, and its later phenomena protect and subserve its earlier; if it has a power of assimilation and revival, and a vigorous action from first to last.”[^c04-26] Newman’s historical project in the _Essay on Development_ is an example of the inductive confirmation model that I have proposed above. Newman identified what he took to be necessary formal features of dogmatic development – the “notes” – and then inductively confirmed that they apply to that faith which he took to be normative. Many overlook that Newman had assumed the hypothesis that dogmatic development was necessary and that it would involve identifiable processes by which dogmatic definitions were made. Newman’s point, then, was not that the “notes” were logically sufficient to differentiate a development from a corruption, nor that developments could be predicted by means of these notes, but rather that the hypothesis of development could be inductively confirmed regarding ecumenical dogma.

This historical argument supported – for Newman – the Catholic Church’s claims to being a legitimate dogmatic development of Scripture and the ancient dogmatic consensus present in the early Church. Specifically, Newman argued [1] that the Catholic Church alone could make a rightful claim to be the legitimate institutional successor to the ancient Church, which understood itself to be exercising dogmatic authority while proposing definite demands for belief, [2] that the resulting body of Catholic dogma could be intelligibly explained as an as a “development” of principles found within earlier consensus positions within the Christian Church, [3] the body of dogma resulting from those dogmatic definitions formed an integral and consistent whole which continued to be mutually logically exclusive with all the early heresies it anathematized alongside all its later dogmatic definitions, [4] there was a logical sequence such that later developments that were logically dependent upon the earlier (and therefore arose later), and [5] that what was later dogmatically defined was “anticipated” in previous ages, even if it was never a universal consensus position at that time. These first five notes pertain to the epistemological or logical content of dogma, and Newman spends the bulk of his _Essay_ providing historical analyses of the way that these notes have been exemplified in church history.

---

<!-- PDF page 167 -->
**[p. 154]**

The other two notes depend upon an implicit hypothesis that heretical bodies are less likely over time to maintain a Christian faith – “a corruption is that which tends to its destruction”[^c04-27] – and thus that corrupt groups will be intrinsically disposed progressively to abandon central principles of Christian faith. That is, as a heresy involves a contradiction with Revelation, heretics will progressively reject more of Revelation to make their views consistent. As interesting as Newman’s theory of heresy is, there are nevertheless logical difficulties in applying these last two notes. First, the sixth and seventh notes rely on hypotheses as to the accidentally necessary attributes of corruption, not development, and thus do not really help us identify developments as such, except by exclusion. Second, these notes subtly shift the topic from notes to distinguish _doctrinal developments from corruptions_ to notes by which we can distinguish _the orthodox Church from heretical sects_, since these notes pertain more properly to group dynamics over time. Finally, and for similar reasons, Newman’s hypotheses about the dynamics of heretical groups are subject to countervailing factors that Newman himself recognized: heretical groups can persist for long periods of time, or maintain apparent life, due to _extrinsic_ factors that “mask” their intrinsic tendencies to dissolution. For instance, Newman argued that Anglican divines maintained the faith only by political factors that were not integral to their principles – “… it was simply their position, their temporal possessions, their civil dignities, as standing round a King’s throne, or seated in his great council, and not their principles, which made them what they were. Their genius, learning, faith, whatever it was, would have had no power to stand by themselves; these qualities had no substance, for, as we see, when the State abandoned them, they shrank at once and collapsed, and ceased to be.”[^c04-28] He likewise explained the persistence of Oriental Orthodoxy and paganism as due to political factors.[^c04-29]

I do not take Newman’s hypotheses about heresy to be unreasonable or lacking in usefulness altogether, but the logical problem for employing them to confirm the hypotheses about dogmatic development is that the sixth and seventh notes do not identify _necessary_ accidents co-extensive with all cases of heresy. Since some heretical groups can persist despite an intrinsic tendency to dissolution, to identify the relevant group dynamics as necessarily co-extensive with every heresy properly so called, we would need to identify a heresy _as_ heresy (dogmatic corruption) to identify that tendency. The transitoriness of a heretical group is not always evident, and, for the same reason, it is difficult to identify the vigor of the orthodox _as_ due to properly dogmatic rather than political factors. This is not to say that Newman’s extrapolations about group dynamics are false, or that we could not employ these hypotheses about heresy in other contexts, but merely to say that it is difficult to see how these theories could function within the inductive confirmation of Newman’s initial historical hypotheses about dogmatic developments. Newman’s sixth and seventh notes at least involve many ancillary hypotheses about heresies, orthodoxy, and history that seemingly undermine their epistemic usefulness in their original intended context.

Nevertheless, these latter two notes can serve a dialectical purpose in responding to some objections to Newman’s account of dogmatic development. Newman is well-known for arguing elsewhere that those views which reject the need for

---

<!-- PDF page 168 -->
**[p. 155]**

sufficient normative reasons to believe any given doctrine thereby take an implicit attitude toward dogma itself that tends toward the proliferation of heresy and toward ultimate dissolution into rejecting any definite beliefs at all as required for salvation.[^c04-30] Newman’s notes extend these logical insights to an empirical prediction: those groups which _reject_ dogmas of various kinds naturally tend toward other heresies or toward “latitudinarianism,” i.e., toward a more inclusive, more agnostic, and less dogmatic position over time. But the empirical prediction is unnecessary for the dialectic. Newman is pointing out that versions of Christianity which purport to reject the necessity for dogmatic development will be faced by historical facts that are impossible for them to account for. In short, a Christianity that rejects dogmatic authority and a corresponding need for dogmatic development will be unable to account for the normative force behind its own beliefs.

These claims can be utilized dialectically in responding to those contemporary Christian theologians who reject Newman’s claim that history confirms his hypothesis of development by arguing that there is no integral, systematic body of doctrine which anyone has inherited from the early Church. They therefore deny that Christians need to affirm any continuous or coherent history of developments. However, denying that logical consistency is a requisite of doctrinal development would in turn imply that there are _no_ epistemic demands of Christian faith or that God demands us to believe contradictions. Nemes holds one variant of such theories, where only nominal adherence to the person of Christ is required for Christian belief. Schillebeeckx and other liberal theologians have appealed to a continuity in Christian “practice,” “life,” or “experience” rather than a logical continuity among doctrines as what is essential to Christian faith.[^c04-31] These theories have seemed attractive to some theologians, as they permit that true contradictions in dogmatic history could occur: i.e., the Church can “reverse” a corporate solemn teaching, adopting a view that is strictly in contradiction with what was authoritatively defined or held. But we have already encountered good reasons to reject such theories. There _are_ epistemic demands made by faith in God’s Word. Christian believers cannot simply neglect their obligations to believe what God wants them to believe, nor can they believe that God would demand that they believe contradictions. As Newman notes, by abandoning anything knowable in what God’s Word declares to us, such views are theoretically indistinguishable from (or simply beg the question against our endorsing) agnosticism. “By embracing doctrinal corruption, religious liberalism ultimately leaves little in Christianity worth retaining.”[^c04-32]

Gavin Ortlund attempts to construct a halfway house between abandoning epistemic duties to Christian faith and affirming dogmatic authority, embracing only what is “essential” or “plain” in the Scriptural deposit. Ortlund admits that serious errors can become widespread or mainstream in the Christian Church – for instance, he considers it to be a serious error to hold that babies who die before baptism are not saved (even though this belief was mainstream in Christian history).[^c04-33] But Ortlund denies that there are any but very minimal dogmatic requirements to being a Christian believer. Thus, differences between Christian bodies are no impediment to their preaching the Gospel, rather being merely superficial differences in “outward forms and expression” as long as they preserve “the

---

<!-- PDF page 169 -->
**[p. 156]**

presence of Christ in word and sacrament.”[^c04-34] Rather, every church body holds or even teaches many dogmatic corruptions; corruption is inevitable and unavoidable.[^c04-35] Ortlund draws the lesson from this fact that the _only_ source of doctrinal continuity with the early Church lies in adherence to the Scripture which we have inherited, and thus we know the “essentials” can be clearly identified as remaining intact among all those corruptions.

Newman’s arguments are directed against views like that of Ortlund (in Newman’s context, Chillingsworth), which try to maintain the “plain sense” of Scripture or its “essentials,” while rejecting any dogmatic beliefs beyond these. Newman argues these positions are historically and theologically unserious. First, these views are in a quandary that “no great number of persons agree together what are these great truths, simple views, leading ideas, or peculiar doctrines of the Gospel.”[^c04-36] Ortlund refrains from stating those beliefs that _are_ essential to Christ’s presence in “word and sacrament,” because such statements are open to reasonable disagreement.[^c04-37] But, clearly, if any given set of beliefs is open to reasonable disagreement, it cannot be the “plain sense” of Scripture which all should immediately recognize to be essential. So too the historical facts do not allow us to be naïve: any given attempt to carve out that subset and propose an interpretation of what is “essential” to Christianity is open to contest. And there is an important theological subtext: all Christians who believe faith involves epistemic duties to believe _definite_ truths revealed by God need to explain the way in which believers are obligated to believe (i.e., have sufficient normative reason to affirm) some subset of truths taught by Scripture. God doesn’t simply want each believer to recognize _the Bible_ without having any definite beliefs about what it says to them.

Ortlund himself argues frequently that Catholicism has introduced practices or beliefs which _do_ involve such a serious difference and which would, from his point of view, undermine our faith in the Gospel. Ortlund cannot then both affirm that _no_ doctrinal disagreement would make a difference for our faith and yet protest that Catholic beliefs are idolatry or serious dogmatic error. When Ortlund argues that Marian dogmas and icon veneration represent doctrinal corruptions, he points to facts that these are not explicitly mentioned in Scripture and there is no “evidence of any awareness of an oral tradition,” which would allow us to trace these beliefs back to the early Church.[^c04-38] Thus, the difficulty is that, for any given doctrine Ortlund himself might want to maintain as “essential” to Christian faith, such as Christological or Trinitarian Nicene orthodoxy, it would not be possible to consistently apply Ortlund’s own criteria to these. There is no explicit Scriptural witness, e.g., to the _homoousios_ clause in the Nicene Creed, or to the distinctions regarding “person” and “essence” that became dogmatic for Trinitarian orthodoxy, and there is no evidence of any “oral tradition” going back to the early Church for these beliefs.

Nevertheless, since dogmatic disagreements can _make a serious difference_ to Christian faith, Christians need to explain the way in which such beliefs can be understood to represent “essential” beliefs, even though they are neither explicitly stated in Scripture nor due to an oral tradition from the early Church. If we reject dogmatic developments on account of their failure to be explicitly present in Scripture, we could not maintain _any_ normative dogmatic content to theological faith at

---

<!-- PDF page 170 -->
**[p. 157]**

all. This is why Newman points out that “the arguments which are used to prove that the Church system is not in Scripture, may as cogently be used to prove that no system is in Scripture. If silence in Scripture, or apparent contrariety, is an argument against the Church system, it is an argument against system altogether. No system is on the surface of Scripture; none ….”[^c04-39]

Indeed, the most telling problem for positions like Ortlund’s is that the canon of Scripture is one area that has been open to doctrinal controversy. That is, Ortlund’s position requires assuming as a dogmatic truth that Scripture is inspired/inerrant in all its parts, and that _these books_ (the canonical ones) constitute the canon of inspired Scripture. While these doctrines are necessary for maintaining Ortlund’s position against the necessity of any dogmatic authority outside of Scripture, these truths regarding belief in biblical inspiration and the canon have themselves _also_ been subject to development – e.g., there was a time when there was no received uniform canon of Scripture; but then there came to be obligations such that those today who reject some books to fit their own theology would be blameworthy. Explaining this fact involves appealing to development of doctrine.

It would be obviously question-begging to cite Scripture itself in favor of those doctrines regarding its constitution and character. Only extra-scriptural facts are sufficient to account for these beliefs. Newman therefore argues that those who reject dogmatic development cannot explain their normative acceptance of Scripture itself as a divine book; “when, then, as at this time, [Scripture’s] divine authority is the very point to be determined, that is, the character and extent of its inspiration and its component parts, then they find themselves at sea, without the means of directing their course.”[^c04-40] We either have divine authority backing our faith that Scripture is God’s Word and for the developments pertaining to belief in Scripture, or all our beliefs about Scripture must be held with merely human credence, since they all rest on fallible human inferences, and cannot be _theologically_ normative for other believers. Without “divine sanction” for dogmatic developments around the canon and our belief in inspiration, we would need to abandon any epistemic duties consequent upon faith, since all our other beliefs as Christians depend upon and are derived from our beliefs in Scriptural revelation.

And, indeed, Newman’s predictions according to which groups adhering to principled rejection of dogmatic development and authority will devolve into dogmatic chaos or inclusivism are arguably borne out by the historical data. Prominent Protestant theologians themselves have been critical of understanding the Reformation “sola scriptura” in such a way as to drive a wedge between the authoritative dogmatic consensus or Tradition of the Church and the text of Scripture.[^c04-41] Stanley Hauerwas states the criticism succinctly in terms Newman would admire:

When sola scriptura is used to underwrite the distinction between text and interpretation, then it seems clear to me that _sola scriptura_ is a heresy rather than a help in the Church. When this distinction persists, _sola scriptura_ becomes the seedbed of fundamentalism, as well as biblical criticism. It assumes that the text of the Scripture makes sense separate from a Church that gives it sense.[^c04-42]

---

<!-- PDF page 171 -->
**[p. 158]**

In general, those attempts to stick to the principle that there is no dogmatic authority or development will either be question-begging in their affirmation of the canon and Scriptural inspiration, and seem fundamentalist (especially in the face of modern biblical criticism), or will involve abandoning implicitly or explicitly those beliefs about Scripture which undergird epistemic duties to adhere to that human word as God’s Word, i.e., with theological faith.

A contemporary version of the latter tends to take the form of appeal to an “eschatological” deferral, where no human interpretation of Scripture or identification of the text as inspired can be considered binding on anyone aside from the individual interpreter. Every interpretation is “provisional and contestable” but supposed to nevertheless be “enough for a faith that wagers upon the continued eloquence of the God into whose service the unruly library of scriptural texts has been conscripted.”[^c04-43] On this view, there should be no expectation of uniformity in belief among Christians aside from commitment “to a way of living and thinking from, with, and before Scripture which is sufficient for faith.”[^c04-44] As Ziegler approvingly puts it, “for Protestant theology _dogma_ is and can only ever be a properly eschatological concept.”[^c04-45] On such views, it is implied that Scripture, as proclaimed or read, can make moral demands of its hearers, but that the preacher of Scripture or the Church cannot make dogmatic claims on anyone.

This reading of “sola scriptura” remains as question-begging as that proposed by Ortlund. Christianity is not simply the academic exercise of reading the Bible as a work of ancient literature; Christianity makes moral claims that we ought to _preach_ the Gospel to others and persuade them to convert or be baptized. Any attempt to proclaim Scripture involves minimally identifying _which_ books are Scriptural and _that_ they (at least potentially) make a moral claim on our conscience or demand our faith. If nobody could make any such identification of Scripture that was more than “provisionally” the perspective of a fallible group of human beings that these words are possibly the Word of God, or nobody could indicate that these books demand our faith, then nobody could preach the Gospel. It is certainly true that we could continue studying or reading these books, but that is not to have faith in their word as God’s Word. It is similarly unclear what it means to have a commitment to Scripture of the right sort that considers Scripture to have a moral claim on oneself, while also rejecting the ability to recognize and assert to others that these books _are_ God’s Word. If God makes moral demands upon any reader of Scripture to submit to God’s Word, He certainly also makes such a demand upon others, and it would not be inappropriate of me to assert these facts, if they are true. Finally, the very attempt to propose the viability or desirability of a commitment to a “way of living” with Scripture is unjustifiable, within this framework, since the theory undercuts any way to identify what Scripture _is_ or what sort of claim it makes on us that is not simply the subjective hypothesis of the individual theologian.

Nevertheless, Ortlund and others allege that there is no good way to avoid the problem that one must appeal to one’s own conscience or judgment to discern the truth and content of Scriptural doctrine. “We all use private judgment for the most important and poignant decisions of life—including whether to be a Christian, which church to join, and whether to remain in that church.”[^c04-46] Ortlund thus

---

<!-- PDF page 172 -->
**[p. 159]**

implicitly poses a _tu quoque_ response to arguments as to the necessity of dogmatic authority and development: “If we appeal to our own ‘private judgment’ to determine the content of Scripture, would we not need to do the same in order to determine the content of the _consensus ecclesiae_ or dogmatic definitions, let alone whether a doctrinal development is legitimate/normative/false?”
