Such responses are irrelevant to the argument in favor of dogmatic authority and development advanced here. Being able to comprehend Scriptural texts (that is, to grasp their intelligible content) is not what is at issue in questions of dogmatic authority. The necessity of dogmatic authority or development does not rest on the belief that we are incapable of _reading_ or _understanding_ sentences written in Scripture. Dogmatic authority and development have to do with judgments regarding our identification of the sources of doctrine _as_ authoritative (Scripture _as_ God’s Word, the Church as testifier to that Word, etc.) and then of other sources as _nonauthoritative_ or _conflicting with_ that doctrine (i.e., heresies). Being able to comprehend the meaning of a sentence in the Gospel of John is neither here nor there in resolving whether John was a true apostle sent by God, whose words are infallible, whether we have received the text of John 1:1 intact and reliably from its author such as to place theological faith in those words as God’s Word, or whether Arius’ theories regarding the status of Jesus Christ conflict with the words of John 1:1.

Thus, it is true that Catholic and Orthodox theologians disagree at times about the content or status of various dogmatic claims in their tradition or how to explain various developments. Determining the facts about dogmatic history or the content and standing of various “magisterial teaching” naturally appeals to evidence or argument just as believers do in determining the content of God’s Word. There might be disputes about what that doctrine entails; and there might be arguments that the belief should be interpreted as X or Y; but these facts do not make dogmatic authority superfluous. Dogmatic teaching functions, first, to generate normative obligations in faith to accept meta-Scriptural testimony – such normative obligations continue to exist even when there might be reasonable disagreement as to the content of that testimony, just as we are still obligated to submit to God’s Word even when we encounter reasonable disagreement regarding its content. Second, the objective facts regarding past definitions constrain interpretations; not every dispute about dogma will be reasonable. Finally, and most importantly, dogmatic authority _continues_ to serve this meta-Scriptural function. The existence of reasonable disagreement about _past_ teaching among theologians does not mean we cannot recognize the way that dogmatic authority can or would be able to decide dogmatic disputes in the present or future. Catholic/Orthodox theologians can be clearly recognized as unorthodox when they fail to recognize and observe these obligations.

Despite reasonable disagreement about the content of past dogma or the content of Scripture, our identification of the authority of Scripture and the Church does not involve independently determining the truth of what they declare to be God’s Word or will but is instead founded on _faith_ that logically encompasses both in one act. In the same way, faith in the word of the apostles as reliably transmitting God’s Word is not logically separable from beliefs about Christ, who sent them and validates their divine mission, but these two beliefs are necessarily jointly a matter of

---

<!-- PDF page 173 -->
**[p. 160]**

faith – and if we believed one without the other, we would not have Christian faith, since we can only believe in Christ’s Gospel as transmitting through the words of the apostles. Similarly, while Scripture endorses many instances of individuals who are commended for “searching the Scriptures” (e.g., Jn 5:39; Acts 8:28; Acts 17:11), Newman notes that these passages “sanction, not an inquiry about Gospel doctrine, but about the Gospel teacher; not what has God revealed, but whom has He commissioned [so that] if an appeal _is_ made to private judgment, this is done in order to settle who the teacher is, and what are his notes or tokens, rather than to substantiate this or that religious opinion or practice.”[^c04-47]

These same considerations are those to which Newman appeals in the context of doctrinal development: we ultimately judge whether a development is legitimate or not in light of our acceptance of the relevant authorities, rather than by independently testing whether we agree with the content of what they teach. As Newman illustrates, then, a judgment regarding whether a preacher is from God, and speaks God’s Word, is not the same sort of judgment which accepts what the preacher says only insofar as independent confirmation allows:

Since men now-a-days deduce from Scripture, instead of believing a teacher, you may expect to see them waver about; they will feel the force of their own deductions more strongly at one time than at another, they will change their minds about them, or perhaps deny them altogether; whereas this cannot be, while a man has faith, that is, belief that what a preacher says to him comes from God.[^c04-48]

Such “independent” confirmation is self-defeating in the context of heresies which, as we have seen, involve appeal to the same texts alongside different extraScriptural principles of interpretation. What we are asking is whether a given set of purported authorities are the “false teachers” mentioned in Scripture more than whether their arguments are independently convincing.

How, then, do we decide claims among those Christian authorities who claim to speak for God? Newman, in his Anglican period, pointed to an ancient test for whether a prophet was true regarded whether their prophesies came to pass (as laid out in Deut 18:22 and Acts 5:38). Newman concedes that the failure or disappearance of a sect (e.g., the Anglican Church) would reveal it to be a false dogmatic authority. But this test is not applicable at any point prior to its decline and disappearance. Another test Newman identifies in his Catholic period is far simpler: does a religious body _purport_ to exercise dogmatic authority and preach infallibly in God’s name? Newman pointed out that

no other religious body has a right to demand such an exercise of faith in it, and a right to forbid you further inquiry, but the Catholic Church; and for this simple reason, that no other body even claims to be infallible, let alone the proof of such a claim. Here is the defect at first starting, which disqualifies them, one and all, from ever competing with the Church of God. The sects about us, so far from demanding your faith, actually call on you to inquire and to doubt freely about their own merits; they protest that they are but

---

<!-- PDF page 174 -->
**[p. 161]**

voluntary associations, and would be sorry to be taken for anything else; they beg and pray you not to mistake their preachers for anything more than mere sinful men, and they invite you to take the Bible with you to their sermons, and to judge for yourselves whether their doctrine is in accordance with it.[^c04-49]

Now, Newman’s claim perhaps needs qualification, as the Orthodox churches do – in fact – lay claim to the same dogmatic authority as the Catholic Church does, and Protestant churches too could _potentially_ claim to possess dogmatic authority (as I will argue in the next chapter).

Nevertheless, for my purposes here, Newman’s general point holds true: there is no viable possibility of independently confirming what a preacher declares to be God’s Word (except by becoming prophets ourselves) and so, for the same reason, no possibility of independently confirming that a dogmatic development _is_ a development apart from a judgment regarding whether the preacher is appropriately commissioned by God to preach in God’s name. The best we can do otherwise is indirectly appeal to various accidental features that function as signs or marks to help us identify _that_ the dogmatic authority is what it claims to be: a reliable testifier to God’s Word. Just as a prophet is someone whose words are fulfilled, a dogmatic authority is an agent that teaches consistently over time. These features are not sufficient by themselves to identify dogmatic authority, but once we assume a hypothesis that dogmatic development is necessary and is always accompanied by legitimate authority, we can examine historical evidence against candidates for that authority or bolster the case that a purported dogmatic authority is what it purports to be.

The key premise in a theory of dogmatic development is therefore the hypothesis that developments are necessary for that Word to persist in the Church and, as God’s Word would be inaccessible to us today (who have no direct access to Christ or His prophets and apostles, except by trusting the testimony of dogmatic authority), it therefore follows that development would be identified not solely by internal criteria but primarily by identifying those (corporate) subjects possessing dogmatic authority. The criteria posed by Newman for discerning a development from a corruption are then accidental features in light of which we can confirm a hypothesis regarding whether a given subject _is_ a dogmatic authority, so that, e.g., we examine whether their decisions are plausibly dogmatically consistent in the right ways, etc. We form the hypothesis that a given body is what it purports to be – an infallible testifier to God’s Word – and then try to confirm empirically that we can trace its consistent acts of dogmatic teaching over time, by which it differentiates itself from heretical bodies. The extent to which this is objectively possible throughout Church history, by appeal to normative political and epistemic criteria tracing their developments consistently from the earliest apostolic period, raises or lowers our credence in their claims to be the one Church.

## Apocalypse, Development, Authority

What I have done thus far is to respond to objections that theories of dogmatic development would be unnecessary because some other principles might be proposed to replace dogmatic authority in licensing developments (such as “sola scriptura”).

---

<!-- PDF page 175 -->
**[p. 162]**

In what follows, I will argue that all Christians should agree with the broad outline of Newman’s account of dogmatic development, including its feature that only dogmatic authority functions as a logically sufficient criterion for dogmatic developments, and I will do this dialectically by responding to allegations that any theory of dogmatic development _in principle_ is question begging. David Bentley Hart argues that we ought to reject theories of doctrinal development in general, on the grounds that it is impossible in principle to discern dogmatic development from corruption except by circular reasoning.

Unlike Ziegler or Ortlund, Hart is more consistent in rejecting any appeal to “sola scriptura” or any other similar attempt to delineate commitments which would be central or essential to Christian faith. Therefore, what functions in my argument as a _modus ponens_ that dogmatic development requires appeal to authority functions for Hart as a _modus tollens_. Hart alleges that any appeal to authority in matters of doctrine commits one to a blind fanaticism – “ecclesiastical fetishism”[^c04-50] – that leads one to accepting anything at all, no matter how absurd or morally pernicious. Hart takes the doctrine of eternal punishment in hell as an exemplar belief that has been endorsed by dogmatic authority and by the consensus of mainstream Christianity, but which is so “degrading, obscene, cruel, psychotic, and (in fact) unscriptural” such as to merit rejection despite whatever “authority” or “tradition” might dictate, and which therefore illustrates the extent to which appeal to dogmatic authority or development is potentially harmful.[^c04-51] And yet, unlike Nemes, Hart believes that he can affirm Christian supernatural faith without positing any theory of dogmatic development. Nevertheless, I will argue that Hart’s alternative ends up in the same place as Nemes’ account, substituting principled agnosticism for Christian faith. I will conclude by reflecting on the way these conclusions show us that all Christians should therefore be committed to dogmatic development and dogmatic authority.

Hart takes it that the goal of a theory of dogmatic development is to “prove the existence of—and then in fact identify—a particular living, continuous, and internally coherent phenomenon that corresponds to” core/essential Christian doctrines transmitted reliably and in their integrity from the apostles until today, and correspondingly to rule out that contemporary Christian beliefs are merely “a product of pure historical fortuity, consisting in a mere mechanically determined series of consecutive viable forms united more by evolutionary imperatives than by internal rationality.”[^c04-52] Hart is highly critical of Newman’s theory, arguing that the notes are not only insufficient to distinguish development from corruption, but that “each and every one of them is a manifest tautology, and all seven of them taken together constitute a perfect logical circle.”[^c04-53] Yet why Hart asserts that Newman’s theory is “tautological” is fundamentally unclear, since there are very many different senses in which Hart makes objection to Newman’s theory, but none of them are obviously alleging that Newman is literally proposing tautologies.

For instance, Hart begins his book by arguing that it is impossible in principle to prove that such a unity exists by means of a historical critical methodology, for two reasons. Hart argues that the kinds of causal process accessible to historical science are not in principle the right sort as to prove that there is “a rationally necessitated unity” within the historical events of Christian tradition.[^c04-54] On the one

---

<!-- PDF page 176 -->
**[p. 163]**

hand, Hart argues that “a tradition’s unity arises largely from the intentional states of rational agents,” which are not accessible to “modern scientific method,” which in principle restricts itself to “a world of purely mechanical processes.”[^c04-55] Consequently, it is impossible to show that people in the past believed what we believe today, because what people believed or believe is scientifically inaccessible (given methodological or practical restrictions?). On the other hand, Hart intimates that the kind of unity posited by Christians within their tradition is the intentional direction provided to history by something like the “rational structure of the world” or God’s Providence, “the ultimate ground of its living continuity across generations and ages”[^c04-56] – and it goes without saying that metaphysical facts about the “rational structure of the world,” or supernatural facts about God’s Providence, are surely empirically inaccessible.

Hart’s first argument against the possibility of a theory of dogmatic development is poor, conflating two distinct sets of claims. It is straightforwardly and obviously false that beliefs are empirically inaccessible or that historical critical studies (methodologically) require reducing all motivations to physical causes that have nothing to do with intentionality. Intellectual histories exist and are possible – and this was Newman’s project in aiming to use historical studies to construct an intellectual history of Christian orthodoxy. Yet Hart bizarrely conflates this historical project, which looks defensible as a work of historical critical study, with a distinct aim of demonstrating or proving a totalizing metaphysics regarding a Hegelian rational structure to history, or God’s Providence guiding Christian history. Many times, Hart implies that Newman’s project was to demonstrate an “intrinsic rational coherence, century upon century,”[^c04-57] or “an intrinsically necessary end,”[^c04-58] to Christian history, or “that orthodoxy invariably triumphed in the past as a result of providence and the subtle cooperation of the Holy Spirit in doctrinal deliberations.”[^c04-59] But Newman’s project was never to do any such thing, nor does demonstrating dogmatic development require any such thing – as he notes, “I would simply confess that no doctrine of the Church can be rigorously proved by historical evidence; but at the same time that no doctrine can be simply disproved by it.”[^c04-60] It is not obvious why showing that the dogmas which resulted from Christian councils (for instance) are properly related to past beliefs, as preserving the same logical content and forming a coherent whole, should require demonstrating grandiose claims about, e.g., Hegelianism to be true.

At times, Hart advances a weaker argument that rests on assuming Newman had set out to prove “that there is such a thing as a genuinely ‘living’ tradition, not merely in the vague sense of something capable of continuous change, but in the more radical sense of something like a single organism with an intrinsic teleology.”[^c04-61] Hart responds that this too is impossible, since “a sequence of historical events, however ‘causally’ necessitated, is not an organism [and neither] is it really possible to identify ‘health’ or ‘disease’ in the life of a tradition, or to distinguish one from the other, because even a ‘living tradition’ is most definitely not a living thing. Thus, whether it should flourish or stagnate or wither away as the result of some new development, this is no evidence whatsoever of either the rational necessity or the irrational perversity of that development.”[^c04-62]

---

<!-- PDF page 177 -->
**[p. 164]**

While Newman often employs organic metaphors regarding _beliefs_, it is a fundamental misunderstanding to take Newman’s project as aiming to _demonstrate_ that dogmatic authority exists or that the Church is an organic whole. Rather, he takes the existence of dogmatic authority and that of the Church, as well as the postulate that dogmatic development is necessary for its life, as a hypothesis he aims to confirm. The _Essay_ empirically confirms or raises the probability of this hypothesis by showing that history can justify that there is intelligible doctrinal continuity in the belief of the contemporary orthodox Christians that can be traced back to the ancient Church. Conversely, Hart has misunderstood the force of the sixth and seventh “notes.” Newman is explicit in recognizing that the persistence of a development over time is _sufficient_ to prove that this development is legitimate, even if it is a necessary condition. But – _pace_ Hart – the extinction of a given sect of Christians, or of some belief among Christians, is good evidence in favor of this sect not being the Church founded by Christ or that this belief is not the Gospel. The theory of development depends upon auxiliary hypotheses which are generally uncontroversial among Christians – such as the belief that, if God revealed Christianity and its doctrine, then it cannot disappear or become entirely corrupted – then, given those assumptions, Newman’s sixth and seventh notes do have some force. While it is true atheists would not admit these hypotheses, Newman’s theory of development never aimed to _prove_ that the Gospel cannot die out or the Church cannot disappear.

Hart’s argument is sometimes stated in an even weaker fashion. Hart notes that Newman’s theory needs to prove that there is a logical “necessity” or “sequence” between historical stages in Christian doctrine. Hart alleges, however, that any such attempt to demonstrate this necessity over time in Christian beliefs will necessarily involve a _petitio principi_, since

the ‘discovery’ of an implicit logical coherence in the historical unfolding of any conceptual transition is, almost by definition, indistinguishable from the retrospective fabrication of some abstract principle that is merely reconcilable with—or simply not repugnant to—the two distinct moments of the disjunction; this principle, once found or fabricated, can then be adduced as the subtle, perhaps even invisible bond uniting two phases of a single conceptual shift. … a genuinely logical sequence of propositions or principles or even sound intuitions, however various they may be, must still terminate in only one possible conclusion. If more than one is possible, then all that can be demonstrated is the logical nonrepugnance of any given stage of development to what either precedes or succeeds it.[^c04-63]

It is hard to see what precisely the problem with Newman’s account of “logical sequence” is supposed to be. It is _patently obvious_ that many doctrinal developments _do_ involve logical dependence relations, and that many later definitions could not have been made without prior definitions: the Council of Ephesus’definition that Mary was the “Theotokos” or “Mother of God” depended logically upon Christological doctrines regarding Jesus’ identity; later Marian dogmas of the Immaculate Conception and Assumption logically depended upon the definition that

---

<!-- PDF page 178 -->
**[p. 165]**

Mary was Mother of God; definitions regarding Christology at Constantinople V or icons at Nicaea II depended upon prior definitions, etc.

One way to interpret Hart’s critique is that, for any given sequence of beliefs that are noncontradictory, they can be arbitrarily grouped under a principle that unites them and appears to make them be entailments of each other or of that principle. But Hart’s critique then looks to rest on patently false claims. The beliefs that Obama is not an American citizen and that the square root of _pi_ is 2 are not contradictory, but I can see no higher principle under which they can be arbitrarily grouped and made to be entailments (except the trivial proposition that the conjunction of these beliefs is true). Another way to interpret this critique is that Newman’s theory has not proven that later Christian dogma is _deduced_ from prior Christian dogmas. But Newman’s theory never poses any such general requirement (even if it _is_ vindicated in some instances, like Ephesus); his theory holds that the _logical content_ found in Scripture and Tradition licenses development of various dogmas, not that all dogmas are entailed by other dogmas. The question theories of dogmatic development aim to address is not whether Nicaea II was a legitimate development of Nicaea I, but whether Nicaea II is a legitimate development of Revelation. So, while it is true that, e.g., the definition of the legitimacy of icon veneration at Nicaea II was not _logically entailed_ by the dogma of the “homoousios” proclaimed at Nicaea I, or even all the other conciliar definitions put together, that is irrelevant to whether Nicaea II’s definition was a legitimate doctrinal development.

It might be thought, too, that all dogmas must be logical entailments of propositions found in Scripture. But Newman too rejects this way of understanding dogmatic development, as he notes that the ancient Church rejected a limitation to the literal sense of Scripture (following the pattern of the New Testament authors, e.g., 1 Tim 5:18, 1 Cor 12:12–27, etc.), and often reasoned from the mystical sense of Scripture.[^c04-64] Newman’s account would make good sense if he had presumed a variety of “content externalism.” “An externalist view of content holds that the content of propositions is not determined by what the people who think them can understand of them, but by the nature of the realities that the propositions are about. … The fact that people who grasp [a given] propositions need not understand that they imply [certain consequences] is neither here nor there, because meaning isn’t in the head.”[^c04-65]

Hart seems to think that, if Scripture or a given Church Father or consensus of the Church did not explicitly rule out Arianism in what they said, then “either side of the doctrinal aporia that ultimately yielded the later dogmas would have been equally consistent with” prior Christian beliefs.[^c04-66] Frances Young likewise criticizes Newman for holding that “later doctrine was already latent in the earliest Christian material.”[^c04-67] She notes by contrast that “doctrines were the result of trying to make sense of scripture, of reading scriptural texts with particular questions in mind, those questions often being entirely new issues,” and therefore concludes that it is false to hold that the earliest Christian material (such as Scripture) latently or implicitly contains orthodox Trinitarian or Christological doctrines.[^c04-68] But it does not follow that, because early sources never _directly addressed_ the issue of (for

---

<!-- PDF page 179 -->
**[p. 166]**

instance) Arianism that those sources do not teach principles which conflict with Arianism. If context externalism is true,[^c04-69] there is nothing obviously impossible with holding that Scripture or early Church thinkers held beliefs which are logically incompatible with Arianism, despite those authors never having encountered Arianism. Clearly, the apostles never knew about Arianism, since Arius had not yet been born. Nevertheless, what the apostles believed might have ruled out Arian doctrines, given the _logical content_ of their beliefs. And we have evidence of what the apostles believed, given what we know is contained in Scripture.

That is, there might be logical consequences or extrapolations of what they _meant_ that they themselves did not infer, but which are incompatible with Arianism. If there are objective facts regarding what Scripture rules out, then there is nothing obviously question-begging in an argument that Scripture rules out, e.g., Arianism. Similarly, there is nothing obviously question-begging in arguing that the early Church Fathers held views that logically anticipated later dogmatic developments – as Newman does in the _Essay_ – since much of the case depends on the details regarding the actual content of their beliefs and their entailments. Development thus aims to explicate “… what in substance was held from the first, what the Apostles said … or would necessarily have said under our circumstances, or if they had been asked, or in view of certain uprisings of error ….”[^c04-70] Young too understands the orthodox theological tradition to be a legitimate development of what is contained in Scripture _when read within the rule of faith,_ the living doctrinal teaching of the Christian community. She notes that the orthodox tradition’s developments were required of the Church as a response to dogmatic challenges that could not be avoided.[^c04-71] Young’s own theoretical account of the development of doctrine is then only nominally in conflict with that of Newman, since her proposal that the Church’s authority (its rule of faith) serves as a normative, binding backdrop for reading Scripture correctly is exactly what Newman underscores. We judge developments not only by their logical coherence with Scriptural principles but also normatively in terms of the teaching provided within the Church. Later developments of doctrine are almost always more than trivial _deductions_ from Scriptural propositions; these developments would only be binding because of Church’s authoritative testimony to them. But the fact that these developments are only binding in terms of later exercises of authority, where the Church’s endorsement of meta-scriptural beliefs thereby ensures that they have sufficient normative character to be binding on believers, does not undermine the fact that those meta-scriptural beliefs have the right intrinsic _logical connection_ to Scripture itself.

Guy Mansini, a contemporary Catholic theologian, therefore argues against 20th-century attempts to formulate theories of theological development which “appeal to some non-propositional possession of the ‘Whole’ in some intuition or experience.”[^c04-72] Such theories (such as arguably that of Schillebeeckx) ended up making revelation a matter not of truth but of some practical attitude or another, as well as entailing (implausibly and ahistorically) that past Christians had conscious or unconscious “intuition” of later developments. Rather, Mansini proposes – as I take Newman to do – that the “material” from which development occurs “is not possessed subjectively in some intuition or pre-conceptual awareness, but subsists

---

<!-- PDF page 180 -->
**[p. 167]**

objectively in the words recorded and the deeds recounted and as recounted in the dual testaments in their mutual illumination.”[^c04-73] The authors of Scripture need not have been _conscious_ or _unconsciously aware_ of Arianism in order for what they said to rule out Arianism – there only need to be facts of the meaning (or public meaning) of their words, such that what they taught rules out Arianism as false. By an extended historical process by which the Church corporately becomes aware – through the work of theological reasoning over time – of the incompatibility of various beliefs with its those provided in Scripture, or of the implications of Scripture for various issues not directly mentioned in Scripture, especially in light of the interpretation of Scripture it inherited historically from apostolic churches, the Church likewise grows in being able to discover more facts about what Scripture teaches in virtue of the ways these implications build upon each other.

A practical example in the development of icon veneration is illustrative. Iconodules can admit that _icons_ and _statues_ were not venerated in the early Church. Indeed, all admit evidence exists that these were held in suspicion within the early Church given their association with pagan practices. Yet, as John Damascene argued, there are both beliefs and practices in the early Church that could represent a _principle_ which – regardless of whether the early Christians would have themselves engaged in the later practices – logically supports the liceity of icon veneration. Despite the fact that no veneration of icons or statues occurred in the early Church, Damascene points out that, if the early Church considered acts of veneration including bowing down or kissing, appropriate when given to the Cross,[^c04-74] to Books of the Gospels or altars and the like,[^c04-75] and to the Eucharistic elements[^c04-76] in public worship. Further, the practice of venerating relics of Christ (such as the empty tomb in Jerusalem[^c04-77]), or of the saints, supports the same principle. Such practices _would_ very arguably logically support a principle that venerating symbols of Christ’s presence is appropriate; there is no clear circularity or question-begging feature in identifying that, logically, those earlier practices support a principle that venerating symbols of Christ’s presence is morally appropriate.

Now, it is not my place in this chapter to defend icon veneration, which requires further support that I can provide here, but merely to point out a logical fact about arguments regarding development of doctrine. There are good antecedent reasons behind Damascene’s claim that there are biblical witnesses to the _principle_ that one can venerate symbols of Christ and the saints. For example, the practice of venerating relics associated with Christ and the apostles is witnessed in Scripture (e.g., Mt 14:35–36; cf. Mk 6:56; Lk 8:43–44; 2 Kings 13:21; Acts 5:15; Acts 19:11–12). It is very likely that these practices continued and that we could find quite early historical textual and archaeological evidence regarding the sign of the Cross or employing the Cross, the Eucharistic elements, altars (possibly even alluded to in Heb 13:10), and so forth in public worship. Newman notes that in one author from the 2nd century, Ignatius of Antioch, we find anticipations of these claims and others besides.[^c04-78] The relevant question for justifying the development of icon veneration is, however, not whether Ignatius or the early Christians knew of or venerated icons, but only whether these practices (objectively) _logically support_ the principle employed to justify icon veneration.

---

<!-- PDF page 181 -->
**[p. 168]**

Hart makes the sweeping objection that we can _never_ assert or empirically confirm in an objective way that these practices do support such a principle. That is, that _any_ attempt to identify a “principle” which would group together worshipping publicly the Cross or the Eucharistic elements and worshipping an image or statue of Jesus would rest on circular reasoning, and that anything could be justified if one could licitly posit as a hypothesis that there is a logical continuity between these practices. But such an assertion seems obviously false. Furthermore, it is false to portray positing such a principle as if it were simply an _a priori_ posit without the ability to test whether that principle holds. For instance, whether there was such an implicit principle in the early Church can be treated as a hypothesis that we can confirm. Newman argues that there is historical evidence that early Christian hesitation to employ images or statues in public worship can be explained by ancillary considerations which are not intrinsically objections to the principle worship paid to a symbol of Christ’s presence is paid to Christ or that worship of symbols of Christ is appropriate – and thus, by extension, that these would not be objections against using, e.g., images or statues of Christ. Indeed, it would be historically _surprising_ to find early Christians make arguments that utilizing Gospel books or altars or crosses or relics in public worship would be idolatrous and thereby logically undermine their own practices.[^c04-79] So, _pace_ Hart, there is nothing obviously _ad hoc_ or questionbegging about such a process of historical inquiry which involves empirically discerning intentions that underlie past liturgical practices or theological claims, or in positing an initial hypothesis that these practices or claims indicate an underlying principle which could (logically) support later developments in theory or practice.

Another way to take Hart’s critique is that he is objecting to the fact that Newman’s theory cannot suitably _predict_ future developments. Hart at times argues that theories of development cannot satisfactorily prove why we should not see “the historical record as a series of accidents rather than as a ‘living’and rational unity whose unfolding is internally warranted and, in fact, necessitated.”[^c04-80] What Hart seems to mean can be interpreted as one of two ways. On the one hand, Hart at times argues that any conception of “tradition” which would prove doctrinal continuity would have to “do more than is logically possible [because] potentially, it [would need to] serve as a defense of anything and so, actually, it would provide an effective defense of nothing.”[^c04-81] Whatever is alleged to be a genuine development cannot be definitively decided one way or another, as any such claim becomes subject to “an unending contest of narratives,” ending in a tautologous narrative that “what happened must have happened in _this_ way, and that we know this because it _did_ happen in this way ….”[^c04-82] Hart therefore highlights that “There is no single doctrinal development described in the _Essay_ as ‘healthy’ that might not, by the same logic, have been described as a ‘corruption’ ….”[^c04-83] Hart seems to be saying that Newman’s historical stories do not provide evidence sufficient to conclude that a doctrinal development is legitimate _beyond reasonable doubt_. But this seems a strange requirement for such a theory (one Newman explicitly rejects[^c04-84]). A theory of dogmatic development providing strong noncircular and reasonable evidence in favor of the legitimacy of a development seems quite sufficient for apologetic purposes.

On the other hand, Hart might instead be arguing that Newman has not proven that future developments were _inevitable,_ being logically necessitated by past

---

<!-- PDF page 182 -->
**[p. 169]**

developments or the content of Revelation. Hart argues that in many cases it was only a later dogmatic crisis or situation which provided clarity for theologians to see the past developments or content in a new light, and thereby develop a new dogmatic definition, justifying the Nicene dogmatic tradition in this way: “the evidence of history is sufficient to say that what happened in the ascendancy of Nicene orthodoxy can be read as a genuinely rational and perhaps necessary synthesis of the tradition of the past.”[^c04-85] But Hart’s own explanation of the Nicene dogma is fundamentally identical with the way in which I have construed Newman’s theory. Dogmatic developments are not “inevitable” logical entailments of past beliefs, developed in a vacuum; dogmatic definitions and developments are necessitated as _responses_ to heretical challenges. Thus, it is entirely unsurprising that dogmatic developments do not occur until after challenges do, since these developments are sometimes intelligible only in relation to those challenges (e.g., Trinitarian and Christological technical vocabulary was developed precisely to avoid heresies).

Indeed, there is an important set of data points that Hart and others often overlook around practices such as icon veneration. Much of the doctrinal development around icon veneration is a logical extension of principles developed over centuries during Christological controversies around interpreting the biblical claims that Christ is the image of the Father. “Part of the debate between the Arians and the Orthodox was over whether Christ represented the Father merely by convention or by commonality. If Christ is a creature and does not share some element in common with God (i.e., the divine nature), then Christ can only represent God by convention. And so, in Himself, Christ reveals nothing about the Father. But if Christ has the divine nature in common with the Father, then we can, so to speak, ‘read off of’ Him the attributes of the Father.”[^c04-86] The principles employed by John Damascene in defense of icons would seem controversial if one is inattentive to the Christological implications regarding the interpretation of biblical language that Christ is the perfect image or icon of the Father (which is at least a partial explanation for the loss of that awareness during the Reformation by folks like Calvin). There are many similar examples; I will mention in Chapter 5 that failures to appreciate the role and place of Mary in Catholic/Orthodox dogma often stem from lack of clarity in Christological principles (which is – ironically – why Mariological dogma has been defined: to maintain clarity of Christological dogma).

Thus, despite the presence of biblical principles that entail the developments logically, clarity and precision regarding basic biblical principles is a precondition for drawing out the full logical implications. It is unsurprisingly that it took _centuries_ to arrive at logical clarity and precision regarding the basic principles pertaining to the hypostatic union in Christ or Trinity; since, while those principles are admitted by all classical Protestants to be logically present in Scripture, stating them perspicuously and exactly, in such a way as to rule out (logically) heretical beliefs, is not an easy task (as witnessed by the dogmatic history throughout the first seven ecumenical councils). But once these principles are clearly stated, further clarification and precision regarding other doctrinal implications can be drawn out from those newly stated principles. Regardless of how long it contingently took to clarify those basic principles, if the principles are indeed Scriptural, then the developments follow logically, as being “implicitly” contained within the principles. And those principles,

---

<!-- PDF page 183 -->
**[p. 170]**

once clearly and precisely stated, are – by necessity of logical principles like exclusion – incompatible with _any_ possible implication we might allege can be drawn from them. That is essentially how development works, in a nutshell.

Since Hart’s own explanation of Nicaea in fact invokes an account of dogmatic development, we should reject Hart’s sweeping claim that “every interpretation of _this_ development as consonant with _that_ principle will turn out to be nothing more than the willful and posterior invention of that principle so that it may then be assigned to this particular development, thus effectively explaining away any scandal, or any appearance of doctrinal novelty.”[^c04-87] Dogmatic developments are indeed retrospective attempts by theologians to respond to new situations, such as a heresy, and to develop explanations as to why those heresies ought to be rejected as _incompatible_ with Revelation. Hart’s criticisms that “an addition to a deposit of beliefs can be simultaneously both accidental and noncontradictory”[^c04-88] are therefore largely beside the point in evaluating theories of dogmatic development. It is true that a development being logically compatible with previous definitions or Revelation is not sufficient to show that it is a logical entailment of Revelation. Attempts at dogmatic development are more properly evaluated in terms of whether the _heresies_ so identified by the development are incompatible with Revelation, rather than whether the definition is itself a logical entailment of Scripture, since the definition itself often involves logical preconditions from previous definitions (which themselves bottom out in Scripture, but not necessarily immediately, and are thus not always directly obvious from Scripture itself[^c04-89]). This is why Newman’s “notes” focus on accidental features regarding whether developments form a consistent whole alongside their rejection of various heresies. In addition, Hart’s claim that the “notes” are entirely useless to rule out future developments is far too broad, as Newman’s theory _does_ identify accidental characteristics of developments which would incidentally help us arbitrate some potential developments that “seem plausible and therefore worthy of further consideration and which appear to be unreasonable and therefore doubtfully authentic at best.”[^c04-90]

Hart’s objections to Newman’s account fail to pay attention to its logical structure as an endeavor to offer historical confirmation of hypotheses grounded in faith. As Meszaros notes, “The notes... are used to illuminate the logical connection that developments have with the Christian idea (i.e., their authenticity) only after Newman has… argued, over the course of more than one hundred pages, that it is reasonable to presume that Christianity should have developments at all, that an infallible authority is necessary to identify those developments, and that Catholicism is full of such examples that render it a historical heir to primitive Christianity. Newman’s theory is meant to ‘account for a difficulty,’ not to arbitrate between what is possible and impossible in the future.”[^c04-91] I therefore conclude that Hart has not offered any serious principled objection to theories of dogmatic development. His criticisms are often equivocal or question-begging, resting on a misunderstanding of what is required by such theories. Hart’s own explanation of Nicene dogma looks to be an application of Newman’s theory of development, properly understood, rather than a rejection of it. Hart’s differences from Newman seem to lie elsewhere than in these criticisms already surveyed.

---

<!-- PDF page 184 -->
**[p. 171]**

Despite Hart’s initially strong claim to reject the existence of any “rational unity within the course of Christian tradition that one could confidently claim has been sustained intact amid the flux of times and cultures,”[^c04-92] Hart concedes that he does not intend to deny “the reality of divine inspiration in the development of doctrine” but rather only that anyone can _prove_ or _demonstrate_ that this development occurs – any such judgment, he alleges, “is [nothing] more than a judgment of faith that can never be fully verified historically or critically.”[^c04-93] Thus, when Hart affirms that he is “not at all determined to prove Christian tradition’s intrinsic unity,”[^c04-94] he means to say that it cannot be _proven_ that the Gospel is true. This is why Hart argues that dogmatic development would necessarily “remain invisible to the historical sciences,” so that (Hart thinks) any project to demonstrate the development of dogma is necessarily doomed to failure.[^c04-95]

Hart recognizes that Newman’s project is apologetic and retrospective, and that Newman indeed was not aiming to respond to atheists but to those who granted substantive hypotheses about Revelation (Anglicans and evangelical Protestants). Newman explicitly disclaimed aiming to demonstrate or prove that dogmatic development occurs (or that historical evidence in favor of Catholicism could not be reasonably contested), on the obvious basis that the demand for evidence which would rule out _all_ rational disagreement about biblical or historical evidence would make it impossible to rationally believe practically any dogma which requires more than tautological inferences from Scripture (e.g., the Trinity or canon are disputed still).[^c04-96] Hart nevertheless criticizes Newman’s project for falling back on these substantive assumptions. His fundamental critique of theories of dogmatic development is that such accounts necessarily rely on _some_ prior assumptions derived from faith. This is illegitimate, Hart thinks, because “faith whose only rationale is faith is not faith at all. Doctrine jealously preserved or confidently espoused solely on the grounds that it is doctrine is nothing but vacuous assertion masquerading as sincere conviction.”[^c04-97] Hart is equally critical of those believers who affirm continuity in doctrine “only because they are confessionally bound to assume” that unity.[^c04-98]

It looks patently unreasonable to require Christians to refrain, e.g., from appealing to their faith that the Bible is God’s Word in explaining dogmatic continuity throughout history. And Hart’s critique would be self-defeating, as Hart himself holds explicitly that faith is the only basis according to which one could know that there is doctrinal continuity in Christian history. Instead, Hart argues that we cannot assume that supernatural revelation has _any_ propositional content at all. As Hart later explains, Christianity has flourished without “any officially defined dogma [or] any single universally recognized canon of scripture”[^c04-99] and – Hart therefore implies – all disagreements about dogma, or even what books are to be included in the canon of Scripture, are irrelevant to the essence of Christianity. What Hart takes to be essential to Christianity is only “apocalyptic expectation—an eager certainty of the imminence of the full and final revelation of God’s truth in a restored and glorified cosmos.”[^c04-100] As Hart makes clear, his notion of “apocalypse” is ultimately a moral message which rejects “the shelters of social, cultic, and political association” and instead demands adherence to a singular “event” that the Kingdom was beginning.[^c04-101] Nevertheless, the Kingdom failed to arrive, and the “civic institution

---

<!-- PDF page 185 -->
**[p. 172]**

of the church became the only concrete, tangible form that Christian hope and expectation could now take in this prolonged interval of indefinite delay.”[^c04-102]

No dogmatic propositional content can be derived from such expectation, which looks to be a pragmatic or moral attitude. For these reasons, Hart thinks we cannot (“_must_ not”) say what is “essence and accident” in what has been transmitted to us purportedly as God’s Word, but can only await the “future apocalyptic horizon” where revelation of the truth about God will occur.[^c04-103] We cannot identify “with absolute finality what the _one true_ tradition is.”[^c04-104] Instead, “only in cases of moral departure from the explicit teachings of Christ can one easily identify what one can rightly call heresies.”[^c04-105] Apart from moral requirements that Hart identifies as excluding one from being a Christian in good standing – e.g., endorsing capital punishment, acquiring “vast wealth,” embracing “libertarian social theory,” believing in an eternal hell, or voting for Donald Trump – the only requirement of faith is submission to the “future verdict” in the apocalyptic future.[^c04-106] Hart thus outlines an alternative sense of “fidelity” to Christian revelation or tradition which rejects that any definitive statement can be given regarding what Christians believe or ought to believe, since all such statements are subject to revision:

- A true fidelity to the tradition must be one that leaves open the possibility that the orthodoxies of the present—including the orthodoxy that tells believers that the tradition has isolated a single recognizable and infallible orthodoxy, or that tells them what dogmatic definitions are and why they should be credited—may yet be surpassed by a fuller revelation of the truth, one that will be wholly given only at the end of tradition’s long historical course.[^c04-107]

Hart takes the upshot of his account that it does not require “either ideological dissimulation of the past … or submission to authority.”[^c04-108] Indeed, Hart endorses sympathetically the idea that it is false that “religion as a system is either true or false in a total sense,”[^c04-109] as every religion might have partial truths that are all encompassed in a greater unity which unites them all. Hart concludes his book by noting that defenders of an alternative notion of Christian faith, who begin with assumptions that faith is dogmatic, will be “entirely unpersuaded or offended by my argument.” For Hart, those who hold positions on which faith could impose some such definite requirements entail that “they and I mean very different, perhaps ultimately incompatible things when we speak of Christian tradition or revelation or faith or doctrine.”[^c04-110]

Hart therefore rejects not doctrinal development, but rather dogmatic definitions about propositional beliefs, because he takes it to be the case that nothing can be defined about Christianity’s definite propositional content, likely because Hart seems to have assumed in question-begging fashion (perhaps for philosophical reasons) that there _are_ no definite propositional beliefs which can be known to be taught in Scripture. I have already argued that these claims are false in Chapters 1 and 2. Further, like Nemes, Hart assumes that dogmatic definitions aim to propose a _comprehensive_ and _final_ grasp of the truth, such that no further definitions could be required, and that a dogmatic definition could not remain “binding” upon all future generations of Christian unless it aimed to propose such a comprehensive

---

<!-- PDF page 186 -->
**[p. 173]**

statement of the truth. But both these assumptions are also false. A heresy is a heresy in virtue of being such that its content is logically incompatible with Revelation. It could then not be the case that a dogmatic definition which accurately identifies and rejects a heresy could cease to be binding in the future, unless the content of Revelation ceased to be logically incompatible with that belief. But Revelation – God’s Word – undergoes (and can undergo) no change.

These objections are nevertheless self-defeating: Hart adheres to a vision of Christianity on which the only apparently knowable dogmatic truth of Christianity is that _there are no dogmas_. The account is incoherent, since if it is in principle indiscernible what the essence of Christianity is, we cannot discern that it is _essentially_ indiscernible, or that it is _sufficient for being a Christian_ (essential to Christian faith) to adopt that apocalyptic attitude. Hart’s objections to dogmatic authority – I will illustrate – instead rest upon a fideistic form of epistemological egoism. This is illustrated by the fact that Hart is vociferous in his insistence that an eternal hell is incompatible with true Christian tradition, despite being a matter of long-standing ecumenical consensus and even dogmatic definition.

Hart argues that belief in hell must be rejected even if it were taught in Revelation or by dogmatic definition: “even if some such dogma existed somewhere, there can be no excuse for believing such a thing on any grounds whatsoever, as there cannot possibly be anyone invested with sufficiently indubitable authority to impose so squalid a teaching upon a healthy conscience.”[^c04-111] Hart thereby indirectly concedes that the doctrine of hell (or its negation) makes a serious difference for the Christian faith and its transmission. It is not a matter incidental to Christianity whether there is an eternal hell. Yet Hart’s positions on dogmatic development would require that he suspend judgment in asserting any such fact regarding the doctrine of hell being _objectively_ inconsistent with Revelation. If we had _no_ normative reasons from faith in God to reject or accept the doctrine of an eternal hell and could make no judgment about the present obligations we had to do so, then Hart’s principles imply that we would be _obliged to suspend public judgment_ about whether there is a hell until the eschaton arrives. Yet, clearly, this is practically impossible, as grave consequences for how we might behave in the interim follow from the truths about hell or heaven (and other eschatological facts about the eschatological future). It seems to me, therefore, that Hart’s views (like those of Nemes) are inconsistent. Hart’s approach to the doctrine of hell belies his view that there is nothing continuous with Christianity beyond a practical attitude of apocalyptic expectation, and that nothing definite may be asserted to be essential to Christianity, and that no possible dogmatic truth could be publicly judged incompatible with faith in Christianity.

Hart’s responses also illustrate the _need_ for appeals to dogmatic authority in judging developments. There is a truth regarding whether God proposes the doctrine of hell for our belief, and so whether we have moral obligations to accept that doctrine in faith. Hart’s appeal to the judgment of the eschatological future is beside the point, since God’s Word makes demands on us _now._ God’s Word is not spoken simply in the future, but in the present, whether in Scripture or in the Church’s testimony to Scriptural teaching. Practically speaking, if we defer all those obligations to believe anything definite about God’s Word only to the

---

<!-- PDF page 187 -->
**[p. 174]**

eschatological future, then we have no obligations in the present to adhere to what God demands of us. An eschatological rule is therefore practically useless for fulfilling the Church’s duty to preach the Gospel, since the Church would be unable to judge any view’s incompatibility with the Word of God on this gnostic basis. Indeed, the eschatological perspective evacuates those moral demands entirely. As Matthew Levering noted in his review of Hart’s book, Hart’s theory is ultimately a gnostic one, in which the true tradition or Revelation “can only be “fitfully perceived, or at least sensed,” by those few persons spiritually and intellectually able to do so.”[^c04-112] And _every_ heretic would identify himself among these insightful few. In a similar fashion, Hart insists that other Christians ought to reject the authority of the Church on the strength of Hart’s own arguments to the contrary, so that it would be irrational for anyone to accept the Church’s teaching the doctrine of an eternal hell. Clearly, however, if we have no obligations to accept _anyone’s_ teaching about Revelation, we certainly do not have an obligation to accept Hart’s claims. And Hart simply begs the question in asserting his own views are beyond reasonable disagreement. They are not.[^c04-113]

Theories like those of Hart or Nemes therefore leave us with a picture on which nobody is obliged by God’s Word to accept whatever they fail to find independently reasonable, and that they have no obligations beyond their own estimation of what is reasonable to accept. Perhaps we can allow appeal to theological authority only within the bounds of what can be independently and conclusively shown to be authoritative, to the exclusion of all reasonable disagreement. But _no_ theological authority, whether of Scripture or the Church or of Christ Himself, can be “demonstrated” to every person in such a way as to put the matter beyond possibility of reasonable disagreement. Views like these entail that supernatural faith in God’s Word could never really make a _demand_ upon us. Such “faith” prefers the authority of private theologians to that of God, only allowing us to make demands upon other Christians by appeal to what is not God’s Word. But these end up as indistinguishable from principled agnosticism or skepticism. Rather than a convicted acceptance of God’s testimony about Himself, an act to which we are morally obliged by reason of God’s speech addressed to us, “faith” becomes a pious opinion proportionate to the strength of the evidence, to which we “faith” have no obligations beyond what we can prove independently of divine testimony or the testimony of the Church. This does not provide a theory of Christian faith, but a justification of heresy or even atheism, rather than a convicted acceptance of God’s testimony about Himself (to which we would be morally obligated).

A theory of dogmatic authority and development rests upon an underlying assumption that faith in God’s Word imposes moral requirements upon us to be accountable to that Word. Newman rejects the assumption of skeptics like Hart and Nemes that we cannot know and publicly demand of others in the Church that they adhere to God’s Word, or that God’s Word could not today in the present make those demands on believers for their faith. If there are, in principle, no possible legitimate doctrinal developments, God’s Word makes no demand on believers today to, e.g., accept or reject Hart’s views on hell. To reject dogmatic authority and development, then, involves implicit commitment that heresy cannot be _publicly_ identified

---

<!-- PDF page 188 -->
**[p. 175]**

by anyone, in such a way as to make moral claims on their faith. It would not therefore matter for our own lives whether we are heretics, just as it would not matter as regards our concern for other Christians that they reject heresies – we would have no individual or communal obligations to maintain the faith. Without dogmatic authority, Christians would be forced to concede that nobody today has such reasons that _bind them in conscience, as a moral duty of their theological faith_ to accept God’s Word as God’s Word, and thus that heresy could not possibly be a sin. However, this is not at all a scriptural attitude, which insists that God’s Word _does_ make demands upon our faith, and that heresy – culpably abandoning that Word – is a moral evil. “Whoever transgresses and does not abide in the doctrine of Christ does not have God. He who abides in the doctrine of Christ has both the Father and the Son. If anyone comes to you and does not bring this doctrine, do not receive him into your house nor greet him; for he who greets him shares in his evil deeds” (2 John 9–11).

Newman therefore argues that the assumption that there _have_ been legitimate dogmatic developments – and correspondingly the necessity for the Church in Christian history to have _given_ testimony to God’s Word infallibly – is not an exaggerated, unnecessary assumption, but is what is strictly necessary to rule out “the wanton admission of a principle which would necessarily lead to the most vexatious and preposterous scepticism, to take it for granted, before proof to the contrary, that the Christianity of the second, fourth, seventh, twelfth, sixteenth, and intermediate centuries is in its substance the very religion which Christ and His Apostles taught in the first, whatever may be the modifications for good or for evil which lapse of years, or the vicissitudes of human affairs, have impressed upon it.”[^c04-114] Believers are accountable to God’s judgment; their responsiveness to that Word requires them directly to accept what God wants them to believe, insofar as they know it. There is something wrong with a believer that holds that she should not be held to believe anything beyond what she can independently confirm to be true. For the same reason, there is something wrong with a Christian believer who does not think it would _matter at all_ whether the faith ceases to exist or whether her beliefs correspond to those of the apostles. Believers have indirect obligations to practice, confess, and maintain _the faith –_ that is, “the faith which was once for all delivered to the saints.” (Jude 1:3).

It is obvious that Scripture reveals to us great mysteries to be believed, and that “great questions exist in the subject-matter of which Scripture treats, which Scripture does not solve; questions too so real, so practical, that they must be answered, and, unless we suppose a new revelation, answered by means of the revelation which we have, that is, by development.”[^c04-115] For these reasons, we should conclude that God provided means for His Church – for believers in every age – to resolve those questions as they touch on what is serious to our faith. As Newman puts it:

If Christianity is both social and dogmatic, and intended for all ages, it must humanly speaking have an infallible expounder. Else you will secure unity of form at the loss of unity of doctrine, or unity of doctrine at the loss of unity of form; you will have to choose between a comprehension of opinions and a resolution into parties, between latitudinarian and sectarian error. You may

---

<!-- PDF page 189 -->
**[p. 176]**

be tolerant or intolerant of contrarieties of thought, but contrarieties you will have. … The doctrine of infallibility is a less violent hypothesis than this sacrifice either of faith or of charity. It secures the object, while it gives definiteness and force to the matter, of the Revelation.[^c04-116]

A person who holds that they should be immune from any accountability to other Christians in the Church as regards their faith, holding that they have hidden access to a true Christianity which is inaccessible to all others, is not a Christian believer at all, as they have no faith in God’s Word because it is God’s Word but only in themselves or their reasoning. Their attitudes instead evince exactly what the tradition has called “heresy.”

What I will argue in the next chapter is that disagreements among Christian churches, such as the Catholic, Orthodox, and most Protestant denominations, should be understood not in terms of disagreements about the existence or necessity of dogmatic _authority_ as such, but rather in terms of what is required for _exercising_ that authority. But what I will propose is that many dogmatic developments which are otherwise controversial can be understood in terms regarding what Christians already agree is required by moral demands for unity in faith and charity. Where we _fundamentally_ disagree regarding the exercise of dogmatic authority is potentially quite minor.

# 5. Safeguarding the Faith

For even if I should boast somewhat more about our authority, which the Lord gave us for edification and not for your destruction, I shall not be ashamed.

(2 Cor. 10:8)

The last chapter argued that dogmatic development is necessary; without developments that rule out heresies over time, those heresies would undermine our preaching of the Gospel, and we would need to abandon our moral obligations to ourselves or others to maintain the faith once transmitted through the apostles. Yet, since development is necessary, so too is authority. Theories of development are unsustainable without an account of dogmatic authority, since identifying normative developments requires that we can identify normative processes of acceptance or sanction. Without “political” constraints deriving from legitimate exercises of dogmatic authority, merely adverting to “epistemic” constraints would be insufficient to sanction those necessary developments of doctrine – i.e., those developments would lack appropriate dogmatic authority backing them, testifying to their conformity to the apostolic faith. Thus, development is simply another instance in which we need dogmatic authority. Development turns out to be necessary because there are obvious developments in Christian history that, if they were not sanctioned by God, Christians today would not have obligations in faith to accept those developments. These developments, I argued, pertain not only to uniquely Catholic or Orthodox doctrines, but to basic issues affecting all Christians, such as the canon of Scripture. Everyone, including Protestants, needs to appeal to dogmatic authority and to a theory of dogmatic development sanctioned by that authority to explain those normative reasons believers have _today_ to accept God’s Word as God’s Word in faith.

This chapter aims to cash out the promissory note made when I insisted (in those previous four chapters) that these conclusions regarding dogmatic authority should not be considered uniquely Catholic or Orthodox claims. These traditions are well-known to insist on the existence of binding, infallible dogmatic authority in the Church, but I intend to show in this chapter that all Christians can accept such claims, including Protestant Christians. My task will involve two steps. The first is to offer a prospective and hypothetical proposal regarding

DOI: 10.4324/9781003663485-5

---

<!-- PDF page 194 -->
**[p. 181]**

where the differences _do_ lie between these traditions, arguing that these differences do not undermine accepting dogmatic authority or development. The second is to offer a hypothetical path for all Christian traditions to overcome the most apparent dogmatic barriers to affirming dogmatic authority and development. These dogmas, I will argue, need present no insuperable obstacle to ecumenical support for dogmatic authority in a (reunified) Church. My case will rest upon arguing that these dogmas can be recognized to serve a _practically_ necessary role in safeguarding the Church from the dual dangers of schism and heresy.

Given the ambitious scope of this argument, I will _not_ undertake the complicated historical or systematic task of justifying comprehensively that Orthodox or mainstream Protestant traditions of every sort could or should be committed to these conclusions. This chapter will instead undertake the very limited aim of illustrating _possibilities_ for that acceptance, in a general way, by those sympathetic to the theological traditions that come from the Reformation. My case focuses only on two exemplary, obvious instances of dogmatic obstacles to reunion among Protestant, Orthodox, and Catholic Christians: dogmatic claims made by the Catholic Church regarding papal primacy and the Blessed Virgin Mary. What I am _not_ doing is arguing that these doctrines are true or biblical. What I _am_ doing is beginning from a charitable assumption that these doctrines are possibly biblical or true, in at least one interpretation, and then defending that a case can be made for these doctrines to be _dogmatically defined_. That is, assuming agreement on fundamentals, I will be arguing that a prospective case can be made for “ecumenical reception” where non-Catholic bodies can have internal reasons deriving from their own tradition to accept the suitability of these doctrines as dogmas within a reconstituted, reunified Christian Church. I begin from contemporary theological statements or agreements among Catholic, Orthodox, and Protestant theologians, showing that widespread agreement already exists on the fundamentals. Given this agreement on fundamentals, I leave aside historical or systematic evidence for papal primacy or these Marian dogmas or attempts to analyze their doctrinal development, only highlighting possible ways to understand what is essential to either dogma, which have proven largely acceptable to all parties within the contemporary theological dialogue. Then, I argue that each tradition has internal reasons to accept the _dogmatization_ of these beliefs; these teachings are suited to be solemnly taught and promulgated by the Church, when properly interpreted.

The upshot of this chapter’s argument is that we can _raise_ the probability of the hypothesis I will propose: that what divides Christians from each other is not necessarily any account of dogmatic authority, or the subsequent developments within each tradition, but only disagreements regarding necessary conditions for holding office within the Church. If that hypothesis is plausible, as I will argue it is, then Christians of different stripes could come to see each other’s traditions through an entirely sympathetic lens. Christianity is not as divided as we might otherwise think it is, and ecumenical reunion is much more achievable than has been previously thought.

---

<!-- PDF page 195 -->
**[p. 182]**

## A “Just-So” Hypothesis About Church Offices

Once upon a time, there were two theories of Church authority. On an “institutional” theory associated with Catholic and Orthodox ecclesiology, ecclesial authority is inherited from the apostles primarily through “valid” sacramental ordination. Only sacramentally ordained bishops and those in union with these bishops are the Church that the apostles founded, and – by implication – only a consensus among such bishops could be normative among Christians or constitute an exercise of the Church’s dogmatic authority. Being appointed by a local congregation might be one way of electing someone to become a bishop, but this election alone would be insufficient to constitute someone a bishop, absent sacramental ordination by which that person inherits their office as bishop by “apostolic succession.” Yet sacramental apostolic succession is not an individually sufficient criterion for holding legitimate church office either. The appointment and ordination to a Church office must _also_ have ultimately been recognized as _lawful_ by those historical churches (and their successors) which inherited their offices from the apostles. Similarly, dogmatic authority which would bind the universal Church occurs _only_ in accordance with lawful conditions for its exercise, again as so recognized by those historical churches (and their successors) which inherited their offices from the apostles. There are therefore two further necessary conditions according to which apostolic authority is inherited by the contemporary Church, aside from being baptized believers.[^c05-1]

As Gavin Ortlund notes, “most Protestants reject apostolic succession.”[^c05-2] Even among those Protestant groups who might accept apostolic succession in some sense, as Anglicans do in accepting ordination to the episcopacy as a _practical_ condition for exercising authority in their church, theologians in these traditions have rejected that the episcopacy was strictly _necessary_ for exercising ecclesiastical authority. Prominent Anglican divine Richard Hooker, for instance, held that valid sacramental ordination was not required by the constitution of the Church, i.e., _divine law_, for exercising ecclesiastical authority.[^c05-3] Protestants typically accept, nevertheless, that the ministry of the apostles is inherited by the post-apostolic Church. On what we can call a “charismatic” theory associated with Protestant ecclesiology, dogmatic authority is inherited from the apostles by each Christian, in virtue of receiving or believing in the historical apostolic preaching of the Gospel. All are part of the “apostolic” Church simply in virtue of receiving a faith that derives from the apostles.[^c05-4] As it is generally accepted that obedience to the Gospel and God’s commands therein requires baptism, baptism being a visible manifestation that someone has “received” the faith, this is often described as the “priesthood of all the baptized.” Nevertheless, as there could be potential disagreement whether that authority is inherited by sacramental baptism or by faith in Christ, it is better to characterize the view as being that _all Christian believers_ corporately inherit ecclesiastical authority.

Ortlund defends the rejection of apostolic succession as a requirement of ministry, because he argues that there is no good historical evidence either that “bishop” and “presbyter” refer to distinct offices in the earliest texts of the New Testament

---

<!-- PDF page 196 -->
**[p. 183]**

or early Church, or that there was any consciousness in the early Church that “the bishop functions in succession to the apostles.”[^c05-5] The first of these claims is ultimately neither here nor there. Indeed, many medieval theologians maintained (following St. Jerome[^c05-6]) that these two offices of presbyterate and episcopacy were not sacramentally distinct; some held that the episcopacy was a “state of perfection,” involving an office that was not constituted by a new sacramental Order but simply the ability to exercise jurisdiction over other presbyters.[^c05-7] While these opinions were ultimately rejected by Catholic and Orthodox dogmatic theology, they were not rejected for undermining apostolic succession. Determining whether apostolic succession is a necessary requirement for ministry refers to _valid sacramental ordination_, not to terminology regarding “bishop” or “presbyter.” So, if it turned out that presbyter and bishops were sacramentally identical offices, this is logically irrelevant to whether being ordained _to this office_ is required for exercising ministry in the Church legitimately.

Ortlund’s argument that the early Church did not recognize the bishop as a successor to the apostles’ ministry is likewise weak, because his argument involves presenting evidence that the institution of a “monarchical” episcopate – i.e., a norm where there is only bishop presiding over each local church – developed over time from an initial situation where there was no clear “monarchical” bishop inhabiting each diocese, but rather a council of “presbyter-bishops” who governed each church collegially. Ortlund recognizes that those who hold to institutional models of Church authority acknowledge this fact.[^c05-8] Ortlund fails to recognize, however, that the reason advocates of the institutional model can accept the development of the monarchical episcopate is that it is not logically an objection to their account of apostolic succession. The view that holding office requires valid sacramental ordination does not logically entail that each local church only has one bishop in office. The same is true for Ortlund’s appealing to evidence that bishops were elected by their local congregations. Such evidence is well-known, but beside the point for institutional models, since they only deny that election is sufficient by itself for exercising ministry without ordination. Thus, for example, Ortlund’s citation of the canon of Hippolytus, which directs, “Let the bishop be ordained after he has been chosen by all the people,” does not support the (charismatic) theory that the bishop would not need to be ordained after being so chosen by all the people.[^c05-9]

Modern scholars do not share Ortlund’s confident narratives regarding early Church office. Stewart argues that “any confident assertion about any true biblical model of ministry, or indeed any narrative of the originally collective leadership of congregations giving rise to ‘monarchical episcopacy’ within those congregations, is to be consigned to the dustbin of exegetical history.”[^c05-10] He presents a case based on the most recent historical evidence and scholarship that early Church offices were essentially episcopal and diaconal. “Presbyter” in the New Testament refers not to a distinct office but to _episcopoi_ considered as/acting as a college or group, according to Stewart, whereas the presbyterate (as a distinct office aside from episcopate) was an ultimately later development. That is, he argues that the offices of _episcopos_ and _presbyteros_ were distinct, without

---

<!-- PDF page 197 -->
**[p. 184]**

denying that evidence of a terminological synonymy or fluidity of sorts in the New Testament. These facts are then quite distinct from the rise of the monarchical episcopacy, which everyone acknowledges took time to develop. Stewart nevertheless concludes by noting that “the emergence of monepiscopacy from domestic episcopacy should be seen as a development in doctrine in itself, as much as a means by which doctrine might be developed in new ways, and the development of presbyteral office as such, which depends on the development of monepiscopacy, may be a legitimate political development enabling the life of the church to proceed.”[^c05-11]

Whatever we might say about the rightness of the development of the monarchical episcopate, it is beside the point regarding whether apostolic succession along the lines of the institutional model of authority correctly identifies a necessary condition for ministry. Historically too, Stewart’s treatment notes in conclusion that evidence regarding ordination in early Church history is unclear: “although the original ministry was episcopal-diaconal, we do not know how these episkopoi and diakonoi were appointed or ordained ….”[^c05-12] Yet even here we can be cautious. Classically, advocates of the institutional model see similar instances in the Scriptural witness that those who are appointed to ministry are appointed with prayer and laying on of hands, as in Acts 6:6 or 1 Tim 4:14. The lack of any fixed and universal form is no indication that there was no act which we can recognize retrospectively as “sacramental” in nature. And, indeed, anyone who succeeds to the office of ministry in the Church – as opposed to a simply political office – is not a holder of authority in virtue of representing the will of the “people” but because they inherit an authority in ministry that derives from Christ and His Holy Spirit. The very logic implied by these uncontroversial theological claims about ministry makes it highly unlikely that Christians of any age took election, by itself, to be sufficient to install anyone into an ecclesiastical ministry without _any_ act of prayer or invocation of the Spirit or Christ on those who were so elected.

Neither the classical Reformers nor modern Protestant theologians are as quick as Ortlund in denying the evidence of a historical pattern according to which apostolic succession through ordination was a necessary requisite for ministry.
