Wolfhart Pannenberg for instance has argued at length that, “as regards the understanding of the nature and commission of the church’s ministry the Roman Catholic and Reformation views no longer have insurmountable differences.”[^c05-13] While I cannot do justice to his full account, Pannenberg argues that the ordination of ministers and their exercise of pastoral authority can be understood by the Reformed tradition (especially confessional Lutherans) as involving succession to apostolic authority, given developments prompted by centuries of reflection and improvements in ecumenical dialogue.[^c05-14] For instance, while Luther held that each Christian held in principle the right to minister Word and sacrament, Luther and the Reformed tradition were clear that such authority could not properly be exercised by that believer without legitimate delegation – and this ends up, for all intents and purposes, as simply a variant understanding of the necessity of ordination.[^c05-15] Nor did Luther deny

---

<!-- PDF page 198 -->
**[p. 185]**

the need for episcopal ordination of presbyters in principle. Rather, Pannenberg argues, Luther only held that

in an emergency situation in which no episcopally ordained minister is available any Christian may be entrusted with the task of proclamation. … But such cases were not normal for Luther. … [He] affirmed the rule of apostolic succession by way of the bishops as bearers of an office that came down from the apostles, and he even regarded this rule as unchangeable. The emergency situation in his own day that made the institution of ministers in another way necessary did not alter what he had to say about this general rule. Hence even after 1530, when it had to set up its own order, the Lutheran Reformation still tried to observe the rule of apostolic succession so far as possible in spite of the fact that circumstances sometimes forced on it unavoidable divergence from the canonical regulation that only bishops have the authority to ordain.[^c05-16]

For reason illustrated by Luther’s position, there is nothing in the charismatic model of authority that needs to _deny_ that ordination could be a lawful condition of ministry, or even that these requirements on ministry are of “divine right,” broadly considered (it would not strictly require rejecting a “divine institution” of the episcopacy to hold that these requirements could be abrogated under appropriate conditions).[^c05-17] Nor is there anything in the charismatic theory of authority that rules out the necessity for lawful constraints on the exercise of authority _among_ a communion of local churches. While the charismatic model is often presented as a _negation_ or _denial_ of the institutional model, there is no logical necessity to construe the model in this way. Indeed, the institutional and charismatic models are logically compatible. The institutional model explicitly holds only that sacramental ordination is _a necessary condition_ for ministry, not a _sufficient_ condition, as the lawfulness of appointment to office is also a necessary condition. Conversely, while the charismatic model typically holds that election by the people is a sufficient condition for ministry, in terms of what is required by divine law, there is nothing in these models that necessarily _rejects_ other grounds for holding that sacramental ordination or lawful historical succession could be required for their legitimacy. Pannenberg himself is supportive of proposals to ensure Reformation churches maintain these traditional requirements for episcopal ordination, seeing them as existing fundamentally in service of the unity of the Church.[^c05-18]

## Extra Ecclesiam Nullus Salus

Ortlund’s case against apostolic succession seems to rest on overconfidence and question-begging interpretation of the evidence. But, as it stands, there is nothing about the charismatic theory of Church authority _as such_ which requires rejecting the necessity of ordination for ministry, just as there is nothing about institutional theories which requires rejecting that each minister ought to be elected by their congregation. Further, the charismatic theory is not incompatible with there being a dogmatic consensus existing in the Church, which would be identifiable through

---

<!-- PDF page 199 -->
**[p. 186]**

the dogmatic sanction of the baptized Christian community’s legitimate pastoral representatives. Nor does the charismatic theory rule out that the whole Church could, by appointing legitimate representatives, decide matters of universal Church policy in a binding “ecumenical council.”

All that the charismatic theory would seemingly imply is that any such decisionmaking would need to be truly representative of the whole population of believers, being properly delegated to that task. On the charismatic theory, no individual believer can exercise that authority in a way as to bind all others without their having in some normative way been appropriately delegated, but it would be possible to imagine at least _some_ scenarios on which these characteristics are uncontroversially met, e.g., if each congregation everywhere elected representatives to a universal council, and the conciliar deliberations on all decisions achieved almost total consensus, it would be hard to see what is objectionable (as regards requirements of the charismatic model) about such an exercise of Church authority. Dogmatic authority (and the making of dogmatic definitions) _need not_ involve any “tyranny” but could in principle be exercised entirely only at the behest of the “governed,” with their implicit consent.

Perhaps Ortlund and those who endorse charismatic models of authority generally are not _really_ objecting to apostolic succession. While Ortlund admits that the monoepiscopate or even ordination is compatible with a Reformed theory of authority, Ortlund’s chief target is the _requirement_ of any such thing for the validity of ministry. Any such requirement, he alleges, “unnecessarily injures and divides the church” – Protestantism is, by contrast, presented by Ortlund as the only way we “can be free from such a requirement.”[^c05-19] Now it seems initially hard to understand what rationale Ortlund gives for his conclusion that such requirement is bad for the Church. Clearly, if Pannenberg and Stewart are correct that monoepiscopacy could be practically required for unity among churches, and thus a legitimate doctrinal development in practice, there is no good reason to think that the (ordinary) requirement of the monoepiscopacy would unnecessarily injure or divide the Church. That structure would instead be required to _prevent disunity_ among Christians. Similarly, if the institutional model of authority is correct that sacramental ordination and lawful appointment through a historical apostolic church are necessary requirements of ministry, these are constitutive elements of the “constitution” of the Church – i.e., God’s will for its structure. And, clearly, God’s will for the Church cannot unnecessarily injure or divide it.

Ortlund identifies as “exclusivism” that which is objectionable in requirements of apostolic succession for legitimate ministry. Ortlund’s objections to apostolic succession are an instance of a wider case against Catholicism and Orthodoxy, which he critiques for denying that not only that Protestant sacraments are valid or salvific (due to the lack of valid sacramental ordination by Protestant ministers), but more fundamentally that the Orthodox and Catholic Church “sees itself, to the exclusion of the others, as the original church Christ founded.”[^c05-20] This points to a more fundamental issue that divides Protestant from Catholic and Orthodox Christians, exemplified by the institutional model of ecclesiastical authority: most of those who adhere to a charismatic theory of ecclesiastical authority (like Ortlund)

---

<!-- PDF page 200 -->
**[p. 187]**

object in principle to the very idea that belonging to _one visible apostolic Church_ could possibly be a requirement for salvation. Yet, I will show that the sweeping critique Ortlund makes against “institutional exclusivism” is inconsistent. Those who reject “institutional exclusivism” cannot do so consistently if they also take there to be moral demands made by faith and charity toward our fellow Christians, as Ortlund does. Ortlund’s criticisms are based on question-begging assumptions about what _is_ essential to the faith, rather than denying the existence of those moral demands. All Ortlund does is identify a _different morally relevant institution_ than that identified by Catholics or Orthodox Christians.

Ortlund takes as the target of his critique the view that “to be outside the church is to be cut off from the grace of the Holy Spirit given in the sacraments and therefore cut off from salvation … [so that] there are no schisms _in_ the church, only schisms _from_ her.”[^c05-21] As noted in regard to apostolic succession, Ortlund means to highlight that Catholics and Orthodox deny that Protestant ministers confer valid sacraments (aside from baptism administered in the name of the Trinity). And, as Ortlund further highlights, the Catholic and Orthodox ecclesiological model denies that even those churches which have valid sacraments, namely, schismatic churches which have violated conditions of lawful communion with other historical apostolic churches, can confer salvation by means of these sacraments, since they exist in a state of separation _from_ the one true Church.[^c05-22] What Ortlund adverts to is that the “institutional model” of ecclesiastical authority entails that there are moral consequences for, e.g., ordaining ministers unlawfully, celebrating sacraments without lawfully ordained ministers, etc. These are all _sinful_ acts in virtue of these acts constituting culpably breaking communion with the Church; such acts are all instances of the sin of _schism_.

Ortlund objects that we could recognize the moral value of sacraments or prayers celebrated without any sacramentally ordained ministers. He poses two different examples of this. The first is that of “a remote village in the Middle East where a number of Muslims begin having dreams in which Jesus appears to them and reveals himself as Lord. They respond with repentance and baptism, and a small community of worshiping Christians is formed.”[^c05-23] This first example seems rather beside the point, for a few reasons. First, as a matter of fact, Catholic and Orthodox theology simply accept that baptism administered in such situations is valid, because baptism does not require ordination to administer. So, it is obvious that those who are baptized in this case are validly baptized and therefore become _ipso facto_ members of the one true Church just as much as anyone. Second, the institutional model does not hold that having sacramental ordination conferred lawfully is a condition of salvation as such, but of holding true ecclesiastical authority. The moral consequences for salvation follow regarding _rejecting_ the authority of the Church, either in regard to those who hold authority lawfully or its lawful procedures in constituting a local church (e.g., ordaining ministers lawfully). But the example involves no such rejection, so it isn’t a _relevant_ counterexample for the institutional model.

Ortlund’s second example specifies that the people of the village read the Bible, “come to believe in Jesus as the Son of God and Savior of the world,” and then

---

<!-- PDF page 201 -->
**[p. 188]**

progressively discover more facts about their belief. The village “eventually discovers the Apostles’ Creed and recites it every Sunday during their worship celebration. Their celebration of the Eucharist is marked by tears of joy.”[^c05-24] In this second case, the village is apparently engaging in celebration of the Eucharist, presumably by simply deputing one member to preside. Catholic and Orthodox Christians can concede that, in this case, the person celebrating does not possess _apostolic_ authority through ordination to do this.[^c05-25] But even in this second example, Ortlund has given those holding the institutional model no reason to think that this village or person is doing anything _wrong,_ i.e., that they are intentionally or culpably _rejecting_ any objective requirements for rightful church authority. They simply are coming progressively to a greater understanding of what their moral obligations are, without having any good way to know the full extent of their obligations.

Further, while Catholic and Orthodox theology are not going to admit that the Eucharist is celebrated in the sense that (for example) transubstantiation occurs, there is nothing about their model that would require them to state that God _would not_ work through or even confer _sacramental grace_ on those who partook in this quasi-Eucharistic celebration. For instance, it was a custom in the medieval period in cases of danger of death, if no priest were available, for lay persons to hear each other’s confessions and administer a symbolic “viaticum” with blessed bread or even leaves or grass.[^c05-26] Thomas Aquinas, as a chief example of the medieval Catholic consensus position around such practices, accepts that in such cases that the penitent had received forgiveness of his sins, “for the reason that he fulfilled, so far as he could, the purpose which he conceived in accordance with God’s command,” although Aquinas notes that such a person would need to confess to a priest before being admitting to the sacraments – Aquinas compares this to the fact that someone can receive the graces of baptism in virtue of desiring to be baptized, but would not be admitted to the Eucharist or other sacraments until they are sacramentally baptized.[^c05-27] In these cases, then, even as staunch an advocate of the institutional model as Aquinas does not reject that God’s grace, and the forgiveness of sins, can be given through non-sacramental acts which _aim_ at expressing a desire for the sacraments, as long as those acts do not manifest contempt for the Church’s authority or religion more generally. In the same way, Aquinas could analyze Ortlund’s second example as an instance where the Eucharist was not sacramentally confected validly, since there was no validly ordained priest, but where the sacramental graces of the Eucharist were indeed conferred.

By the same token, it is open to those who hold an institutional model to simply concede that, by all outward signs, those in Ortlund’s examples become full members of the one true Church even though they are unaware of its authoritative leadership or other aspects. And this would not be surprising, since someone can become a believing Christian without embracing all beliefs “entailed under closure” from those contained in Scripture (even though some of those beliefs might be very important), as long as the believer makes a good faith attempt to do what they believe is required of them. That’s a natural consequence of the moral demands made by faith, as it regards our moral capacity to respond to God within our epistemic situation. Yet, the fact that extraordinary situations exist in which

---

<!-- PDF page 202 -->
**[p. 189]**

a believer is _morally excused_ from ordinary demands of faith does not show us that no such demands apply in other situations. For instance, even Ortlund would likely concede that a moral fault would exist if the Muslim believers who have those dreams of Christ, and hear the call to be baptized, and nevertheless reject that call to be baptized by alleging that salvation can be achieved without baptism. Presumably, such persons are _not_ believing at all, because they are rejecting God’s call to be baptized. Even though it is true that someone can be saved without being baptized, that is not to the point, because the moral demands on _those believers who hear the call_ are distinct from the extraordinary situation. Similarly, if someone knew that the Church taught the celebration of the Eucharist was restricted to validly ordained priests, and ignored this teaching, then the act would involve contempt of the law. Contempt for that dogmatic authority is where the possibility of sins like heresy or schism enters in, on the institutional model, not simply acting innocently _aside from_ ecclesiastical authority.[^c05-28]

Ortlund’s position is mistaken historically speaking, since Aquinas admits that grace can be given extra-sacramentally, when the recipient does not act in contempt of proper authority or sacraments. Ortlund, however, alleges that Catholics and Orthodox cannot avoid the consequences of their institutional model as seemingly pertains to heretics and schismatics. Ortlund argues that the view that no heretic or schismatic could be saved or be in the grace of God, in any way, was representative of the traditional view of almost all Christians before the modern era. Ortlund therefore thinks that such claims should be strictly interpreted to rule out salvation or forgiveness of sin for anyone other than Roman Catholics or Orthodox Christians in good standing.[^c05-29] And Ortlund implies that such positions fly in the face of obvious facts that non-Catholic or Orthodox Christians seem to be practicing Christianity in good faith! Protestants can be virtuous in exemplary _supernatural_ ways as much as Catholics: “Satan cannot produce virtues. He is incapable of producing joy, peace, goodness, kindness, love for Christ, love for God, love for the Holy Spirit, love for the Scripture, love for truth. Only Christ produces these things. Therefore, such fruit testifies to the genuine work of Christ.”[^c05-30] Therefore, Ortlund implies, the fact that Protestants have God’s grace, despite acting outside of the one, true Church as identified by the institutional model, is a counterexample to that model.

Nevertheless, nothing about the institutional model – as we have already seen – requires denying that God’s grace can be given extra-sacramentally. And nothing about that model requires denying that someone could find themselves without properly ordained ministers, through no fault of their own, and end up mistaking invalidly ordained ministers for proper Church authorities, or that it would be generally impossible to receive grace through sacraments celebrated outside of Catholic communion. By the same principles Aquinas appealed to earlier, we can infer that Aquinas would think it is possible to receive God’s grace from a heretical or schismatic minister, just as a non-properly ordained one, if the recipient was acting in good faith and inculpably (“invincibly”) unaware of these facts. In fact, Aquinas explicitly endorses that, if one cannot be baptized save by a schismatic, heretic, Jew, or pagan in case of necessity (i.e., danger of death), then one _ought_

---

<!-- PDF page 203 -->
**[p. 190]**

to seek baptism from them. The fact that the minister is out of communion with the Church (and that such sacramental requests are not ordinarily lawful) does not constitute a sin on their part – and, we can infer, this sacramental act bestows grace upon the recipient.[^c05-31]

So, there is nothing in the scenario presented by Ortlund that cannot be accommodated within the institutional model as a case where those who exemplify such virtues and grace in their life, despite participating in Protestant worship or belief, are not viciously, sinfully rejecting the authority of the Church. They can be blamelessly unaware of those (objective) demands. Consequently, their baptism, prayers, participation in quasi-sacramental Eucharistic celebrations, etc., is not any obstacle to obtaining God’s grace by these means, since such persons have not _culpably_ put themselves outside of the Church, despite being in “impaired” or “partial” communion with the Church (given the fact that they operate outside of it, without full participation in its life, as rightly constituted). This is precisely the way in which the Catholic Church has described its relations to non-Catholic Christians, as “imperfectly” in communion with the Catholic Church by reason of their faith and baptism – “the children who are born into these Communities and who grow up believing in Christ cannot be accused of the sin involved in the separation ….”[^c05-32]

Ortlund recognizes that the Catholic and Orthodox churches have, in modern times, moved away from affirming a restrictive sense in which nobody is saved outside of visible communion as a _Catholic or Orthodox_ Christian, but explicitly countenance that Protestant Christians, non-Christians such as Jews or Muslims, and even atheists or agnostics might be saved. For instance, the teaching of the Catholic ecumenical council Vatican II qualifies the teaching “nobody is saved outside the Church” as follows:

Basing itself on Scripture and Tradition, the Council teaches that the Church, a pilgrim now on earth, is necessary for salvation: the one Christ is the mediator and the way of salvation; he is present to us in his body which is the Church. He himself explicitly asserted the necessity of faith and Baptism, and thereby affirmed at the same time the necessity of the Church which men enter through Baptism as through a door. Hence they could not be saved who, knowing that the Catholic Church was founded as necessary by God through Christ, would refuse either to enter it or to remain in it.[^c05-33]

The Catholic Church does not abandon claims that nobody is saved outside the one, true, unique Church but distinguishes: as it alone possesses the full doctrinal and sacramental means for salvation (the Word _fully_ rightly preached, and sacraments _fully_ rightly administered), the Church of Christ “subsists in” the Catholic Church[^c05-34] – term chosen specifically to distinguish that “some and even very many of the significant elements and endowments which together go to build up and give life to the Church itself, can exist outside the visible boundaries of the Catholic Church: the written word of God; the life of grace; faith, hope and charity, with the other interior gifts of the Holy Spirit, and visible elements too.”[^c05-35] In this way, the Catholic Church can affirm that not only Protestant ecclesial communities and

---

<!-- PDF page 204 -->
**[p. 191]**

Orthodox churches have the Word and sacraments, but that there is an underlying unity among these, since all graces flow from and to the one visible Church. “All of these, which come from Christ and lead back to Christ, belong by right to the one Church of Christ.”[^c05-36] There is therefore a compelling cause for unity precisely because nobody is saved outside of the Church, even when saved outside of its visible boundaries.

Ortlund’s critique at this point shifts away from “institutional exclusivism” and rather to the possibility of development of doctrine on this point. Ortlund objects that such a move away from the restrictive sense in which “nobody outside the Church could be saved” was previously interpreted also stands in strict contradiction with past infallible, magisterial, and irreformable teachings of the Catholic and Orthodox Church. Ortlund takes as a particularly egregious example of such a dogmatic contradiction the contrast between the above statement of Vatican II and that of the medieval papal bull _Unam Sanctum_, which declared that “it is absolutely necessary for the salvation of all human creatures that they submit to the Roman pontiff.”[^c05-37] Ortlund alleges that “virtually no one in the medieval church interpreted Unam Sanctam [sic] like post-Vatican II Catholics do today. … post–Vatican II Roman Catholics have to interpret it differently from the way virtually everyone interpreted it when it was written.”[^c05-38] Ortlund is objecting that any explanations which would introduce a qualification into the teaching of documents like _Unam Sanctam_ and thereby avoid contradiction between Vatican II’s declaration and previous magisterial statements are _ad hoc_. Nobody in the past would have been aware of those qualifications; indeed, Ortlund implies that authors of these previous documents would have denied them.

Ortlund rejects appeal on these points to “development of doctrine” to explain the shift in Catholic/Orthodox practice and teaching.

If hardly any Roman Catholic Christians held this view prior to the modern era, why should it be allowed as an authentic interpretation of the doctrine “no salvation outside the church,” rather than a change in its meaning? This is a significant problem with the Roman Catholic notion of doctrinal development: its tendency to move the goalposts in order to make the goal. Besides representing a potential falsification of the Roman Catholic claim of infallibility, changes in the “no salvation outside the church” doctrine raise a practical concern about the actual value of an allegedly infallible magisterium. … Is such an entity really the solution to division and confusion if nearly everyone can misunderstand it for six hundred years? How do we know that current universal understanding of magisterial teaching won’t be similarly overturned in another six hundred years?[^c05-39]

Ortlund thus objects to the possibility of development of doctrine on _any issue_ where Christians in the past were completely unaware of the future developments in doctrine or practice, one way or another, or even appear to have denied those developments when presented with them in their own day. For this reason, Ortlund’s point is not restricted to _Unam Sanctum,_ but to many other issues, such as

---

<!-- PDF page 205 -->
**[p. 192]**

icon veneration, papal primacy, or the Immaculate Conception or Assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary. Ortlund points out, for instance, that ancient Christians were entirely unaware of the Blessed Virgin’s bodily assumption or of the liceity of venerating icons. There are instances where the Church Fathers state beliefs in contradiction with later dogmas, as when some seemingly held (like Basil or John Chrysostom or Origen) that Mary committed at least some venial sins, or (like Aquinas) argued that Mary was subject to original sin, or (like Epiphanius) rejected the practice of depicting saints within the church building.

Newman’s _Essay on Development,_ in fact, already noted all this data and more besides. But Newman noted that this problem is not unique to Catholicism or Orthodoxy. Fathers like Gregory of Nyssa denied that anyone would be punished eternally in hell, many ancient apologists were seemingly “subordinationist” about Jesus Christ’s status as less-than-equal to the Father, Augustine and many other Fathers accepted the “deuterocanonical” books of the Catholic/Orthodox canon as canonical (which Protestants do not), and so forth. Ortlund’s own historical case against Catholicism/Orthodoxy works against him, since he himself demonstrates a practically unanimous, long-standing consensus of the Church in favor of “institutional exclusivism,” holding that nobody outside the Church is saved, or that unbaptized infants are excluded from salvation. If Ortlund argues that we should reject such claims, while affirming the necessity of Christian belief in the possibility of eternal damnation, the Protestant canon of Scripture, or the _homoousios_ equality of Christ with His Father, then Ortlund’s own position _requires_ an account of dogmatic development to explain why we should hold that the views he takes to be essentials (or as incompatible with Christian faith) should be considered as such today, and one that would require that ancient Christians were not _explicitly aware_ of many issues being dogmatic that Ortlund takes to be essential.

Newman’s theory aimed to provide a framework in which we consider the _content_ of what the Fathers believed. Newman argued, historically, that many of the apparent historical “contradictions” or “reversals” were a preservation of what was _in fact affirmed_ by the Fathers, despite appearing to be a change in position, because there were often implicit qualifications or context that (when considered) made clear what the Fathers actually held or intended to teach. For instance, I have already shown that Aquinas himself recognized that sacramental grace could sometimes involve extra-sacramental reception, in extraordinary conditions, and that there were conditions under which people operate morally in invincible ignorance. We can therefore draw a line of inference from Aquinas’ qualifications regarding extra-sacramental reception of grace to the possibility that those who participate in improperly celebrated sacraments receive grace, as well as that they are not committing any sin if they thereby express no contempt for the Church’s order or religion – these are logically analogous situations with no apparent morally relevant difference. The case illustrates that the question of development of doctrine, then, concerns not so much whether, e.g., Aquinas explicitly considered and introduced qualifications such that those born in heretical or schismatic ecclesial bodies could be saved or receive grace, but whether _what Aquinas taught_ can support such qualifications.

---

<!-- PDF page 206 -->
**[p. 193]**

And it is not hard to see that such a similar qualification is plausible in the case of _Unam Sanctam._ Vatican II glosses the phrase “necessary for salvation” in terms of what it calls a “necessity of means,” meaning that nobody who _culpably rejects_ those moral demands or requirements imposed by God’s will to enter the Church can be saved. There is no reason to think that Boniface’s declaration would not admit there to be a necessity of means in the same way that individuals submit to the Roman Pontiff, since Boniface does not plausibly mean that everyone must always be consciously submitting to the Roman Pontiff at every moment to be saved, or that it would be impossible for the Old Testament patriarchs to be saved (since the Roman Pontiff did not exist at the time of Moses). Similarly, even medieval popes were explicitly aware of the moral conditions under which an act is not sinful if it is not culpable. For these reasons, it is highly plausible that what _Unam Sanctam_ means to say is that culpably to reject communion and submission to the Roman Pontiff would constitute a grave sin (of schism). But that is exactly what Vatican II continues to state the moral requirements to be – there is no obvious _ad hoc_ shift in meaning.

For similar reasons, while it is true that past popes or councils did not also foresee the later teaching that Jews or Muslims could be saved, classical moral theology likewise admitted conditions on moral culpability and various other possibilities which later Catholic teaching appealed to in carving out the qualifications which currently are employed to think about such cases. The way these arguments appeal to the very distinctions drawn by prior theologians to construct those later qualifications gives us at least _prima facie_ reason to think that the earlier theologians _could have_ agreed with the later qualification, if it was explained to them appropriately. Ortlund is too quick to assume that later theologians would necessarily reject later extensions of their views simply because they did not, at the time they were working, explicitly consider them (I grant this hypothetically, although it is an empirical question). So, even if Boniface as a private theologian would himself have rejected that application put to his views by Vatican II, it is not quite the same thing to conclude that this application is an illegitimate extension of the doctrine taught by him, as that faith belongs to the Church and not to Boniface. Consider that many think Aristotle’s own attitude toward “natural slavery” is fundamentally inconsistent with his own principles – what Aristotle _would say today_ is not necessarily relevant to whether _his principles_ are inconsistent with that conclusion. What is more central is what Aristotle’s principles actually were and what they entailed, logically speaking. The same is true of dogmatic development, I argued, given the fact that Christian doctrine is objectively that which Scripture teaches, or what the Church defines, not simply what anyone believes Scripture or the Church to teach.

It is therefore true, in line with the epistemic constraints on development introduced in the last chapter, that a later definition (or matter of normative consensus) cannot logically contradict a previous definition (or matter of normative consensus), let alone what Scripture teaches. But a contradiction is not proven simply in virtue of an absence of explicit witness to the later doctrine in earlier Christian consensus, or even in virtue of an _apparent_ or _nominal_ contradiction between the wording of a later definition in comparison to an earlier definition. Conversely,

---

<!-- PDF page 207 -->
**[p. 194]**

the fact that one explains apparent counterevidence in history as a merely apparent or nominal contradiction is no proof that such explanations are necessarily _ad hoc_. This is not unique to dogmatic development, since we find the same thing in Scripture itself – e.g., the fact that one can find an apparent or nominal contradiction between passages is not conclusive evidence that there is such a contradiction. If offering explanations on which passages are merely apparent contradictions was illegitimate, then the presence of even one apparent contradiction would be proof that Scripture is uninspired. No Christian would accept such a hermeneutical constraint – and for good reason, since it is unreasonable.

Whether any dogmatic developments are legitimate, then, is decided in line with general epistemic constraints, but deciding whether those constraints are violated is often a tricky business, just as interpreting Scripture is tricky. Sometimes there is apparent or truly reasonable disagreement. The proof is in the pudding of these arguments, naturally. But proving that a given belief does not contradict the teaching of Scripture or of prior consensus or definitions is not sufficient to prove that this belief is a legitimate _dogmatic development_, i.e., an authoritative or normative belief we ought to hold today, following from what God proposes for our belief in Scripture. That is because we cannot prove that any theological belief is _true_ except by appeal to God’s authority – there is no independent means to “confirm” what is proposed for faith. It was for this reason that I noted Newman’s account ultimately appeals to _political_ constraints alongside epistemic ones. Only appeal to a dogmatic authority would give believers sufficient normative reasons to decide whether a given dogmatic belief really _is_ taught by God, making moral demands on our faith, since only God’s authority (derivatively exercised by those He sends) is sufficient to do so. Only prophets and apostles can speak God’s Word; only the Church Christ founded carries His promise that they will be guided by the Holy Spirit into all truth. Thus, deciding which group represents that Church will be the ultimate criterion according to which whether those beliefs which might otherwise seem to receive scanty support in Scripture are to be accepted as ultimately grounded in Scripture and demanding our belief – including most notably _which canon of Scripture_ is the authoritative one.

The arguments of previous chapters did not depend upon any institutional models of ecclesial authority, so there is no reason to think charismatic models can escape this problem of authority either. Evangelical appeals to the “reception of the canon by the Church” or the forming of a historical consensus against various heresies are just such an appeal to dogmatic authority. Yet, the charismatic model also entails just the same “institutional exclusivism” that Catholic/Orthodox models do, as I will now illustrate. Ortlund sums up his view as follows: “what distinguishes the Protestant view of the church is not a denial of the visible church but rather the claim that this visible church coheres within multiple institutions. To put it negatively, Protestantism denies the claim that any one institutional hierarchy constitutes the ‘one true church.’”[^c05-40] We have already seen in Luther and Pannenberg that denying “institutional hierarchy,” in the sense of the necessity of valid sacramental ordination, does not require denying a lawfulness condition attaching to our appointment of Church leaders. That is, even while denying that it

---

<!-- PDF page 208 -->
**[p. 195]**

is necessary for holding ecclesial authority that one is sacramentally ordained by a validly ordained bishop, the charismatic model does _not_ entail that anyone can “appoint themselves” a minister or that anyone at all can elect another person a pastoral minister for a particular local church. Being lawfully or legitimately deputed to act for a community involves clear political constraints, so, e.g., an election conducted under duress would not be a _valid_ or _legitimate_ election, and it would not follow (on the charismatic model) that the ministers thereby elected under duress come to have ecclesiastical authority. There are institutional criteria for ministers outlined in Scripture (e.g., 1 Tim 3), which the charismatic model by itself gives us no reason to think fail to apply today.

But there are also those features which imply that the Church has fixed normative elements of ecclesial structure. Ortlund himself identifies two such conditions: possessing the Word, including the canon of Scripture, as well as those sacraments instituted by Christ.[^c05-41] And these elements are normative in virtue of divine institution: “The church’s origin is a miraculous gift of the Holy Spirit; it cannot be a creation of human willings. For that reason, it must have a government of its own; polity is not a matter of complete adiaphora. While Scripture is not a book of church order, it does provide important principles that cannot be ignored without spiritual injury. Although circumstances affect particulars of church order, the churches are not at liberty to abolish offices or give their task to civil authority.”[^c05-42] If Ortlund’s convert group in the desert had instead decided to celebrate their “Eucharist” with pig’s blood, and did not acknowledge most of the canon of Scripture as inspired, these groups would objectively _not_ be members of the Church, by Ortlund’s own criteria, even if they would consider themselves to be such and protest that it is unfair to exclude them as Christians. And Ortlund could rightly argue that their failure to practice their Eucharist according to the words of Christ (with bread and wine) illustrated a failure to obey His Word, just as their failure to adhere to the canon of Scripture placed them objectively outside of the group of those Christian believers who do adhere to the canon as received by the Church. What it comes down to, in the end, is whether those groups are faithful to the _Word_ as received.

Now, Ortlund is inclusive to a large degree. For instance, he is willing to call the Catholic Church a true church “insofar as she has Christian people, Christian sacraments, and Christian doctrines.”[^c05-43] But Ortlund also protests that Catholic/Orthodox practices of venerating icons are more than simply difference of opinion with other Christians. Such practices are (for Ortlund) strictly _idolatrous_ and sinful, despite the practice and liceity of icon veneration being dogmatically defined and enjoined by these churches.[^c05-44] After arguing that the Catholic/Orthodox churches cannot appeal to such practices as legitimate developments of doctrine, given the lack of explicit awareness of these practices in the early Church,[^c05-45] Ortlund concludes by arguing that the Protestant position is superior on this point as: “The Protestant tradition … allows us to obey the second commandment. Further, it obligates no anathemas.”[^c05-46] But, indeed, these two juxtaposed claims that icon veneration is a violation of the second commandment _and_ that it obligates no anathemas is hard to make sense of. If icon veneration violates the second commandment, i.e., _is_ idolatrous, in what way should Protestants like Ortlund accept the doctrine or practices

---

<!-- PDF page 209 -->
**[p. 196]**

of idolaters? To quote Ortlund’s own words: “Given the dire mentality of Scripture regarding idolatry, why are such practices not regarded with greater concern?”[^c05-47]

Ortlund’s own account of an “anathema” is that to pronounce an anathema is to state that someone is committing a grave sin, or endorsing a “damnable heresy,” which would warrant exclusion from the fellowship of believers.[^c05-48] But, since Ortlund acknowledges that idolatry is a damnable sin, it is strange to think it would be inappropriate not to anathematize those who commit idolatry. If the dogmatic binding declaration regarding the liceity of icon veneration is not a damnable heresy, by contrast, and is only a variant theological opinion that makes no difference for the salvation of those who practice icon veneration or insist that others are bound by faith to do so, then what would be the justification not to submit to these same practices and accept ecclesial communion with such persons? Indeed, to say that endorsing the liceity of icon veneration is not a damnable heresy would clearly imply it is licit for Christians to venerate icons in good faith. Even if we reject the propriety of ecclesial sanctions or anything like “coercion” imposed against icon veneration, Ortlund himself is _stating_ that icon veneration is sinful. Why should it therefore be inappropriate for the Church corporately to _state_ (in a definition or something like it) that icon veneration is not licit? Indeed, the Church’s ministers would seem to have moral obligations to do this, if Ortlund were correct, since the Church’s ministers exist precisely for the purpose of helping members confess, practice, and maintain the faith. Clearly, if they have such obligations, the Church’s ministers would have moral obligations to do what they could to stop people from believing icon veneration is legitimate or engaging in it.[^c05-49]

Ortlund elsewhere notes that Protestants _do_ “set boundaries to what the church is,” but only deny that those boundaries are “determined institutionally.”[^c05-50] But these boundaries _are_ institutional, _pace_ Ortlund; they only represent a “charismatic” theory of leadership within that institution rather than one that depends upon sacramental ordination. There are ways to commit a sin in terms of those institutional requirements, even within the charismatic model, such as to coerce a congregation to elect a minister against their will, or to corrupt the practice of the Word or sacrament. But the most basic institutional requirement has gone overlooked by Ortlund, because it is so obvious: to be a member of the Church requires _right faith_ on at least the essentials. Ortlund rejects Catholic dogmatic requirements on that faith as “accretions” which “added requirements on to the gospel that Christ himself would not require,” but is at pains to emphasize that he does not endorse a picture on which there are no requirements at all.[^c05-51] It would be unsurprising if Ortlund would exclude Arians (who reject the divinity of Jesus) as failing to have right faith, and thereby excluding themselves as members of the Church – as failing to receive the Word in its integrity. So, while Ortlund might admit that even Catholics believe what is necessary to be members of the Church, it is false that anyone at all who believes themselves to be Christian counts (for Ortlund) as a member of the Church.

But that simply begs the question: how does Ortlund determine what _is_ a “damnable heresy” which would exclude someone from membership in the Church? Ortlund expresses the sentiment that what is essential to Christian faith is simple and obvious, and that Protestantism sets the dogmatic boundaries very low,

---

<!-- PDF page 210 -->
**[p. 197]**

seeking to “pare off novel deviations from catholicity and to return to those mainstream practices and beliefs that can be plausibly related back to apostolic teaching,” but Ortlund never clearly states what the _content_ of that “apostolic teaching” is.[^c05-52] And it looks obvious that some beliefs potentially have widespread logical implications, which would require us to deny the content of that teaching, no matter how minimal the content is. The mere fact we are obliged by God to believe _something definite_ is what generates moral obligations indirectly to reject what is incompatible with God’s Word. At one point, for instance, Ortlund notes that “at the heart of what the Protestants sought to recover was the Pauline teaching that ‘one is justified by faith apart from works of the law’” (Rom. 3:28).[^c05-53] But even interpreting this doctrine involves quite a lot of dogmatic work, as evinced by the dogmatic history of Protestant theologians who disagreed among themselves (as well as with Catholics) regarding what the _content_ of this view entailed and _required_ us to believe.

Ortlund appeals to Scripture alone as the one infallible standard to which we are required to adhere in determining these questions.[^c05-54] But what we disagree upon here is not whether to adhere to Scripture but rather _what Scripture requires_. And Scripture does not resolve that question. Ortlund, however, blurs the issue so that adhering to Scripture is implicitly construed as adhering to his own preferred interpretation of Scripture. Yet it is obvious, even by Ortlund’s own lights, that simply professing to believe Scripture is authoritative is not _sufficient_ to be a rightly believing member of the Church. If Ortlund admits that we can “misinterpret” or otherwise fail to accept those beliefs necessary for salvation that Scripture asserts for our faith, it is no longer a sufficient criterion of membership in the Church – the community of rightly believing Christians – that they accept the legitimacy of the same Scriptural canon. But, since accepting the Scriptural canon does not ensure that one is a member of the Church, there are requirements of membership that involve accepting the _minimally true_ beliefs regarding Scripture.

Further, Ortlund holds that belonging to the Church is not merely a matter of beliefs. Church membership involves actions, such as attending worship, being baptized, celebrating the Lord’s Supper. For these reasons, Ortlund’s position implies that belonging to the Church is necessary for salvation. The only theologically relevant difference between Ortlund and Catholic/Orthodoxy is that he draws the boundaries of that Church in such a way that it has far less dogmatic requirements and no sacramental ordination requirements for leadership, as well as having no universal communion (e.g., canon law). But then Ortlund’s theory is (in principle) no less “institutionally exclusivist” than the Catholic/Orthodox theory. Indeed, Ortlund seemingly could endorse the characterization of the Catholic doctrine on which “they could not be saved who, knowing that the [Church] was founded as necessary by God through Christ, would refuse either to enter it or to remain in it,” both in terms of adhering to the Word as preached by orthodox Christianity and as adhering to its institutional characteristics related to the sacraments. On the face of it, then, Ortlund’s call to reject “institutional exclusivism” looks inconsistent: his general objections to the doctrine of “no salvation outside the Church” apply just as much to his own view of salvation.

---

<!-- PDF page 211 -->
**[p. 198]**

Since Ortlund’s own characterization of the Church entails that belonging to the Church involves visible institutional membership (professing the same essential creed, baptism, communal worship, etc.) and that this belonging is a requisite for salvation, in at least the same sense Vatican II at least thinks it is, Ortlund’s objections to “institutional exclusivism” do not fundamentally involve rejection of _extra ecclesiam nullus salus._ I take it that those objections involve two different arguments. First, while admitting that corruptions or “accretions” creep into every church, including Protestant churches and traditions, Ortlund claims the decisive difference between Catholicism/Orthodoxy and Protestantism is that Protestant corruptions cannot be “enshrined within allegedly dogmatic authority.”[^c05-55] I have already argued previously that the objection begs the question against infallibility, and that infallibility does not prevent church reform in any meaningful sense. But Ortlund makes a further ecclesiological error. Ortlund lays out all the scandals that he thinks justified the Reformation, as justification for his congregationalist ecclesiology, and thereby underlines an _obligation_ to separate from churches that are corrupt, whether in doctrine or in morals. Ortlund implies that these scandals would be counterevidence to an imperative that we ought to maintain visible or “institutional” Church unity. A congregational ecclesiology on which the true Church is found “wherever Christ is present in Word and sacrament,”[^c05-56] rather than restricting the Church to “a single, visible hierarchy,”[^c05-57] is – Ortlund implies – the only way to fulfill our obligation to separate from churches that are corrupt while maintaining a charity toward fellow Christians. Congregationalist ecclesiology allows us to recognize any church that shares relevant essential beliefs and practices as fully Christian.

But it seems to me there is a logical lacuna in this reasoning. We have _communal_ duties toward other Christians, not merely to those in our own congregation. And Christianity rests centrally upon insistence that we each have moral and epistemic duties of theological faith to believe or do what God wants us to do or believe. We ought to love our brothers and sisters in the Church, the community of faith. St. Paul insists on raising money not only for providing charitable support within local congregations, but across the known Church of that time. But _spiritual_ support is even more critical, as being of higher value. Within a congregation, we are called to submit ourselves to the judgment of the Church when in dispute with their brothers (cf. Matt 18:17). It would be strange to think congregations, groups of Christians, are exempt from that requirement, “letting the sun set on their anger” (Eph. 4:26) and reject in principle seeking arbitration of their disputes according to an ecclesiastical procedure that would allow them to continue in communion. If we are commanded to receive the preaching of the Word and sacraments only from someone who professes sound doctrine (cf. 1 Tim 4:3) and only from those legitimately sent from God through authorization by the Church and, ultimately, the apostles (cf. Rom 10:15), we ought likewise to have obligations to maintain a “visible” or legal institutional framework for ensuring that this is maintained in, e.g., formation and commissioning of pastors and establishment of new local congregations. The fact that there will be exceptions to such rules is not itself a license to _carte blanche,_ nor does away with their fundamental necessity. It would be

---

<!-- PDF page 212 -->
**[p. 199]**

contrary to our obligations _within_ the Church (for instance) to allow anyone at all to found their own church, since such regulations are for the common good and are necessary for fulfilling our obligations to Christ. It seems we would have obligations, instead, to maintain unity in confessing the faith on such a critical matter. For instance, we might convoke an “ecumenical” council regarding these purported corruptions, address those with whom we disagree, and resolve our disagreements in such a way that we bind ourselves to the communal outcomes.

Ortlund gives a second reason to reject Orthodox/Catholic ecclesiology. Ortlund thinks that questions about where to find the true Church generate “deep unsettledness and anxiety,” and that Protestantism is preferable to Catholicism/Orthodoxy insofar as it “keeps the gospel central and clear” and thereby produces “clarity and assurance” for its members.[^c05-58] Ortlund implies that it is desirable to avoid “salvation anxiety” about whether you are within the right church or have done what is necessary to be saved, and that those ecclesiological positions which produce more assurance are therefore to be preferred. Now, it seems to me we can distinguish an accidental, question-begging feature of Ortlund’s objection and its essential core. Ortlund implies that Orthodoxy/Catholicism pose to each believer a greater burden than Protestantism: as these traditions are dogmatically committed to adhere to more doctrine and believe in more sacraments than most Protestants do, these traditions therefore impose _more_ requirements on salvation. But this is false. Notice that Protestants, Catholics, and Orthodox Christians fundamentally agree on the basic obligation that each believer ought to be _disposed_ to reject heresies. There is no obligation to know and list each possible heresy in advance. But, similarly, being _corporately_ dogmatically committed to reject heresies does not necessarily impose a greater _individual_ burden on each believer regarding what is required for them to be saved. There is no requirement in Orthodoxy or Catholicism that each believer’s salvation depends on their familiarity with or ability to list all heresies condemned officially in authoritative Church decisions. Such traditions require each person to be familiar with core doctrines of the Gospel and to profess a creed (typically the Nicene-Constantinopolitan) at baptism and at worship. For further doctrinal commitments, believers are obligated to _defer_ to the Church communally, being responsive to directions from their pastors. This is no different, in principle, from the way that all Christians are committed to the teaching of Scripture but will not be aware of _every_ passage or implication that can be drawn from the text. Deferring to communal testimony about Scripture, however, is just as much present in Protestant communities. When believers encounter theological claims that seem unusual, they will ask their pastors or seek counsel from those they trust as experts. And Protestants too ought to concede that each believer is _obliged_ to do this, since heresy is a serious matter. That is, it would be sinful for a Christian to be negligent in accepting or treating as acceptable a given doctrine without appropriately testing whether it conforms to Scripture.

The essential core of the objection, however, lies in the claim that Protestant ecclesiology gives “assurance,” and thereby implies that whether a doctrine gives “assurance” is a key test of whether that doctrine is true. On the one hand, whether a belief produces subjectively less “anxiety” or more “assurance” is no proper

---

<!-- PDF page 213 -->
**[p. 200]**

test of truth. Atheists who hold that there is no God, or who reject the possibility of survival after death, would experience subjectively less anxiety about a host of matters, but – if Christianity is true – these subjective experiences are no sign that their beliefs are true. On the other hand, I see no reason Catholic/Orthodox ecclesiology generates any anxiety beyond that of Ortlund’s own views. I suspect Ortlund implicitly imports the perspective that Catholicism and Orthodoxy involve aiming to “earn salvation” by “works,” rather than relying on God’s grace. However, such claims are simply false. Catholicism and Orthodoxy have fundamentally the same dogmatic perspective that saving faith is the necessary and sufficient condition for salvation, and that this faith is a gift which can be earned or merited in no way. Similarly, it might be suggested contextually by Ortlund’s criticism that Orthodoxy/Catholicism’s ecclesiology implies that choosing to belong to the “true Church” is a condition that must be met by our own efforts, prior to God’s grace, which choice thereby “earns” someone God’s grace through the sacraments, or something along these lines. But this is likewise false. Belonging to a given Church is itself a product of grace and Providence, just as much as the providential means by which we come to hear the Gospel, find baptism, and so forth. There is no reason non-congregational ecclesiology implies any particular view of salvation.

Further, Ortlund’s arguments would be self-defeating. Ortlund too thinks we have obligations to leave a corrupt local church. It will thus certainly matter for our salvation that we belong to the _true_ Church rather than a heretical or schismatic sect. And we might sin by rejecting what graces we have been given to know or suspect that we have not rightly obeyed God’s Word to the churches, just as those sin who reject the graces that incline them to think Christianity might be true. “Oh, look well to your footing that you slip not; be very much afraid lest the world should detain you; dare not in anything to fall short of God’s grace, or to lag behind when that grace goes forward.”[^c05-59] But there is no cause for _anxiety_ here, because God is gracious, merciful, and will fulfill His promises. He has not left us orphans but has left us shepherds “after His own heart” to preach His Word to us (cf., Jer 3:15, Acts 20:28), and the Spirit to bring us to recognize His voice spoken to us (cf. Jn 10:27, 14:18–26) and to guide us into all truth (cf. Jn 16:13). There is consequently nothing to fear for those who love God and seek sincerely to do His will in all things. Perfect love which comes only from faith in God casts out all fear (1 Jn 4:18).

The fundamental takeaway from this discussion is that the charismatic model of Church authority does not avoid the need for a theory of dogmatic authority or a theory of dogmatic development. Everyone should affirm that God’s Word makes obligations upon us, and that God would not allow His Word to be ineffective or to fail – we each have sufficient normative reasons to _recognize_ His Word and to _respond_. We can, for instance, recognize that it would be a sin against the Holy Spirit to reject that canon as received by His Church, for that recognition of the canon can have resulted from nothing but His own guidance. A charitable way, then, to interpret Ortlund’s position (as with many other Protestants) is that he simply does not recognize he has implicitly taken

---

<!-- PDF page 214 -->
**[p. 201]**

dogmatic authority for granted. His “charismatic model” leads him to think that only what is a consensus among some relevant subset of baptized Christians is authoritative. But he’s not sure who the right subset is, so he defaults to the lowest denominator. From this perspective, any issue that would be subject to rational disagreement among those bodies that broadly agree on Christian doctrine could not be supported by the normative ecclesial consensus of the Church, and could not be a dogma normatively required for being a member of the one true Church, whereas only those doctrines that are so supported across _all_ major denominations are the only binding beliefs backed by the dogmatic authority of the one true Church. Therefore, if one is an Arian or denies that Revelation is canonical, one is outside of the Church; but, e.g., the Assumption is essentially optional.

Ortlund’s positions end up ultimately _ad hoc_, since Ortlund has implicitly _excluded_ many self-professed Christians from this consensus, for no principled reason. Ortlund does not take Arians and universalist Christians to be among the normative consensus, for instance. This then raises a serious question about how we ought to _identify_ the boundaries of the Church, such as to identify that normative consensus and dogmatic authority. Because, if there are no such objective and public criteria by which we draw those boundaries, then this implies that God’s Word does not demand us to believe anything _definite_ at all. Nemes follows out Ortlund’s implicit principles to their logically consistent conclusion in denying, first, that there would be any minimum beliefs binding on every Christian beyond adherence to (roughly) self-identity as a “follower” of Jesus Christ, and then, second, that there could be any conceivable moral demands made by such self-identity, because nobody has sufficient normative reason to believe God has “revealed” anything definite at all. Nemes’ position is false, both in fact and in theory. God’s Word is living and powerful, a discerner of the thoughts and intents of the heart (cf. Heb 4:12); and it could be nothing else, or it would not be _God’s_ Word.

Consequently, anyone – like Ortlund – who wants to affirm that God’s Word makes concrete moral demands would need to consistently also embrace those moral demands made by God’s call to enter the Church through faith and baptism, as well as to recognize that only those to whom its divine Founder entrusted His Word, and sent to preach the Gospel, could be a suitably reliable source upon which we can apprehend what His Word is and demands. We are commanded in God’s Word to “stand firm and hold fast” to that Gospel once historically transmitted through Christ’s apostles (cf. 1 Thess 2:15). Consequently, we have obligations to test every spirit (cf. 1 Jn 4), as many false apostles have gone into the world who present themselves as ministers of God (cf. 2 Cor 11:13), so that we rightly identify and remain within that one, true Church. The reasons given in God’s Word for this command are clear: outside of Christ’s Church, the “pillar and ground of the truth” (1 Tim 3:15), founded by the apostles and prophets with Christ as the capstone (cf. Eph 2:20), there is no reliable testimony to the Word and no promise of the Spirit upon which we can rest our faith.

---

<!-- PDF page 215 -->
**[p. 202]**

## The Move From Definitions to Reception

I have argued that the Church possesses the power to engage in dogmatic definitions, and that this power would serve an important role in sanctioning necessary dogmatic developments. There is nothing _per se_ characteristic of the charismatic model that rules out the ability of the Church to make such definitions – we just get enough Christians together, in the right way, and achieve (at least moral) consensus. But Ortlund’s situation illustrates a difficulty: Protestants, Catholics, and Orthodox Christians disagree fundamentally on how to identify who can exercise such authority and therefore disagree fundamentally on whether there _are_ any dogmatic definitions that are ecumenically binding, especially after the first four or five ecumenical councils. Even if all could agree that definitions are in principle possible or even desirable, dogmatic definitions in this (epistemically) divided Church face a clear _practical_ problem: no group of authorities demands universal assent. Even among Catholic and Orthodox Christians, who agree on sacramental ordination as a necessary feature of exercising that dogmatic authority, the same problem arises as each insists there are further requirements of lawfulness which are not fulfilled by the other ecclesial body – e.g., the Catholics can have ecumenical councils which are not recognized by the Orthodox as being truly ecumenical in authority.

What I will do is advance the proposal that there is a _practical_ way to begin to resolve this problem: we do not need to decide between institutional and charismatic models; we can instead look for shared reasons to endorse dogmas from a common perspective and to reconstitute ecumenical dogmatic authority that would be exercised across all ecclesial bodies. Specifically, we can build a dogmatic consensus that would _ipso facto_ constitute an ecumenical reception of those dogmas that other groups currently acknowledge only to be of sectarian value, thereby endowing those dogmas with the dogmatic authority of the Church. It is noteworthy that such cases have some historical precedent, e.g., the Council of Carthage and Second Council of Orange (despite being local councils) were “received” later by, respectively, the ecumenical council of Ephesus and the wider Latin Church, thereby constituting their decisions “retrospectively” as being infallible and having authority for the whole Church.[^c05-60] The fact that a given dogma can possibly be received by all parties would help us move beyond the dilemma, without needing to decide between institutional and charismatic theories of authority.

Any Christian ought to endorse both that _heresy is bad,_ and that _schism is bad_. These normative judgments follow as corollaries from indirect duties of the faith, I will argue, and these demands give us reason to understand the development of the Church historically in such a way that the Church ought to facilitate its members fulfilling these duties. I will employ these hypotheses about the moral duties of faith to argue that even Christians endorsing the charismatic model of authority have good reasons to accept that the Church’s divine constitution involves a “Petrine office,” that is, a Church office which would be a necessary condition for identifying and preventing schism from occurring. Further, Christians endorsing the charismatic model of authority have good reasons to accept that there might be dogmatic definitions which delineate dogmatic “tests” that facilitate their members

---

<!-- PDF page 216 -->
**[p. 203]**

avoiding serious heresy. These “tests” aim at central biblical truths indirectly rather than directly, to facilitate their acceptance without technical and complicated terminology. Marian dogmas can be recognized to serve such a function and thus merit ecumenical reception as dogmas. After proposing reasons to endorse these dogmas, I will conclude by reflecting that the way we can construct ecumenical reasons for receiving both dogmas raises the probability of my initial hypotheses regarding what divides the churches, and thereby also clarifies how we ought to think about identifying the true Church, the locus of dogmatic authority for Christians.

### Schism Is Bad: The Case for Rome

Thomas Aquinas distinguishes heresy from schism, following distinctions introduced by Augustine and Jerome:

Heresy and schism are distinguished in respect of those things to which each is opposed essentially and directly. For heresy is essentially opposed to faith, while schism is essentially opposed to the unity of ecclesiastical charity. Wherefore just as faith and charity are different virtues, although whoever lacks faith lacks charity, so too schism and heresy are different vices, although whoever is a heretic is also a schismatic, but not conversely.[^c05-61]

Nevertheless, Aquinas notes a _practical_ connection between schism and heresy: schism tends to generate heresy, for “there is no schism that does not devise some heresy for itself, that it may appear to have had a reason for separating from the Church.”[^c05-62] Schism is then a special sin against charity, love of God and neighbor, where “schismatics properly so called are those who, willfully and intentionally separate themselves from the unity of the Church.”[^c05-63] This rejection of union with the Church is identified when one “obstinately scorns the commandments of the Church and refuses to submit to her judgment,” which Aquinas underlines (in the context of polemic with Eastern Orthodox Christians) consists in failing to submit to the judgments of the Roman Pontiff, who holds the supreme place among bishops, or to maintain communion with all those churches that acknowledge this supreme place to the Roman Church.[^c05-64]

Putting aside momentarily Aquinas’ controversial claim about the Roman Pontiff, he has indicated that the reasons schism is sinful are that acts of schism constitute a violation of duties to maintain charitable unity in mind and heart with other Christians. A failure to _acknowledge_ having such duties would require denial of repeated, insistent Scriptural witness to that effect, as the unity normatively expected to hold among Christian believers is a strong theme in practically every New Testament book or letter: e.g., Phil 2:2, Gal 3:28, 1 Cor 1:10, 1 Pet 3:8, 1 Cor 13:11, Rom 15:5, Rom 12:16, 1 Jn 1:1–15, Phil 1:27, Jn 13:34, etc. No less a Reformed authority than John Calvin shares Aquinas’ perspective in declaring that each Christian has obligations “to pay due deference to the authority of the Church,”[^c05-65] since our salvation is found only in communion with that Church, and

---

<!-- PDF page 217 -->
**[p. 204]**

therefore that Christians ought to hold as detestable those “who make it their study to cause divisions in the churches.”[^c05-66] Calvin even insists in strong terms that the Lord Jesus Himself “considers every one as a traitor and apostate from religion, who perversely withdraws himself from any Christian society which preserves the true ministry of the word and sacraments” merely on account of failures to agree with matters of doctrine that are unimportant or “indifferent” or on account of moral corruptions among the members within that society who have not been publicly excommunicated.[^c05-67]

For this reason, Calvin recognizes too that the Church has duties to maintain the faith as well as the integrity of the sacraments, for “as soon as falsehood has made a breach in the fundamentals of religion, and the system of necessary doctrine is subverted, and the use of the sacraments fails, the certain consequence is the ruin of the Church, as there is an end of a man’s life when his throat is cut, or his heart is mortally wounded.”[^c05-68] Calvin’s critique of Roman Catholicism – and justification for breaking communion with the Catholic Church – rests on his claims that it has forfeited the title of “church” in virtue of introducing heretical doctrines that undermine central beliefs of Christianity as well as subverting the integrity of the sacraments. He does not then deny that breaking communion with the Church is ordinarily a sin but argues that the Reformation creation of separate churches was justified by the heresies being taught (and indeed dogmatically required) by Roman Catholic authorities as well as its widespread moral and sacramental corruptions.[^c05-69] Calvin then justifies the constitution of these resulting Reformed churches by appealing to a “charismatic” theory of Church authority, where they need no sacramental ordination to qualify as being a continuation of the true Church that ceases to be present (except, at best, in an impaired way) in the Roman Catholic communion.[^c05-70]

Calvin is nevertheless one of the foremost defenders within the Reformed tradition of a “high” characterization of the Church’s power (“jurisdiction”) to discipline its members and maintain unity among them.[^c05-71] Calvin clearly recognizes that there would be chaos if every Christian were empowered to judge whether other members are living their life of faith well, or to exclude them from communion in the sacraments (i.e., excommunication), or to make _public_ decisions that would affect others in terms of Church order in public worship or discipline – but he also recognizes the necessity of making such decisions for the public good.[^c05-72] Calvin insists that merely human law (whether civil or ecclesiastical) does not bind “in conscience.” But this is slightly confusing terminology since Calvin accepts that they are necessary to be observed, and that failure to observe such laws can be sinful, distinguishing that “the necessity of observing [merely human law] has reference to the general object of laws, but does not consist in the particular things which are commanded.”[^c05-73]

Whereas Calvin thinks his position here contradicts Catholic view of ecclesiastical law, it is not clear whether his position in fact contradicts it. A typical view today is that canon law is binding in conscience, as any reasonable law made by proper authority for the common good would be, and that there are necessarily morally excusing factors.[^c05-74] Thus, rejecting the authority of the Church to make laws, or to fail to obey those laws out of contempt, would be sinful. Yet, nobody

---

<!-- PDF page 218 -->
**[p. 205]**

disputes that Church laws (“merely ecclesiastical law”), aiming at the common good of the Church, have a different relation to conscience than natural or divine law. Further, nobody disputes that injunctions of an ecclesiastical superior – even the Pope – ought to be disobeyed when they conflict with natural or divine law.[^c05-75] (A humorous example can be found in Dante’s _Divina Commedia, Inferno_, Canto 27, where a man finds himself in hell for having committed a murder on the command and assurance, from Pope Boniface VIII, that he would be absolved of his sins in advance – Dante’s devils concur with the judgment of Catholic canonists and moral theologians that absolution is invalid in such circumstances). More fundamentally, Calvin’s claims that many prescriptions in medieval canon law were harmful to souls, useless, foolish, etc., or that bishops have totally abdicated their responsibilities in enforcing good discipline within the Catholic Church[^c05-76] seem to consist largely in (controversial) value judgments which would not, by Calvin’s own stated principles, justify his separation from the Catholic Church (since alleging a fault in the canonical system is not to allege doctrinal heresies or deviations), Calvin’s criticisms that enforcement of the law is neither effective nor taken seriously reinforce my overall point. That is, Calvin does not deny the principle that the Church requires a system of canon law with enforcers and penalties for non-compliance, instead insisting upon the ability of the Church to order public affairs as well as to sanction its members, primarily by way of excommunication from participation in public worship and the sacraments.[^c05-77]

Calvin justifies and lays out his preferred form of “church polity” for local churches. But it is difficult to find in Calvin’s system any place for a _universal_ lawful communion among _all_ churches. Yet it seems that there ought to be such an institution of universal canon law, since otherwise the problems Calvin alleges regarding lawfulness within a local church would necessarily affect the interaction among churches: e.g., those who were excommunicated in one church would have no reason to reform their ways, since they could seek communion elsewhere.[^c05-78] A universal code of canon law that local churches could jointly endorse is not only a necessity for simple practical matters, but more generally for, e.g., agreeing on conditions for lawful ordination, maintaining high standards for formation of ministers, and so on.[^c05-79] Controlling the spread of heresy is obviously also an intrachurch matter and not simply the concern of local churches; it would be practically impossible to impose sanctions on heresy without universal communion, for the same reasons. As Calvin himself says: “… when we see the approach of certain beginnings of a dreadful desolation in the Church, since there is no solicitude or means to keep the people in obedience to our Lord, necessity itself proclaims the want of a remedy; and this is the only remedy which has been commanded by Christ, or which has ever been adopted among believers.”[^c05-80]

Having rejected the errors he thinks infect the Roman communion, Calvin would sternly reject any claim that communion with the bishop of Rome is a necessary condition for being a rightly ordered church polity – communion with the Pope would be, from Calvin’s perspective, communion with heresy. Further, a primary point of difference in terms of ecclesial polity is that Calvin rejects any centralization in appointment of bishops for local churches, as is largely the practice of the

---

<!-- PDF page 219 -->
**[p. 206]**

Orthodox and Catholic Church today. This historically occurred first in terms of metropolitan and patriarchal sees, and then in the later Middle Ages became centralized around the bishop of Rome in the West (ironically, for reasons of promoting reform of the Church).[^c05-81] Calvin rejects such centralized appointment both as a violation of strictly divine law and as ahistorical, seeing his “charismatic” model vindicated in ancient practice where the local church would ordinarily elect its own bishops.[^c05-82] Nevertheless, Calvin’s _primary_ objection to the papacy seemingly rests upon understanding the Pope as the “monarch” of the universal Church[^c05-83] and therefore that the Pope’s position as supreme judge among bishops would be such that he is immune to judicial proceedings:

… the most intolerable of all things is their leaving no judicial authority in the world to restrain and curb them when they licentiously abuse their immense power. ‘No man (say they) is entitled to alter the judgment of this See, on account of the primacy of the Roman Church.’Again, ‘The judge shall not be judged either by the emperor, or by kings, or by the clergy, or by the people.’ It is surely imperious enough for one man to appoint himself the judge of all, while he will not submit to the judgment of any. But what if he tyrannises over the people of God? if he dissipates and lays waste the kingdom of Christ? if he troubles the whole Church? if he convert the pastoral office into robbery? Nay, though he should be the most abandoned of all, he insists that none can call him to account.[^c05-84]

That is, Roman primacy is objectionable (for Calvin) since there would appear to be no _legal remedies_ against abuses such as would merit breaking communion with him, whether moral turpitude, abuse of power, or even heresy.

The doctrine of the Catholic Church is that the bishop of Rome “by virtue of his office … possesses supreme, full, immediate, and universal ordinary power in the Church, which he is always able to exercise freely” (CIC[^c05-85] 331), as defined in Vatican I’s _Pastor Aeternus_[^c05-86] as well as in previous conciliar canons, e.g., at Second Lyons[^c05-87] and Florence,[^c05-88] and then reiterated by Vatican II.[^c05-89] In current law, “it is for the Roman Pontiff alone to convoke an ecumenical council, preside over it personally or through others, transfer, suspend, or dissolve a council, and to approve its decrees” (CIC 338 §1). Further, as the Pope functions as the ultimate supreme court of appeals under canon law, so that “no appeal or recourse is permitted against a sentence or decree of the Roman Pontiff” (CIC 333, §3); the Roman Pontiff is therefore such that no ecclesiastical tribunal is competent to try a case against him – “the First See is judged by no one” (CIC 1404). Finally, the Roman Pontiff enjoys the prerogative to make solemn dogmatic definitions that are infallible, which “definitions, of themselves, and not from the consent of the Church, are justly styled irreformable, since they are pronounced with the assistance of the Holy Spirit, promised to him in blessed Peter, and therefore they need no approval of others, nor do they allow an appeal to any other judgment.”[^c05-90]

These claims, while strong, do not fully support Calvin’s view that the Catholic Church understands the Pope as universal and unaccountable monarch in the order

---

<!-- PDF page 220 -->
**[p. 207]**
