of governance. Most obviously, under Catholic theology, legal prescriptions from any superior cannot be obeyed if they are contrary to divine or natural law. The Pope’s authority is therefore already circumscribed in matters of governance: not even the Pope could bind anyone to sin or destroy the constitution of the Church, since nobody would be bound to obey him in these decisions. The same is even true theoretically of the papal power to define dogma in an extraordinary situation. Such a power is highly circumscribed both by the natural limits that the Pope cannot in principle change doctrine already so taught or defined by the Church and given the way in which the exercise of this power is only under very definite “legal” conditions (roughly, that he be speaking _ex cathedra,_ with intent to define for the universal Church, on a matter of faith and morals[^c05-91]). Newman expounds at length all the limits which tightly constrain the scope of papal definitions.[^c05-92] This is without taking into consideration the fact that papal definitions are, in principle, no greater danger to the faith of Church than that of an ecumenical council.

Further, Vatican II introduced an explicit qualification that the Catholic Church is not a monarchy but a collegial body in which the Roman Pontiff is the “head” of the College of Bishops; thus, the supreme and full authority in the Catholic Church is not vested solely in the Roman Pontiff but rather “the order of bishops, which succeeds to the college of apostles and gives this apostolic body continued existence, is also the subject of supreme and full power over the universal Church, provided we understand this body together with its head the Roman Pontiff and never without this head.”[^c05-93] And, in particular, the most representative theorization along these lines comes in the encyclical _Ut Unum Sint_ of Pope John Paul II, where he acknowledges explicitly that Catholic doctrine needs to introduce further qualifications such that the “Petrine office” of the bishop of Rome does not become an impediment to unity among the churches, when its very office is (in theory) dedicated to preserving rather than usurping the authority of each particular bishop. Thus, while John Paul insists that the Petrine office is a necessary condition of unity and that this office needs definite legal authority if it is to be effective in securing that unity universally, he proposes that the Catholic Church needs to “find a way of exercising the primacy which, while in no way renouncing what is essential to its mission, is nonetheless open to a new situation.”[^c05-94] For instance, there is no necessity in Catholic doctrine (_pace_ Calvin) that all episcopal appointments be centralized, and no reason that bishops could not be locally elected by their churches (even under current canon law, several Latin dioceses retain ancient privileges to elect their own bishops, and the Eastern churches appoint bishops through a synodal process).

The only outstanding question that exists regarding the exercise of papal primacy, it seems to me, is whether _legal remedies_ are possible against the Roman Pontiff. Despite popular misapprehension, Catholic theology has _not_ defined or definitively concluded that there is no legal remedy against papal malfeasance. As with other chief executives or supreme judicial officers in secular governments, the fact that the Pope functions in an analogous role has caused many to think it reasonable that the papal office entails a wide immunity from prosecution under canon law. Nevertheless, many Catholic theologians have also concluded – again in parallel with secular governments – that there would be a possibility of “high

---

<!-- PDF page 221 -->
**[p. 208]**

crimes” that would necessitate a remedy, even potentially a legal one. Traditionally, Catholic theologians held that the Pope would be immune to prosecution of any crime _except_ heresy. In canon law prior to the modern period, provision was seemingly made for this exception.[^c05-95] Famous Baroque theologians such as Cajetan, Bellarmine, John of St. Thomas, and others offered theories as to way in which this could occur, even if differing on many important details. Some have argued (to the contrary) that a heretical or bad pope poses no _fundamental_ danger to the Church, and that therefore no procedure would be needed.[^c05-96]

Whatever the case may be regarding particular theories of the procedure to be taken with regard to a heretical pope, or its theological underpinnings, it is eminently plausible – given statements by the _relator_ at Vatican I, Vincent Gasser[^c05-97] – that Catholic dogmatic claims even after Vatican I do not rule out such possibilities or procedures in principle.[^c05-98] In any event, then, there is no principled objection to the doctrine of papal primacy _per se_ grounds that no remedies are possible in extreme cases. Thus, although Catholic theology has never taken any definitive stand for or against any theory, it affirms _in abstract principle_ that there are definitive moral and constitutional limits on papal authority. Catholic theologians like Klaus Schatz argued that further exploration of those prior theories regarding the limits of papal power and the potential steps taken against heretical popes is _necessary_ for the modern Church.[^c05-99] Contrary to public perception, there is nothing theoretically preventing this development.[^c05-100]

And Catholic thinkers have spent a great deal of effort aiming to work out a systematic and historical account of papal primacy which might be more historically accurate and more ecumenically acceptable to Orthodox and Protestant Christians.[^c05-101] Orthodox and Protestant thinkers have also offered proposals regarding the place of a rethought Petrine office in a reunited Christian Church. Orthodox and Catholic official theological dialogue fundamentally agrees that “the Church is not properly understood as a pyramid, with a primate governing from the top, but neither is it properly understood as a federation of self-sufficient Churches.”[^c05-102] Further, they fundamentally agree that, in the first millennium, the bishop of Rome occupied a first place in the order (“taxis”) of bishops and was the first (“protos”) among patriarchs.[^c05-103] In particular, they accept the need for communion at a universal level:

Each local Church is in communion not only with neighbouring Churches, but with the totality of the local Churches, with those now present in the world, those which have been since the beginning, and those which will be in the future, and with the Church already in glory. According to the will of Christ, the Church is one and indivisible, the same always and in every place. … a local Church cannot change a fundamental point regarding the form of ministry by a unilateral decision, and no local Church can celebrate the Eucharist in willful separation from other local Churches without seriously affecting ecclesial communion. In all of these things one touches on the bond of communion itself – thus, on the very being of the Church. It is because of this communion that all the Churches, through canons, regulate everything relating to the

---

<!-- PDF page 222 -->
**[p. 209]**

Eucharist and the sacraments, the ministry and ordination, and the handing on (paradosis) and teaching (didaskalia) of the faith. It is clear why in this domain canonical rules and disciplinary norms are needed.[^c05-104]

There is agreement among Catholics and Orthodox that universal _legal_ means for governing the Church, ensuring communion among local churches, are both necessary for its well-functioning and constitutive of that constitution given to the Church by its divine Founder. And historically speaking, even those Orthodox theologians who rejected union with the Catholic Church at the Council of FerraraFlorence (e.g., Mark of Ephesus) and other late Byzantine theologians (Gregory Palamas) seem to have both accepted the necessity of primacy in principle and afforded that primacy to the Roman See by right – rather, they seem to have held that the primacy was not unchecked by law, so that there needed to be ways to counter a _heretical_ pope who opposed the canons, and therefore only opposed any obligation to submit to popes who (they thought) had lost rights to exercise that primacy lawfully, by virtue of heresy.[^c05-105]

Lutheran theologians in dialogue with the Catholic Church have likewise proposed that “the office of the papacy as a visible sign of the unity of the church is not excluded, provided that, through a theological re-interpretation and practical restructuring, this office is subjected to the primacy of the Gospel,”[^c05-106] as they agree that the papal office is practically necessary for achieving universal communion among churches.[^c05-107] Wolfhart Pannenberg indeed proposes that papal primacy exercised by the bishop of Rome is compatible with the deepest sympathies of the Reformation. First, he notes that there is no feasible alternative except the bishop of Rome to serve this function within a universal communion of churches: “If any Christian bishop can speak for the whole church in situations when this may be needed, it will be primarily the bishop of Rome. In spite of all the bitter controversies resulting from chronic misuse of the authority of Rome in power politics, there is here no realistic alternative.”[^c05-108] He argues historically that “the Lutheran Reformation never rules out in principle a ministry to protect Christian unity on the universal level of Christianity as a whole,”[^c05-109] and agrees in principle that infallibility in witness to the Gospel is promised to the whole Church, so that ecumenical councils can be rightly understood to “participate especially in the promise of Christ that the gates of hell will not prevail against his church” (Matt 16:18).[^c05-110]

Further, while Pannenberg notes that Roman primacy developed over time and denies that there is good evidence for holding that Jesus’ words to Peter intended to found any special office, Pannenberg can accept Catholic claims that this office is of “divine right” in a wide sense. As he puts it, “the alternative of divine or human right is … unsatisfactory” because “as a representation of the unity of the whole church, [the Petrine office] is not just an expression of human arbitrariness but a special instance of all church ministry as a ministry to the church’s unity at every level of its life.”[^c05-111] We might say that, for Pannenberg, the Petrine office can be understood as “co-instituted” by Christ in virtue of His institution of Church authority generally; the Petrine office is a universal instance of what is required for the Church to exercise and maintain that authority. Catholic theologian Roch

---

<!-- PDF page 223 -->
**[p. 210]**

Keresetzky agrees that “the primacy of the bishop of Rome did not emerge as a result of exegetical arguments from the Petrine texts of the Bible; it was initially derived from the joint presence, teaching, and martyrdom of the chief apostles Peter and Paul in the church of Rome. But many other doctrines of the church were first practiced and lived before the church found the scriptural foundations for their confirmation.”[^c05-112]

That is, papal primacy is a development of doctrine just like any other – while there is Scriptural confirmation that was naturally supportive of the theory, the doctrine does not rest solely on inferences from isolated Scripture passages. Papal primacy can be understood to rest on something like an inference to the best explanation regarding the unity Christ intended for His Church, what is necessary to maintain the indirect duties of faith to maintain that unity, and the historical facts regarding the inheritance of the Gospel through the transmission of the apostles. The Church of Rome _represents_ within the universal Church the place Peter held within the apostolic college, and the Pope succeeds to that role given the place of that church within Christian history, for the edification and preservation of the Church’s universal communion.[^c05-113] As with other developments, the argument that the early witness is silent about specific claims made in _Pastor Aeternus,_ or that early popes do not exercise their authority in ways typical of the modern ear, leaves open whether the development is legitimate. So too the fact that the Roman church took time to develop a monoepiscopate is not to the point either, since the fact that at those early times the Roman church either had a chief among its bishops, where they acted as a collegial unity, is fully compatible with Catholic dogma.[^c05-114]

Conversely, all parties (even Ortlund)[^c05-115] admit that the Roman church possessed _some_ primacy in the early Church period. And there is a trajectory traceable within Church history from the letters of St. Clement (where the Roman church authoritatively addresses other churches) to further developments that continue to undergird the unique role possessed by the Roman church as first in the “order” (_taxis_) of churches, with increasing reflection on what that position as “first” (“protos”) would imply, especially when faced with doctrinal challenges which required intervention by just such an office.[^c05-116] There is a good case to be made that this doctrine is a legitimate development, including extrapolations to infallibility, given the way that the papacy functions to represent the universal Church. That there needs to be a _first See_ seems theologically and historically defensible, as normative dogmatic consensus is traced precisely through historical facts about “apostolic succession.” Already in the 1st century, Ignatius of Antioch had identified the Roman See as “presiding in love” over the other churches of the world, alluding to its place as first in the _taxis_ of the churches.[^c05-117]

It is no accident, then, that reflection on the historical position that the Roman Church occupied among other churches as “first,” rather than the Church of Jerusalem, eventually led to appeal to the status of Peter (and Paul) among the other apostles. As Augustine inferred, “… this is the reason for Peter’s acknowledged pre-eminence, that he stood for the Church’s universality and unity, when he was told, _To you I am entrusting_, what has in fact been entrusted to all.”[^c05-118] The See of Rome represents the unity of the College of Bishops today in an analogous way

---

<!-- PDF page 224 -->
**[p. 211]**

to that by which Peter represented the unity of the Church’s confession of faith and the apostolic college (cf. Matt. 16:16–19), inheriting a dual patrimony from the martyrs Peter and Paul who died there and confirming its priority among other churches. When functioning in his capacity as that “first see” whose primacy extends to deciding controversies at lower levels in the Church, the bishop of Rome would have the right and capacity to make decisions that would not permit further appeal. In the same way that we reason that an ecumenical council is infallible, as it represents decisions of the whole Church that cannot be appealed to a higher ecclesiastical authority, the Pope occupies an office which represents the unity of the whole Church and his solemn definitions regarding dogmatic matters (“ex cathedra” definitions on matters of faith and morals, with intent to define a dogma for the universal Church, etc.) are not subject to appeal to higher authority. These dogmatic definitions would be providentially protected from solemnly teaching error in the same way as definitions of ecumenical councils. Thus, papal infallibility follows from the principle that the Roman See is first in rank among all dioceses, and that no higher appeals are open after that See’s solemn judgments.

For my purposes, I leave aside questions of infallibility. Since the kind of infallibility afforded to papal dogmatic definitions – and their scope or the requirements incumbent upon the papacy in making such definitions – arises solely from the nature of the primatial office that the bishop of Rome exercises, resolving the dogmatic status of papal primacy is a necessary prolegomenon to any discussion of infallibility. Modern proposals, like that of Avery Dulles’ moderate infallibilism,[^c05-119] interpret papal infallibility in a moderate, prudential manner that corresponds to his office, and which does not make the dogmatic status of papal infallibility itself a necessary condition for reunion. What is more theoretically basic is the canonical, _practical_ role played by the papacy in the structure of the Church – a role highlighted by Pannenberg in proposing that the Petrine office is required for the Church to protect herself from _schism_. This viewpoint is witnessed among the Fathers of the Church. Jerome justifies the papacy as instituted to prevent schism; “one among the twelve is chosen so that when a head has been appointed, there may be no occasion for schism.”[^c05-120] Maximus the Confessor affirms too that the Church of Rome holds a position “from the incarnate Son of God himself” of a “universal and supreme dominion, authority, and power of binding and loosing over all the holy churches of God which are in the whole world,” which he appeals to in the context of ensuring heresy is prevented throughout the world.[^c05-121] Yet, even if we concede that it was difficult to prove historically or dogmatically that communion with the bishop of Rome is a necessary condition (of divine institution) for being that rightly constituted Church Christ founded, the effects of the denial of this doctrine are not.

Let me begin by way of informal personal reflection, based upon my experience as an Eastern Catholic frequently in theological dialogue with Eastern and Oriental Orthodox clergy or theologians, and regularly attending their worship or otherwise interested in their situation. The Orthodox agree with Catholics that schism is seriously sinful. Nevertheless, while admittedly partial and biased, my distinct impression is that it is a widespread attitude among Orthodox believers, including

---

<!-- PDF page 225 -->
**[p. 212]**

clergy, not to treat schism as very seriously wrong. It seems absurd, for instance, to many Orthodox believers that it would be a serious sin to leave the Ecumenical Patriarchate and join the Moscow Patriarchate,[^c05-122] despite these two bodies now being in ecclesiastical separation from each other. The break in communion between Moscow and Constantinople is just the most recent example of a tendency.

For many believers, nothing was “really” broken in the unity of the Church when two or more churches broke communion formally, ceasing to concelebrate the Eucharist or commemorate their hierarchs in the liturgy. Most believers cannot distinguish between a legitimate diocese/eparchy, one that is canonical and in communion with the rest, and one that is not, and there is no good answer forthcoming from anybody on what constitutes the necessary and sufficient condition for “autocephaly.” What does schism, sans heresy, consist in? Who counts as the rightful group that can impose a binding excommunication on another group? Since these questions become objectively unanswerable, schism becomes either practically impossible or never treated as serious. A local church is practically without any accountability to anyone else in the universe; and so schism really becomes impossible to commit. No amount of lack of communion between two independent churches, no amount of anathemas, really “breaks” anything. “Uncanonical” Orthodox priests, monks, and bishops have broken away in various sects, and many laypeople follow their advice or associate themselves with these groups without any serious ecclesial consequences from canonical clergy. And, strikingly, the attempt of the Orthodox to host a “Great Council” was largely a failure. Orthodoxy tends to downplay this situation as serious, for the simple reason that they lack any clear, impartial, objective way to say _who_ broke communion with whom – both sides in their inter-church fights think they were justified, that they are preserving the true faith or doing the right thing.

The lack of being able to see schism as wrong seems to result from the fact that contemporary Orthodox believers have a problem of identifying in a public way when those norms are to be followed and when they are not. Erikson has pointed out the anomalous nature of this situation, criticizing justificatory appeals to canon 28 of Chalcedon and instead noting that “the manifest disunity of Orthodoxy whether in America or globally contradicts the most basic principles of Orthodox ecclesiology.”[^c05-123] Proposing an ecumenical council, or even a local council, to resolve these differences is unhelpful if we decide in advance that there are no penalties and no _norms_ binding upon each party for non-compliance. Nobody can then constitute a truly _legitimate_ council, such that it would be able to craft canon law that binds all the churches. Local churches can otherwise always claim, for example, “We didn’t agree to those rules!” In the past, Orthodoxy had been held together by mutual norms to recognize pre-Great Schism canon law as binding; this is also why they could not revise canon law in any way – because it would always be questionable whether that revision was legitimate (as at the “Great Council of Crete”!). That appeal to pre-Schism canon law is what allowed each body to delineate what counted as a canonical Orthodox Church in terms of their communion with Constantinople or another historical body, but those norms are breaking down, because that law does not work as a clear standard for what counts for constituting a canonical,

---

<!-- PDF page 226 -->
**[p. 213]**

autocephalous church in the context of current disputes between such churches. Basically, who gets to be the patriarch with whom communion constitutes being a canonical entity, Moscow or Constantinople? “Both” doesn’t work if they anathematize each other. “Neither” doesn’t work either. An Orthodox priest has succinctly summarized the problem: “the very absence of the authority that a _primus_ would have exercised at the pan-Orthodox level hinders the efforts of remedying [these] institutional lacuna,” and thus, paradoxically, “the Orthodox churches cannot unite with Rome as long as they are not united with Rome.”[^c05-124]

It goes without saying that many Protestant churches are in a far worse position than the Orthodox as regards their disregard for schism as any serious offense against God. A church disagreement even _ordinarily_ can produce a distinct denomination – and this becomes seen as an ecclesial virtue rather than a vice (even though the Reformers like Calvin would be turning over in their graves at the thought!). Schism as a sin is precisely not a private infraction _among_ certain groups against other groups, like the way someone might sow disrespect for a particular group of people, but a sin of breaking _from_ the One, Holy Church. In the Old Testament, people who broke off to form their own conventicle and presumed divine approbation were miraculously punished (Numbers 16:1–40). There was one visible Israel, with one divinely appointed priesthood, and it was sinful to appropriate the gifts of God to oneself without being called by God. So too in the New Dispensation the Church is a visible body that is One, with pastors appointed by Christ (and His appointed successors) but not by themselves. Schism is a serious sin because it involves a lack of charity, a lack of respect not for a private individual but for Christ, who founded the one visible Church, with pastors He appointed.

If schism is a sin, then it is cowardly, not heroic, to break with the Church. Creating my own conventicle, to set up my own preferred pastors who are not part of the Church Christ founded, is to commit the sin of Korah all over again. There is a visible unity, made concrete in things such as a universal code of canon law by which the Church makes decisions for its own governmental practices. Breaking communion with the Church is a public, identifiable act, because it is to create your own more “genuine” Christian Church, separate from the universal body of bishops united with the bishop of Rome. And it is a sin to do that. Having pastors who are sinful, preaching that is bad, governance that is corrupting or selfish, liturgy that is sloppy and ugly – no one of these is an excuse to arrogate to yourself, or those you like, the authority to found a new church aside from Christ’s. No matter how much sin there is, the Church remains the Church Christ founded and its sacraments are still efficacious signs of His love, just as the Word of God is not corrupted into something else because the Devil can cite it for his own purposes. Just so, nowhere in Catholic doctrine does it say that the bishop of Rome cannot do stupid or sinful things. We can and should aim to _reform_ this one Church (as the Second Vatican Council taught: _Lumen Gentium_, no. 8). It is hard to do so, but that strikes me as exactly why Christ set up a hierarchy of fallible, sinful people to run it: because it takes a lot of patience and charity to live with the people He put in charge! If we have no obligations to maintain visible communion with other

---

<!-- PDF page 227 -->
**[p. 214]**

Christians – no obligations to maintain unity in charity and faith – then we ought to think nothing is lost when Christians remain divided by indifference and heresy, should establish our own church whenever the going gets tough, and ought to treat schism as a virtue. But it is not.

The Petrine office therefore seems a necessary constituent of adverting to (and fulfilling) those duties of charity toward our fellow Christians which require us corporately to avoid committing the sin of schism and to help others avoid being tempted to do so. We can summarize this case for the Petrine office very simply:

- 1 If there were no objective, public standard for determining universal communion among churches, there would be no identifiable sin of schism from the Church, only schism within the Church.

- 2 But there is a sin of schism from the Church. (All parties agree, Calvin and Ortlund included).

- 3 Therefore, there is an objective, public standard for determining universal communion among churches.

The above argument is independent of my previous arguments in favor of dogmatic authority and the necessity for development in the life of the Church. But, if those arguments are sound, we would have even better reasons to endorse Roman primacy in terms of _both_ our duties to avoid schism _and_ heresy. As I have argued, identifying the Church is a necessary condition for identifying that group whose theological consensus in, e.g., receiving the canon of Scripture, would be guided by the Holy Spirit and therefore _reliable_ such as to give every believer today a sufficient normative reason to take their identification of Scripture as making a theological demand upon their faith. And I have argued we do need such sufficient normative reasons. Yet, Ortlund himself has given us clear reason to think that, absent criteria for universal communion, like that played by communion with the Roman church, there could not in principle be anything like schism _from_ the Church and only schism “in” the Church (i.e., intra-ecclesial disputes).[^c05-125] But, if we were entirely lacking any public, objective criteria to determine when a group has committed schism and broken _from_ the Church, rather than simply constituting an intra-ecclesial dispute, we would be unable to identify that visible Church and its normative consensus. Similarly, if it were not important to identify schism as such for any practical purpose, it would not be important to identify the true canon of Scripture. But I have shown that every Christian has good reasons to reject these implications. Therefore, we ought to reject that there are no identifiable public, objective criteria for schism.

- 1 Identifying the visible Church (and so when schism _from_ the Church occurs) in a public and objective way is necessary for identifying the normative dogmatic consensus among Christians, which is necessary for identifying essential dogmatic beliefs and the canon of Scripture.

- 2 If we cannot identify in principle, or it is not important, that we identify whether anyone is in communion with the universal Church in a public and objective

---

<!-- PDF page 228 -->
**[p. 215]**

way, then we would be unable to identify in principle, or it is not important, that we identify essential dogmatic beliefs or the canon of Scripture.

- 3 But our beliefs as Christians entail that _is_ important that we identify these beliefs and the canon of Scripture, and that there is a way to identify essential dogmatic beliefs or the canon of Scripture.

- 4 Therefore, there is a public and objective way to identify the visible Church and so identify when schism _from_ that Church occurs.

The only viable, historically and biblically sanctioned candidate for the role indicated by the conclusion of either of these arguments is the Roman church, given its historical place among the communion of churches. Further, as good Christian theology is always constrained by Scriptural evidence in identifying necessary features of the Church’s constitution, we can fulfill these norms by way of an analogical reasoning from New Testament texts regarding the place of Peter among the apostles as a justification for Roman primacy, but for no other currently existing church. Theoretically, all other issues regarding legal remedies against papal malfeasance, or even our _justification_ (historical or systematic) for papal primacy, and the specifics regarding possibly circumstances in which the Pope might have prerogatives to speak infallibly in virtue of his office as first among bishops, are ultimately negotiable theological or legal niceties rather than being critical issues that need to be determined before accepting the _principle_ that the Petrine office is a necessary feature of the Church’s constitution. And, as Vatican II and the Catholic Church’s subsequent theological dialogue have introduced qualifications into the account that make the theory of primacy more widely acceptable to Orthodox and Protestant Christians, ecumenical partners have no good reason _not_ to follow Pannenberg in affirming that the Roman church ought to occupy this role.[^c05-126]

### Heresy Is Bad: The Case for the Blessed Virgin

By contrast with papal primacy, which raises many legal or systematic ecclesiological complications, it seems to me that the appropriateness of Christian devotion to the Blessed Virgin Mary is _much_ more straightforward. Despite her brief appearances in Scripture, each is highly significant within the plan of salvation, particularly in her role at the Incarnation, and of central importance to Christian dogma. Thus, it is easy to reconstruct the basic rationale for Marian devotion.

- 1 The Blessed Virgin is presented in Scripture as praiseworthy.

- 2 Whoever is presented or treated in Scripture as praiseworthy is someone we ought to venerate inasmuch and to the extent that their faith was praiseworthy.

- 3 Therefore, we ought to venerate the Blessed Virgin inasmuch and to the extent that her faith was praiseworthy.

- 4 The Blessed Virgin is praised in Scripture precisely for her faith in God’s promises to her, such that her belief was not incidental to her role in God’s plan.

- 5 The Blessed Virgin’s role in God’s plan was that she truly brought Christ our God into the world, being the mother of the God-man and not (for example)

---

<!-- PDF page 229 -->
**[p. 216]**

merely a temporal or physical part of Him or a person who was not God or a merely human being.

- 6 Therefore, the Blessed Virgin’s personal faith was not incidental to her role in God’s plan, which is to say her faith was instrumental to her becoming mother of God and bringing Christ into the world.

- 7 Becoming the mother of God through one’s faith, i.e., bringing Christ into the world, exemplifies the most praiseworthy kind of faith for Christians.

- 8 Therefore, from [6] and [7], the Blessed Virgin bringing Christ into the world by means of her faith exemplifies the most praiseworthy kind of faith for Christians.

- 9 Therefore, from [3] and [8], the Blessed Virgin ought to be venerated in a unique way compared to other saints, since her faith exemplified the most praiseworthy kind of faith for Christians.

While these inferences do not conclude that Christians ought to ask her intercession (a logically subsidiary conclusion involving appeal to auxiliary theories regarding the appropriateness of asking other Christians to pray for us and whether the saints can be petitioned to do likewise), the above inferences show that veneration of her memory as an exemplary Christian hero of faith is highly appropriate. This argument also illustrates the basis for unique character of Marian devotion compared to devotion to other saints – namely, other saints were not involved in the Incarnation of Christ as she was – although it justifies devotion to her on fundamentally the same basis as we would venerate other saints: namely, that we ought to treat as praiseworthy anyone whom God’s Word indicates is or treats as praiseworthy. Since God’s Word (especially in the New Testament) treats people as praiseworthy particularly for their faith, it is not surprising that Christians would understand Mary as venerable for her faith. And that faith played a unique role in salvation and thus seems uniquely praiseworthy.

Nevertheless, “Marian dogma and Marian devotion remain contentious, churchdividing issues”[^c05-127] for many contemporary evangelical Protestants. A popular sentiment among Protestant apologists would go so far as to say that these affirmations regarding the Blessed Virgin constitute “idolatry, plain and simple,” and go so far as to reject the “false Mariology” of Cyril of Alexandria as a serious heresy.[^c05-128] Many Catholics would echo Newman’s sentiment that, “if it be idolatry in us to let our affections respond to our faith, He would not have made her what she is, or He would not have told us that He had so made her; but, far from this, He has sent His Prophet to announce to us, ‘A Virgin shall conceive and bear a Son, and they shall call His name Emmanuel,’ and we have the same warrant for hailing her as God’s Mother, as we have for adoring Him as God.”[^c05-129] Nevertheless, the objection also involves a fundamental confusion. Many seemingly think that accepting these titles or dogmas logically necessitates the appropriateness of requesting Mary’s intercession or other devotional practices associated with that. But this is clearly false: _Marian devotion is logically separable from Marian dogma_.

It is true that the Reformers had severe criticisms of Marian piety, especially as it took form in the medieval period. Yet many evangelical Protestants have lost sight of the positive places where Marian _dogma_ was never at issue in the

---

<!-- PDF page 230 -->
**[p. 217]**

Reformation. The _truths_ about the Blessed Virgin represented either at Ephesus or later Marian dogma were almost entirely uncontroversial at the Reformation. The Reformers themselves – most notably, Luther[^c05-130] as well as Zwlingli and Bullinger[^c05-131] – explicitly defended the position that the dogma proclaimed at Ephesus was necessary for integral Christological faith. The Lutheran “Formula of Concord” (1557) states this belief, and thereby represents that such a belief was considered important enough to incorporate into Protestant dogmatic statements.[^c05-132] Even Calvin, who was hesitant in calling Mary the “mother of God,” was not opposed to attributing to her the title “mother of the Lord.”[^c05-133] Other instances may be adduced where Reformers express beliefs that Mary was perpetually a virgin, assumed bodily into heaven, and sinless. Reformers were open to acknowledging these claims at times, presumably because they saw no necessity for anyone affirming Marian dogmas to endorse specific devotional practices. Logically, we are right to distinguish dogma from devotion. Defending the necessity of Marian dogmas does not logically require defense of any exaggerated Marian piety that might be found, nor does it follow that because such dogmas are invoked in support of false piety or even idolatry (in the worst case) that these dogmas would either require or lend their support to any such thing. Affirming these dogmas does not even require affirming the fittingness of invoking Mary’s intercession. It would be perverse for Christians to deny the underlying Scriptural warrant for referring to her as “mother of God” simply because one does not approve of certain devotional practices involving this title, or merely because the Pope used this title or defined it as Catholic dogma, just as if one were to reject the dogmas of the Trinity or Christology just because they were defined at ecumenical councils which took themselves to be speaking infallibly.

Defending Marian dogmas first requires showing that these dogmas are grounded in Scripture, legitimate developments of those revealed truths. Devotional invocations of the Blessed Virgin’s prayers are found potentially as early as the 3rd or 4th century,[^c05-134] although there are earlier statements _about_ the intercession of the Virgin from the second,[^c05-135] even if the first dogmatic definition about the Blessed Virgin is not until the ecumenical council of Ephesus in 431 AD. What ultimately prompted the first dogmatic definition regarding the Blessed Virgin was the Christological threat of Nestorianism. In short, bishop Nestorius’ denial the appropriateness of a traditional title “mother of God” or “Theotókos” for the Blessed Virgin Mary. Devotion had, in this case, nevertheless played an important preparatory role for dogmatic definitions (as it has done in many other instances of dogmatic development). Irenaeus, writing in the 2nd century, exemplifies a typology repeated in subsequent centuries that “the knot of Eve’s disobedience was loosed by the obedience of Mary. For what the virgin Eve had bound fast through unbelief, this did the Virgin Mary set free through faith.”[^c05-136] As Newman notes, these meditations were common and so the title for Mary was found in many disparate Fathers who had meditated extensively on Mary’s exemplary role as “New Eve” in the salvation of humanity – later 5th-century historian Sozomen refers to Origen (a 2nd-century theologian) as one of the first to utilize the title “Theotókos” in his biblical commentaries, although Origen’s surviving works do not preserve the relevant texts.[^c05-137]

---

<!-- PDF page 231 -->
**[p. 218]**

The title “mother of God” does not look to be a particularly controversial instance of doctrinal development, as it constitutes a straightforward logical inference from two beliefs that most Christians admit are Scriptural: Jesus is God, and Mary is His mother. If these truths hold, Mary is rightly called the “mother of God.” The only ways to block the inference involves implicit confusions about the divine nature, the Trinity, or Christ; e.g., a heretical Trinitarian belief that Jesus is not a concrete divine Person (and rather a part of the Godhead), or where it is only appropriate to refer to the Godhead by the term “God” and not to a Person, or where the Blessed Virgin did not give birth to that _subject_ (“hypostasis”) or _person_ who is divine (and rather to a part of that person or something else), etc. Especially after the dogmatic claims outlined at the preceding ecumenical councils, Nicaea I and Constantinople I, many, such as Cyril of Alexandria, took Nestorius’ denial of this title to indicate a problematic Christological metaphysics. This was verified for them when Nestorius argued, for instance, that the “God-Word was not the son of David,” and that it was inappropriate to say that God suckled at the Virgin’s breast or died on the Cross.[^c05-138] Nestorius’ denial implied that Christ was not _one_ “person” (“hypostasis”) or unitary agent that was both divine and human, and therefore that Mary was not the mother of the divine person, the God-Word or Son, but only the mother of the human person, Jesus Christ. Consequently, the council Fathers at Ephesus condemned Nestorius’ position as heretical and endorsed Cyril of Alexandria’s position that the divine and human natures of Christ Jesus were constituted as one _hypostasis_ or person.[^c05-139]

Endorsing this originally devotional title “mother of God” for the Blessed Virgin was constituted, because of the conciliar definition, a _doctrinal test_ for orthodoxy. The clear upshot of the definition was that the title allows every believer to readily, easily, intuitively grasp and profess Christological orthodoxy without appeal to any complicated metaphysical distinctions regarding _ousia_ and _hypostasis_, such as those given in Cyril’s fuller case against Nestorius and subsequent metaphysical developments. This is what made the devotional developments around the person of the Blessed Virgin Mary an appropriate resource for maintaining Christological orthodoxy among ordinary believers, such that the spread of Marian devotion occasioned by the Council can be understood as essentially catechetical, as leading believers by means of these devotions to a greater grasp of Christ’s nature. Cyril’s own title for Mary as the “sceptre of orthodoxy” represents the core of this perspective on Marian dogma.[^c05-140] As Ratzinger underlines, there was then a deeper rationale for Ephesus’ definition that went beyond the simple logical deduction represented above. Marian dogma came to play a role as a necessary protective “fence” around Christological orthodoxy:

The Christological affirmation of God’s Incarnation in Christ becomes necessarily a Marian affirmation, as _de facto_ it was from the beginning. Conversely: only when it touches Mary and becomes Mariology is Christology itself as radical as the faith of the Church requires. The appearance of a truly Marian awareness serves as the touchstone indicating whether or not the Christological substance is fully present. Nestorianism involves the fabrication of

---

<!-- PDF page 232 -->
**[p. 219]**

a Christology from which the nativity and the mother are removed, a Christology without Mariological consequences. Precisely this operation, which surgically removes God so far from man that nativity and maternity—all of corporeality—remain in a different sphere, indicated unambiguously to the Christian consciousness that the discussion no longer concerned incarnation (becoming flesh), that the center of Christ’s mystery was endangered, if not already destroyed. Thus in Mariology Christology was defended.[^c05-141]

However, two controversial Marian dogmas were proclaimed after Ephesus that have proven much less ecumenically tractable. In 1854, Pope Pius IX proclaimed a dogmatic definition in which it is to be held by Catholics that “the Most Blessed Virgin Mary, at the first instant of her conception, by the singular grace and privilege of almighty God and in view of the merits of Jesus Christ, the Savior of the human race, was preserved immune from all stain of original sin.”[^c05-142] In 1950, Pope Pius XII further defined that “the ever virgin Mary, having completed the course of her earthly life, was assumed body and soul into heavenly glory.”[^c05-143]

When Ortlund therefore alleges that these beliefs in the Immaculate Conception and Assumption cannot be justified by appeal either to historical witness or to Scripture, his arguments against both views generally rest (as with his critique of other developments) on the silence of tradition, and thus to the impossibility of anything like an “oral tradition” regarding the fate of the Blessed Virgin. Ortlund also points to instances of Church Fathers who seem to deny the bodily assumption, the sinlessness, or the preservation from original sin of the Blessed Virgin.[^c05-144] (Ortlund sometimes seems to assume these dogmas require belief Mary did not die, and present evidence from the Fathers who held that Mary died,[^c05-145] but whether Mary died or not was explicitly left undecided by these definitions.) In general, Ortlund’s arguments involve the same difficulties which would undermine the phenomena of development generally.

The overall shape of Ortlund’s arguments is summarized in two questions he poses for his readers. First, “if Mary was as important in the early church as she is in Roman Catholic theology, is it really plausible that so many church fathers would discuss bodily assumptions to heaven and simply fail to mention that it happened to the most important creature?”[^c05-146] Ortlund implies Mary could not acquire a prominent place in the faith of Christians, because, if there was not a prominent place for Mary in the faith or devotion of the early Church, her acquiring such a prominence would only be explainable as a corruption of what Scripture and the early Church believed. Second, “we can justifiably wonder why the Holy Spirit would teach about a specific historical event so many centuries after its alleged occurrence, and only after it was circulated first in heterodox contexts, and alongside so many competing alternative beliefs that arose simultaneously. It seems difficult to locate any reason to conclude that the source of such a process is really the Holy Spirit.”[^c05-147] Ortlund implies that the belief Mary was assumed could not be a development of doctrine, since these beliefs regarding that contingent historical fact seems to have arisen far after the early Church period, and therefore should be ascribed an origin in mere fantastic speculation or even heterodoxy.

---

<!-- PDF page 233 -->
**[p. 220]**

As Ortlund rightly noted, there does not seem to be any “oral tradition” for these beliefs – but that is not sufficient to show that these beliefs are illegitimate developments. As explained in the last chapter, _all_ dogmatics requires us to justify claims about Scripture which had no oral tradition or precedent. The facts of development teach us that, as with Arianism or Marcionism, the life of the Church depends upon our ability to move beyond simply repeating the words of Scripture and affirm that our meta-Scriptural interpretations can be legitimate when we declare that the canon or the condemnation of Arianism is binding on all the faithful, under pain of sin. Whether these developments are legitimate therefore depends upon whether the content of Scripture – that is, what it teaches not only in those indicated places but as a whole – can support these inferences. And no dogmatic development is the product of simply reading one verse and inferring a conclusion: each results from a comprehensive “inference to the best explanation” regarding a slew of such passages. It is no surprise that we can come to new awareness of what Scripture implies, in light of subsequent discoveries made over time, “the fruit of the assimilation of those Scriptural truths under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit.”[^c05-148] And other facts derived from the consensus of the Church and its definitions (which are all interpretations of other aspects of Scriptural teaching) bolster this tradition of interpretation. In just the same way as other meta-Scriptural beliefs are justified, the Marian dogmas are held to have resulted from a process of Scriptural interpretation.

Newman (for instance) explains the logic behind both beliefs as an inference that rests on facts about the Blessed Virgin Mary’s role in salvation, her motherhood and status as “New Eve.” As he notes, there is _overwhelming_ consensus behind Mary’s preeminent sanctity from the early Church, even among those (like Origen or Basil) who apparently denied her sinlessness, where the fundamental principle is clearly that Mary plays a theological role as being the _foremost_ or _exemplar_ holy person. As an example, her sinlessness was remarked upon incidentally by Augustine where, in discussing the universal dominion of sin over the human race, he notes in passing that “we must except the holy Virgin Mary, concerning whom I wish to raise no question when it touches the subject of sins, out of honor to the Lord; for from him we know what abundance of grace for overcoming sin in every particular was conferred upon her who had the merit to conceive and bear him who undoubtedly had no sin.”[^c05-149] While the witness of a few outliers is not a counterexample to a general consensus, Newman points out that when those few Fathers who do so seemingly implicate Mary in doubt or sin, they disclaim that these sins are serious (at least by context) and indeed never deny Mary’s place as _exemplar_ of holiness or faith.[^c05-150] These claims are grounded by those Fathers directly in Scriptural claims made about Mary, e.g., in the Gospel of Luke and Matthew. If this is true, as Newman defends at length, then the Marian dogmas about Mary’s immaculate conception or bodily assumption constitute the logical extension of that same explicit biblical principle considering other biblical claims regarding salvation, predestination, and eschatological assurance, taking Mary as the exemplary instance of fulfillment of Christ’s promises.

---

<!-- PDF page 234 -->
**[p. 221]**

Thus, beginning with explicit Scriptural witness, expanded in the light of reflection within the Church upon Mary’s exemplary role in redemption, prompted by dogmatic definitions regarding Christ and an overall growing consensus, the process ending in the drawing of conclusions regarding the consequent privileges they imply for the beginning and end of her life. Even if nobody believed in the _Assumption_ or the _Immaculate Conception_ as so formulated in later centuries, these dogmas are just another step of a logical extension which _was_ familiar in the early Church: employing the principle that, as Mary appears as the exemplary Christian in places like Luke 1:28, she therefore _rightly_ is taken to fulfill all ancient prophets in her person. If such typological interpretations _were ever appropriate_ in the early Church – going back even to the early 200s – then there is no radical discontinuity in applying those same principles of Scriptural exegesis to read further verses in Scripture, e.g., Revelation 12 or Genesis 3:15, as eminently fulfilled in her person too, as in the Assumption and Immaculate Conception.

A comprehensive analogical reading of several passages provides the basis for a typological affirmation that, since Mary is the exemplary disciple who fulfills all those promises and prophesies in her person that are corporately made to the Church, we can, in principle, apply any such prophecy about the Church to Mary’s own person in a _sensus plenior_ application. Mary is that exemplary person of whom Jesus declared to His beloved disciple, “Behold your mother” (Jn 19:27). In short, that typological principle holds that, for any _promise_ in Scripture applying to the Church, that promise is personally fulfilled in Mary, who is the icon of the Church and what it is to be an exemplary Christian believer. Consequently, if there is a promise that the Church is preserved from sin by God’s prevenient grace, and a promise that the Church will be bodily caught up to heavenly places, we have biblical warrant to draw the inference that Mary is immaculately conceived and assumed bodily into those heavenly places. There is a promise to the Church in St. Paul that the Church would be preserved by God’s grace, “that He might present her to Himself a glorious church, not having spot or wrinkle or any such thing, but that she should be holy and without blemish” (Eph 5:27). By typological principles, this is fulfilled in Mary’s person. Hence, the Immaculate Conception. St. Paul expresses a promise in terms of “anticipated fulfillment” that Christ has already “raised us up together and made us sit together in the heavenly places in Christ Jesus,” (Eph 2:6). We can analogously infer that this promise has been fulfilled in a unique way for the exemplary member of His Church, His mother, whose body and soul is already seated in the heavenly places.

Newman, responding to his former colleague Edward Pusey, lays out an inference that he thinks represents the reasoning that leads ultimately to the doctrines of the preservation of the Virgin from sin. Her preservation from sin follows, he proposes, from analogical reasoning. Mary, as the New Eve, was not endowed with less of a gift from birth than what was needed for her task in “co-operating [with God] in the redemption of the world” – thus, when Gabriel declares that she is “full of grace,” we should infer that this salutation confirms the background reasoning that, just as “Eve had this supernatural inward gift given her from the first moment of her personal existence,” so too we cannot deny that “Mary too had this gift from

---

<!-- PDF page 235 -->
**[p. 222]**

the very first moment of her personal existence.”[^c05-151] Newman notes that much apparent difficulty for Protestants comes in their theory of original sin as a corruption of the soul akin to actual sin, and that Mary being born without original sin would have exempted her from the need to die. “We hold nothing of the kind; we consider that in Adam she died, as others; that she was included, together with the whole race, in Adam’s sentence; that she incurred his debt, as we do; but that, for the sake of Him who was to redeem her and us upon the Cross, to her the debt was remitted by anticipation, on her the sentence was not carried out, except indeed as regards her natural death, for she died when her time came, as others.”[^c05-152]

Among Catholic theologians, Mary being preserved from sin from the first moment of her existence was controversial (e.g., Thomas Aquinas) primarily because later distinctions had not yet been proposed, such as those in the definition of Pius IX, which indicate that Mary’s exemption from the stain of original sin in no way prejudices her need for redemption. However, in light of such qualifications, her preservation “in view of” the merits of Christ defines clearly that her sinlessness was an undeserved and prevenient act of God’s graciousness, just as her role in redemption was from beginning to end an act of God’s grace.[^c05-153] That same insight that her role in redemption was _exemplary “_ from beginning to end” is what likewise ends up undergirding belief regarding a supernatural quality to her end. Levering argues that these historical insights, progressively discovered, are what lead to analogical interpretation of Revelation 12’s image of a woman who gives birth to Christ being translated to glory, and so to belief that Mary’s own life _ended_ in an exemplary way.[^c05-154] In response to questions regarding the justification for dogmatically defining Mary’s assumption, Levering notes that, “only Christ saves us, but we cannot rightly perceive that salvation when we exclude Mary (and Israel/ Church) from the picture.”[^c05-155]

The Assumption is not a belief about a particular hidden historical fact known to us by oral tradition. Levering points out that Pius XII’s definition “does not present itself as the defense of Mary’s Assumption on historical grounds, but rather confirms the truth of centuries of liturgical veneration of Mary.”[^c05-156] Ratzinger notes that “the point at issue cannot be historical tradition of a historical fact; the affirmation [of the Assumption] is misunderstood if it is considered or presented as such.”[^c05-157] The Orthodox theologian Bulgakov summarizes an insight which is common to churches East and West, as a direct inference from the role afforded to Mary as “New Eve” by the early Fathers, that Mary is in her own person an image of the Church: “what is said about the Church is also said about Mary. It will be incorrect to say in a general form that Mary is the Church, and yet it is possible to say that the Church is represented by Mary, in as much as in her person are united all the properties of the Church, in a personal incarnation that is sublime and ultimate.”[^c05-158] Consequently, “… in the Dormition of the Mother of God we praise the glorification of human nature. The latter, having been resurrected, divinized and raised to the right hand of the Father, as the flesh of the Son, now is being glorified in itself, as such, in the person of His Most Pure Mother.”[^c05-159] Daley summarizes the view of Andrew of Crete, an early witness to developing belief in the bodily assumption of Mary, that “in raising Mary from the dead to share in the fullness of life … God has

---

<!-- PDF page 236 -->
**[p. 223]**

done for her what he plans to do for all who believe in and follow Christ, namely, made her into a paradigm of Christian eschatological hope.”[^c05-160] Thus, if Mary is the exemplar disciple who embodies the promises made to the Church in her own person, and there is an eschatological promise made in Scripture which would be fulfilled by Mary’s bodily assumption, then “this doctrine [of the Assumption] coheres, in an eminently fitting manner, with what Scripture teaches about creation and fall, the election of Israel, and the Incarnation of the Son,” as being a logical extension of those basic principles that are found in Scripture.[^c05-161]

Typological hermeneutics explains the way in which Scriptural data, and beliefs present in the early Church, logically support the later developments. My arguments do not aim to convince those who do not already accept the typological principles according to which the Blessed Virgin Mary instantiates or represents in her own person the Church and the promises made to it, but simply to show the _plausibility_ that these dogmas are indirectly revealed in Scripture, and that they can be deduced following principles that are themselves admittedly found in the New Testament. Typological interpretations abound in the New Testament, and so cannot be in principle illegitimate.[^c05-162] Modern biblical scholars have defended that this typological principle regarding the Blessed Virgin _is_ revealed to us in Scripture, and thus is defensible within the best scholarship as an appropriate reading of Scripture.[^c05-163]

As Newman noted, there is a widespread and early attestation to a typological principle about the Blessed Virgin from the early Church, if not in Revelation 12 itself. Since this is the case, the lateness of the appearance of explicit belief in the dogmas of the Immaculate Conception or Assumption is no counterevidence that the logical principles from which they were derived are not present in Scripture or even the early Church. Many early Fathers had begun to extend those claims that Mary was made “without spot or wrinkle” by God’s grace (Eph 5:27), incarnating what it is to be the Church, and experiencing God’s eschatological promises fulfilled in her person.[^c05-164] The late development of these specific Marian dogmas is instead explained by the logical precision required in formulating them appropriately (although there are “presentiments” of both in the early Fathers, as Newman catalogued). Other historical or theological considerations that can be adduced in favor of these doctrines only bolster the case for the Marian dogmas in terms of repeated applications of a typological principle that is well-attested and defensible within the practice of the early Church. So, it is possible and plausible that these Marian dogmas constitute inferences solidly grounded in the content of Scriptural truths, even though the Church was not explicitly aware of them until later in its history, in just the same way that the early Church was not explicitly aware of Christ’s consubstantiality with the Father but embraced beliefs which are logically continuous with the Nicene dogma.

Yet, even if we grant in principle that typological interpretation could logically support either the Immaculate Conception or Assumption dogmas, it does not immediately follow that we ought to dogmatize these beliefs. For instance, the Anglican–Roman Catholic International Commission has proposed an “agreed statement” which holds that the Marian dogmas of the Immaculate Conception and Assumption “understood within the biblical pattern of the economy of grace and

---

<!-- PDF page 237 -->
**[p. 224]**

hope … can be said to be consonant with the teaching of the Scriptures and the ancient common traditions.”[^c05-165] The only serious objection posed is “whether these doctrines concerning Mary are revealed by God in a way which must be held by believers ‘as a matter of faith.’”[^c05-166] For those such as the Orthodox or Anglicans, who accept the typological principle for Marian claims, they recognize that the Scriptures “bear normative witness to such revelation” regarding the Marian dogmas.[^c05-167] They nevertheless think that their centrality to Christian faith is not obvious without ecumenical conciliar affirmation: “it would be the consent of an ecumenical council which, teaching according to the Scriptures, most securely demonstrates that the necessary conditions for a teaching to be de fide had been met. Where this is the case, as with the definition of the Theotókos, both Roman Catholics and Anglicans would agree that the witness of the Church is firmly and constantly to be believed by all the faithful.”[^c05-168]

The strongest arguments to be made in favor of dogmatizing these beliefs, ironically, can be derived from those who offer theological objections that we ought _in principle_ to deny these truths about the Blessed Virgin, rather than merely prudential considerations that definitions would be inopportune or unnecessary. It might have been hard to see an abstract necessity for dogmatizing that Mary is “mother of God” _until_ it became historically clear that the denial of this title to Mary exemplified a deficient Christology. It became clear in dialogue with Nestorius that Cyril’s inference represents a central biblical truth (i.e., that Mary is the mother of Jesus, Jesus is God, and that therefore Mary is the mother of God), and that to abandon the title for Mary was to abandon a central biblical truth. If we think that Mary gave birth to the same Person who is God, and that this belief is taught by Scripture, this is what gives us good reason to think that the dogma defined at Ephesus ought to be professed by all Christians. (Clearly, too, wholesale rejection of Cyril’s theology would require rejecting the Trinitarian and Christological orthodoxy represented by the first five councils.) Many Protestants, by contrast with the apologists, are not as inclined to abandon early conciliar theology and accept the “Theotókos” title as dogmatic. Pannenberg holds it to be “ecumenically binding.”[^c05-169]

Marian dogmas are not dogmatized because belief _Mary as such_ is central to our faith, but because her role in salvation is intimately related to central articles of faith, just as her status as mother of God _is_ a central Scriptural truth, and so dogmatizing beliefs about her ensures that believers have sufficient reason to profess the faith in its integrity. Giving every believer sufficient reason to preserve and maintain integral faith around the central mysteries of Christian faith _is_ a strong reason in terms of public ecclesial goods that would justify dogmatizing these truths about the Blessed Virgin Mary, so these truths about Mary were dogmatized for good reasons that other Christians also have reason to accept. Marian dogma constitutes a “test” of orthodoxy about other central beliefs in Christianity. Many Protestants and Orthodox concede that to deny that Mary is an exemplar for Christian believers is to deny a directly revealed truth in Scripture. Further, if that typological principle behind Marian claims is admitted, the only remaining way to deny these dogmas of the Immaculate Conception or Assumption requires implicit denial of important Scriptural truths the truths asserted of her in would be true of the exemplary

---

<!-- PDF page 238 -->
**[p. 225]**

Christian believer. These dogmas relate, however, to doctrines regarding God’s prevenient grace and assurance of salvation, which Protestants above all should not deny are essential dogmas or which would apply to such an exemplary believer.

Karl Barth is probably one of most significant, theologically sophisticated Reformed voices who argued that the devotion and dogmatic claims attributed to Mary disclose “the one heresy of the Roman Catholic Church which explains all the rest” – specifically, Barth identified Mary’s dogmatic role as being “the principle, type and essence of the human creature co-operating servantlike (ministerialiter) in its own redemption on the basis of prevenient grace, and to that extent the principle, type and essence of the Church.”[^c05-170] Barth’s criticisms naturally apply less to the doctrine that Mary is “mother of God,” which can be interpreted without any appeal to Mary’s role as co-operating, and more to the later dogmas, which Barth sees as undermining Jesus’ unique role as mediator of salvation and as having merited her own salvation. But Barth radically misunderstands the Immaculate Conception and Assumption as asserting that Mary “merited” her own redemption. This is precisely what such dogmas undermine. Being predestined to be preserved from sin _before she even existed_ could not have been a product of Mary’s merits.[^c05-171] She did not cooperate in meriting prevenient grace; the dogma _defines_ that she was predestined from eternity to play the role that she did, through no merit of her own, but only through the merits of Christ her redeemer (“in view of the merits of Jesus Christ”). Any merit Mary has could only come from Christ. Similarly, she as a creature would not have been able to merit, by any act, that she would be exempt from the (un)natural condition of human beings – that consequence of the debt required by original sin – which would otherwise require her to die a natural human death, and her body corrupt in the tomb.

As an exemplar of the salvation of humanity, beliefs about Mary in this regard radically undermine the proposal we can “merit” salvation. These dogmas delimit any role for human beings to participate in Christ’s Sacrifice, since Mary is interpreted to have _only_ a role entirely subordinate to Christ’s grace and redemption – her role is that of the exemplary Christian believer, whose sacrifices and prayers for the salvation of others is a graced cooperation with applying the fruits of the redemption of Christ to the world (cf. Jn 15:5, Col. 1: 24, Jam 5:16). No Protestant theologian could make this point more clearly than Vatican II: “No creature could ever be counted along with the Incarnate Word and Redeemer … the Church does not hesitate to profess the subordinate role of Mary.’”[^c05-172] Timothy George therefore notes that there is no danger to those dogmatic claims regarding titles of Mary like “mother of God” or referring to the Blessed Virgin as the “New Eve” who “cooperates in the redemption of the world” overshadowing the unique role of Christ the Redeemer, since “orthodox Catholics interpret … Marian titles in a way that they believe leaves intact the unique role of Jesus Christ as the mediator between God and man.”[^c05-173] Tim Perry[^c05-174] has likewise offered extensive defenses of Marian dogmatic claims as preserving the unique mediatorship of Christ. There is no logical implication that, if Mary functions as the exemplar believer whose example and prayers for us remain of perpetual value, then Christ’s work would be incomplete without Mary.

---

<!-- PDF page 239 -->
**[p. 226]**

Karl Barth indeed offered an extended defense of the dogma of the virgin birth in _Church Dogmatics_ I/2, arguing that even if the dogma itself were not stated in Scripture but had to be inferred or collected out of various passages which (individually) might involve reasonable disagreements about interpretation, affirming the virgin birth was crucial to preserve the supernatural character of Christian belief, such that to deny this dogma would _practically_ entail denying central truths of the Gospel. Marian dogmas are defensible in exactly the same way that Barth defends the virgin birth. Marian dogmas underline that whatever cooperation a creature can have with God’s salvation is entirely a product of unmerited grace – and further that the promises made to each person who dies in Christ _have been_ fulfilled for the Church, are already beginning, so that our confident assurance in God’s promises is warranted. Mary exemplifies what is true of all Christian believers: that we are saved by grace _alone,_ and that God’s promises give us _assurance_ that those God has predestined will be saved, body and soul. To deny these truths is to deny central truths of the Gospel.

“At the heart of what the Protestants sought to recover was the Pauline teaching that ‘one is justified by faith apart from works of the law’” (Rom. 3:28).[^c05-175] Protestants like Barth and Ortlund typically take such messages as central to the Gospel, and argue that belief in such claims about Christ’s unique mediatorship and assurances of salvation are among the most essential (if not the only) beliefs worth ensuring that every believer is aware of, providing the surest hope to every believer that they should rest themselves upon the merits of Christ alone and put aside all fear or anxiety. But it is precisely these central Protestant commitments about the nature of salvation that are fulfilled by way of exemplar in the case of the Blessed Virgin Mary, and which these Marian dogmas attest to. The Marian dogmas exemplify two truths about grace and assurance of predestination. First, that grace/predestination is _unconditional,_ relying not on our merits but on God’s gracious promises, as exemplified in the Immaculate Conception of the Blessed Virgin. Second, that the assurance of the saints is that it is _entirely sure,_ relying on the fidelity of God, who has already begun to fulfill His eschatological promises in the person of the Blessed Virgin as surely as He will do for each of us on the last day. “Mary, therefore, should be looked on as the living pledge of Christ’s promises to the Church: that where he is, we also be (John 14:3); then the glory given him by the Father he will give to us, as he received it” (John 17:22).[^c05-176]

Anglican theologian John Macquarrie[^c05-177] and Lutheran theologian Wolfhart Pannenberg have each independently proposed a case for ecumenical reception of Marian dogma as succinctly encapsulating these key Reformation principles regarding grace: “Christology is the explication of the meaning of an historical event, while Mariology attempts to personify the characteristics of the new mankind of faith.”[^c05-178] Consequently, a case can be made that Marian dogmas succinctly formulate or illustrate the central truths of the Gospel as many Protestants see it. For this reason, the Marian dogmas are ironically potentially a pathway to find doctrinal convergence around the central truths of the Reformation. These dogmas avoid engaging in technical polemics around “salvation by faith _alone_,” the extrinsic imputation of grace, the nature of free will, or the unconditional

---

<!-- PDF page 240 -->
**[p. 227]**

predestination of the elect – formulations of which doctrines have been controversial among Protestants themselves. Rather than being “accretions” which obscure, Protestants might accept such dogmas as capturing the “simple truth of the Gospel” that moves us beyond denominational division toward full unity in professing the same basic beliefs.

Once we put aside the misinterpretations, the only way to deny the principles regarding salvation expressed by these Marian dogmas would require radical denials that believers can _in any way_ participate in extending or applying the fruits of Christ’s sacrifice through prayer for others or through preaching the Gospel, even when acting under God’s grace and in His Providence. But it seems obvious that prayer or public preaching/witness to the Gospel truly _does_ make a difference in the salvation of others. This claim would not undermine Christ’s unique salvific role, as the fact that we can make a difference is not independent of God’s grace. Congar argued that Barth’s view on which believers cannot even play subordinate roles in their salvation points to deep Christological confusions among Protestant theologians, where (despite an explicit affirmation of Nicaea, Ephesus, and Chalcedon) they implicitly hold “that the humanity of Christ plays no part in the causality of Redemption.”[^c05-179] Congar’s views proved prophetic, as Christological and Trinitarian heterodoxy practically does follow rejection of Marian dogmas, as witnessed by, e.g., William Lane Craig’s embrace of Apollinarianism and recent controversies among other analytic theologians regarding “theistic personalism” versus the Trinitarian orthodoxy of the first seven ecumenical councils. But Congar was simply echoing Newman, who had already pointed out to Edward Pusey that there is empirical evidence that Marian dogma and devotion were valuable for maintaining Christological and Trinitarian orthodoxy.

Two large views, as I have said above, are opened upon our devotional thoughts in Christianity; the one centering in the Son of Mary, the other in the Mother of Jesus. Neither need obscure the other; and in the Catholic Church, as a matter of fact, neither does. … There is this broad fact the other way;—that, if we look through Europe, we shall find, on the whole, that just those nations and countries have lost their faith in the divinity of Christ, who have given up devotion to His Mother, and that those on the other hand, who had been foremost in her honour, have retained their orthodoxy. Contrast, for instance, the Calvinists with the Greeks, or France with the North of Germany, or the Protestant and Catholic communions in Ireland. As to England, it is scarcely doubtful what would be the state of its Established Church, if the Liturgy and Articles were not an integral part of its Establishment; and, when men bring so grave a charge against us, as is implied in your Volume, they cannot be surprised if we in turn say hard things of Anglicanism. In the Catholic Church Mary has shown herself, not the rival, but the minister of her Son; she has protected Him, as in His infancy, so in the whole history of the Religion. There is then a plain historical truth in Dr. Faber’s words, which you quote to condemn, ‘Jesus is obscured, because Mary is kept in the background.’[^c05-180]

---

<!-- PDF page 241 -->
**[p. 228]**

## Conclusion

If the arguments of the preceding section were plausible, we have cumulative good reasons to agree that papal primacy and the Marian dogmas play a _practically_ necessary role in the life of the Church. These dogmas are means to prevent schism and heresy, much like the dogmatic definitions against Trinitarian and Christological heresies whose necessity for the life of the Church was defended in Chapter 3. My case did not rest on views proper only to the charismatic or institutional model. If there is dogmatic authority in the Church, as I have argued all parties ought to acknowledge, then there is good reason for all Christians to acknowledge and receive these beliefs as _fittingly_ dogmatized, even if they do not recognize each other’s ecclesial authorities. And, if we can recognize these truths despite differences among our dogmatic authorities, there is far less dividing the churches than we might have initially thought. Those differences might be thought of as largely _political_ rather than theological, in the sense that we need to resolve how we might practically overcome centuries-long divisions and begin to recognize the same dogmatic authorities together, rather than persist in misunderstanding of each other’s Christian faith.

At the time of the Reformation Martin Bucer and Philip Melanchthon were exemplary for their “ecumenicity” in attempting to avoid schism from the Catholic Church as well as preventing internal fragmentation among Protestants. We should follow that spirit: if there is a suitable orthodox interpretation of those controversial dogmas which remain obstacles to union, one which does not paper over differences but finds a theological _core_ set of beliefs which represent the Gospel as each understand it, and we can recognize in each other’s confessions the way in which God’s Word demands of us to profess those same beliefs, then we will likewise have strict obligations in faith to do so _together._ Until then, Protestant, Catholic, and Orthodox Christians all have strict obligations in charity to strive toward that reunification by engaging in such efforts at both theological dialogue and constructive spiritual fellowship. That is simply what love of God demands.

[^c05-45]: Ortlund thinks Nicaea II involves a dogmatically required falsehood, since he interprets Nicaea II to claim that icon veneration is the “faith of the apostles” and “ancient legislation of the catholic church” (Ortlund, 211), and that these claims are empirically false. On the one hand, what dogmatic definitions define is infallible, not the preface or other material surrounding the definition; there is no reason to think these claims are dogmatic, since they are not what is defined by Nicaea II. On the other hand, if icon veneration is a legitimate development of the faith, these phrases would be true, even if nobody in the ancient Church _practiced_ veneration of icons. The phrases would mean simply that the liceity of icon veneration follows from what the apostles believed, which would follow necessarily if icon veneration is a legitimate development.

[^c05-100]: The practical necessity of public determination of these limits to papal power can be illustrated by what results from the lack of them. Either explicit or implicit accusations of heresy against a Roman Pontiff were arguably central in precipitating both the Protestant Reformation and the earlier Great Schism between Orthodoxy and the Latin Church. And, in recent years, under the papacy of Pope Francis, there is a small subset of Catholic theologians have questioned publicly whether the Pope endorsed heresy in public or private statements, some going so far as to accuse him of heresy. Whatever the facts of the case regarding the facts of the matter in all these cases, none was helped by a lack of public, doctrinal clarity regarding these issues. In line with _Ut Unum Sint_’s call for discussion, it seems to me that the primary issue remaining for the churches concerns these legal issues around limits to papal power, and that the onus of this burden lies with the Catholics to advance the discussion by delimiting clearly what options are doctrinally possible for mutual agreement. In this way, past controversies need not always limit us going forward. If the papal office potentially plays a role that is critical for the unity of the Church, as I will argue can be recognized by all parties, that gives us all the more reason to ensure that procedures or norms are laid out regarding what to do in such situations.
