**[p. 236]**

# Conclusion

Stand fast therefore in the liberty by which Christ has made us free, and do not be entangled again with a yoke of bondage. Indeed I, Paul, say to you that if you become circumcised, Christ will profit you nothing. And I testify again to every man who becomes circumcised that he is a debtor to keep the whole law. You have become estranged from Christ, you who attempt to be justified by law; you have fallen from grace. For we through the Spirit eagerly wait for the hope of righteousness by faith. For in Christ Jesus neither circumcision nor uncircumcision avails anything, but faith working through love.

(Gal. 5:1–6)

The premise of this book, and the thread that has guided the argument of each chapter, has been quite simple: _heresy is bad_. The case of the last chapter depended upon assumptions that Christians should care about whether they are within the Church and helping to ensure that visible unity in faith and charity among Christians is maintained. Those of the fourth chapter argued that every Christian has an obligation of the faith in ensuring that the Word of God remains whole and integral throughout history, and that this task would be impossible if we were to concede either that no possible continuity could ever be identified or if we thought there was no way for the Church to respond legitimately to challenges that arise for the faith. The arguments at the core of the book, those given in Chapter 3 which aim to justify a need for dogmatic authority, and which undergird all of the others, was (in brief) that if there were no dogmatic authority, we would be unable to violate or fulfill our duties to God to believe what He teaches us, and that species of unbelief which is heresy would not be a sin under any circumstances. This argument would clearly be unpersuasive to those who fail to accept the implicit assumption that heresy _should_ be a sin under at least some circumstances.

The first two chapters aimed to outline the way in which we have direct duties to believe God, which give us obligations to do so when we hear His Word spoken to us, and then the way in which these duties to obey and believe God entail both epistemic and moral duties. Believing God as First Truth gives us obligations not to receive other words in place of God’s Word and to reject whatever is incompatible with that Word. God’s Word also gives us moral duties to confess, practice, and maintain the faith – the faith is _valuable_ for our salvation and that of others. But

DOI: 10.4324/9781003663485-6

---

<!-- PDF page 250 -->
**[p. 237]**

many of those who might grant that faith in a generic sense is valuable might nevertheless be skeptical of the way in which heresy fits into this story. “Heresy” calls to mind witch-hunts, coercion of innocent believers even to the point of torture or burning alive, confession of principles or dogmatic claims that are held simply because an authority dictates them, and, ultimately, a practical attitude where bringing others under your _power_ is more important than bringing them to see the _truth_ for themselves. It is unsurprising, in a world where these images are commonly associated with the word “heresy,” that “The Magisterium” plays the part of villain in at least one fantasy novel!

This book has already been overly long, so I cannot anticipate the various objections that have or could be raised against considering heresy to be a sin. But, in this conclusion, I want to draw out that Christian _love_ demands that we not set aside our concern for the faith of others and follow this temptation of our time to ignore the sinfulness of heresy. What is problematic about the sin of heresy that has nothing to do with power and everything to do with our duties toward others. Heresy is nothing less than a disguised form of selfishness or egoism, under an apparently religious veneer. It is a species of hypocrisy. When I speak of “heresy” in this way, I obviously do not mean simply disagreement with me or my religious opinions or those of my Church. Catholic moral theology has traditionally distinguished “material” and “formal” heresy. “Material” heresy is not a sin. A Christian believer is a material heretic when they make an innocent mistake about what God wants us to believe, instead believing something that is false and potentially seriously so. What sets apart a “material” from a “formal” heretic is then their intention and attitude. A “formal” heretic is thus someone who persists in believing the false and potentially serious wrong belief, despite being aware of their obligations to correct their belief – obligations that come ultimately from God. As Aquinas puts it, the heretic “intends to assent to Christ, yet he fails in his choice of those things wherein he assents to Christ, because he chooses not what Christ really taught, but the suggestions of his own mind” and attributes such “choosing” variously to pride, perverse desire for novelty, or greed.[^c06-1]

The tradition takes persisting in erroneous theological belief despite attempts at correction by the Church’s authorities to constitute a clear indication that one is a heretic, following Augustine: “Those, therefore, in the Church of Christ who savor anything morbid and depraved, and, on being corrected that they may savor what is wholesome and right, contumaciously resist, and will not amend their pestiferous and deadly dogmas, but persist in defending them, become heretics, and, going without, are to be reckoned as enemies who serve for her discipline.”[^c06-2] Catholic canon law thus specifies that a heretic is _obstinate_ in the face of attempts to correct their misapprehensions (CIC 751). But, clearly, not any kind of mistake about the faith is serious enough to be a sin – Aquinas, for instance, recognizes that there are many indifferent matters, where there are many legitimate opinions. For Aquinas, appeal to the dogmatic authority is going to be the criteria as to what counts as a _serious_ transgression of the rule of faith “… certain doctors seem to have differed either in matters the holding of which in this or that way is of no consequence, so far as faith is concerned, or even in matters of faith, which were

---

<!-- PDF page 251 -->
**[p. 238]**

not as yet defined by the Church; although if anyone were obstinately to deny them after they had been defined by the authority of the universal Church, he would be deemed a heretic.”[^c06-3]

But the Reformation has largely called into question this traditional rule of thumb as widely applicable for any but very narrow concerns about theologians operating within confessional educational institutions (seminaries and the like), where employees voluntarily accept the duty of teaching specific doctrines. It seems fair that, if they do not fulfill that burden, the denomination has the right to fire them, although many would not see much sin involved beyond perhaps a broken promise. For instance, the apocryphal story of Martin Luther, standing before a tribunal of cardinals and princes, declaring, “Here I stand; I can do no other,” is not told to illustrate amoral obstinacy, nor knowingly violating God’s will. Nor does Jan Hus who, while being threatened with being burned at the stake (as was in the end his fate), declared that, “I am minded to obey the Apostolic mandates and to obey them in all points, but what I call the Apostolic mandates are the doctrines of Christ’s Apostles, and so far as the mandates of the Roman pontiff are in accord with the Apostolic mandates and doctrine, that is, according to the rule of Christ, so far I intend most certainly to obey them. But, if I find them to be at variance, I will not obey them even if you put before my eyes fire for the burning of my body.”[^c06-4] And when even in our own day and age, theocratic governments still sentence people to death for blasphemy or for converting from one religion to another, none of this strikes us as a truly loving or caring attitude, let alone a concern for the truth about God. It just looks morally abominable.

I agree in rejecting such attitudes, including Aquinas’ own attempt to justify capital punishment for heretics. But it is common to draw the wrong lesson from such cases, in view of widespread historical forgetfulness. On the one hand, there was nothing about “high” theories of dogmatic authority that necessitated such religious violence, just as there was nothing about “low” or non-existent theories of dogmatic authorities that prevented it. Different theories of ecclesiastical governance and authority made little difference within Christendom in preventing violence – Protestants and Catholics were (at the time) in agreement that heretics like Michael Servetus or Giordano Bruno should be burned; the Anglican, Lutheran, and Reformed governments persecuted Catholics just as much as Catholic governments did the same; Zwingli saw the drowning of Anabaptists as the symbolically fitting punishment for their belief in rebaptism; and, even in Puritan New England, the witch trials ended up showing that even non-conformism was capable of religious violence. It was, in fact, a common consensus view of the ancient world – whether Christian or not – that those who held deviant religious views and publicly spread them ought to be punished. Persecution of “dangerous” religious ideologies by governments in the Middle East and Asia, whether under Sultans or Emperors, had nothing to do with Christianity or even any particularly Christian notion of orthodoxy. Theories of dogmatic authority are not a good explanation for the phenomenon of such religious and ideological violence; those who rejected dogmatic authorities engaged in the same deplorable acts.

---

<!-- PDF page 252 -->
**[p. 239]**

On the other hand, many draw the lesson that such cases require us to hold a studied skepticism about religious and moral truth. Modern totalitarian governments and their revolutions enforced atheist, fanatical political forms of s ideology on mankind with more mindless fervor than any religious person had ever dreamed. These regimes (e.g., Maoist, Stalinist) ended up with far greater collective quantities of bloodshed than all religious history put together, given modern technological means to murder such vast numbers of innocent people. The advocates of totalitarianism proclaimed that no authorities should supersede that of the State or Party, and the danger such ideologies have posed to humanity requires a definitive response to the threat posed by their potential to arise in the future. Principled skepticism or relativism, whether moral or religious, does nothing to assist in preventing such violence – indeed, such positions undermine any coherent attempt to _condemn_ such violence as deeply morally evil and pernicious. A better explanation of decrease of religious violence in the modern period is that, prompted to reflection first by internal religious conflicts among Christians and then later from these totalitarian regimes, a common consensus began to emerge that this violence was an offense against “human rights” or “human dignity.” What occurred historically was not a consensus that religious or moral truths were unimportant or unknowable – rather, the insight was something closer to the view that our differing moral or religious views did not prevent a common consensus that it was right to condemn that violence, and indeed that each view had the resources to do so, based on its own internal moral or religious principles.

From my perspective, there are a few takeaways from these historical facts. First, the danger posed by fanaticism and ideological violence (whether religious or political) needs to be opposed by embracing and encouraging others to embrace the truth about morality and religion, not abandoning truth claims about morality or religion. It is neither “intolerant” nor unreasonable to condemn violent religious/ political fanaticism – let alone egregious instances of moral wrongs such as rape or genocide – as being objectively morally _wrong_ in every time and place. It is simply to state the moral and religious facts. Second, an endeavor to bring others to embrace the truth about their moral duties and endorse moral or religious facts is not intolerant or unreasonable. Clearly, to educate people regarding the truth is good, and to educate people in their moral or religious duties would greatly help them fulfill those duties – we find it unproblematic to think children, for instance, ought to be educated in this way. Yet it is obvious, beyond these individual benefits, that educating the wider adult community in their moral duties toward each other can serve a necessary function in protecting the community. For instance, making it our goal that we ought to persuade all people and their governments to accept, endorse, and effectively enforce human rights protections is not an imposition of one worldview upon others, but a step taken precisely in service of protecting each other from abuses of power and the evils associated with fanaticism or ideology. Again, it would be unreasonable and publicly destructive to dismiss any such educational campaigns; instead, what matters is that we educate the community in the _right_ or _true_ principles, in the appropriate ways, such as to aim to publicly persuade rather than to dominate or coerce (and to bring others to embrace these same norms).

---

<!-- PDF page 253 -->
**[p. 240]**

Third, these considerations do not even rule out coercive sanctions of the right sort. A campaign to promote popular endorsement of a philosophy of human rights, for instance, would seem feckless without advocating human rights _law,_ alongside recognized procedures for public and international _enforcement_ of that law. An apparently “pragmatic” solution of allowing each country to enforce human rights law as it sees fit, let alone to be the sole interpreter of what human rights involve, would be to abandon the goals of the project: historically, the human rights project was aimed precisely at those countries like Nazi Germany that claimed to be the sole authority within their domain of justice or morality, by nature exempt from the judgment of outside “non-Aryan” powers, and so their State’s actions and commands were unaccountable to any higher earthly or spiritual power for any “purported crimes” involving, e.g., the genocide of the Jewish people. The judgment of the world was that there _was_ a higher law than the Fuhrer and Reich, and that the world would henceforth take it upon itself to enforce that law by agreed upon international procedures, so that the crimes of that regime would never happen again. While it is (I think) not the case that the world has effectively fulfilled its duty in that regard, they rightly recognized that preventing those crimes required legal enforcement beyond good words or wishes. A rules-governed public order, by which we prevent uses of coercion by either government or individuals, is precisely what is needed to protect an environment in which there is freedom of speech, religion, and other similar freedoms.

In the religious domain, we have similar incentives for preventing unreasonable religious violence to develop dogmatic authorities that can enforce effective legal protections which both impose sanctions for the right ends (e.g., protecting the rights of religious persons against one another) and effectively provide legal remedies so that there is a regime in which “rule of law” takes precedence over arbitrary and unaccountable exercise of power. Catholic canon law was modified after Vatican II, for instance, to lay out the legally enforceable rights and duties of lay faithful and clergy (e.g., CIC 208–231), along with the processes by which they could ensure their effective protection in Church courts. Similarly, the Church legally incorporated norms by which theologians could ensure their freedoms for theological research alongside clearly delineated processes by which doctrinal disputes would be handled (e.g., _Donum Veritatis_). Publicly enforceable norms such as these aim to prevent the danger of arbitrary sanctions against individual lay faithful, clergy, or theologians. If we value such protections, we have good reason to endorse dogmatic authorities as part of the solution, rather than the impediment, to avoiding arbitrary religious violence.

Fourth, while it is often true that we can mistake our own worldview for universal norms or principles, or be blinded by bias and fail to appreciate the moral facts accurately, the solution to this issue is again not skepticism but _public norms_ of dialogue and persuasion. That is, willingness to be corrected and to modify our principles in response to criticism. By a similar token, modern political institutions have gravitated toward regimes which we popularly call “democratic” but are more properly _limited_ governments, whose powers are usually enumerated, with separations of power between branches of government (or “checks and balances”), and in

---

<!-- PDF page 254 -->
**[p. 241]**

which the authorities are popularly accountable and can be replaced peacefully, by voting, and in which the system as a whole (i.e., its constitutional structure) is subject to revision through public means. The insight behind such systems is both that not _any_ objection by any single party or individual is sufficient reason to change the system, and that the majority’s wishes can often create a “tyranny” as much as the domination of any single individual. The best we can do is find the appropriate balance in the structure of our accountability systems (i.e., law or government) in such a way that they are not subject to arbitrary change but likewise flexible enough to admit remedies and revision as is necessary.

In the religious domain, likewise, I have argued that dogmatic authority in the Church need not be any different. While the “institutional” and “charismatic” theories of authority differ on necessary conditions for holding religious authority in the Christian Church, given varied perspectives regarding the will of its divine Founder Jesus Christ about the necessity of sacramental ordination, both stand in agreement that the “rule of law” takes precedence over both these theories. Nobody can appoint themselves a pastor, just as sacramental ordination does not, by itself, entitle anyone to lawful authority (“jurisdiction”) within the Church. They likewise agree that neither democratically elected nor sacramentally ordained individuals act lawfully or ought to be obeyed in every instance – e.g., excommunications can be imposed unjustly – and that the desires of even the vast majority of believers, whether lay or clerics, would not be sufficient reason to change the fundamental or basic constitution of the Church or its theology. These derive ultimately from fidelity to God’s Word. Catholics thus agree the Pope cannot set aside divine Revelation or bind the Church to beliefs that are simply his own opinions, and Orthodox agree on the same limits as regards ecumenical Councils, as do Protestants regarding any given regularly constituted local congregation. “Scripture cannot be set aside” (John 10:35).

As noted in the chapter on development, however, we need to appeal beyond simply epistemic criteria that a given belief is _false_ to identify a corruption of Christian dogma, since whether those criteria are violated is central to the disagreement. The ways I have presented by which we consider the Church community and its dogmatic authority from the “political” perspective give us a better way to understand what makes heresy a sin. Even as classical authors acknowledged, it is not necessarily a sin to believe what is false. It _can be_ an epistemic fault to fail to properly acknowledge the weight of consensus among the experts, as happens with conspiracy theories or quack pseudo-science, and heresy usually involves such failures, both moral and epistemic, to be properly deferential to community norms or expertise.[^c06-5] Yet epistemic criteria are insufficient, since no heretic agrees that their own views involve setting aside Scripture, and so will not acknowledge the competence of those who disagree with them _at least in this case_. And the heretic is not necessarily committing a fault in affirming this possibility, which is possible, and there is nothing, by itself, wrong in making an exception to expertise if we (blamelessly) think we have good reason to do so. We have no _a priori_ assurance that what is identified as a heresy by even a large group of people is in fact such. Many times, all churches have acknowledged that an _apparently_ normative

---

<!-- PDF page 255 -->
**[p. 242]**

dogmatic consensus proved not to be normative at all, in retrospect, involve the apparent consensus in favor of religious violence or slavery, but also, e.g., as when heliocentrism was held in suspicion on religious grounds by both Protestants and Catholics, until scientific evidence came to convince all that this suspicion was unwarranted.

Failure to be deferential to the right experts involves something closer to a moral fault when it leads one to cults or conspiracy theories; insistence on our own insight, that we “crunched the numbers,” etc. For this reason, the fault in heresy lies in the _will_, not the intellect. A better way to identify heresy is in terms of its “political” aspect, as a failure of responsibilities not only to God but toward others in the Christian Church. Specifically, that sinful attitude which makes heresy sinful lies in failing to acknowledge proper _accountability to other Christians_. So, it is not that heretics propose that the consensus among theologians (past or present) _could be wrong,_ or that a dogmatic definition _could be incomplete_ or even _needs correction,_ that makes their attitude sinful. Rather, what is morally problematic is an attitude by which a Christian believer would insulate themselves from theoretical or practical accountability to other Christians for what they teach or preach the demands of God’s Word to be. As where an individual rejects submitting to the moral demand of being accountable to the law (as contrary to their conscience) and refuses to accept any penalty or would demand that others in the community follow their contentious moral vision of what the law ought to be, without being proposing this vision to the “test” of a public procedure that involves the input of all members, so too heretics are those who _insist_ on being accountable only to themselves in making public demands on the community. The heretic insists that they deserve recognition by other Christians as holding the same faith, that other Christians do them an injustice by failing to accept their views as orthodox, but their sin lies in the assumption (whether implicit or explicit) that it is an injustice that _anyone_ (apart from themselves) could be competent to judge their cause. No possible evidence from the testimony of past or present Christians, the judgment of its ecclesiastical authorities, etc., would be counted by them as counterevidence to their own theories and conclusions. In accusing others of dogmatic blind obedience to the authority of the Christian community, the heretic is, ironically, a dogmatic follower of their own reason.

Appealing solely to God’s judgment in the future can be a subtle practical means to ensure that no lawful demand can be made upon the heretic by other Christians for their beliefs or behavior, whether past, present, or future. A theologian who rejects that anyone in the Church could ever convince them to believe that (for example) an eternal hell exists, that all other Christians who fail to agree with them in rejecting that belief is a wicked person, and that they should therefore in principle be exempt from any censure or correction of any kind – indeed, they _deserve_ to be treated as a Christian in good standing by other Christians – evinces an attitude every Christian can recognize as incompatible with Christian faith. We recognize this attitude as wrong for the same reasons that no Christian agrees that the Church – no group or individual – sits in judgment on God’s Word. Apart from setting aside what Scripture demands, heretics aim to _redraw_

---

<!-- PDF page 256 -->
**[p. 243]**

the boundaries of the religious community, on their own initiative, in such a way that nobody else can hold them accountable. But no Christian should agree that every individual has the power to rewrite Scripture or redraw the boundaries of the Church as they see fit and demand that others submit to their judgment. That would be to arrogate God’s own authority. In a civil government, the shape of government is ultimately decided upon by its citizens, and no decision of that government or constitutional measure is immune (in principle) to change by its citizens. In ecclesiastical government, the fundamental shape was given to the Church by Christ.

To affirm dogmatic authority in the Church is not to affirm that any individual (even the Pope!) holds authority _over_ God’s Word, or over the shape of the Church’s constitution, as heretics implicitly do. To affirm the necessary role of dogmatic authority, and the badness of heresy, aims precisely to _prevent_ abuses of authority and redrawing of the constitution of the Church by any individual or group within it. Dogmatic authority is vested in the Church as a whole; the _Church_ was what Christ Himself founded and endowed with a mission to preach His Gospel, rather than any specific individual in it. Papal power can be exercised unlawfully, just as Councils have been convened unlawfully, or individuals or congregations have tried to arrogate and abuse ecclesiastical power. And the Church is not identical with its leadership at any given point in time. Popes too are held accountable to the decisions of lawful ecumenical councils, previous papal definitions, and the objective facts concerning the past normative consensus of the Church, the limits imposed by the divine constitution of the Church, divine law surrounding the sacraments, and (of course) Scriptural teaching.[^c06-6] The fact that the Church’s future decisions are constrained by its “irreformable” past follows naturally from the fact that Christ Himself is the only source of our authority, and Christ’s founding of the Church and God’s speaking the definitive Word to His people lies in our temporal past. It would be blasphemous to admit the very possibility that any Church decision or “development” could _change_ what God revealed to us; the Church’s job is to protect and maintain, in the face of challenges, that which God has given once and for all.

Christians therefore have no choice but to draw boundaries, given that they have _moral duties_ to maintain the faith once given through the apostles. This task requires us to take what we have received, and the solemn testimony of past ages to God’s Word, as an inheritance that we preserve with fear and trepidation. It requires confession to the truth about God’s Word; it requires definitions, when threats arise to the faith; but it requires repentance, too, when the Church fails in its mission. As I have noted, the heretic is not wrong in affirming that the Church, even the whole Church, can make a mistake. There have been false beliefs that were common among Christians at various points in time. A Luther or Hus who _conscientiously_ protests that the Church’s teaching has fallen into error or other abuses need not be a heretic simply because they fail to agree with the authorities – they could be _right_ in their criticisms. To say that dogma is irreformable and infallible is to make a claim that the whole Church could never _bind_ everyone for all time to believe what is seriously

---

<!-- PDF page 257 -->
**[p. 244]**

heretical and thereby lose the Gospel (as if, e.g., the Church could have recognized the wrong canon of Scripture and bound all believers to accept something other than God’s Word!).

Many misunderstand infallibility as a claim about the present – that the Spirit is speaking to leaders of the Church or giving them special insight. Rather, the claim properly regards the future. To believe in the infallibility of the Church stakes our faith on God’s Providence _continuing_ to guide the Church; not that the Church can never or never has made a mistake, but that – whatever mistakes are made – those mistakes will never be _irreparable_. When Newman responds to criticisms of papal infallibility, he notes that history affords us with examples where there was a fear that a Council or a Pope had bound the Church to error. Nevertheless, history bore out orthodoxy and the Gospel, never allowing God’s Word to be eclipsed by the failures of His Church.

It was natural for men who were in the minority at Ephesus to think that the faith of the Church had been brought into the utmost peril by the definition of the Council which they had unsuccessfully opposed. They had opposed it on the conviction that the definition gave great encouragement to religious errors in the opposite extreme to those which it condemned; and, in fact, I think that, humanly speaking, the peril was extreme. The event proved it to be so, when twenty years afterwards another Council was held under the successors of the majority at Ephesus and carried triumphantly those very errors whose eventual success had been predicted by the minority. But Providence is never wanting to His Church. St. Leo, the Pope of the day, interfered with this heretical Council, and the innovating party was stopped in its career. Its acts were cancelled at the great Council of Chalcedon, the Fourth Ecumenical, which was held under the Pope’s guidance, and which, without of course touching the definition of the Third, which had been settled once for all, trimmed the balance of doctrine by completing it, and excluded for ever from the Church those errors which seemed to have received some sanction at Ephesus. There is nothing of course that can be reversed in the definitions of the Vatican Council; but the series of its acts was cut short by the great war, and, should the need arise (which is not likely) to set right a false interpretation, another Leo will be given us for the occasion; “in monte Dominus videbit.”[^c06-7]

Newman’s example of Providential guidance concerned facts where the incompleteness of the definition of an ecumenical Council _required_ further development in a future definition and had left undone what was left as a task to later generations. George Lindbeck, for instance, found such accounts of dogmatic authority and infallibility consonant with his own perspective as a Lutheran theologian, arguing further that these claims to irreformability were necessary to avoid naïve Protestant inability to accommodate development in doctrine; these claims are part of a theoretical framework that prevents us from falling into dual errors of a historically naïve fundamentalism where all doctrine must have been held explicitly or

---

<!-- PDF page 258 -->
**[p. 245]**

consciously by the apostles themselves and a liberal approach where no doctrine could be either necessary for Christian belief or asserted confidently.[^c06-8]

Contemporary theological dialogue also affords an example where reconciliation was achieved by recognizing a mistake in what was done (or, better, left undone) at a Council, without acknowledging that the past definitions of that Council were false. Adherents to the Council of Chalcedon’s Christological definitions (Orthodox/Catholic) and those who refused to subscribe to those definitions (Oriental Orthodox) have now openly acknowledged that these Oriental Orthodox Christians do not _actually_ reject Chalcedon. The situation has now been retrospectively understood to have resulted from what was largely a terminological misunderstanding. Consequently, Oriental has been recognized by Catholic and Orthodox churches officially to be orthodox, and the definitions at Chalcedon acknowledged to have been incomplete, and the ancient sanctions of excommunication leveled against the Oriental Orthodox churches to be undeserved. As the Orthodox report puts it, “we have now clearly understood that both families have always loyally maintained the same authentic Orthodox Christological faith, and the unbroken continuity of the apostolic tradition, though they have used Christological terms in different ways … Both families agree that the lifting of anathemas and condemnations will be consummated on the basis that the Councils and Fathers previously anathematized or condemned are not heretical.”[^c06-9]

There is nothing, consequently, in the doctrine of infallibility that requires us to reject a need for the Church to be self-reflectingly critical of its own past (even _doctrinal_ past) – and so embrace its identity as an _Ecclesia semper reformanda_ (incidentally, a slogan from the Reformers that the Catholic Church has now adopted[^c06-10]). Faith in God’s Providence regarding the Church therefore does not rule out the need for action and even protest from its members against abuses, whether practical or doctrinal. Yet there is an obvious and staunch moral difference between two ways of protesting abuses in an apparent cause for “ecclesiastical disobedience.” That seems to me most obviously illustrated by the subsequent development of our modern concept of nonviolent civil disobedience. Martin Luther King Jr., in responding to criticisms of his nonviolent peaceful civil disobedience in Montgomery, underlined that his protests aimed not to undermine respect for the law. He was not calling into question the _legitimacy_ of the United States or its government; “There will be nobody among us who will stand up and defy the Constitution of the nation.”[^c06-11] Famously, in his debates with Malcom X and the Black Power movement, King rejected the violence they advocated – he “could imagine nothing more impractical and disastrous.”[^c06-12]

Instead, King took the defining characteristic of his movement that the protestors _accepted the penalty imposed, in conscience,_ and suffered gladly for their beliefs. “I submit to you that any individual who decides to break a law his conscience tells him is unjust and willingly accepts the penalty for it, is at that moment expressing the very highest respect for law.”[^c06-13] King’s proposals derived directly from what he (I think rightly) took to be Christian principles: to return love for hate and aim by protest to transform the enemy into a friend; to have “a willingness to accept suffering without retaliation,” to avoid “not only external physical violence

---

<!-- PDF page 259 -->
**[p. 246]**

but also internal violence of spirit,” and to be convinced that “the universe is on the side of justice.”[^c06-14] I see in King’s proposals what clearly differentiates a _reformer_ from a _heretic._ A _heretic_ can be distinguished by their unwillingness to pay the penalty for their conscience, not to accept suffering without retaliation, but to end in a violence of spirit which would bring anarchy to the Church, rather than respect for the law. By contrast, a willingness to suffer with _joy_ for the sake of Christ’s name, even when persecuted by their own Church, has always impressed Christians – of every stripe – as a clear sign of holiness.

There have been many courageous and virtuous reformers who have denounced abuses in the Church from the rooftops. My own Dominican Order can count a large number. My own heroes include Catherine of Siena, who admonished fearlessly the Pope, the Curia, and the College of Cardinals as to the failures in their duties, and Girolamo Savonarola, who for criticisms of Alexander VI paid the price of his life and reputation, dying an excommunicate who was burned at the stake as a false prophet and heretic. Savonarola, like Catherine, has nevertheless been venerated as a saint by many other canonized Catholic saints (e.g., Philip Neri) and probably will end up canonized alongside Catherine. A great modern theologian of my Order, Yves Congar, was silenced by the Church’s authorities for several years (due to an unfair suspicion of heresy) before being allowed to publicly teach, preach, and write. In his classic work _True and False Reform in the Church,_ Congar speaks from personal experience in describing the spirit that motivates true reform rather than heresy or schism: “Genuine church reform can never accept either chaos or anarchy; that would be contrary both to the charity that is the heart of the church and to the ordered structure that was given to the church by the Lord.”[^c06-15] To be a reformer, rather than an anarchist, is to embrace that “the universe is on the side of justice” – that the Church _is_ infallibly guided by the Spirit – and therefore that it would never be _harmful_ to our souls that we put our trust in the testimony and guidance of the whole Church, past and future. This belief has Christ’s own backing when He gave as the procedure for dealing with disagreements among believers: “… if your brother sins against you, go and tell him his fault between you and him alone. If he hears you, you have gained your brother. But if he will not hear, take with you one or two more, that ‘by the mouth of two or three witnesses every word may be established.’And if he refuses to hear them, tell it to the church. But if he refuses even to hear the church, let him be to you like a heathen and a tax collector” (Matt 18:15–17).

Renowned Catholic theologian Louis Bouyer, himself a former Lutheran pastor, dedicated a book (_The Spirit and Forms of Protestantism_) to exploring why, despite the positive biblical and patristic support for the great insights of Protestantism, unity was apparently so far outside of reach. Bouyer was critical that, while the Reformation was not wrong to place the Word of God and its authority above all human authority, nevertheless, “in place of the divine authority in the Church Protestantism set up purely human ones, with the inevitable consequence of an enslavement of man to man, stifling the idea of personal religion and Christian liberty. … In so far as they manage to attain any authority at all, it is always the authority of a man, either of a founder or organiser or of a simple minister, and, if that fails, they

---

<!-- PDF page 260 -->
**[p. 247]**

break up into fragments, to the sole profit of the authority of each individual, his private views, tendencies or experiences.”[^c06-16]

While two groups could begin by having faith in the same things, due to a common tradition which is often unthinkingly and unconsciously influencing their views, one group’s rejection of shared dogmatic authorities _in principle_ inevitably will lead them to develop views at variance with the other and with the tradition. It is unsurprising to find that rejecting dogmatic authority in principle leads to theologians like Barth, Tuggy, Hart, and Nemes calling for widespread, radical rejection of classical Christian theological conclusions. Rejecting their conclusions (e.g., Arianism, anti-Trinitarianism) does not require rejecting demands for reasons or evidence that the orthodox Christian views are correct – as there is such evidence. But no such reasons or evidence are going to make public demands on others such that every believer simply has obligations on pain of irrationality to accept all those orthodox Christian views, nor will it make the right demand in faith that we reject them. If we admit the principle that there are no present ecclesial authorities to which we have _normative_ obligations, then all that is left is human reason and tradition.

Without any demands that our faith ought to be _responsive_ to the right counterevidence from the Christian community’s decisions over time, historical Christian beliefs (both orthodox and heterodox equally) will be presuppositions inherited from mere human authority and tradition. The argument of this book, then, is that affirming authority is what allows Christians to have intellectual humility regarding what counts as private theological opinion and what does not, permitting dialogue in the light of shared authorities. Without shared dogmatic authorities in the _present_ ecclesiastical community whose judgments could constitute potential counterevidence to our theological views, we are left with appeal only to what is past (including past ecclesial decisions!). Every person will constitute their own authority regarding what faith requires them to believe, brooking no counterevidence from anyone else. It is obvious in this case that, if theological disagreements about serious matters arise, all Christians _could_ do is either form their own sects with a bunch of like-minded people or pronounce the matter irrelevant to Christian faith – no dialogue toward a common profession of faith is really possible.

Despite what seems initially a well-known critique of Protestant theology, the thesis of Bouyer’s book (like mine in this book) was ultimately the other way around: “the essence of Protestantism lies, not in any negation, but in certain great positive assertions of Christianity.” Bouyer’s argument lay in his criticism that much of what ended up being a cause for division among the Reformers had been outside of those great positive assertions and principles which they held from Scripture; “In the actual development by the Reformers of these principles, there were inserted at the outset negative elements having no intrinsic connection with them, in fact, in formal contradiction with the Scriptural teaching the Reformation claimed for itself.”[^c06-17] He therefore defended Catholic principles from the perspective of the Reformation, and vice versa.

For instance, he argued that, “the Word of God is really sovereign only in so far as He himself continues to utter it … this Word can keep its sovereignty only

---

<!-- PDF page 261 -->
**[p. 248]**

where the divine presence continues to be; in other words, where the Church is not merely an abstraction drawn from the ideas contained in scriptural texts, but a reality created and kept in being by the ‘apostolate’ of the Son of God, of the living Word made flesh, prolonging himself in all times and places, in human form, by the ‘apostolate’ of those the Son sent in his turn, as he had been sent by the Father.”[^c06-18] Bouyer therefore called upon Protestant Christians to reexamine whether their rejection of dogmatic authority, as well as other Catholic doctrines, _really did_ constitute a reason for schism, or whether their “anti-Roman” attitude and rejection of dogmas was a product primarily of prejudice and historical misunderstanding, leading them (in the end) to contradict their own stated principles. He likewise challenged Catholics to reassess their evaluation of well-known Protestant doctrines and whether they could be given a Catholic sense.

After Bouyer’s time, a proliferation of academic work has taken up Bouyer’s approach toward these issues, such as _Was the Reformation a Mistake_,[^c06-19] and informal ecumenical initiatives such as that represented by, _Evangelicals and Catholics Together,_ and the journal _First Things_ have continued Bouyer’s overall project and made strides toward achieving a mutual reception and critique of each side’s practices and principles. Formal theological dialogue with groups such as the Anglican communion or World Council of Churches has produced agreements on our shared doctrinal principles, most notably that agreement with the Lutheran World Federation, the “Joint Declaration on the Doctrine of Justification,” which while not uncontroversial (and does not resolve all theological ambiguity) has progressively been a subject of increasing consensus among Protestant denominations.[^c06-20] Similarly, on the side of Orthodox-Catholic dialogue, the _Orientale Lumen_ conferences, and the work of the North American Catholic-Orthodox dialogue, has not only helped theologians achieve consensus that the “filioque” is no longer a church-dividing issue,[^c06-21] but has achieved broad theological consensus regarding our common beliefs about the place of synodality and primacy in the history and practice of the Church – and, as already noted, achieve notable inroads in recognizing common Christological confession with the Oriental Orthodox.

Formal high-level theological dialogue between Catholic, Orthodox, and Protestant Christians is necessary but nevertheless insufficient by itself for completing a process that must take place in the minds and hearts of both theologians and ordinary believers. We need not deny that differences exist, let alone hold implicitly that orthodoxy is unimportant or may be put aside for practical aims. True ecumenical dialogue consists “not in a reduction of the faith to the bare minimum required to maintain our chosen extra-ecclesiastical organizations, but in an honest confrontation over differences about things that really do matter.” That is, our incentive for ecumenical dialogue can be founded precisely on common recognition that orthodoxy matters and heresy is bad. And yet things have also moved on _theologically;_ clarity has led to modern theologians recognizing that our situation among Christians is not identical to that which existed either immediately after the Great Schism, the Reformation, or the Vatican I.[^c06-22] Many Protestants and Catholics today similarly endorse sentiments that Reformation Day has become a time for mutual repentance: “… we must confess openly that we have been guilty

---

<!-- PDF page 262 -->
**[p. 249]**

before Christ of damaging the unity of the church”[^c06-23] and the recognition that “the temptations to regard Reformation-era conclusions and condemnations as timeless and above criticism, and in ways that conveniently support the credibility of one’s own religious position” apply to all sides.[^c06-24] Since Pope Paul VI and the Ecumenical Patriarch Athenagoras’ symbolic lifting of mutual excommunications in 1964, and the Joint Declaration of 1965, many steps have been taken to remove other obstacles (e.g., Orthodox and Catholic Christians are currently aiming to explore possibilities for a unified date for the celebration of Easter), as all sides recognize the lamentable state in which we agree on almost every essential point of doctrine and yet persist without visible unity.

My task in this book is similarly premised on this belief that _we must do better_. And I say “we” intentionally, as embracing all Christians. Nobody denies that _each_ of our churches has, in the past, failed to evince pastoral concern for souls and has misused its authority to condemn people in ways that today we find abominable. So too schisms have arisen that divide the Body of Christ, prompted by pettiness and egoism not only of the schismatics, but of the legitimate and lawful ministers of each of our churches, who created an intolerable situation for others. God has nevertheless sent us to witness to His Word to the world and to each other, amid precisely these challenges of our time. Our authority comes not from us but from the Spirit who will teach us what to say (cf. Lk 12:12) and who sends us in Christ’s name. As long as we are prayerful, deliberate, and make our decisions according to the Word that Christ left us, we cannot go wrong – even when we fail to state or express that Word as well as we ought, we know Christ’s Spirit will not leave us as flock without a shepherd, that we will eventually come into greater grasp of the Word and we will always be able to come back and state the truth more fully. The ecumenical movement took its inspiration from Christ’s prayer that all might be one as He and His Father are one (cf. Jn 17:22).

In line with these initiatives, my book proposes that all Christians are as much committed to dogmatic authority, development of doctrine, and visible unity as those of Orthodox or Catholic persuasions. I have not aimed to justify the full scope of doctrines that divide our churches, but rather to show that even the apparently most difficult issue – that of dogmatic authority – can be a subject of mutual agreement, within the right framework. This book aims to show that _all_ Christians today – Protestant, Orthodox, and Catholic – have reason to appreciate the ways in which God’s Word constitutes the Church as a community of faith and the Spirit constitutes Her as a community of love. These bonds are what remain normative for Christians, and what make it sinful for us to violate those demands that God’s Word and Spirit imposes upon us. Acknowledging that our historical schisms and disagreements are the product of all-too-human pride is the first step toward healing. And God’s Word points to a unity _in faith and love_ that cannot be accomplished merely by good wishes. It requires Christians to rediscover and more deeply appreciate that their situation of division in faith and communion _is not what God intends._ That spiritual goal goes not least for the heretics themselves. To condemn a heresy is not necessarily to condemn a heretic – ironically, to call them back to unity can evince the highest form of _pastoral_ care. To become inured

---

<!-- PDF page 263 -->
**[p. 250]**

and indifferent to heresy and schism, by contrast, is an act by which we abandon concern for the _souls_ of our Christian brothers and sisters and let them perish in the wilderness.

Given my diagnosis of the sin involved in heresy, we can see that heretics and schismatics operate within either presumption or despair: they insist on their own way or doctrine, often presenting themselves as martyrs who are unfairly excluded, while making one-way demands on others’ voices or claim on their attention. But they do this because they cannot see that the Church either _can_ be rescued or _ought_ to be worth their personal sacrifices to maintain its unity and therefore abandon Her communion one way or another. But the good news is that these attitudes are founded on falsehoods about God. Pannenberg notes that theological dialogue _has_ made appreciable progress, and (as perhaps the contemporary example of the Oriental Orthodox exemplifies best) “contradictions that we previously tolerated in our understanding of the faith can come to be seen as contradictions in our confession of Christ himself. On the other hand, disagreements that were once regarded as crucial to our confession of faith in Christ may, in the light of a later time, lose their force and validity.”[^c06-25] Pannenberg notes that, among Protestants, “the disagreements of [the past] have lost any significance great enough to divide churches. … This verdict is capable of an intellectual justification, but it is essentially a spiritual verdict, which can take definitive form only through the efforts of those churches and their offices ….”[^c06-26] I too have argued that the apparent obstacles to reuniting our Christian Church – even those that go to the deepest place of division between Orthodox, Catholic, and Protestant theologies of authority and infallibility in the Church – are not at all insuperable.

To embrace an ecumenical frame of mind, to see beyond our differences and to seek common visible unity in a shared confession, practice, and maintenance of the faith is not an optional task for Christians – whether Catholic, Orthodox, or Protestant. We can all agree that it is central to our faith. Whatever the mistakes or shortcomings of perspective in the past, our future has not yet been written. The full, visible unity of Christ’s Church might look unattainable, but the faith Christ left us is that no sins are irreparable and that all things are possible for those who love God, even if we must give up our lives for our brothers and sisters. Those who are moved by the Spirit to see this imperative and duty of unity must, in any event, decide for themselves whether they are to end their lives within the unity of the Church or stay outside of the banquet God has prepared. “Hope does not disappoint, because God’s love has been poured into our hearts through the Holy Spirit, who has been given to us” (Rom 5:5).
